-- 10 of 360 --

Preface
For its Sixth Annual Conference, the Centre of Construction Law and
Management took on the ambitious subject of the environment and its
growing influence in the field of construction law. The subject is perhaps
ideally suited to the multi-disciplinary approach of the Centre, spanning
the full range of legal and technical areas which the Centre espouses. It is
noteworthy that the speakers with a technical background were just as
much at home with their subjects as were the legal contributors, and those
with dual qualifications. This latter category included the Lord Mayor of
London who opened the proceedings with some pertinent observations on
the particular problems of the capital.
This Preface does not seek to summarise or encapsulate the many
themes and strands of the papers delivered at the conference which are
published in full in this volume and, in some cases, updated and aug-
mented following the conference. There were, however, a number of
recurrent issues which may serve as pointers to the subject and perhaps
its future development. Notable among these was the evolution of en-
vironmental protection measures, where new statutes and regulations
seemed to appear in a piecemeal fashion, from a variety of sources not
obviously co-ordinated. While the detailed provisions could often be
obscure an even greater problem was to discover what provisions were
currently in force, and the possible dates for bringing into effect pending
laws. Some of these matters have been the subject of wide press coverage,
such as the Contaminated Land Register. But others have not and the
problem of ascertaining and predicting what the law would be at a future
date could be a worrying matter. This type of problem was by no means
confined to the UK.
The conference was fortunate
	in
being
	able
	to
	cover the
	European
Community
	environment programme,
	including
	a
number
	of future
	and
pending
proposals; and
also the
environmental framework
	in the
two other
major economies
	of the world,
	USA
	and Japan. These
	three
	groupings
indeed form
	an interesting contrast with
much
	in
common
	but also with
major differences. The
	attitude to
	freedom
	of information and public
participation in the environment debate differed sharply between Japan
and the West. It is also of some intcrest to trace the course of environment
Vil

-- 11 of 360 --

Vili Preface
programmes
	abroad which
	have
	brought
	into
force
	measures
	still
at
the
stage of
debate
in
Europe,
such
as
the
US
Superfund.
	|
In response to the challenges of the environment, major new tech-
nologies have evolved, particularly in the leading industrial countries.
These must be the first line of environment protection, rather than the laws
and regulations which, ultimately, can provide only legal sanctions. The
problem of pollution and hazardous waste in its many forms is a very real
one. Few of the delegates will forget the truly horrifying account of
pollution in Eastern Europe, said to have the effect in one instance of
significantly lowering the average life expectancy; and in another example
to have caused major debilitating illness among children resulting in
serious loss of education. One speaker suggested that implementation of
proper environment controls in Eastern Europe would result in a major
collapse of industry with mass unemployment, which could lead to migra-
tion on a scale hitherto unknown. These are frightening prospects which
remind us that pollution is indeed a global problem which cannot be con-
fined to those able to afford a clean environment.
In the West, the major environment problems might be seen as a com-
bination of dealing with historical pollution while attempting to regulate
current industrial practices on an optimal basis, as exemplified by the
acronym BATNEEC (Best available techniques not entailing excessive
cost). An even shorter expression of the problem is the question “‘Jobs or
Environment?”’: how far can we afford to protect the environment? With-
out doubt a large part of the technical solution lies in new and developing
environmental technologies, which ought at the same time to offer con-
siderable potential for economic growth here and abroad.
How then are the principles of environment protection through the
application of law to be developed? Clearly major policy issues arise. It is
thought that in its application environment policy should be flexible rather
than rigid and so capable of reacting to current thought and opinion. The
common law would indeed be an ideal vehicle for this, were guiding princi-
ples to exist. In their absence, we must live by regulation. Perhaps in the
long term, the solution lies in an environmental court capable of enforcing
broad principles, but in real cases, rather than in secondary legislators
drawing up abstract regulations to be applied irrespective of the circum-
stances. Beyond any doubt, this conference showed, through the quality of
the contributions and. the large audience, that environmental issues are
very much alive and a key aspect of the construction and development
process.
This conference was devised and organised jointly by the Centre of
Construction Law and Management at King’s College, London and
Nabarro Nathanson, Solicitors, who also sponsored the conference. This

-- 12 of 360 --

Preface ix
joint approach reflects the policy of the Centre in seeking to reflect the
current interests and concerns of the construction industry and the
professions which serve it, and the policy of Nabarro Nathanson of giving
support to academic ventures of relevance to industry and commerce.
From Nabarro Nathanson’s involvement in the construction and develop-
ment process, we know of the ever increasing role which environmental
issues play. We are sure that this conference has been of considerable
importance in stimulating debate on such issues, and we commend to you
the conference papers which follow.
David Bramson

-- 16 of 360 --

Keynote Address by the Rt Hon the
Lord Mayor, Sir Francis McWilliams
Chairman, Director, Sheriff, Ladies and Gentlemen.
As a Lord Mayor, whose theme for the year is ‘““The City and Industry in
Partnership’, and whose professional background is that of engineer,
arbitrator and barrister, it gave me a great deal of pleasure to be asked to
address you today at your 6th Annual Conference.
My only regret is that another engagement means that I must leave
immediately after I have spoken. My going should not be treated as
cowardice.
The subject of your conference, whereby you will be exploring the inter-
face between environmental issues and construction, together with the
links with legal and contractual matters are all highly topical issues
throughout the world today. Environmental concern is an area of increas-
ing importance in view of the ever increasing anxiety about the future of
the world’s ecosystem.
Recent experience in London has focused primarily on the regeneration
of the former docklands area to the east of our capital city. Central govern-
ment recognised the great difficulties, both social and economic, which
need to be addressed when undertaking urban regeneration on this scale.
The establishment of the London Docklands Development Corporation
with wide ranging powers was clearly an essential pre-requisite for success.
What was essential in establishing that Corporation was that the organ-
isation was given all the powers necessary to undertake such a massive
task, whilst at the same time providing satisfactory safeguards and checks
to ensure that the local populace were properly consulted at appropriate
stages, and that they could acquire a sense of ownership as the plans
progressed into reality.
Fundamental to the whole is a clear need to identify the necessary poli-
cies, strategies and long term funding to underpin the whole plan. In
establishing the policies and strategies it is obviously of great importance
that the local populace is consulted at that stage, in parallel with discus-
sions with potential developers and other business and financial interests
who will come together to undertake the necessary development work.
Xill

-- 17 of 360 --

XIV Keynote Address
There is a clear need for the local populace to feel involved and to have
their aspirations as to the future fulfilled. Whilst it may not be possible to
have a totally consensus approach to each and every element within the
whole plan, it is desirable that there should be some benefit to all parties if
out and out opposition is to be avoided.
One of the more obvious failings of the London Docklands Develop-
ment Corporation was its failure to establish an integrated transportation
policy linked to proposed land uses. In this regard it, would perhaps be
fairer to say that the failure to establish such a policy lay with central
government.
Over the last 10 years the progressive development within the docklands
area east of the City of London has brought with it ever increasing traffic
flows, culminating with the opening of the Limehouse Link earlier this year
which alone has resulted in an increase of traffic at the Tower Hill junction
of approximately 12%. A massive overall increase in traffic of some 30% is
primarily due to the lack of an integrated transport policy and the almost
total dependence of the docklands area on vehicular access rather than a
balance with public transport.
The
	belated
	efforts
to
upgrade
	the
Docklands
	Light
Railway, and
	even
now
	the
uncertainty
over
the future of the
Jubilee
Line
extension,
	fills
onewith
dismay.
If
there
had been
clear
thought
applied
at
the
outset,
these
vital
public transport
links
could
	have
	been
	constructed and
funded
	by
govern-
ment
	in
partnership
with
the
developers
	out of the benefits
that
have
come
from
the
regeneration
of
this
area,
and
the
increase
in
land values
in particular.
One
of the
first
matters
that
must
be
addressed
	in
urban regeneration
is
the
	question
	of
contaminated
	land
	and
	hazardous
	substances and
	how
these
will
be
tackled
and disposed
of,
and
by
whom.
	It is
fundamental
	that
afull
geological survey
is
undertaken
in
order
to
establish
exactly
what
prob-
lems
	exist
within
	the
	development
	site.
Once
	you have
	identified these
problems
	and any
hazardous
	substances
	that
exist,
the
difficulty that
then
arises
	is
handling
	these
	substances,
	in
terms
	of
both
	their
	removal and
subsequent
	disposal.
There
	may
	even be
problems
	with transporting
	cer-
tain
	particularly
	hazardous
	materials. This
	whole
	subject
	is
currently
	a
developer’s nightmare.
There
	are
many
	action
groups
	that
now
	exist
who
	will
watch every
step
that
is
taken very closely
and
will
rightly
expect those
	concerned
	to
deal
with
these
issues in
a
wholly professional and appropriate manner. Short
term stop gap solutions
are
no longer accepted by
the
people
of
this
coun-try,
and I
would be surprised
	if this
wasn’t the case
	elsewhere within the
developed world.
Dealing with contaminated land and hazardous substances
is
an expen-
sive activity which could, of course, have a major impact on the overall

-- 18 of 360 --

Keynote Address XV
development costs and the returns on investment anticipated by
developers. Nevertheless, these are initial costs that must be properly iden-
tified and must be borne by those who seek to derive benefit in the future.
It is therefore important that whole life cost analyses are undertaken at
the outset, because too often in the past developers have been accused of
not undertaking the necessary initial investigations and thereby identifying
all those additional early costs, only for the contaminated land issue to
arise once development was started and the major environmental concerns
commence at that time. It is very much harder for the developer to absorb
what are potentially massive additional costs at that stage, at least readily!
A further reason that often arises with major urban regeneration pro-
grammes is that governments, anxious to procure early achievement pro-
grammes, relax the normal statutory procedures to facilitate the planning
and implementation process. This has to be thought through very carefully
whenever relaxation of statutory provisions is contemplated. The short
term easy solution may well lead to medium and long term problems for
organisations other than the initial developer and this causes massive
resentment locally and with those organisations, generally the local author-
ities, who have to pick up the bill.
Even if all the necessary pre-planning stages have been properly under-
taken, it is still absolutely essential that the whole construction programme
is undertaken with the necessary supervision and control. The removal of
hazardous substances I have previously mentioned. But the impact on
individuals, both those working on construction sites and those living close
to construction sites, can be quite marked. These problems relate to sub-
jects which go under the general heading of health and safety and are
generally covered by appropriate legislation.
Developers and their contractors must ensure that they take every poss-
ible measure to reduce the incidence of noise and dust, and possibly even
vibration, to acceptable and pre-determined limits. These limits may be set
by local environmental health officers, but they may also be the subject of
consultation, discussion and agreement with the local populace. Similarly
access routes for construction vehicles must be identified in advance and
must be complied with at all times.
In
my own Authority we recognise
the
need
to
control
activities
on
site,
and
in
particular
in relation
to
the interface
between
building
activities
and
highway
users.
Some
	6 years ago
the
Corporation
	of
London
	established
a
Considerate Contractors
	Scheme
	and
	this
has operated very successfully
with limited additional resource implications ever since.
The
	basic principles of
this
scheme require
	all
builders to register with
the Corporation and
to
undertake certain obligations whilst executing their
works. These range from simply providing safe hoardings and scaffolding,

-- 19 of 360 --

XVi Keynote Address
which are generally decorated, but always well lit both at head level and at
foot level, conspicuity being the key.
There must also be no wires, nails or other snags on which the clothing of
passers-by could be caught. The footpath and highways immediately adja-
cent to all construction activity must be kept clean and free of dust and
mud at all times. In general, works must be conducted having regard to the
safety of both the people working on the site and anybody passing the site.
This initiative has been welcomed in the City of London both by de-
velopers and the general public -— efficiently organised sites are safe sites
and are more economic to operate! |
Failure to comply with the appropriate standards that have been agreed
in connection with each construction activity can lead to lengthy legal
action, frustration and cost, and there are really no winners in the end once
problems have deteriorated that far. “Bridges” must be- built at the begin-
ning and they must not be dismantled until the very end. That leads me to
my final point within this address, the question of tidying up and leaving
the area in a proper condition on completion of all construction activity.
Many excellent projects of varying scale are spoilt, along with the reputa-
tions of the developers and the companies who carry out these works,
simply because they do not tidy up the area disturbed by their activities on
completion of the major works. It may well be that a separate contract is
proposed to undertake this work, but should this be really necessary?
In my experience, it is overlooked on too many occasions and leads to
alienation of the local populace just at the point in time when they should
be welcoming the completion of any major new project and all the associ-
ated benefits, both social and economic, that this will bring. The tidying up
of an area on completion of the works should be part of the original plans
for the site and should not be compromised in any way or at any time by
the developer or his contractors.
This has
been
	a
most
	satisfying
	occasion
	for
me.
	Thank
	you
	for
your
patience,
and
I wish
you
all
a fruitful
and stimulating conference.

1 Environmental protection: statutory provisions in
the United Kingdom
Peter Aeberli
Introduction
There is a growing body of law in England and Wales that is more or less
directly concerned with controlling human activity in order to prevent or
minimise harm to the environment by pollution of air, water or land. This
paper is an introduction to the central core of this growing body of environ-
mental law. It does not cover more specialist areas of control such as those
dealing with nuclear processes or genetically modified organisms.
The nature and sources of domestic environmental law
The bulk of domestic environmental law is statutory. Nevertheless the
common law has a role in controlling polluting activities.
Common law controls over pollution
Common law actions in public and private nuisance, Rylands v Fletcher’,
trespass and negligence may provide remedies to those affected by pollu-
tion. Given the difficulty of establishing fault in relation to polluting
activities the expansion of the strict liability torts might have proved useful,
but this development was blocked by the House of Lords in Cambridge
Water Co. v Eastern Counties Leather Plc.?
The principal difficulty with the common law as a means of pollution
control is that, generally, it only recognises individual rights not environ-
mental rights.
Exceptionally, activities that materially affect the reasonable comfort
and convenience of a class of subjects may give rise to an action in public
nuisance. Criminal sanctions can be imposed and injunctive relief can be
sought by the Attorney General or, in certain circumstances, by local
authorities.
1 (1868) L.R.3.H.L.330 (H.L.); affirming sub nom. Fletcher
v
Rylands (1866) L.R. 1 Exch.205.
2 The Times 10 December
1993.

-- 39 of 360 --

4 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Statutory controls over pollution
Historically, statutory protection for the environment has been piecemeal.
The Environmental Protection Act 1990 indicates a move towards a more
general system of environmental protection. Nevertheless the piecemeal
approach remains dominant and there are numerous statutes containing
measures for the protection of the environment.
Apart for this piecemeal approach to environmental legislation there are
other difficulties with this area of law. :
The widespread use of appointed day orders to bring into force provi-
sions of statutes that have previously obtained the Royal Assent can pro-
vide a trap for the unwary. The Environmental Protection Act presents a
particular difficulty in this respect as the timetable for. its full implementa-
tion has been delayed. ,
Great reliance is placed on delegated legislation, not simply to flesh out
particular statutory provisions but to enact what is, in effect, primary legis-
lation. Thus, many environmental law statutes envisage the use of regula-
tions to enact European Community environmental law directives into
domestic law. Wide powers may be reserved to the Secretary of State to
determine the processes and substances to be controlled by the legislation,
to direct the nature and manner of controls over particular activities and to
issue guidance notes indicating how controls should be exercised.
Regulatory
bodies
exercise
considerable
discretion
in
operating
what
are,
apparently,
	mandatory
	controls
	and
sanctions.
	Thus,
pollution offences
	are
generally
expressed
in
terms
of
strict liability.
Nevertheless regulatory bodies
entrusted
	with
enforcement
	are
reluctant
	to
bring
prosecutions
	unless the
conduct
	resulting
	in
the
offence
	is,
in
some
	way,
	wilful or
reckless
	or
the
consequences
	are
extremely
	serious.
	In
fact
this
discretionary
	approach
	to
prosecution
has
gained
official
recognition with
the
publication
in
1989
of the
National Rivers Authority’s
Guidance
Note
on
Enforcement and
Prosecution.
The
basic structure of
domestic environmental
	legislation
Despite
	the
apparent
	diversity,
much
	recent
	environmental
	legislation
has
certain principles in
common.
	Action should be taken
to
prevent
damage
to
the
environment, any environmental
damage
that
does occur should, preferab-
ly,
be
rectified at
source and
the polluter should
pay.
Furthermore
	there are
similarities
between
the regulatory systems imposed under
different statutes:
-
	Administrative responsibility
	is
delegated
	to
	regulatory authorities,
while the Secretary of State retains significant powers over the activities
of that authority and those it regulates;

-- 40 of 360 --

Statutory provisions in the UK 5
— control over potentially polluting activities is usually exercised through
the use of conditional authorisations which can be varied or revoked in
certain circumstances;
— regulatory authorities are entitled to levy fixed and periodic fees from
those it regulates to recoup administrative costs;
— appeals lie from the authorisation decisions of regulatory authorities,
generally to the Secretary of State;
— authorisations may be subject to an implied condition that the activity
must be carried out using the best available techniques not entailing
excessive cost (‘‘batneec’’) or, occasionally, the best practicable means
(“‘bpm’’), to avoid pollution;
— regulatory authorities have wide investigatory powers, they can require
polluters to clear up or abate pollution or can take action themselves
and seek reimbursement from those responsible;
— criminal sanctions are imposed on those who carry out activities with-
out the necessary authorisations or in contravention of authorisation
conditions. Culpable employees or company directors can be pros-
ecuted as well as their companies. Substantial, often unlimited, fines
and, occasionally, imprisonment can be imposed; and
— persons affected by pollution are enabled to bring civil proceedings in
order to obtain compensation from those responsible.
An overview of domestic environmental legislation
At
present,
domestic environmental
legislation
provides
a
small
measure
	of
integrated pollution control
and
more
	general,
separate systems
of control
for
water,
	air
and
	land.
	There
	is
also
a
range
	of
miscellaneous
	pollution
controls and,
in
some
	circumstances, planning
	authorities
	are
required
	to
consider
the
environmental
	implications
of
proposed developments.
Integrated pollution control
The Environmental
	Protection Act
establishes
	a
limited
	system
	of integ-
rated pollution control over
major
industries
in
respect of
emissions
to
air,
water
or land.
Industrial processes subject
to
integrated pollution control
	are
those
	that
have most
potential for
harmful pollution because
of
size,
toxicity
and impact
on the
environment
	as a
whole.
They
are
designated by
the Secretary of State
under
	the
Environmental Protection (Prescribed Processes and Substances)
Regulations 1991. Prescribed processes are listed either under Part
A
or Part B.
Part
	A processes are subject to integrated pollution control by Her
Majesty’s Inspectorate of Pollution (“HMIP’’). Part B processes are subject

6 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
to air pollution control by Local Authorities. Certain substances are also
prescribed for emission to air, water or land and are subject to prohibition or
control. These controls will not simply maintain the status quo but will be
used to achieve a progressive improvement in quality standards and objectives.
The Part A list of prescribed processes includes major fuel and power,
waste disposal, mineral, chemical, ferrous and non-ferrous metal, cement,
asbestos, coatings, and timber facilities.
Such processes are regulated through a system of authorisation, supervi-
sion and enforcement procedures.

	Authorisation

	conditions.

between

	Harm

	It is

or

	the

or

	must consult with and take account of the views of other regula-

HMIP must periodically review authorisations. In certain circumstances

Supervisory procedures
HMIP inspectors have wide powers over premises on which prescribed
processes are conducted. These include powers of entry and inspection, to
take and sample articles and substances and to require questions to be
answered and records produced. These powers, particularly those concern-
ing the answering of questions and the production of documents are subject
to safeguards.
HMIP is required to maintain public registers containing specified infor-
mation relating to its authorisations, notices and other decisions.

8 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Water pollution control
The Water Resources Act 1991 provides controls over the pollution of
controlled waters. Controlled waters include all coastal, inland freshwater
and ground water.
The controls are administered by the National Rivers Authority
(“NRA”) save as regards discharges to controlled waters from Part A
prescribed processes. These are dealt with by HMIP under integrated
pollution control, after consultation with the NRA.
Authorisation procedures
Those
	wishing
to
discharge matter
	into
controlled waters
	should
	obtain
	a
discharge consent
from
the
NRA.
	Normally
applications
are
publicised
and
when
	evaluating
these,
the
NRA
	must
consider
representations
made
	to
it
along
	with
	water
	quality
	criteria
	and discharge
	limits laid
down
	by
the
Secretary
of State
giving
effect,
for
instance,
to
European
	Community
	and
other international
water
quality
controls.
The
NRA
	can attach
conditions
	to a
consent
	as
it
thinks
fit.
Conditions
can
relate
to
matters such
as
the
manner
	and
nature
of the
discharge and
its
pre-treatment
	to
	minimise
	pollution.
	The
	NRA
	has
	wide
	powers
	to
prohibit
	discharges
	to
controlled waters
	either
	absolutely
	or
	subject
	to
conditions.
The
	NRA
	is
obliged
	to
review consents periodically and,
	in
certain
circumstances,
it
can
modify
or
even
revoke them.
The
	Secretary
	of State can
	also
designate
	Water
	Protection
	Zones
	in
order
to
prevent
	or
control
	discharges
	of
poisonous, noxious
	or
polluting
matter
	from
	specified
activities
into
controlled
	waters.
	He
can
	also
desig-
nate Nitrate Sensitive
Areas.
Special
	procedures
	apply
	to
sewerage
	undertakers.
	They
	must
	obtain
discharge consents
	from
	the
NRA.
	In
turn,
they have powers
	to
control
discharges
into their
sewers
under
the
Water
Industries
Act
1991.
This
Act
permits occupiers
	of
premises
	to
discharge surface water and domestic
sewage
into
sewers but provides
that
trade effluent can
only be discharged
into
a
sewer with the
consent
of
a
sewerage undertaker and
on

	of
an
appropriate charge. Certain types of discharge, such
as
petroleum
spirit
and calcium carbides
are
prohibited.
Applications
to
discharge trade effluent
should identify matters such
as
the
composition, frequency and amount
	of the
proposed
discharge. In respect of
certain types of discharge,
	‘“‘special
category effluents’, a
sewerage under-
taker must, unless
it
refuses consent, obtain the Secretary of State’s deter-
mination whether the discharge should be prohibited or permitted subject to

-- 44 of 360 --

Statutory provisions in the UK 9
conditions. These functions are delegated to HMIP. Where the effluent is
produced by a Part A prescribed process, it is not treated as a “special
category effluent”, instead authorisation must be obtained both from HMIP
under integrated pollution control and from the sewerage undertaker
Special category effluents are listed in the Trade Effluent (Prescribed
Processes and Substances) Regulations 1989 as amended (‘“‘the red list”).
This gives effect to European Community obligations in respect of dis-
charges of dangerous substances.
Sewerage undertakers must have regard to the need to allow trade
effluent to be discharged into public sewers and to dispose of such dis-
charges. Nevertheless they can, in appropriate circumstances, refuse con-
sent or give consent subject to conditions similar to those that can be
imposed by the NRA. They will be particularly concerned to ensure that,
after treatment, discharge to controlled waters complies with the condi-
tions imposed by the NRA. Sewerage undertakers can, in certain circum-
stances, vary existing discharge conditions.
Supervisory procedures
NRA inspectors have similar supervisory powers to those of HMIP inspec-
tors. The NRA is required to maintain public registers of its activities.
Enforcement procedures
There is a range of offences of causing or knowingly permitting
unauthorised discharges to water covering matters such as entry of
poisonous, noxious or polluting matter or solid waste to controlled waters;
entry of matter, other than trade or sewage effluent, to controlled waters
from a drain or sewer in contravention of a prohibition; discharge of trade
or sewage effluent to controlled waters; discharge of trade or sewage
effluent in contravention of a prohibition from buildings or fixed plant to
land or lakes or ponds which are not inland freshwaters; and entry of
matter to inland waters which tends to impede their flow in a manner
leading or likely to lead to substantial aggravation of pollution, or the
consequences of pollution, from other causes.
For
the
purposes
	of these offences prohibitions
	may
	be
notified
	by
the
NRA. They
	are also
	deemed
	to exist in
respect of discharge
	limits or
prohibitions affecting prescribed substances.
Where
	there has been contra-
vention of
a
prohibition,
	a
discharge licence will not, generally, provide
a
defence.
Sewerage undertakers have additional defences where the contravention
resulted from unauthorised discharges into their sewers.

-- 45 of 360 --

10 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Fines of up to £20,000 and/or three months imprisonment can be
imposed in the Magistrates’ Court and unlimited fines and/or two years’
imprisonment on indictment. In certain circumstances, the NRA can carry
out preventative or remedial works in respect of pollution of controlled
waters. It can recover the reasonable cost of such works from those respon-
sible. It may seek injunctive relief in the High Court to prevent pollution of
controlled waters.
There is a range of offences relating to sewers including discharge of
matter likely to injure a sewer or interfere with the free flow of its contents
or their treatment; discharge of chemical and heated refuse or liquids
which are dangerous or likely to cause injury to health; and discharge of
petroleum spirit or calcium carbides.
An exemption from these offences is given to those who discharge trade
effluent into a sewer in accordance with a sewerage undertaker’s consent.
Fines can be imposed in the Magistrates’ Court and unlimited fines and/
or imprisonment on indictment.

Under

Those

	If

	If

	this will
	be

	for

new controls are
now,

Authorisations are subject to an implied condition that the process will
be carried out using the best available techniques not entailing excessive

cost (“‘batneec’’) for preventing or, where not practicable, reducing to a
minimum and rendering harmless, releases into the air of substances
prescribed for air and for rendering harmless other substances which might
cause harm if released into air.
Express conditions can be imposed covering a similar range of air pollu-
tion matters to those covered by HMIP. Authorisations must be
periodically reviewed and, in certain circumstances, can be varied or

Local

The Clean Air Act
Further
	controls over
	emissions
	of
	dark
	smoke,
	dust
	and
	grit
	from
chimneys
	and
industrial
and
trade
premises
	are
provided
in
the
Clean
	Air
Act
1993.
These
	controls only apply
to
processes
	that are
outside
Part I of
the
Control
of
Pollution Act.
It
is,
in
general,
a
criminal offence
to
emit dark
smoke
from
a
chimney
or
from
industrial
or
trade premises.
In Sheffield City
Council
v
ADH
	Demoli-
tion
	Limited (1984) L.G.R.
	177 the later
	offence was held
	to
have been
committed by
those responsible
	for a
bonfire emitting dark
	smoke
	on
	a
demolition
	site.
There
are various statutory defences
to
these offences. In particular, with
regard
to
smoke from industrial and trade premises,
it is a
defence
	to
have
taken
all practical steps to prevent or minimise emissions. Regulations also
give limited exemptions to certain types of activity, including burning of

-- 47 of 360 --

12 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
certain types of waste from demolition works and certain activities relating
to road resurfacing works.
The Clean Air Act also imposes controls and prohibitions over the emis-
sion of dust and grit from furnaces and provides for the authorisation of
certain categories of furnaces and chimneys.
The Act also allows for the designation of Smoke Control Areas and for
further constraints on the emission of smoke in such areas.
Land pollution control
Land
pollution
is
controlled primarily
through
regulation
of
waste

ment.
Prior

Environmental
	Protection
Act
control over

	provided
	by

Control
	of

	Act
	1974
and

	(Amendment)
	Act
1989.
The Environmental
Protection
	Act
	introduces
	greater controls over
	waste
	management
	and
separates
the

of
waste regulation
and

disposal.
The
	transition
	to
	the
	new
	statutory
	regime
	is
not yet
complete
	and
various provisions
of
the
Environmental
	Protection
Act, particularly those
concerning

	licensing
and

are

The Environmental
	Protection
	Act
	regulates
	the
	management
	of
controlled waste
from

to

deposit
or

Controlledwaste
	comprises household,
	industrial
	and
commercial
	waste.
	This
defini-

is
further
	elaborated
	in
the
Act and
	in
regulations.
	Waste
	includes
scrap, effluent,
	unwanted
	surpluses
	from
	processes,
	broken
	or
worn
	out
items
	and

	material. Industrial

Different regulatory authorities are
responsible for different aspects of
the administration of waste control.
The principal licensing and enforcement authorities are
Waste Regula-
tion Authorities (““WRAs’). These are, generally, non-metropolitan

-- 48 of 360 --

Statutory provisions in the UK 13
county councils or, in metropolitan areas, district councils or specially con-
stituted bodies.
WRAs are required to make waste disposal plans covering matters such
as the amounts and methods of disposal of waste and the location of
disposal sites. At present they are responsible for licensing waste disposal
sites under the Control of Pollution Act and for registering waste carriers
under the Control of Pollution (Amendment) Act. In due course they will
be responsible for waste management licensing under the Environmental
Protection Act and for the supervision of closed landfill sites.
Waste Disposal Authorities (““WDAs”’) are, generally, the same local
authorities that are designated as WRAs. Their principal functions are to
arrange for the disposal of waste collected in their area by waste collection
authorities and to provide refuse amenity sites for the public. They have an
involvement in waste recycling. They must carry out their functions
through the use of waste disposal contractors.
Waste
	Collection Authorities
	(““WCAs’’)
	are,
generally,
district or
bor-
ough

They

They

	in

16 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Enforcement procedures
Under the Control of Pollution (Amendment) Act it is an offence for
unregistered persons to transport waste in the course of a business or with a
view to profit. Transport of waste within particular premises is exempted
and regulations provide further exemptions. For instance, producers of
controlled waste, other than building or demolition waste, are, generally,
exempt. There are also various statutory defences to this offence.
On summary conviction fines not exceeding level 5 on the standard scale
can be imposed. In certain circumstances vehicles involved in unlawful
waste disposal can be confiscated.
Under the Control of Pollution Act it is an offence to cause or knowingly
permit the deposit of controlled waste on land, unless that land is occupied
by the holder of a waste disposal licence authorising that deposit and the
deposit is made in accordance with the conditions of the licence. It is also
an offence to use or cause or knowingly permit plant or equipment to be
used to dispose of, or deal with, controlled waste in a prescribed manner
unless the site of the plant or equipment is occupied by a licence holder.
Prescribed processes include incinerating, pulverising, sorting or storing
waste.
Regulations provide
various
	exemptions
	to
these offences.
	Exemptions
include
the
keeping
or
disposal
of
household
	waste
within
the curtilage ofthat
	dwelling;
	the
	deposit
	of
construction
	or
	tunnelling wastes
	on
	land
where
	construction
	works
	are
being
carried
out, for the
purposes
	of
those
works,
or
its
short
term storage on
that land;
storage
of
waste
on
premises
where
	it
is
produced;
	or
short
term deposit
of small
amounts
	of
waste
in
a
builder’s
	skip on
a
highway.
	In
general
	these
exemptions
	do
not
apply
to
waste
	that
is
liable to
give
rise to
environmental
	hazards.
	There
	is
also
a
limited
range
of statutory
defences
	to
these offences.
Fines of
up
to
£20,000 and/or
six
months’
	imprisonment
	can
be
imposed
by
	the
	magistrates’ court and, on
	indictment, unlimited
	fines
	and/or
imprisonment
	up
to
two
years
or,
in
certain
more
	serious
cases,
five
years.
The
	WRAs
	and
	WCAs
	can
	require
	the
	occupiers
	of land on
	whichcontrolled waste
	is
unlawfully deposited
	to
remove
	it
or to
deal with
it
in
specified ways. In certain
circumstances they
can take such
measures them-selves
and recover any
costs
necessarily incurred from
those responsible
for
the deposit.
Under
	the
Control of Pollution Act persons
	who
	unlawfully deposit
or
knowingly cause
	or
permit deposit of waste which causes
	damage may
	beliable in civil
proceedings
for any damage
caused, unless
it
was wholly due
to the fault of the person who suffered
it or was suffered by
a
person who
voluntarily accepted the risk.
Under the Environmental Protection Acct it is

-- 52 of 360 --

Statutory provisions in the UK 17
an offence to treat, keep or dispose of controlled waste in a manner likely
to cause pollution of the environment or harm to human health. It is also
an offence to fail to comply with the statutory duty of care.
Once the waste management licensing scheme is enacted a range of
offences relating to unlicensed management of waste and management of
waste in contravention of waste management licence conditions will be
introduced. As under the Control of Pollution Act, regulations will provide
various exemptions to these offences and a limited range of statutory
defences will be recognised.
The penalties for these offences are similar to those applicable to
offences under the Control of Pollution Act. Offences involving special
waste will be subject to the higher penalties but imprisonment cannot be
imposed for breach of the duty of care. WRAs and WCAs will retain power
to require the removal and treatment of unlicensed deposits of waste and
to carry out such works themselves. The scope of statutory civil liability will
also be widened once the relevant provisions of the Environmental Protec-
tion Act come into force.
Miscellaneous pollution controls
Local
	authorities
	are
	responsible
	for
	administering
	a

other

relating,

Supervision procedures
Provisions controlling statutory
	nuisances
	are
	now
	contained
	in
	the

These

	by

	Statutory nuisances
	comprise
	premises,
	accumu-
lations
or

that are

or

Local

	If a

Persons aggrieved by

	abatement

	Additional

administered by

Under the Control of Pollution Act local authorities can issue “Section
60 notices” in respect of noise from building sites, specifying required

-- 53 of 360 --

18 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
abatement measures. Alternatively, prior approval for proposed abate-
ment measures can be obtained by seeking a ‘‘Section 61 consent”. When
exercising these controls local authorities must consider matters such as the
best practicable means to minimise or prevent noise on the site. Appeals lie
to the Magistrates’ Court.
The Control of Pollution Act also contains provisions under which a
local authority can designate noise abatement zones and impose noise
emission levels on premises in that area. These procedures are complex
and seldom used.
Under the Environmental Protection Act local authorities are required
periodically to inspect their areas for noise nuisances. They can serve an
abatement notice on those responsible, requiring the abatement, restriction
or prohibition of the noise. Where a section 60 notice could have been
served the requirements of a section 80 notice should not be more onerous.
Persons aggrieved by noise nuisance may seek an order from the Magis-
trates’ Court in similar terms to a noise abatement notice.
Enforcement procedures
It is an offence under the Environmental Protection Act to contravene or
fail to comply with the requirements of a statutory nuisance abatement
notice.
There are various statutory defences. In particular where the proceed-
ings relate to industrial, trade or business premises, it is a defence to prove
that the best practical means (‘‘bpm”’) were used to prevent or counteract
the effects of the nuisance. The legislation indicates how this test is to be
applied.
Fines on summary conviction may be up to £20,000.
A
	local
authority
	may
	also
seek injunctive
	relief
in
the
High
	Court
	to
secure
	the
abatement,
	prohibition
	or
restriction of
a
statutory nuisance.
Under
the
Control
of
Pollution
Act
it is
an
offence
to
contravene
	a
section
60 notice
or
section 61 consent.
Under
the
Environmental
	Protection Act
it is
an
offence
to
contravene
	a
noise
	abatement
	notice.
	There
	are
	special
	defences
	available
	to
	those
whose
	noisy
activities are also
controlled under
	the
Control
	of Pollution
Act.
Environmental protection under planning
legislation
Historically, planning legislation had
a
limited role as a
means
	of environ-
mental protection. It
was more concerned with the orderly management
	of
change and with economic arguments than with environmental concerns.

-- 54 of 360 --

Statutory provisions in the UK 19
The use of planning permission conditions to duplicate controls available
under other legislation has been judicially disapproved and ministerial
disapproval has been expressed of attempts to introduce environmental
concerns unrelated to land use, such as air quality objectives, into local plans.
On the other hand the use of the planning system to control environmen-
tally disruptive activities such as mineral works and waste disposal to land
has long been accepted. Planning authorities are obliged to prepare
mineral and waste disposal plans for their areas. Applications relating to
such developments can be considered in the light of these plans.
More recently, there has been a greater recognition of the role of the
planning system in environmental protection. Environmental information
must now accompany applications for certain types of development;
consultation is growing between planning authorities and environmental
regulatory authorities and some encouragement has been given to the use
of planning conditions for environmental protection.
Environmental assessments
The Town and Country Planning (Assessment of Environmental Effects)
Regulations 1988 require those seeking planning permission for major
developments to submit environmental statements along with their
applications. Environmental assessment is compulsory for developments
listed in Schedule 1 of the Regulations such as crude oil refineries, large
power stations, integrated iron and steel and chemical works, major in-
frastructure projects and special waste treatment and disposal facilities.
Schedule 2 lists developments for which environmental assessment will be
required where proposals are likely significantly to affect the environment,
for instance, because of their size, nature or location. These include large
scale agricultural facilities, mineral and other extraction industries, smaller
power stations, bulk fuel storage, waste disposal, urban development, and
medium scale manufacturing, industrial and infrastructure projects.
Local
	authorities can
	determine
	when
	environmental assessment
	is
required
	with
	a
particular application. In certain
	circumstances
	this
deci-
sion
	should be
made
	after
	consultation with
	HMIP
	and
	the
NRA.
	The
Department
	of the
Environment
	has issued
guidance
notes
to
explain
how
the
environmental assessment procedures should be
used.
If
environmental assessment
	is
required, the timetable
	for the
planning
application process
	is
extended and additional publicity
requirements
	are
imposed.
	In addition regulatory authorities concerned with environmental
protection, such
as
HMIP
	and the
NRA, must be notified of the application
by the planning authority. These authorities may
	assist the applicant in
preparing an environmental assessment.

-- 55 of 360 --

20 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
The Regulations stipulate what information must and may be included in
an environmental assessment. In particular information must be given to
enable the main effects of the development to be identified and assessed, to
describe the likely effects on the environment and to identify proposed
measures to avoid significant adverse environmental effects. Information
concerning the general characteristics of the development, its production
processes, and the type and quantity of residues and emissions and their
environmental effects may be included. :
In considering an application to which environmental assessment applies
the planning authority must consider representations made by the notified
regulatory authorities and environmental issues.
Consultation procedures
In addition to consultation procedures relating to environmental assess-
ments there are other procedures under which environmental regulatory
authorities may be consulted. In particular a planning authority must con-
sult the NRA over applications for oil storage or waste disposal facilities.
Where ground water could be adversely affected the NRA is likely to
oppose the application. Consultation is required with the Health and
Safety Executive over developments that involve the manufacture or use of
notifiable quantities of hazardous substances. WRAs must be consulted in
regard to developments concerning landfill sites.
Where the application concerns the redevelopment of contaminated
land or land adjacent to such land Planning Authorities can consider
whether the development takes proper account of the contaminated land.
If not, they can, in certain circumstances, refuse permission or grant it
subject to conditions requiring investigation and remedial action.
Planning conditions and planning agreements
Generally, planning conditions and
	agreements
	should
	not
	be used
	to
impose
controls
that are
provided
for
under
other regulatory regimes. But,
more
	recently,
	Department
	of the
Environment
	guidance
	has
suggested
that
conditions
may
be
imposed
to
protect the
environment from apprecia-
ble risk
of
harm,
but
that
onerous conditions should
not be
imposed where
environmental problems
	are less
certain
to
occur;
	adequate
	control being
provided under other
environmental
	legislation.
The imposition of after care conditions raises particular
difficulties.
His-
torically these have been imposed
	on permissions for landfill sites
	and
mineral developments and they now have legislative recognition. Notice
can be served by the planning authority requiring those in control of the

-- 56 of 360 --

Statutory provisions in the UK 21
relevant land to comply with such conditions. Failure to comply with such a
notice is an offence subject on summary conviction to a level 3 fine.
Future directions
Most future initiatives concerning environmental law are likely to come
from the European Community. The government is, however, actively
considering the further integration of the various environmental regulatory
authorities into a new Environmental Agency: The intention is to provide a
“one-stop shop” for the administration of environmental controls. The
planning system will not be affected. As for existing legislation, it will be
some time before its full implications for industry are felt.
There is already some evidence that regulatory authorities are becoming
more intolerant of industries that cause pollution. Government circulars
emphasise the need for fines that reflect profits or savings made by the
offender in committing an offence and Crown Court prosecutions are on
the increase. In one serious case-a fine of £1 million was imposed.
A further uncertainty concerns the use that regulatory authorities will
make of their enhanced clean-up powers. Widespread use of these powers
could impose significantly greater costs on industry than those imposed by
crimitial sanctions. Recent concern over the proposed contaminated land
registers appears to have ignored the fact that it is these clean-up powers,
not the maintenance of registers, that gives teeth to much environmental
legislation.
In consequence purchasers of land will be increasingly concerned to
ensure that they are not acquiring open-ended liabilities for clean-up costs.
This may be reflected in an increased demand for environmental audits and
vendor warranties or indemnities. These may, in certain circumstances, be
appropriate but they should not become blanket requirements. A realistic
assessment of the actual risks imposed by the legislation in the light of the
past and present use of the land should be made.
Uncertainty also surrounds the effect of recent environmental legislation
on the civil liability of polluters. The legislative tendency is to favour a
limited strict liability regime when imposing civil liability for environmen-
tal harm. It is increasingly unlikely that this tendency will also infect the
common law and lead to a re-emergence of the strict liability torts. In any
event the courts will be left to resolve difficult issues of causation and
damage assessment.
Finally, it should not be assumed that existing insurance arrangements
will cover all the risks imposed by this growing body of environmental law.
Public liability policies do not provide indemnity from criminal sanctions
nor from claims resulting from deliberate acts or omissions. They may not

-- 57 of 360 --

22 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
cover clean-up costs imposed by regulatory authorities. Civil liability res-
ulting from gradual pollution may also be excluded. If cover is required,
special environmental liability policies may have to be obtained.

2 Building
	a
greener
	Europe:
	EC
	environmental
legislation
	and
	the
	construction
	industry
Ingrid Persaud
Summary
This paper is an attempt to assess the relevant EC environmental legisla-
tion for the construction industry and to give some indications as to the
shape of future legislation.
The contention is that the EC’s environmental programme prior to 1992
can be characterised as a piecemeal response to consumer sentiment.
While 1992 marks a shift in the scope and nature of the EC’s environmental
policies, nevertheless, the programme continues to be responsive to public
perception of the level of environmental protection required. Arguably the
Community is moving from piecemeal legislation to more grandiose goals
in its fifth environmental action programme! such as “‘sustainable develop-
ment” and is seeking to give a more pro-active direction to Member States.
Furthermore, it has the legal powers to make these plans a reality.
However, these plans are currently being thwarted by a Europe-wide
recession which has created competing goals of protection of employment
and of the environment. This should not give rise to complacency in the
construction industry. A change in economic fortunes and political will
would mean that those aspects of EC environmental legislation which are
presently without much bite, may in fact soon grow sharp teeth. The warn-
ing signs are there.
EC environmental legislation—form and substance
Community legislation in the field of environmental law, as in other
spheres of Community activity, can take one of two binding forms—
The author would like to thank Avinash Persaud, Vice-President, JP Morgan and Michael
Bowsher, Barrister, for their assistance with the preparation of this paper. All errors and
omissions remain the author’s responsibility.
1 “Towards Sustainability—An EC Programme of Policy and Action in Relation to the
Environment and Sustainable Development’, COM (92) Final, 27 March 1992.

-- 59 of 360 --

24 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
regulations and directives.2 A regulation, such as the eco-audit regulation,?
is binding as to both form and substance and confers rights on individuals
which are enforceable in domestic courts from the moment it comes into
force. It is therefore said to have “direct effect” in creating enforceable
rights both against the state and private individuals. Regulations do not
require any implementing legislation.
Directives, such as the environmental assessment directive,‘ are bind-
ing as to the end result to be achieved but leave discretion to Member
States as to the appropriate form and method necessary to achieve the
stated objectives. In order to be binding at the domestic level directives
require implementing legislation. In certain circumstances they may be
enforced against the state or emanations of the state after the date for
implementation has passed.5 It may even be possible for an action in
damages where the state has failed to implement a directive.®
Additionally, there appears to be an obligation to interpret domestic law
in the light of the wording and purpose of a directive even where that
directive has not yet been implemented.’ It is therefore important to be
aware of proposed directives and to be prepared to act as if it were in
place from the time they are agreed.®
By far the most popular form of legislation in the environmental field is
the directive. Of the thirty-eight pieces of relevant legislation only one
takes the form of a regulation.? This is of itself a significant fact. Directives
as a legal instrument leave more discretion to states as to the content, form
and interpretation of Community legislation. As such there is less chance
of establishing a single market in environmental protection.
Whether the Community legislation takes the form of a regulation or a
directive one thing is clear—in an instance where there is a conflict
between Community law and domestic law, it is the Community law which
will prevail. This is the principle of supremacy of Community law that is
seminal to any appreciation of the effect of Community law at a national
level.1°

EC legislation and construction 25
Community
	decision-making
	and
the
enforcement
	of
Community
law
The Community bodies responsible for enacting Community legislation are
principally the Council, Commission and the Parliament. For our purposes
the Commission is the most important since it is the initiator of all legisla-
tion. It is also entrusted with the task of enforcing all Community law—
including of course environmental law. It is therefore this body which sets
the environmental agenda and seeks to enforce it.
The Commission has set out its environmental agenda in the form of
“action programmes”. We are currently on the fifth action programme
covering the period 1993-2000.!2 These action programmes are worth con-
sidering since they are an indication of the general framework within which
the Community is likely to operate. It is suggested that the first four action
programmes, despite the reiteration of lofty ideals, in fact produced nothing
more than piecemeal measures confined to the traditional areas of environ-
mental concern—air, water, land and waste, which we examine later.
To ensure that its agenda can be attained, the Commission has, by a
process of gradual accretion, acquired more powers under the Treaty of
Rome. By the Single European Act}? environmental protection was made
a Community goal in itself. Through the insertion of Article 130r (1) the
Community has a mandate to “‘preserve, protect and improve the quality of
the environment . . . contribute towards protecting human health. . . (and)
to ensure a prudent and rational utilisation of natural resources.”
Article 130r also gives legal backing to the principle that the polluter
pays. Most uniquely though it states that ‘‘(e) environmental protection
requirements shall be a component of the Community’s other policies.”
This is the shot across the bow that we must all note. We have in place the
legal mandate to allow quite extensive Community action relating to the
environment in what might at first sight appear to be totally unrelated
policies, such as the provision of services. At present there is no evidence
that the Commission intends to make full use of its powers but the capacity
to act is there.
By the SEA, Article 130t aiso allows Member States to maintain or
introduce more stringent protective measures. Article 130r (4) further con-
firms the idea of subsidiarity,!4 that is, that Community action should only

of Community law since it gives a qualitative requirement to Commission

to pass legislation concerning the environment by introducing qualified
majority voting for proposals which use Article 130r as its legal basis.15
In addition to these specific measures dealing with the environment, the
Commission also has at its disposal more general enforcement powers
against Member States based on Article 169 of the Treaty of Rome.16
EC environmental policy—the products of the first four action
programmes
We have earlier characterised the products of the first four environmental
action programmes as piecemeal and within traditional areas of concern.
The following is a selection of the most relevant Community legislation for
the construction industry which illustrates this phenomenon.
General legislation
Environmental assessment directive!7
This directive is an expression of the general principle that environmental
law should be concerned with preventative action and therefore seeks to
subject activities, such as construction projects, to an ‘“‘environmental
assessment” if they are likely to have significant effects on the environ-
ment. Accordingly, the Directive states that “the best environmental policy
consists in preventing the creation of pollution or nuisances at source
rather than subsequently trying to counteract their effects” coupled with
“the need to take effects on the environment into account at the earliest
possible stage in all technical planning and decision-making processes.”

Environment Information Directive?
The public in 1993 demands to be informed about the level of environmen-
tal protection that decision makers are aiming for and this is achieved in
part by the Environment Information Directive which provides for greater
transparency of administrative action. Under this directive the public
should be given free access to information held by public bodies on en-
vironmental matters. Information may only be refused on certain specific
grounds, such as defence or sub judice. A refusal to grant information must
be reviewable by a judicial or administrative body.
It is one thing to require access to information but the measure falls short
in so far as it does not require the gathering of information. Therefore if a
state does not have the information already stored your right of access
becomes worthless.

Waste legislation
The Community has agreed a “Directive on waste” commonly refered to
as the Waste Framework Directive*° and a Directive on hazardous waste?!
both of which are of relevance to the construction industry. The Waste
Framework Directive is premised on three notions: prevention of waste,
recycling of waste, and safe disposal of waste which cannot be recycled. It
covers almost all types of waste and introduces a system for waste manage-
ment, disposal and control. Under the scheme Member States are required
to comply with four different strands of the waste policy:
(1) encourage clean technology, prevention, reduction and recovery of
waste,
(2) ensure waste disposal without environmental harm,
(3) establish a regime of authorisation and control for those engaged in
producing, collecting and disposing of waste, and
(4) promote self-sufficiency in waste.
While these are clearly laudable aspirations the legislation lacks proper
enforcement mechanisms or standards that could promote environmental
protection within the goal of a single market. The Directive leaves it to
individual Member States to establish how these objectives are to be
achieved. It should have come into force in April 1993. For the UK it is
clear that many of the requirements were already encompassed by the

applies to machinery, appliances, plant and installations or components
thereof used on civil engineering and building sites.

Water legislation
While there are no environmental measures pertaining to water aimed speci-
fically at the construction industry again there are several general measures
which are applicable. One of the most important measures is the Directive
on urban waste water treatment*+ aimed at the reduction of pollution of
coastal waters and freshwater by setting minimum standards for sewage
treatment and discharge. There are also rules on the dumping of sewage
sludge and a ban on dumping sludge at sea. Secondary treatment of sewage is
regarded as the key safeguard against the dumping of insufficiently treated
waste water. Communities are divided into “‘sensitive’”’ and “‘less sensitive”
areas all of which require the construction of sewage systems by 1998 for the
sensitive areas with a little more leeway for the less sensitive. The financial
implications of compliance are estimated by the Department of the Environ-
ment at £1 billion. Member States have until the end of 1993 to draw up
implementation programmes but already the impact of this directive is
changing government behaviour in that the government no longer require
only primary treatment for estuary and coastal sewage discharges.
Other directives of note include the Directive relating to the quality of
water intended for human consumption (Drinking Water Directive)‘
which lays down qualitative standards for all water for human consump-
tion. It covers private and public water supplies as well as some bottled
water but excludes mineral waters.

EC legislation and construction 33
measure that is not based on existing legislation from a Member State and
is therefore a departure from its previous passivity.
Whilst it is binding on Member States to provide the infrastructure for
eco-auditing, the scheme itself will be a voluntary one. It is designed for
certain industrial activities to provide:
6eé
. a Management tool comprising a systematic, documented, periodic and
objective evaluation of the performance of the organisation, management system
and equipment designed to protect the environment with the aim of:
(i) facilitating management control of the environmental practices;
(ii) assessing compliance with company policies including observance of exist-
ing regulatory requirements.’’55
The objective of the eco-audit is therefore to “promote improvements in
the environmental performance of industrial activities” in three ways:
“(a) the establishment and implementation of environmental protection
systems by companies;
(b) the systematic, objective and periodic evaluation of the environmental
performance of such systems;
(c) the provision of information on environmental performance to the
public.’’>6
This should relate to matters such as emissions to air, land and water. It
should also take into account legal constraints and public perceptions of
the level of environmental protection required.
Should a company decide to take part in this voluntary endeavour the
audit would follow certain defined stages. The first stage involves a
definition, by the company, of its “‘environmental objectives’, that is, “the
detailed goals, in terms of environmental performance, which a company
sets itself’’. The company must also have defined its ‘environmental pol-
icy”, that is, ‘‘a statement of a company’s overall aims and principles of
action with respect to the environment’’.5’ All goals expressed must be
capable of some form of measurement.
The second stage of an eco-audit is the setting up of an “environmental
management” team with responsibility for “‘the aspects of the overall man-
agement function that determine and implement the environmental policy”.>8
Once the environmental objectives have been defined, and policy put in
place with an environmental management team, the “sites” to which the
scheme will apply must be selected. A “‘site” refers to “all land on which
55 This definition borrows heavily from that proposed by the ICC.
56 See Art 1 of the final version.
57 See Art 2(f) and 2(c).
58 See Art 2(d).

-- 69 of 360 --

34 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
the industrial activities under the control of a company at a given location
are carried out, including any connected or associated storage of raw mater-
ial, by-products, intermediate products, end-products and waste material,
and any equipment and infrastructure involved in the activities, whether or not
fixed’’.59 This definition has been recently expanded so that in the construction
sector, for example, it would cover temporary work sites. The “company”’ in
charge of the site is also widely defined to include “any organisation which has
direct operational control over activities at a given site”’.6°
It is for each company to decide which sites it wishes to bring within the
scheme. This is therefore not a scheme about a company’s general level of
environmental consciousness but rather its chosen site-specific exposure.
Once the site has been selected the company must prepare an environ-
mental review of it with a view to establishing an environmental protection
system. The environmental statement of the site must then be prepared and
submitted for validation by external, accredited auditors.6! The auditor’s
evaluation will then be kept by a body deemed competent by the Member
State and the information will be accessible to the public. Audits are to take
place periodically at intervals of between one and three years. The UK has
established a National Environmental Auditors Registration Board who will
oversee the external assessors and decide matters of accreditation.
Those
who
take

in

will
be

EC legislation and construction 35
If one is concerned with ensuring proper environmental protection the
voluntary nature of the scheme, as well as the fact that it is site-specific,
makes the notion of eco-auditing as a method of making companies ac-
countable for the environmental damage they may cause, not worth the re-
cycled paper it is written on. The scheme does not introduce Community-
wide standards and hence does not further the harmonisation process.
Additionally since companies may choose the site(s) they wish to be part of
the scheme it is possible to eliminate those less-green aspects of one’s
business from scrutiny.
The scheme will be reviewed in four years’ time and it is expected that if
the recession has lifted and the political will is there, the scheme may move
from being a voluntary to a mandatory one. In this way, firms who particip-
ate now will have a competitive edge over their rivals. It is also a good
public relations exercise to have an eco-logo displayed. That circle of stars
could keep influential corporate customers and shareholders impressed.
There are also financial incentives available to small and medium-sized
firms to assist with the cost of this initiative.
If the Community were to move towards mandatory “‘green accounts”’
there are two styles of accounting presently followed. There is the ““Anglo-
American” model followed, for example, by Dow Europe which tabulates
pollutants and lists the extent to which these are reduced over time. The
‘“‘Teutonic’’ model instead seeks to capture the difference between what
companies take in and what they produce, that is, a sort of “‘eco balance
sheet’’.64 The Community scheme gives no guidance as to which of these
models it would prefer. In fact companies can decide just how little or how
much information they want to put forward.
Conclusion
The fifth environmental action programme with its theme of sustainable
development could herald a new direction for the construction industry.
The measures so far have admittedly lacked proper enforcement and not
promoted harmonisation. However, if the decision-makers believe that,
with the predicted recovery in economic fortunes by 1994, industry is better
able to bear the cost of, for example, mandatory eco-auditing, then it is
likely that Community policy will evolve towards this goal.
Indeed, post-1992 marks
	a
turning point
	in the
	EC
	as a
whole with
the Treaty on
	European Union and the setting of grand
	targets such
	as
64 For further discussion of these models
see
‘“‘“A
Green Account”, The Economist, 4 Septem-
ber 1993, p.89.

36 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
monetary and political union—all of which is being held back by a deep
recession and the attendant lack of political will. The grand plans remain in
place so one must be aware that priorities could re-adjust themselves in the
future. This would in the environmental field lead to a new generation of
legislation that is less piecemeal but still responsive to consumer sentiment.
This is not to underestimate the contribution the construction sector
makes to society. The activities of the construction sector are vital to
society’s socio-economic development in so far as it provides shelter,
infrastructure and employment. This must be balanced against the fact that
it is also an industry that can be responsible for major depletion of natural
resources, degradation of fragile eco-zones, chemical pollution and the use
of building materials harmful to human health. The challenge of sustain-
able development is about reconciling society’s needs while minimising the
harmful side-effects on human health and the biosphere. The Community
appears poised to grasp this challenge.

3. The international perspective, including Japan
David Jones and Linda Spedding
Introduction
As improvements in communications make the world seem an increasingly
smaller place, potential markets for commercial opportunity become ever
more global. For any company whose activities extend beyond the UK and
Europe into the international arena it is quite clear that it will be essential
to consider various standards, regulations and requirements—both
national and international—which are operative in the jurisdiction in which
they intend to do business. Such considerations will be closely interlinked
with less tangible concepts such as culture, work ethic and economic
history, and as a company’s horizons expand beyond the confines of the
UK and Europe an appreciation of these broader concepts can be
increasingly relevant to commercial decision-making.
Japan is particularly interesting when considering the international
perspective in terms of this broader appreciation, as it is a significant
example of a jurisdiction where economic development has been a priority
to the degree that environmental issues have often been ignored in the
quest for economic achievement. In order to grasp the impact of Japanese
environmental laws on business, it is useful to realise that the Japanese
legal system is fundamentally a civil law system, that is statute-based,
rather than relying extensively on judge-made case law.

38 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Environmental policy
Although we are aware that similarities exist between environmental policies
in different countries and regions of the world, it is true to say that each
nation’s policy has distinctive elements resulting from factors such as its legal
structure, economic system and cultural context. It has been remarked that
Japan’s system of pollution control is a valuable example for other countries,
but that Japan also has lessons to learn from other countries, especially with
regard to nature conservation and public participation.
The structure and form of Japan’s environmental policy-making has not
changed greatly since 1971 when the Environment Agency (EA) was
established. Although, therefore, very recently there have been efforts
towards the greening of Japan through the initiative of a comprehensive
environment bill, these have been blocked by the force of the lobbyists in
other ministries and industries, and therefore the older regime still applies.
As seen below, the EA is not the sole agency concerned with environmen-
tal matters: it does not have any overall control. For example the EA has
no jurisdiction over aircraft noise, generation, storage and disposal of
radioactive wastes, pollution control in specified factories, discharge of
hazardous substances, special government financial measures for pollution
control, settlement of pollution disputes or pollution crimes relating to
human health. Furthermore, the EA only has an advisory role on the
regulation of agricultural chemicals, waste disposal and sewerage and mar-
ine pollution, which are in the ambit of other agencies. Moreover the EA
has no control over land use and development plans. In addition, although
the Nature Conservation Act (1972) and the National Land Use Planning
Act (1974) may be used to control development they are in general ineffec-
tive against the pressures favouring development. It is true to say that the
EA’s efforts to overcome jurisdictional rivalries and to promote an integ-
rated environmental policy have been unsuccessful as a result of ministerial
interference and vested interests. In fact the major ministries have even
moved members to key positions in the EA: the recent death of the
Environmental Bill (discussed below) illustrates (in this instance) the
power of the construction ministry. Japan’s National Environmental Policy
has therefore been affected by the lack of a comprehensive environmental
policy with a strong centralising force at the national level.
At
the local level,
while the local
authorities have played
a
leading
role
in
developing environmental
	policy their
powers
	are restricted. In additiona difficult
consensus has to
be sorted out before environmental laws can be
implemented,
	initially
between
	the

	the other ministries and the local
government, the polluter and the regional administrators. The implementa-
tion of legislation and policy-making is therefore complicated and delayed.

Nevertheless, it can be said that Japan has instituted one of the world’s
most comprehensive environmental legislative systems, under-pinned by
the Basic Law for Environmental Pollution Control (1967). Among the
national environmental protection laws derived from the Basic Law are the
Air Pollution Control Law, the Water Pollution Control Law, the Waste
Disposal and Public Cleaning Law and the Industrial Health and Safety
Law. Selected key national laws concerned with the control of specific
types of pollution are:

— Gas Industry Law (1954)
— Road Traffic Law (1960)
— Electricity Power Industry Law (1964)
