-- 75 of 360 --

40 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
It is important to know that Japan’s national environmental laws emanate
from the Basic Law for Environmental Pollution Control of 1967. The main
feature of that Act is that it authorises the enactment of national environ-
mental laws and authorises the prefectural and local government to imple-
ment and enforce those laws. Breaches of any of the national environmental
laws lead to civil and criminal sanctions. As a result of the Basic Law the
prefectural and local governments can enact stricter laws provided that they
are consistent with the National Environmental Laws. This has happened in
various areas, particularly the waste disposal area, where many of the
prefectural governments have enacted stricter laws and standards. In this
connection the Prefectural Governor has the power to enforce the
legislation: such power may be delegated to a prefectural environmental
agency. Sometimes the municipal governments in the larger cities are
similarly actively involved: they may, for example, require a locally issued
permit to enable the construction of a manufacturing plant having regard to
the plant’s pollution control. In general, a company must submit a report to
the prefectural government describing the facility and planned activity in
some detail prior to the installation, construction or modification of a plant,
that is before commencing any environmentally sensitive operations. A
company should also determine whether one of the national ministries, such
as the Ministry of International Trade and Industry (MITI), or the
municipality requires that such a report be submitted to it. The Prefectural
Governor then reviews the report within a certain time limit (usually 60
days) in order to decide whether to reject, modify or approve the project.
Where there are already serious pollution problems an EIA may be required
which has to be submitted to the appropriate government body including the
results of each study to determine the impact of the proposed plant on the
surrounding environment. Again, in many large cities, such as Tokyo, spec-
ific arrangements exist in the form of an EIA Council which participates in
the process and finally issues an opinion letter to the agency dealing with the
approval or otherwise of the proposed activities.
As in other jurisdictions, in Japan, a plant, once operational, must not
discharge pollutants in excess of national or prefectural standards. Reporting,
monitoring and record retention requirements may be imposed on the plant,
together with provisions relating to the release of hazardous substances.
Where a plant fails to apply the relevant applicable environmental standards
there may be an investigation and issuance of administrative guidance by the
prefecture. The guidance may require corrective action to be taken; failure to
follow the guidance may result in the issuance of an order, violation of which
can result in civil or criminal penalties.
Although it is true to say that individuals or citizen groups do not
appear to have the same extent of voice in Japan as in other places, they

-- 76 of 360 --

The international perspective 41
can report alleged polluters to the local government for an investigation.
Another possibility is that the company may enter into a private
agreement (noted further below) with individuals, groups or the local
government which provides them with enforcement rights. As regards
clean-up, the primary procedural means of obtaining this is by way of
administrative guidance.
The Air Pollution Control Law (1968 as amended in 1970)
This Statute is concerned with ambient air quality standards in order to
control emissions from plant, as well as mobile source controls. It
established national emission standards for sulphur oxides, nitrogen
oxides, carbon monoxide, particulate matter, photochemical oxidants and
hydrocarbons. These limits may vary according to the area and type of
facility.
Environmental pollution control programmes have been established by
the Prefectural Governors specifying discharge limits and discharge reduc-
tion schedules for certain large facilities. Each regulated facility is given an
annual discharge limit for a specific pollutant and newly constructed facil-
ities are subject to even more stringent standards.
The owner of a plant is strictly liable for personal injuries caused by the
emission of regulated air pollutants. However, this strict liability does not
extend to property damage and circumstances of force majeure, together
with any contribution from surrounding facilities, may be taken into
account in assessing liability.
The Water Pollution Control Act (1970)
This Statute establishes water quality criteria for designated Public Water
Areas and sets effluent standards for industries and businesses discharging
certain pollutants to water. Public Water Areas include rivers, lakes, ports,
harbours, coastal areas, public and irrigation waterways, and underground
percolation of ground water.
The
water
quality
criteria are
intended
to
protect
human
	health
and

	In

42 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Prevention of Marine Pollution and Maritime Disaster Law
This generally prohibits the discharge of oils or oily mixtures, noxious
liquid substances or waste from vessels at sea and specifies pollution con-
trol equipment for seagoing vessels. Discharge prohibitions also extend to
offshore facilities. The Maritime Safety Agency enforces this law.
Waste Disposal and Public Cleaning Law (1970 as amended in 1976 and
1983)
This legislation deals with industrial and domestic solid and hazardous waste
disposed in inland and coastal landfills, offshore reclamation sites and the
ocean. The Ministry of Health and Welfare sets the standards for the
collection, treatment, storage and disposal of solid and-hazardous wastes,
while the Ministry of Transport has jurisdiction over coastal reclamation and
ocean dumping of waste and the EA has authority to identify substances as
hazardous. The waste law extends to hazardous industrial waste and
designated hazardous chemicals. The Statute requires a reduction in the
amount of hazardous waste generated and the proper transportation and
disposal of such waste. Generators must submit annual reports and must
obtain the prefecture’s permission prior to the disposal of hazardous
industrial waste apart from the case of on-site disposal. The prefecture also
grants permission to operate a hazardous waste treatment facility.
The Statute regulates the construction and maintenance of waste dis-
posal facilities, the main types of waste disposal practices being inland
landfills, coastal reclamation and ocean dumping. The law has been
described as being less stringently regulated than, for example, the
Resource Conservation and Recovery Act Programme in the US.
Chemical Substances Control Law
The manufacture, use or implementation of all chemicals is prohibited
unless MITI and the Ministry of Health and Welfare have been notified.
Analysis for bio-degradability, bio-accumulation and carcinogenicity must
occur for all chemicals to be manufactured or imported and those that
exhibit a slow rate of degradation, bio-accumulate or unknown
carcinogenicity may be restricted.
Industrial Safety and Health Law (1972)
This Statute requires industrial designers, manufacturers and importers of
machines or other equipment, importers of raw materials and building

-- 78 of 360 --

The international perspective 43
designers and constructors of buildings to comply with health and safety
standards in order to minimise industrial accidents. The Ministry of
Labour, as well as the Central Labour Standards Council administer this
law. Employers must protect workers from exposure to hazardous sub-
stances by testing new chemical substances to determine if they are harmful
and by monitoring air in the workplace to determine exposure levels.
Other environment related laws include the Nature Conservation Act
(1972) which established an administrative framework for nature conserva-
tion and, the Pollution Related Health Damage Compensation Law (1973)
which set up a system charging polluting facilities and automobile owners
for the amount of pollution they generate and distributing funds to certified
pollution victims (considered further later).
The enactment of the wide selection of environmental laws illustrates
Japan’s ability to adapt quickly once organised and motivated. Japan had
one of the most complete statutory frameworks for environmental policy
and some of the strictest environmental standards in the world by the mid
1970s. These laws emphasised compensation and dispute resolution and
in many cases were enacted before their counterparts in the West. To
understand how the law and policy evolved it is important to appreciate
the division of governing powers within the Japanese system: the DIET
(consisting of a House of Representatives and a House of Councillors)
has the legislative powers; the Prime Minister (who is elected by the
House of Representatives) has the executive power; the Supreme Court
of Japan has the judicial power. In addition Japan’s 47 prefectures are
headed by elected governors and the municipal governments by elected
mayors.
As in many parts of the world, in Japan one of the major difficulties
relating to effective environmental protection has been the number of
ministries involved and the lack of co-ordination among them. It is useful
therefore just to run through the relevant agencies that are concerned.
These are:
(1) The national government acting through agencies and ministries and
the Prime Minister’s Office: generally these establish environmental
policy and objectives such as the establishment of air quality stand-
ards. Three main bodies regulate environmental policy: the EA, the
Environmental Pollution Control Committee and the Pollution Ad-
justing Committee. Other ministries such as the Ministry of Health
and Welfare and MIT] are also involved in environmental legislation
and regulating certain industries;
(2) the prefectural governments who (as seen) may
	set
more stringent
pollution standards than those in the national laws; and

-- 79 of 360 --

44 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
(3) Municipal governments in larger cities, such as Tokyo, Osaka and
Kobe which promulgate and enforce environmental laws.
The environmental ordinances of local governments are a key feature in
Japan’s environment policy. For example the Tokyo Metropolitan Govern-
ment was ahead in enacting such ordinances, with regulations for the Con-
trol of Industrial Pollution (1949) Noise (1954), Smoke and Soot (1955)
and Environmental Pollution (1969). The latter ordinance aimed to pre-
vent and eliminate all pollution that could disrupt the. quality of life of
Tokyo citizens and was unique in its emphasis of environmental issues over
the need for economic growth. Osaka also enacted a Pollution Prevention
Ordinance in 1964 and initiated the preparation of Regional Environmen-
tal Management Plans in 1973. Indeed regional requirements are often
noteworthy for their more rigid environmental standards.
Having said this, Japanese environmental policy-making has not dealt
with or has misinterpreted several factors considered in other jurisdictions.
(1) Japan’s environment laws still stress remedial pollution abatement
measures rather than preventive measures, such as EIAs (discussed
below).
(2) Public participation in policy making is very restricted and is usually
limited to letters of complaint, opinion surveys and public hearings.
When hearings do occur they tend to be strictly regulated explanatory
sessions rather than forums for meaningful participation. In spite of
continuous efforts by citizens and lawyers to introduce freedom of
information legislation like that of the USA, the Government is
unlikely to expand public participation because it considers that the
administration can be entrusted to control pollution problems. Since
most citizens have been ready to accept this view, their acceptance
has generally undermined environmental protest groups.
(3) The policy appears unaware of ecological issues, with probable grave
implications for nature conservation. The Basic Law defines pollution
as air, water and soil pollution, noise, vibration, land subsidence and
offensive odours while omitting radioactivity. It highlights known pol-
lution causes which affect human health and the living environment
without considering eco-system degradation unless it affects human
health. (Although some local authorities have adopted a wider defini-
tion, such as that of the Tokyo Metropolitan Government which de-
fines pollution as “any infringement on the environment” and applies
to yet unknown forms of pollution.)
(4) Whereas,
	as seen below, Japan has raised its level of international
activity since UNCED Japan has, historically, failed adequately to

-- 80 of 360 --

The international perspective 45
consider global environmental issues and has been reluctant to con-
trol environmentally destructive activities of Japanese companies
overseas. Environmental laws have not been interpreted to apply
outside Japan and administrative guidance has not covered external
environmental issues. (It should be noted that Japan, in its National
Report to UNCED 1992, has sought to raise its awareness of interna-
tional environmental obligations, as well as the domestic environmen-
tal requirements).
The ways in which environmental policy is implemented in Japan include:
— Administrative guidance;
— compensation system;
— environmental monitoring;
— environmental standards;
— nature conservation;
— pollution control agreements; and
— regional pollution control programmes.
Methods of enforcement
The four main methods of enforcement of environmental legislation are
detailed below.
Administrative guidance
Following Japanese history and tradition, in practice Japanese environ-
mental agencies enforce the environmental laws primarily through admin-
istrative guidance. This is an informal, non-authoritative process whereby
the Government aims to enlist industry’s voluntary co-operation. It in-
volves discussions and consultations and may take the form of non-written
directions, requests, warnings, suggestions or encouragement. A Govern-
ment agency may well issue a number of administrative guidances before
any formal enforcement action is taken. The reason, of course, is that
Japanese bureaucracy has possessed greater power than, for instance,
American bureaucracy because the orders of Government officials were
considered to be almost equivalent to the rule of law; judicial review of
these orders was carefully circumscribed. In addition Japanese culture has
placed considerable emphasis on governmental decision-making through
consensus. It should also be noted that Japanese industry tends to comply
with administrative guidance because the Government has extensive dis-
cretionary authority over the issue of environmental permits (referred to

-- 81 of 360 --

46 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
above) required for the operation of a plant. As a result any violator may
find it difficult to obtain the future co-operation of the Government. His-
torically, therefore, because of the shared basic objective of Government
and industry relating to post-war economic rehabilitation, there is rela-
tively little formal administrative or judicial environmental enforcement in
Japan. The growing emphasis on environmental protection and awareness
has, however, meant an increase in the formal enforcement mechanism
although the basic administrative guidance concept remains deeply rooted
in Japanese bureaucracy.
Private agreements
These are often used to achieve environmental controls whether between
individuals or citizen groups and companies. They result from private party
negotiations or from the initiation of civil litigation; Government agencies
generally do not rely on these agreements.
Criminal enforcement
Many of Japan’s individual pollution control statutes provide for criminal
enforcement and the umbrella law—the Law for the Punishment of Crimes
Relating to Environmental Pollution—makes endangering as well as
harming the environment a criminal offence, establishes a presumption of
causation and provides for punishment of both the individual and the
corporation. However, such provisions are rarely used in Japan. Here again
the main reason is a strong tendency to rely on negotiation and administra-
tive guidance and to avoid direct coercive measures. In addition criminal
enforcement requires referral of the matter to prosecutorial agencies with a
corresponding loss of control by the bureaucracy. In general the agencies
regard the guidance as much more effective than lengthy criminal trials.
The Pollution Dispute Settlement Act
This Act,
which was passed
	in
1970
in an effort to
codify
environmental
mediation and dispute resolution procedures,
	is
primarily
	aimed

	against pollution sources. Relief can
include the

	of the

who
acts as

to

to

facts.
These councillors have been effective in assisting citizens to obtain
relief.

The international perspective 47
The alliance between Government and industry
One of the crucial points which comes out of the examination of Japanese
environmental protection is the alliance between Government and indus-
try. To illustrate both the national and international ramifications of such
an alliance one can look at Japan’s ‘‘new approach” to CFCs - the decision
to phase out ozone depleting chemicals by the end of 1995 (the interna-
tional comment occurs below). MITI hurried to set the deadline earlier to
bring Japan into line with Europe and the USA because both the regula-
tors and the regulated appeared to be whole-heartedly in favour of this
target. In the car industry Honda Motor was the latest large corporation to
set the 1995 deadline following Nissan’s deadline of the end of 1994. Mean-
while Toyota announced the official deadline of the end of 1995. Further, in
electronics, Nippon Electric Corporation (NEC) which has claimed to have
been the first Japanese company to set up an environmental control divi-
sion in August 1970, intends to be on schedule to eliminate all CFCs in
production processes by March 1995; its competitors generally have
announced the year’s end even prior to the Government’s new approach.
MITI has encouraged companies to exchange information on environ-
mental protection measures. The head of NEC’s environmental engineer-
ing subsidiary has said that informal contacts have already taken place
between Toshiba, NEC and Fujitsu on environmental matters and govern-
mental approval has been given to such contacts taking place. In addition
the Government has assisted second and third tiers of the Japanese econ-
omy and small firms, in view of the recession. The initiative of interest free
loans for investments that reduce consumption of ozone damaging chemi-
cals has ensured that industry has not argued against deadlines. The power
industry provides another example of cross industry collaboration: nine
electricity generators have set up a group to prepare policies on carbon
dioxide emissions. (Further comments on corporate initiative occurs below
in the discussion on recent developments.)
Prognosis regarding Japan’s environmental policy
Whereas the EA was created to ensure an integrated environmental policy
as yet this policy has been concerned with the protection of human life and
health and with the abatement of damage caused by environmental
pollution, causing it to rely excessively on technological aspects of pollution
control. In addition Japan has been seen to want to export this approach to
developing nations, supporting the idea that only money and technology are
needed to solve global environmental problems. In order to achieve sustain-
able development, however, a fundamental change in values is required.

-- 83 of 360 --

48 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Unfortunately, however, no consensus exists about the nature of an environ-
mentally sound economy nor has there been any real blueprint for an alter-
native approach to decision making. Moreover there has been no authentic
political leadership on environmental issues and political activity is seldom
tuned into the public interest. Indeed the ruling party has equated public
interest with economic growth. Although recommendations had been made
from 1972 concerning the need for anticipative and preventive policies the
EA report of 1981 noted that very little progress had in fact been made.
Although there have been some improvements regarding pollution research
and monitoring and concerning the intervention of local governments and in
expanding participation, the essential problem remained that neither the EA
nor local government had the power to forbid environmentally structured
projects from being built in relatively clean areas nor their further concentra-
tion into already polluted areas.
Within the Government itself, there has been a sober in the sheer
number of bodies involved in planning and paying for activities to improve
the environment. For instance, a study by a Government audit agency
found that 17 state supported bodies were organising environmental re-
search, many with overlapping programmes. Government policy on haz-
ardous substances is complicated by the involvement of three Government
agencies: MITI, the Ministry of Health and Welfare and the EA.
At the international level Japan’s failure to ratify the Basel Convention
on Toxic Waste Shipments is a case in point to show the struggle between
the different agencies. The three Ministries still argue over whose respon-
sibility it would be to enforce the law. The creation of a single powerful
environment ministry would solve some of these problems. However MITI,
which carries huge political clout as the architect of Japan’s economic
miracle, is still fighting this suggestion.
Insofar as Japan’s environmental policy only defines pollution in terms
of human health its scope and effectiveness has not been fully realised, and
a real consensus at the national level has not occurred. The history of the
development of EIA, as well as discussion of recent environmental issues
(below) in Japan illustrate the problems and opportunities that the process
of improving environmental policy faces.
Environmental impact assessment
A
discussion of
EIAs
	is
clearly relevant
to the construction industry.
It
has
already been commented
	that cultural issues are significant in the develop-
ment of environmental protection;
this
comment

very well illustrated in
the instance of environmental impact assessment. In the traditional
Japanese view environmental preservation is not “right” in the same sense

-- 84 of 360 --

The international perspective 49
as western societies view it. Therefore an essentially western concept like
EIA which emphasises protecting the natural environment from human
destruction could not have been imported into Japan without some adap-
tation. As a result the ‘“‘Japanised’” EIA has become a much weaker
version in comparison with that of the West. If one is to consider the goal
of EIAs to be that of preventing environmental damage according to some
schools of thought an ideal EIA system would:
(1) apply to all projects anticipated as having a significant environmental
impact and address all such significant impacts;
(2) compare alternatives to the proposed project, management
techniques and mitigation measures;
(3) include wide public participation and stringent administrative review
procedures;
(4) include monitoring and feedback procedures;
(5) have a timetable which provides information for decision-making;
(6) be enforceable; and
(7) end in a clear statement which conveys the importance of the likely
impacts and the specific characteristics to non-experts as well as
experts.
Such an ideal system may be reflected upon when considering Japan’s
system. As is clear from the discussion below relating to a comprehensive
Green Bill initiative, EIA in Japan does not have a legal basis partly
because of the Japanese preference for administrative guidance and non-
interventionism, bearing in mind the close relationship between industry
and the government. Whereas there have been recent efforts to make EIAs
compulsory, the close link between industry and government has ensured
that such an initiative has failed and therefore the list of projects subject to
the EIA remains severely limited. When comparing the projects with those
covered under the EC directive and environmental assessment it should be
noted that the following types of development which are considered to
have a significant environmental impact under European law are not
subject to Japan’s national EIA procedures:
— crude oil refineries;
—
	installations for
extracting and processing asbestos;
—
	installations for storing and disposing of radioactive waste;
— integrated chemical installations;
—
	integrated works
for the initial
smelting of cast iron and
steel,
—
	thermal and nuclear power stations;
— trading ports.

Furthermore, whereas the European directive lists eighty types of develop-
ment which may have significant environmental effect and should be
assessed if the Member State considers their characteristics are required,
only eight of these are covered by Japan’s national guidelines.
It should be noted, however, that Japan’s local authority EIAs do tend to
be more comprehensive than the national system (and therefore take
longer) and apply to:
— recreation facilities;
— waste treatment facilities;
— ports and harbours;
— factories;
— distribution centres;
— redevelopment projects; and
— power plants.
Nevertheless there is not any real examination of environmental con-
sequences of policy options. Since assessment occurs at the project stage of
the planning process any alternatives would have long been discarded
despite the introduction by local authorities of a system of environmental
management based on the concept of carrying capacity which integrates
the EIA and environmental monitoring. Another form of assessment
would include a two-tier arrangement in terms of major resource projects
to include aims, demand forecast, safety and environmental protection in
the first stage and the repercussions of individual projects in the second
stage.

It should be said that the EIA system is improved by its higher degree
of technical sophistication because Japan has very advanced systems of
data collection, analysis and simulation. In addition environmental
monitoring is well established. On the other hand, the level of public
participation illustrates how Japan still does not see this as necessary in
terms of a more environmentally and socially conscious decision-making,
in strong contrast to the West. Both the role of public participation (to
provide information to decision makers rather than helping to set policy)
and the mode of public participation (inspection and comment often
occurring after the decision-making process) have narrowed the potential
of the EIA. A further weakness of the EIA system as it stands is that
public hearings are often so formal and given at such short notice that any
opponents cannot prepare for them adequately in terms of large national
projects. Although local authorities generally have more open decision-
making, more public involvement, and more accountability, the EIA does
not seem to have affected the development of any of the projects under
its scrutiny. Indeed since local governments rely to a large extent on
national funding and often there is a close link between industry and the
national Government, the projects are often judged by ministries which
are intrinsically in favour of economic development and therefore of the
project. Indeed review by the EA (which is the only agency concerned
purely with environmental protection) is only at the request of the con-
cerned ministry and only after the final Environmental Impact Statement
has been completed and made public; as the EA’s advice is usually not in
the best interest of the concerned ministry, it is unlikely to ask for such a
review. In addition, the EA is sensitive to its negative image among the
ministries and tries to accommodate them.
Whereas in the US the Freedom of Information Act has played a key
role in opening up the decision-making process, Japan has no equivalent
although some local authorities have produced their own version. Indeed
in Japan EIA has not altered the balance of power in this process and is
often seen merely as a form of project justification.
Finally, Japan’s EIA procedures are often unnecessarily complex,
non-uniform and duplicative, varying purely on the basis of geographical
location without real co-ordination. For the developer and government, the
system ensures that environmental factors are considered in the decision-
making process without undue delay and cost. The imposition of strict time

-- 87 of 360 --

52. Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
scales with a ‘‘go-ahead” default gives the developer much power and eases
administrative responsibilities. In contrast the US system with its minimum
time scales and “‘stop” default leads to frequent and costly delays in project
implementation, uncertainty about approval of the projects and more
administrative work. Environmental groups would obviously prefer a sys-
tem like that of the US whose process of scoping and timing enable a more
comprehensive analysis of a project’s environmental impact negotiation
and administrative guidance. Legally speaking mandatory EIA systems
also offer more citizen control and guidelines. In addition, although the
principle of EJA in Japan is fairly well established the practice is far from
satisfactory insofar as it is often used as a tool to justify development and
overcome local opposition. As most impact studies tend to conclude that
the project’s environmental impact will be small the consideration of long
term environmentally detrimental effect seems to be a side issue.
A bill for a new law that would have mandated environmental impact
reports prior to starting major construction projects was abandoned at the
end of March of this year (see Appendix III). The Bill had been
re-submitted to the Diet in March by the EA. However, evidently the EA
signed an official Government memo through the persuasion of dissenting
Government ministries and agencies promising that the bill would not be
put before the Diet any time in the near future. The original submission of
the bill, which provides for a new environmental standard law, was in 1981.
The EA had intended that the bill would serve as the basis of Japan’s
environmental policies. Although the Agency was apparently keen to le-
galise environmental assessment within the context of its comprehensive
Green Bill evidently the demands of other Government bodies and influen-
tial industry lobbyists forced it to concede. In particular the Construction,
International Trade and Industry and other ministries and agencies
opposed mandatory environmental assessments and even opposed the bill
as re-submitted which in fact stopped short of a legal requirement. The
ministries and agencies have argued that the step might trigger a barrage of
law suits aimed at stopping construction projects and make large projects
extremely difficult. Although the EA has made a verbal pledge not to
introduce such legislation in the near future the Deputy Director General
of the EA had denied at a news conference hearing of such an accord.
Recent trends
General awareness
A growing environmental awareness has led varying grass root projects
from a debate over a proposed national green tax to proposals for making

-- 88 of 360 --

The international perspective 3)
the country more eco-friendly in terms of the use of recycled paper (for
such items as toilet tissue) to. writing the comprehensive environmental
legislation. The Government had planned to lay a new green foundation
under the nation’s economy, key elements being the institution of a system
of deposits on product containers, imposing a green tax, as well as the
requirement of the EIAs. Container deposits would be added on to the
prices of various products and refunds on the return of empty containers
made in a manner similar to the systems already in existence for bottles and
cans in US and parts of Europe. However, the idea to regulate carbon
dioxide emissions by imposing a levy on fossil fuels (the consumption of
which generates most of Japan’s carbon dioxide emissions), was opposed
by industrial leaders and MITI. Whereas the EA was convinced that no
harmful economic effects would occur with careful planning, industry and
MITI remained convinced that the tax would affect Japan’s international
competitiveness. This again has demonstrated the battle between MITI
and the environmental policy making. The Ministry has in fact supported
the raising of the existing energy tax and use of the revenue to develop
energy saving technology. While the EA officials were able to argue that a
green tax would actually improve corporate investment in anti-pollution
measures and that the pollution control technology would raise product
quality and create new markets—just as it has done in the US and Euro-
pean markets—the industrial leaders and MITI did not accept the tax
proposal. Although the Ministry of Finance did cautiously support the
green tax concept it preferred to use revenues for general rather than green
purposes largely because of the poor state of the economy and the
possibility of igniting a debate about totally overhauling the national tax
system.
Whereas environmentally unfriendly principles of mass production and
consumerism have been flourishing in Japan, it is to be noted that more and
more municipal governments and citizens are demonstrating an increased
awareness which the Government and industrialists should well take note
of. To give a few examples:
(1) Shoppers in Tachikawa in suburban Tokyo can pick Tachikawa orig-
inal brand toilet paper made from the city’s re-cycled milk cartons.
The local government has stocked schools and municipal offices with
the tissue since 1991, the manufacture of which is contracted out to
local paper makers.

In Nagoya the local government covers the costs of re-cycling for
shopping areas that collect waste and try to do away with surplus
packaging.
Simultaneously Tokyo, Chiba Prefecture, Hokkaido and other re-
gions are moving on with ambitious plans to use heat generated in
urban areas as an energy source to operate air conditioning systems.
Kanagowa Prefecture is trying to harness energy from sunlight and
rainwater.
Local governments fairly often try to use electric vehicles and meth-
anol powered autos for official transportation.
Six prefectures including Tokyo and Osaka will start loaning less
polluting cars to local businesses this year with the support of the
EA.
Authorities in Wakayama, south of Osaka, and in Kitano in
Fukuoka Prefecture are toughening the penalties for litterbugs.
Other municipal bodies have been looking overseas for co-operation
with their green efforts.
Last August the city of Kitakyushu commenced an “International
Environment Co-operation Centre” and a ‘Maintenance Co-
operation Centre” to foster anti-pollution technologies developed
jointly with local companies and to take on trainees from developing
countries. This city, an industrial town, was commended at the Rio
Earth Summit for its commitment to combating its serious pollution
problems.
This summer the Government acknowledged the expanding national
role of non-government organisations in environmental conservation
by setting up a funding system for those involved in green efforts.
Whereas independent civic organisations are less involved in environ-
mental protection in Japan than in the US and Europe an increasing
number of Japanese businesses are donating part of their revenues to
such groups. In June the Government held the symposium at the
request of NGOs that attended the Earth Summit to examine the
extent of progress made on projects agreed at the summit.

In order to cope with global environmental issues development and
transfer of technology are considered essential. The UNEP Govern-
ing Council approved a Japanese proposal that an international
environmental technology centre of UNEP should be established in
Japan in May 1991. This centre was intended to focus on environ-
mentally sustainable management of big cities and fresh water lakes/
reservoir basins. It would provide training and consulting services
and establish a database, using appropriate technologies from all
over the world.
(15) Very recently, there has also been disappointment over the EA’s
rejection of a proposal to conduct priority monitoring of three Jap-

the Ramsar Bureau (which administers the International Ramsar
Treaty on Wetlands and Water Fowl Protection) the Japanese
Government decided not to place the wetlands on the Montreux
record, a list of Ramsar sites where ecological changes merit priority
monitoring and therefore nothing could be done. The EA has said
that no marked environmental damage has been observed.
Corporate awareness
Companies are starting to monitor their own efforts at protecting the en-
vironment largely through pressure from international initiatives. They
have been setting up internal bodies to check on their own protection
efforts in industries such as electrical machinery, electrical power, con-
struction and textiles by responding to the global environment charter
established by the Federation of Economic Organisations (Keidanran) in
1991 and the work of the Rio Summit 1992.
Corporate environmental activities differ from company to company but
they include updating the end of the use of CFC gases as noted above,

-- 91 of 360 --

56 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
accelerating the re-cycling of used paper and plastics and enforcement of
afforestation projects. As regards forests, Japan had promoted the idea of
an “international charter for the world’s forests” at the first session of the
UNEP preparatory committee in August 1990. This would be a non-
binding instrument including basic principles and initiatives to be taken for
the conservation and sustainable development of forests. The essential
philosophy behind a non-binding instrument is to adopt a practical step by
step approach so as to avoid prolonged negotiations. which might hinder
the timely action. Regarding tropical forests, evidently Japan has been
increasing its assistance to the ITTO (located in Yokohama Japan) whose
main task is to promote sustainable trade in tropical timber and to develop
and conserve tropical forests in order to maintain their eco-systems. As
noted above, Japan has shown a commitment to promote mobilisation of
financial resources to assist developing countries in tackling environmental
problems. The Japanese delegation of UNEP has expressed the view that
the financial and institutional aspects should be integral parts of the con-
ventions to be adopted (see Appendix I and I).
As has been discussed, recycling initiatives have become very import-
ant in Japan and a law concerning the promotion of utilisation of recycled
resources was approved by the Diet and promulgated on April 26 1991.
An amendment to the law concerning waste disposal was approved and
promulgated on October 5 1991. This new statute requires citizens and
certain industrial operators to comply with the programmes and mea-
sures initiated or taken by the State and municipalities for the reduction
and recycling of waste materials. Industrial operators are required to
present a prior assessment of the feasibility of recycling or proper
disposal of waste products.
As seen earlier, corporate targets are usually provided in concrete
numerical terms. As noted, Toshiba has worked out an action programme
intended to abolish totally CFCs in the fiscal 1993 and a 30% reduction of
industrial waste emissions from the fiscal 1990 level. The company will
expand the annual internal monitoring already being carried out by its
environment control centre at each plant to cover office buildings and
research facilities within its annual internal monitoring. It will rate them in
regard to about 100 items and site facilities that score high points. The
company is encouraging all employees to do their part in the environmen-
tal campaign. Sharp Corporation will introduce fully fledged monitoring in
the fiscal year 1994. Tokyo Gas Company intends to introduce a monitor-
ing system in this fiscal year.

Environmental auditing is quite new in the local market although some
local and foreign firms have begun to use audits to assess and minimise
future liabilities. Certain pre-purchase audits have occurred on sites.
National standards for air and water pollution are not the final word because
each prefecture and municipality has the right to use their own stricter limits
for effluent and emission discharges. Companies intending to establish new
industrial facilities must negotiate an environmental agreement with the
prefecture or municipal government. In addition the new recycling laws
introduced in 1991 place a greater burden on business to minimise waste
within the production process and produce recyclable products.
Although many major Japanese trading houses and manufacturing com-
panies have established environment management divisions or R&D
sections these are largely seen as a public relations or business develop-
ment tool.
Liability for environmental damage : recent case law
Although, generally speaking, case law has not been highlighted in this
paper, it is relevant when considering liability for environmental damage.
Under Japanese law this is based on the civil code and certain laws regarding
pollution, which determine civil, criminal and administrative liability. The
many cases of environmental damage to persons and property have resulted
in Japanese judges interpreting the laws in favour of the injured parties. As a
result the laws concerning air and water pollution discussed earlier have
been amended so that the polluter is liable for any damage even in cases
where it is not his fault. As regards bodily harm the principle of strict liability
applies while in other cases the fault of the polluter has to be proven. In
order to improve the injured party’s position Japanese law has developed
two theories, that of tolerance limits and of the probability theory. The first
theory means that certain nuisances must be tolerated by people and if these
are exceeded the injured party may take legal action, the limits being deter-
mined according to the nature of the damage. Such limits may be bodily
harm, damage to property or nervous shock. The second theory means that
the injured party only has to show the possibility of the existence of the
causal link between the wrongful act and the damage itself.

-- 93 of 360 --

58 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Where the polluters are not identifiable there is a compensation fund
which gives immediate assistance to all parties who have suffered bodily
injury. Under the Japanese law of October 5 1973 (amended in 1988) on
compensation for bodily injury resulting from pollution, any injured party
suffering damage to health caused by water or air pollution receives com-
pensation, after examination by a board, without having to identify the
person responsible or prove any fault. The fund is constituted from levies
on pollutant emissions and from a proportion of the tax on motor vehicles.
Compensation is automatic, however, only in major risk areas and for
specifically listed illnesses. (It should be emphasised that whereas concern
for public health rates high in Japan the natural environment has suffered
although the 1988 amendment did focus more on the prevention of pollu-
tion by the establishment of a pollution prevention fund).
As stated, mention should be made here of the relevant Japanese case
law as an important second tier to Japanese legislation. In particular, a
recent decision—on March 29 1991—in the Osaka District Court, the
Nishi-Yodogawa Air Pollution case. Nishi- Yodogawa has been designated
one of the worst atmospherically polluted areas in Japan and a suit was
filed by 117 residents against 10 corporations operating in the area, the
State of Japan and the Public Highway Authority, claiming a total of 2.8
billion Yen as compensation, and requesting an injunction regarding omis-
sions of certain chemical substances into the atmosphere. The 117 residents
were patients and their heirs designated under the Law on Pollution Re-
lated Health Damage Compensation. This case involved extremely difficult
causation problems, including the relationship between the local air pollu-
tion and emissions from the defendants’ corporations’ factories on the one
hand and Emissions from the vehicle traffic on the highways controlled by
the defendants’ state and the public authority on the other, as well as
between ambient pollution (sulphur oxide, particulate matter and nitrogen
oxide) and the plaintiffs’ impaired health. While the court did not accept
the plaintiffs’ assertions that the factories had been the major cause of the
pollution, nevertheless it was held that the corporations were liable as joint
tortfeasors. Thus the court accepted a portion of the plaintiffs’ claim for
compensation while dismissing the request for an injunction. (This case is
subject to appeal in the Osaka High Court).
Closing remarks
It
should be recalled that the regulatory national pollution standards
	are
only guidelines—the actual regulations that local and foreign factories
must follow are set
by the prefecture or municipal authorities in a pollution
control agreement with the company involved. These are generally stricter

-- 94 of 360 --

The international perspective 59
for new plants and usually go beyond the national guidelines. Local author-
ities base their standards on national figures together with whatever is
necessary to maintain what they judge to be an acceptable level of pollu-
tion for their particular area.
As elsewhere, benefits from tougher regulations have been offset by
steadily increasing pollution levels. For example, while the 1970 Water
Pollution Control Law has been amended several times, waste water loads
from urban areas have also increased and the infrastructure cannot cope,
despite the trend toward drafting new laws and regulations to deal with
pollution issues. Here again, MITI and the Ministry of Health and Welfare
seem to vie with the EA in terms of the legislation. Nevertheless, overall
business will be forced to become less wasteful in its use of resources which
will probably increase costs in the short term, but should result in long term
savings. It should also be noted that, given the current renewed concern for
the environment arising from global and local pollution problems, the
outlook for the Japanese environmental goods and services market may
well rise significantly: Crédit Suisse estimates that it will rise to yen 10.15
trillion by the year 2000—an annual average growth rate of 5.2%. A final
point is that while the majority of future investment will be in industrial
waste management, municipal refuse collection and disposal and sewage
collection and treatment, foreign pollution control firms will have difficulty
in obtaining a market there. Japan prides itself on its own technological
development and its waste management market is controlled by the central
and municipal governments who are generally more comfortable doing
business with Japanese firms.

60 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
Addendum
In preparing my paper for the Sixth Annual Construction Conference, I am
greatly indebted to the enormous help I received from Dr Linda Spedding,
an International Law Consultant to Masons, together with assistance from
our Hong Kong Office and in particular their Japanese-speaking solicitors.
In collating my material I was struck by three themes which not only
highlighted certain aspects of the Japanese approach to environmental
management but also reflected the variety of attitudes which exist through
the international community. I raise the following three points with a view
to encouraging debate beyond the Conference about the existence of
different approaches to environmental law:
— compliance versus enforcement;
— the preventative approach versus ‘“‘end of pipe” solutions; and
— astrong environmental agency versus “‘administrative guidance’’.
Compliance versus enforcement
There seem to be two fundamental approaches which can be adopted in
relation to the application of environmental legislation—that which relies
upon compliance, and that which assumes the necessity of enforcement. As
is apparent from Jack Kannry’s paper, the USA adopts a fairly tough
enforcement approach to the environment through use of a strong
Environmental Protection Agency. Meanwhile Europe tends to adopt a
mixture of the two approaches, as is evidenced by the UK which has fairly
tough enforcement agencies such as the National Rivers Authority and to a
lesser extent, Her Majesty’s Inspectorate of Pollution, yet in the area of
health and safety the compliance approach tends to be favoured by the
Health & Safety Executive. In addition, there are a number of voluntary
schemes encouraging compliance, in particular, the Responsible Care
Programme initiated by the Chemical Industries Association. The Dutch
Government has recently developed an initiative which relies heavily on
the compliance approach, and encourages industry to buy into improved
environmental performance by negotiating discharge permits in return for
long-term commitment to overall emission reductions. In its Fifth Action
Programme the European Commission stresses the importance of shared
responsibility in relation to sustainable development; if sustainable de-
velopment is to be achieved, all sectors of society must be committed to
that end, which tends to favour voluntary compliance rather than external
enforcement.

-- 96 of 360 --

The international perspective 61
As
	a
	nation,
	Japan
	relies
	heavily
	on
	the
	compliance approach
	to
environmental
	law.
Since
World
War
II,
development
	of
its
economy
	has
been
a
key
goal,
and
although
the
1960s
and
1970s
saw
a
number
	of
toxic
tort
	problems
	which
	resulted
	in
a
lot
of
fairly
stringent
	legislation not
dissimilar
	to
UK
	legislation of the
time—the
	Control
	of
Pollution
	Act
1974
and
the
Health
and
Safety
at
Work
	Act
1974—it
is
only
recently
that
the
environment
	has
become
	a
major
	political
issue.
	Since 1967
	an
En-
vironmental
	Pollution
	Control Act
has
been
	in
force
	and
in
1971
	Japan
created
an
Environmental
	Agency.
However,
	the
Environmental
	Agency
is
not
the
sole
agency
concerned
with
environmental
matters
and
it
has no
overall
	control.
	While
	local
	authorities
	have played
	a
leading
	role in
development
	of
environmental
policy
at a
local
level,
their
powers
too
are
restricted.
	A
difficult
consensus
	has
to
be
sorted
out
before
environmen-
tal
	laws
	can
	be
	implemented,
	initially
	between
	the
	Environmental
Agency,
	the
other
ministries
and
local
government
	and
then
between
	the
polluter
	and
	the
	regional administrator.
	In practice,
	Japanese
environmental
	agencies
	seek
	to
	enforce
	environmental
	law primarily
through
administrative
	guidance
in
accordance
with
Japanese
history
and
tradition.
	This
	is
an
	informal,
	non-authoritative
	process
	whereby
	the
Government
	seeks
	to
	induce
	industry’s
	voluntary co-operation.
	It
involves discussions
	and
	consultations and
	may
	take
	the
	form
	of
non-
written
	directions, requests,
	warnings, suggestions
	or
	encouragement.
Government
	agencies
	may
	well
issue
a
number
	of
administrative
	‘‘guid-
ances” before any formal
enforcement
	action
is
taken.
The
reason
for
this
stems
	from
	the
	fact
	that historically,
	Japanese bureaucracy
	generally
possesses greater
power
	than,
for
instance,
that
of
America;
	orders
from
Government
	officials
are
considered
	to
be
virtually
equivalent
to
the rule
of law,
	and
	judicial
	review
	of these orders
	is
carefully
	circumscribed.
Furthermore, Japanese
	culture places
considerable
	emphasis
	on
govern-
mental decision-making through
consensus.
It should also be noted that Japanese industry tends to comply with
administrative guidance because the Government has extensive discre-
tionary authority over the issue of environmental authorisations required
for the the operation of a plant. As a result, any violating organisation
will find it difficult to obtain the future co-operation of the Government.
Thus there has tended to be relatively little formal administrative or
judicial enforcement in Japan because of the shared objective between
the Government and industry relating to post-war economic rehabilita-
tion. The growing world emphasis on environmental protection has,
however, meant an increase in formal enforcement mechanisms although
the basic concept of administrative guidance remains deeply rooted in
Japanese bureaucracy.

-- 97 of 360 --

62 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
The preventative approach versus “end of pipe” solutions
One of the most significant factors in this debate is the changing attitude
towards increased openness in the decision-making process in relation to
the environment. In America there is already a considerable body of law
relating to freedom of access to information, and Europe is fast following
along similar lines. The EC’s Fifth Environmental Action Programme stip-
ulates that the general public should be viewed as a.resource not as a
nuisance, and in the United Kingdom we have seen the recent introduction
of the Environmental Information Regulations which are intended to
implement the Freedom of Access to Information Directive.
However, as has been discussed, Japan has a rather fragmented system
of regulatory control which tends to mean that opportunities for public
participation are limited. Furthermore, industry and government alike
each have a long history of rating achievement of economic goals as a
priority, which tends to discourage allocation of resources to improving
public awareness of commercial activities. Japan has no equivalent of the
US Freedom of Information Act, although regionally some local
authorities have sought to produce their own version. In the UK, plan-
ning and development is perhaps the area which provides the greatest
opportunity for public participation during the decision-making process,
particularly where Environmental Impact Assessments are involved.
However, Japan’s planning process involves the imposition of strict time-
scales, with a ‘‘go ahead” default which gives the developer considerable
power and eases administrative responsibilities. This is to be contrasted
with the US system with its minimum timescales and “stop” default
which often lead to frequent and costly delays in project implementation,
uncertainty about approval of the project and additional administrative
burdens.
The area of environmental impact assessment provides an example of
the way in which Japan has tended to prefer ‘‘end-of-pipe”’ solutions to
adoption of a more preventative approach. In contrast, Europe is com-
mitted to this latter approach, as demonstrated by the new Directive on
Environmental Impact Assessment. In Japan there is no such legal
requirement, although environmental impact assessments for a very
limited range of projects are the subject of administrative guidance.
Generally speaking, development in Japan is considered necessary and
environmental policy merely aims to counteract any negative effect it
might have. Since there is no requirement to look at alternatives, the
developer has no incentive to consider less environmentally-damaging
developments or management methods, or even the possibility of not
developing a particular site at all. Moreover, the approach of major

-- 98 of 360 --

The international perspective 63
Japanese
	construction
	companies
	to
dealing
	with
	environmental
	prob-
lems
	tends
	to
revolve
	around
	“bolting
	on’
	pollution
	control
	measures
rather
than
considering
	any
other
alternative
	development.
	This
enables
industry
to
push ahead
with
the
knowledge
	that
should
an
environmental
difficulty
arise,
a
technical solution
must
be found.
This

is
felt
to
be
preferable
	to

	in

	with
the

that

The
third

	To

	are

encouraged.
	On

Europe
	has
already witnessed
	a

stan-

	have
	been
	produced
	with
the
aim

ensuring
good manage-

practices,

as

and
the

	series.
However,
management
	obligations
	are
	now
	beginning
	to
be

	An
example
	is
the
	EC
	Construction
	Sites

	which
	is
to

mented
	in

	by

	Construction (Design and
	Management)
Regulations.
	To
	a

	one
	can
	see
	a
similar
	approach
	applied
in

	of
	public
	procurement
	and
	the
	management
	of
	tendering
procedures. There
is
also
the

scheme
introduced
by

Eco Audit
	and

	Regulation which

	may become
	compulsory
	in

	time
	if

	is

interest
in
the
Scheme.
Japan has no legal requirements in respect of management systems. Its
economic goals are, for the most part, founded within the culture of total
quality management (TQM), which has become an essential part of the
philosophy of Japanese industry and therefore a legal requirement to
support this philosophy is not required. However, what is interesting is the
fact that the environment is rarely seen to have a part to play within the
TOM culture because it does not have prominence as a “‘stakeholder”’ in
Japanese organisations, as is increasingly the case elsewhere in the business
world. As TOM principles are based on “‘what the customer wants”’, Japan
seems unlikely to change in this regard until its customers begin to demand
that the environment is viewed as a priority stakeholder in all industrial
activities. Seeing the relatively low level of public participation and limited
access to information in the Japanese environmental arena, changes in this
area are not like to occur overnight.

-- 99 of 360 --

64 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
A strong environmental agency versus “administrative guidance”
As evidenced again by Jack Kannry’s paper, it is apparent that America
has a very strong environmental agency. Europeis now in the process of
establishing a European Environmental Agency and the UK is anticipating
the merger of HMIP and NRA to form one environmental agency by 1995.
How powerful these agencies will be in reality remains to be seen, but
current indications suggest that they will take their role very seriously and
will seek to become more powerful in their enforcement of environmental
laws. Cynics might suggest that funding of the UK agency will be deter-
mined by the degree to which it is deemed to be a “‘success”, which may
well be judged in terms of the number of successful prosecutions it achieves
in a given financial year. Certainly the US EPA is very “‘result-oriented”’ in
order to ensure continuing Government support for its operations.
Inevitably agencies under pressure to get results in order to keep going
will become increasingly aggressive and enforcement-oriented. This is in
sharp contrast with Japan where the various agencies with environmental
responsibilities tend to be fairly weak in comparison to the Government
and the variety of industrial lobby groups, such as that of the construction
industry. “Administrative guidance” is about the use of negotiation and
compromise to achieve compliance, which is very different from the dis-
tancing effect achieved by environmental agencies in other parts of the
world who tend to be unwilling to. give industry advice on compliance,
particularly where they recognise a breach of regulations has occurred.
Conclusion
There must be an increasing risk that with so much regulatory law emanat-
ing from Brussels one may place too much of a legal stranglehold around
industry and create too many administrative barriers to allow Member
States to trade and to develop effectively. Signs of the resulting frustration
which is developing are beginning to manifest themselves: the UK Govern-
ment’s drive towards deregulation, and its attitude to Europe’s inability to
sign the Uruguay round of the GATT talks because of one Member State’s
difficulty, in particular, over its farming lobby are both examples of this
mounting pressure. Japan, however, with its ‘‘administrative guidance”
approach, has not become immersed in regulatory law. It pushes ahead
with development and seeks, when required, “bolt-on” solutions to
environmental problems. Although many pressure groups are critical of
Japan, their approach does have the benefit of speed. At a time when many
people believe that the planet has a very short fuse in terms of global
energy use, limited natural resources and population explosion, Japan’s

-- 100 of 360 --

The international perspective 65
approach may have more to recommend it than perhaps many of its critics
believe. From Europe’s point of view, the desire to legislate as a means of
environmental control, however well-intended and commendable, may be
viewed as the slippery slope to total immersion in bureaucracy, rather than
a means of pioneering solutions to the many environmental problems
facing us today. ,

Appendix II
Specific conventions and international instruments affecting Japan
London Dumping Convention
Convention on the Prevention of Marine Pollution by Dumping of Waste
1972, amended 1979.
The amendments are addressed chiefly to the industrialised West and
Japan.
The Convention convened with representatives from: Western indus-
trialised nations, Eastern Bloc, and developing nations, as well as non-
governmental organisations (NGOs), governmental organisations and UN
organisation observers. The convention is based on the notion that the
ability of the sea to accept and absorb waste is limited and furthermore the
right of a sovereign nation to exploit its resources is limited by the need not
to damage the resources of other nations in the process. The signatories
pledge as a group to use the best practicable means possible to prevent
pollution, according to each nation’s scientific, technical and economic
capabilities.
The Convention
	defines
dumping
	as
the
deliberate
	discharge
of
waste,
and
	not the
	incidental
	waste
	generated
	and discharged
	by
the
	normal
operation
	of
seagoing
	vessels.
The
	wastes
	generated by
the
exploitation
of
off-shore
	minerals
	is
not
	addressed
	in
this
	convention.
	Parties
	are
required
	to
establish
	a
system
	of
permits
	for
dumping,
	including appro-
priate authorities
	to
issue
both general and “special”
	dumping
	permits.
General
	permits govern reoccurring
	or
	cyclic
	dumpings,
	“‘special’’
permits govern extraordinary occurrences and
exemptions
	for
prohibited
dumpings.
	No
	permits
	are
	required
	for
emergency dumping
	that saves
human
	lives or
protects
	vessels,
	but such incidences must be reported.
Special permits
	may
	only be issued
	after
	the
impact
	has
been
	well
	re-
searched, and
	affected
	states
	consulted. National
	authorities
	must keep
records
	of
all
permits,
	monitor dumpings, and report
	their findings. All
parties
	are
	responsible
	for vessels flying their flags, as
well
	as
those
	in
their territorial waters.
Sanctions and enforcement
Signatories must use all
appropriate measures
	to prevent and punish con-
travention in their jurisdictions, and co-operate for enforcement on the

-- 103 of 360 --

68 Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
high seas. Parties are encouraged to engage in regional, bilateral and multi-
lateral agreements to facilitate the Convention. An international codification
of signals to be used by vessels engaged in dumping is to be enacted.
Classification of materials
Annex I describes materials that shall only be dumped with a special
permit, including organo-halogens, mercury, cadmium, persistent plastics,
hydrocarbons, high level radioactive materials, biological or chemical war-
fare agents, or any other material making sea foods inedible or endanger-
ing human health. Annex II describes materials that should be dumped
only with special care, including arsenic, lead, copper, zinc, organo-silicons,
cyanides, fluorides, pesticides, scrap metals, and low level radioactive
materials. No permit for the dumping of any of the above materials may
be authorised without a precise scientific study of the character of the
material, including its toxicity, bio-accumulation, etc., as well as the charac-
teristics of the dumping site, including the characteristics of the waters and
the methods to be used in the dumping.
1979 amendments by the ad hoc committee on incineration at sea
These amendments are addressed chiefly to the industrialised West and
Japan, and include all current and any possible future methods of incinera-
tion. The organisation must be given notice of any special incineration
permit issued. The permit must include such information as the issuing
authority, period of validity, origin of waste, industrial process giving rise
to the waste, area of incineration, and expected frequencies. Incinerated
wastes that are incidental to the normal operation of vessels are excluded
from the convention. Parties are required to research alternatives to incin-
eration before issuing a permit. The appropriate authority must monitor,
record, and report all discharge from incineration. Destruction must
exceed 99.9% of wastes incinerated. Pilot test burns must precede issuance,
with a “black box” in place to ensure that the incineration does not exceed
organisation standards for carbon monoxide, carbon dioxide or visible
smoke. Only wastes whose exhaust will be rapidly rendered harmless or
present only as trace contaminants may be authorised permits. Permits will
be allowed for certain wastes only after extensive consultation with other
parties, and the materials are found to be non biodegradable, not persis-
tent, and leave no acute or chronic toxic affects or bioaccumulation,
especially in food species or humans. Finally, all alternative methods of
disposal of the waste must be itemised. Chile, Portugal, and the former
USSR all opt out of the dispute settlement procedures.

-- 104 of 360 --

The international perspective 69
Montreal Protocol on substances that deplete the ozone layer
Made: 16 September 1987. In force: 1 January 1989
The protocol establishes graduated decreasing levels of emissions, based
on 1986 consumption, for various substances to be listed in subsequent
amendments. It also established a timetable after which such substances
may not be traded to non-members, from which such substances may not
be imported from non-members, and a ban on imports of products manu-
factured with, but not containing, the enumerated substances. Signatories
are obliged to control the emissions of CFCs, inter alia in aerosols, by any
means at their disposal, including controls on production or use, to the
maximum extent practicable. The protocol encourages members to urge
non-members to become signatories. Under certain circumstances, and
with notice to the Secretariat, members who under-produce their quota
may transfer the difference to other members. Parties are discouraged
from the export of CFC technology except that used in containment, recov-
ery, recycling or destruction. The special situation of developing nations is
noted. It sets forth the method of adjustment of allowable levels and the
method for the calculation of levels. It calls for increasing public awareness
and establishes a procedure for dealing with non-compliance.
Approved by the EC by Council Decision, 14 October 1988.
[See 1990
Amendments
	and
Vienna Convention,
	1985].

Vienna Convention for the protection of the ozone layer
22 March 1985
The Convention is based on the notion that a state has a sovereign right to
exploit its resources with a responsibility not to cause damage outside the
limits of its national jurisdiction. The Member States agree to conduct
research and exchange information regarding the ozone layer and to adopt
the necessary legislative and administrative measures to limit activities with
adverse effects, with special attention given to alternative substances and
technologies, as well as the socio-economic ramifications. Member States
are to co-operate in establishing standards for implementing the conven-
tion and in creating protocols to the convention. Signatories are encour-
aged to take into account the special needs of developing countries, and to
promote direct and indirect transfer of technology and knowledge to them
via competent international bodies. The convention establishes a Secre-
tariat to conduct meetings, issue reports, carry out the functions of the
convention, and to act in co-ordination with other international bodies.
[See

Montreal
	Protocol, 1987,
and

September 1992
Two
	advisory councils
	to

	proposed
	a

to

	environmental
	protection, with

	The

The

72° Public Law Aspects of Environmental Protection
December 1992
The actual draft hints at the introduction of a new environmental tax and
mandatory assessments, the draft being tentatively entitled ‘‘Environmen-
tal Standards Law’. It specifically proposed that planners of major
development projects be required to detail what effects their projects
would have on the environment and to make provisions for protecting the
environment. Even projects not involving construction would be prompted
voluntarily to file environmental assessments, detailing the proposed use of
products and methods to be used in waste disposal.
The draft continued that the Government ‘‘take necessary and appropri-
ate measures to levy economic costs”’ on entities that emit polluting sub-
stances into the atmosphere. The statement hinted at the future
introduction of an environment tax.
January 1993

Re-submitted to the DIET by the EA.

The

	as

4 Waste: its disposal and use*
Peter Barber and Andrew Jackson
Introduction
The law governing waste management is currently in a state of flux, caught
principally between old and new statutory regimes. Whilst the Environmen-
tal Protection Act 1990 has been on the statute book for three years, its
implementation, particularly in relation to its waste management provisions,
has been piecemeal. A substantial part of the Act providing a new statutory
regime for the licensing of waste management is not yet in force. Those
involved in the construction process and confronting waste disposal issues
must therefore consider a range of old, new and prospective provisions.
A number of issues arise:
(1)
	Whether
	the
handling
of
waste
	(including
its
use)
in
the
construction
process requires
either
a
waste
disposal
licence
or
(in the
future
under
the
Environmental
	Protection
Act)
a
waste
management
	licence;
(2)
	The
	effect
	of the
	“Duty
	of
Care’
	on
	the
	management
	of
waste
imposed
under
the
Environmental
	Protection
Act;
(3)
	The
	control of the
transport
	of
waste
	to
and from
	a
site
under
	the
Control
of Pollution
	(Amendment)
	Act
1989;
(4)
	The
control
of
waste
that
is
“special” waste;
and
(5)
	Civil
liability
for
damage
	caused
by
waste.
The requirement
	for
a
waste disposal
or
waste
	management
	licence
At
the
time
of
this
paper,
the statutory regulation
of
waste
management
	and
waste disposal
in
the
UK
is
by
a
mixture
of the
Control
of Pollution
Act 1974
and
the
Environmental
Protection
Act 1990 and
regulations
made
under
these
Acts.
Though
these Acts do
not
specifically
implement
it,
regard must
also
be
had
to
the
EC
Waste
Framework
	Directive.
Both
UK
statutory
regimes
centre
on
	a
requirement
	for a
licence
	where
	waste
	is
deposited
	or
	treated in a
particular
	manner,
	with the
	Environmental
	Protection Act imposing
	an
additional control
by way
of
a
duty of care on the handling of waste.
*
This paper was prepared by
the authors according
to the law
as at
23
September
1993.
The
reader
is
therefore referred to the changes brought about by the Waste Management
Licens-
ing Regulations 1994 and the accompanying Waste Management Papers and Circular.

76 Impact on Construction Activites
Under both UK statutory regimes, a number of important exemptions
limit the effect of the statutory control, in particular on the use of waste
material in the construction process. The exemptions are not such,
however, that the statutory provisions can be ignored.
The Control of Pollution Act 1974
In terms of waste disposal the central provision of the Control of Pollution
Act 1974 is section 3(1). By that section it is a criminal offence to:
(a) deposit “controlled waste” on any land or knowingly permit controlled
waste to be deposited on any land; or
(b) use any plant or equipment or knowingly permit any plant or equipment to
be used, for the purpose of disposing of controlled waste or of dealing in a
prescribed manner with controlled waste
unless the land in question is occupied by the holder of a waste disposal licence
authorising the deposit and the deposit is made in accordance with any conditions
specified in the licence.
Liability
	on
	summary
	conviction
	is
to
	imprisonment
	for
	a
term
	not
exceeding
	six
months
	or
a
fine
not
exceeding £5000
	or
both. Liability
on
conviction
	on
indictment
	is
to
imprisonment
	for
a
term
not
exceeding
two
years
	or
a
fine or
both.
The
	penalties
	are
increased!
	where
	the
waste
	in
question
is
“poisonous
noxious
or
polluting”
and
its
presence
on
the
land
is
likely
to
give
rise
to
an
“‘environmental
	hazard’’.2
It
is
a
defence?
for
a
person charged
with
an
offence
under
the section
to
show:
“(a) that he
(i) took care to inform himself, from persons who were in a position to
provide the information, as to whether the deposit or use to which
the charge relates would be in contravention of; [s3(1)]
(ii) did not know and had no reason to suppose that the information
given to him was false or misleading and that the deposit or use might
be in contravention of that sub-section;
_— $3(3)(a), (b) and (c).
By
s4(5) of the
Act
the
presence
of waste on land gives
rise
to
an
environmental hazard
if
“the waste has
been deposited
in
such
a
manner
or in
such
a
quantity
(whether
that
quantity
by
itself
or
cumulatively
with other deposits of the
same
	or
different
substances)
as to
subject persons
or
animals
to a
material
risk
of death, injury
or
impairment
of health
or as
to
threaten the pollution
(whether
on the surface
or
underground)
of any water supply”.
Bysubsection (6) the
degree
of
risk is to
be assessed with particular regard
to the
measures
taken
for
minimising
the risk to the likelihood of the waste
or
any container
in

it is
deposited being tampered with by children or others.

	that

were done.”
Three
	particular
issues
arise
which
	are
of
importance
	for
the
construction
process:
_—
	what
constitutes
waste
and
controlled waste
for
the
purposes
of section
3(1);
—
	what
constitutes
a
deposit
or
the
use
of
plant
or
equipment
	for
dealing
with controlled waste
in
a
prescribed
manner;
	and
—
	subject
to
the
specific
exceptions,
	whether
	a
waste
	disposal
	licence
	is
required
for
the
management
	and
use
of
waste
on
a

The meaning of waste
The
definitions of
“waste” and
“controlled
waste”
are
fundamental
to
both
the
Control
	of
Pollution
	and
Environmental
	Protection
Acts.
For
the pur-
poses
of the
Control
of
Pollution
Act “waste”
	is
defined
as:4
“(a)
	any substance
	which
	constitutes
	a
scrap
material
	or
an effluent
or
other
unwanted
surplus
substance
arising
from
the
application
of
any
process;
and
(b)
	any substance
or article
which
requires
to
be disposed
of
as
being broken,
worn
out,
contaminated
or
otherwise
spoiled.”
Despite
	its
apparent
	simplicity
this
definition has
been considered
	jud-
icially
on
a
number
	of occasions.
The important
issue
has
been whether
the
definition of
“unwanted”
	should be considered from
	the
point of
view
of
the
producer
	or
whether
	the possibility of future use of the
material
	in
question
by someone
	else
can
alter
its
character.
A number
	of the cases
have involved construction contexts particularly
in relation
to
material received on
site
for use in the
construction process.
In
Long

	that

	Limited had wished
	to

	and

site. Nearby, the owner of a disused quarry wished to cover the regraded
slopes of his quarry with soil before grassing them over. He was
approached by Wimpey who on two occasions tipped their excavated ma-
terial on to his land for the privilege of which they paid him the sum of
£2.50 per load. The value per load equated to the cost of the bulldozer used
to spread the material. There was no profit from the tipping.
The quarry owner was convicted in the Magistrates’ Court of knowingly
permitting the deposit of controlled waste on land which he occupied with-
out being the holder of a waste disposal licence authorising the deposit,
contrary, as above, to section 3 of the Control of Pollution Act.
The quarry owner had argued that the excavated material was not waste
as it had some value to him for use on his land. On appeal the Crown Court
dismissed this argument stating that although one man’s waste may con-
stitute another man’s valuable material, on its true construction the Act
defines waste from the point of view of the person discarding the material.
To be waste, therefore, it need not be useless, merely unwanted.
That
ruling
was
	specifically
approved
	in

Waste: its disposal and use 81
Despite these decisions the starting point in deciding whether material
received or produced on site is waste is clearly to consider the material
from the point of view of the producer. Then if, on the above principles, it
is waste then it is also “controlled waste’’ within the meaning of the Con-
trol of Pollution Act and therefore potentially subject to control?
Controlled waste
For
	the
purposes
	of the
	Control
	of
Pollution
	Act
	“controlled
	waste”
	is
defined
in
section
30(1)
as
‘‘household,
industrial
and
commercial
waste
or
any such
waste’’.
	For
present purposes,
	the
important
	category
	is
“indus-
trial
waste”.
By
the
same
	section,
“industrial
waste”’ consists
of:
“waste from

82 Impact on Construction Activites
Clearly, this means that if what is produced on a construction site is
“‘waste’’, then it will almost certainly be controlled waste in any event. By
contrast, for used material received on site, something of its history will
need to be known to determine whether it is controlled waste. What is of
interest is that construction waste (given the volumes produced) was not
specified in its own right as a particular category of waste. It may perhaps
be questioned whether the Secretary of State acted strictly within his
powers in prescribing that construction related waste be treated as “‘indus-
trial waste” when that description (at least according to its statutory defin-
ition) does not apply to it.
If material does constitute controlled waste then, as noted above and
subject to the exceptions made in respect of the construction process, a
waste disposal licence will be required if that waste is either deposited on
land or knowingly permitted to be deposited or any plant or equipment is
used or knowingly permitted to be used for the purpose of disposing of the
waste or of dealing with it in a prescribed manner.
