84 Impact on Construction Activites
questions need to be asked as to whether it is in fact “deposited” such that
the site requires a waste disposal licence. If, for example an existing building
is demolished, the material produced will be controlled waste. If that mater-
ial is then stored on site for any period before removal then, apart from any
statutory exceptions, there could be an argument that a deposit of that waste
has occurred and that a waste disposal licence is required. The position is
even more clear with materials brought on site for use in construction. If
these constitute controlled waste then their temporary storage and perhaps,
in the case of material used for infill or landscaping, even their final use will
constitute a deposit and again, strictly speaking, would require a licence.
As previously noted, however, the Act was not intended to cover all
these situations. Thus, the Collection and Disposal of Waste Regulations
1988 provide for exemptions from the requirement for a licence. The ex-
ceptions were originally provided for in the Control of Pollution (Licensing
of Waste Disposal) Regulations 1976. Department of the Environment
Circular 76/76, in commenting on the Regulations, indicated that the
exemptions were aimed at ensuring that a licence is not required where
waste produced during the ‘“‘course of building and certain civil engineering
work” is made use of in other construction work without causing an
environmental hazard (para 12). The specific example given is the use of
builder’s rubble as foundation material.
By Regulation 9 and Schedule 6 of the 1988 Regulations, therefore,
certain specific deposits are exempt. The most important exemption in the
present context is that in paragraph 4 which exempts from the need for a
licence:
(1) “‘... the deposit on land of wastes of the descriptions set out in paragraphs 6
and 7 of Schedule 3 or of ash, slag or clinker, provided that the deposit is
made for the purposes of construction currently being undertaken on the
land on which the waste is deposited
(2)
	...the
deposit
on land,

not

three
months,

What constitutes dealing with waste in a “‘prescribed manner’’, however,

Regulations again set out exemptions which will cover some of the ac-

provide that the following are included amongst the activities which will
constitute treating waste in a prescribed manner:
1. Bailing, compacting, incinerating, pulverising, sorting or storing
waste;
5. Producing fuel from waste;
8. Using untreated waste as fuel to produce electricity or heat.

disposal authority.2° In the majority of cases this will be the relevant county

A qualification, however, is that a licence is not to be issued for use of
land, plant or equipment for which planning permission is required unless
planning permission is in force. This could, of course, create difficulties as it
is unlikely that any planning permission that exists for the development of
a site will specifically cover the use of the site for the disposal of waste.
Were that problem to arise it would have to be argued that the particular
act of disposal, or treatment, of waste was ancillary to the construction or
demolition process (if not an integral part of it) for which permission exists
so that a separate permission is not required.

Section 33(1) of the
Act provides

a

“(a) deposit controlled waste,
or

26 See ss 5 and 30 of the Act.

-- 123 of 360 --

88 Impact on Construction Activites
licence authorising the deposit is in force and the deposit is in accordance
with the licence;
(b) treat, keep or dispose of controlled waste, or knowingly cause or know-
ingly permit controlled waste to be treated, kept or disposed of:
(i) in or on any land, or
(ii) by means of any mobile plant
except under and in accordance with a waste management licence.”
Though subsections 33(1)(a) and (b) are amongst those provisions not
yet in force, it is worth commenting on them as their eventual implementa-
tion is inevitable.
By section 33(6) a person who contravenes subsection (1) or any
condition of a waste management licence commits an offence. Summary
conviction for such an offence may result in a fine up.to a maximum of
£20,000 (presently the statutory maximum) or imprisonment for a term not
exceeding six months or both. Conviction on indictment may lead to a fine,
imprisonment for two years (five years where the offence relates to special
waste??) or again both.
Defences are provided for in section 33(7). The first defence is different
from the corresponding defence under the Control of Pollution Act. It is a
defence for a person charged with an offence under section 33 to prove:
“. . . that he took all reasonable precautions and exercised all due diligence
to avoid the commission of the offence.” The defences of emergency and
acting on the instruction of an employer remain as before.
Section 33 is clearly an extension of section 3(1) of the Control of Pollu-
tion Act. Subsection 1(a) reflects the prohibition on the deposit of
controlled waste without a waste disposal licence. Subsection 1(b) is an
extension of the previous provision at section 3(1)(b) of the Control of
Pollution Act. No longer, however, is there a reference to the use of plant
and equipment for the purpose of disposing of controlled waste or dealing
with it in a prescribed manner. Instead, there is the much wider categorisa-
tion of the treating, keeping or disposing of controlled waste “in or on any
land or by means of any mobile plant’’.
What
	constitutes waste and controlled waste
	will
again be
key
issues in
determining whether
	a
waste
management
	licence
will
be required
for the
handling
of material on
site.
By
section 75(2) of the
Act waste
is
defined
as
it
was
	in the
Control
	of Pollution Act.
	Cases on the definition of waste
under
that
Act
will
therefore remain
relevant.
Similarly, controlled waste
is
defined by
section 75(4)
as
meaning house-
hold, industrial and commercial waste
or
any such waste.
The
definitions of

Section 34 Environmental Protection Act 1990—The “Duty of
Care”
Section 34 of the Environmental Protection Act represents another exten-
sion of the regulation of waste management over and above that contained
in the Control of Pollution Act. As with section 33(1)(c) of the Act it is a
provision which has a wide and general effect beyond the traditional waste
disposal industry. Like section 33(1)(c) it is also one of the provisions of
this part of the Act which is already in force.
Section 34(1) provides that:
“Tt shall
be
the
duty
of
any person
who
imports,
produces,
carries,
treats,
keeps
or
disposes
of
controlled waste
or, as
a
broker,
has control
of
such
waste,
to
take
all
such
	measures
	applicable
	to
him
	in that
	capacity
	as
	are
	reasonable
	in
the
circumstances
(a)
	to
prevent any
contravention
by
any other
person
of section 33 [of
the
Act];
(b)
	to
prevent
	the
escape
	of the
waste
	from
	his
control
or
that
of
any
other
person;
and
(c)
	on the transfer of the
waste
to
secure
(i)
	that
the transfer
is
only
to
an
authorised person
	or to a
person
	for
authorised
transport purposes; and
(ii)
	that
there
is
transferred such
a
written
description
of the
waste
as will
enable other
persons
to
avoid
a
contravention
	of
that
section
and
to
comply
with
the
duty under
this
sub-section
as
respects the
escape
ofwaste”’.
By
section
34(6) any person
who
fails
to
comply
with
the
duty
imposed by
section 34(1)
will
be
guilty
of an offence.
The
duty
is
not
a
civil
law duty
in
the sense that
a
breach
will
sound
in
damages.
Liability on
summary
	convic-tion
is to a fine
not
exceeding
£20,000. Liability on conviction on
indictment
is to
an
unlimited
fine.
However
in
this case,
there
is
no

of
imprisonment.“Controlled waste” has the
same meaning
as
for section
33.43

The

	of Practice
is

	it

Who is subject to the duty?
Section
34(1),
as
noted
	above, provides
that
this
is
anybody who
	“imports,
produces,
	carries,
keeps,
treats or
disposes
of
controlled waste
or, as
a
bro-
ker,
has control
of
such waste.”
	In
a
construction context
	this
needs
	to
be
seen
in
terms
of the
employer/client,
the
various consultants,
the
contractor
and
	sub-contractors.
	Clearly,
	anyone
	directly
	responsible
	for
	controlled
waste
	(for
example,
	a
subcontractor
	carrying
the
material
	to or
away
	from
the
site
or
the
contractor
itself
handling
the
waste
on
site) will
be
subject
to
the duty.
There
is
more room
for
doubt
with those
not directly
involved.
It is
conceivable
	that
a
consultant,
	or
even
	the
employer/client,
	may
	be
found
subject
	to
the
	duty
	if
indirectly
	involved
	in
the selection
	or
	approval
	of
controlled waste
as
a
construction
material.
Equally,
it
could be
argued
that
the
employer/client
produces
or
keeps
controlled waste
to
the
extent
that
the
construction
is
subject
to
its
ultimate control
and
is
for

Department
	of the
	Environment
	Circular

	recognises
	that
	these

will

in the

	indus-

at

	is

	subject

The

	in

	of Practice.
The
	starting
	point
for

	who
	is

	is
said
	to

in

	the material
became
	waste.
Where
	an action
is
involved
	the
producer
	will
	be
	the
	person committing
	the
	act.
	Where
	no action
	is
involved the
producer
	will
be the
person holding
	the waste
	who
	decides
that
	it is
waste.
	The Department’s view
	is
that the
	producer
	is to
be
regarded
	as
the person undertaking
	the
works
	that give
rise to the waste,
not the person who
issues instructions or lets contracts.
Although
the client
for the works may issue instructions that give rise to waste he does not
physically produce the waste.

94 Impact on Construction Activites
Where there are several contractors on site the producer of a particular
waste is the particular contractor who takes the action that produces the
waste or who treats something as if it were waste.
A client or contractor may, however, act as a broker of waste, by letting a
contract for its removal and disposal away from the site. Paragraph 17 of
the Circular concludes that every contractor (i.e. the main and all sub-
contractors) on site will either be producing or carrying waste and so will
be subject to the duty. :
Clearly all parties need to be aware of the potential liabilities. It needs to
be ensured that, where necessary, warranties are given that the duty under
section 34 will be complied with. The existing provisions of the ICE and
JCT standard forms probably cover this point in general but it may be
useful to include more specific provisions.
The extent of the duty

Authorised transport purposes are described in subsection (4). These
are:
— the transport of controlled waste within the same premises between
different places in those premises;
— the transport to a place in Great Britain of controlled waste which has
been brought from a country or territory outside Great Britain not
having been landed in Great Britain until it arrives at that place; and
— the transport by air or sea of controlled waste from a place in Great
Britain to a place outside Great Britain.

The duty to provide a written description on transfer needs, as noted
above, is to be considered in conjunction with the Environmental Protec-
tion (Duty of Care) Regulations 1991.48 These Regulations were made
under section 34(5) of the Act which enabled the Secretary of State to
make provision imposing requirements on any person who is subject to the
duty imposed by section 34(1) as respects the making and retention of
documents and the furnishing of documents or copies of documents. The
Regulations deal solely with that issue. Failure to comply with regulations
made under section 34(S) is itself a criminal offence,49 subject to the same
penalties as failure to comply with the duty.
The Regulations refer to the Act and define the transferor and transferee
of waste as, respectively, the person who in compliance with section 34(1)
of the Act transfers a written description of waste and the person who
receives that description. Note the definition by reference to the document
rather than the product.
Regulation
2
then provides:
(1)
The
transferor
and
the
transferee
shall,
at
the
same
	time
	as
the
written
	description
	of the
waste
	is
transferred,
ensure
	that
such
a
document
	as is

Details of any problems previously encountered with the waste should
be identified as should changes to the description since any previous load

drums and other closed containers with a description of the waste. Though
only a recommendation, labelling would actually seem to be fundamental
in most cases.
The duties of transfer will apply not only to waste removed from a site,
but also to any waste received on site. Any contractor bringing waste to the
site will want to ensure that the contractor or person responsible for its
receipt is an ‘‘authorised person” (which may present difficulties in the
absence of a waste disposal licence>>) and that a transfer note is produced.
The person receiving the waste should check back through the transfer
note chain and also see that the transferor is a registered carrier.

The Code does, however, indicate that it may be necessary to consider
what will happen to the waste further along the disposal chain where, for
instance, waste needs to reach its final disposal or treatment place in its
original container. Then the holder will need to satisfy himself that that will
happen.
Otherwise, the Code recommends>” that every waste holder should be
alert for evidence that his waste is not being dealt with properly, including
where they become aware of problems further down the waste chain over
which they may have no control. If unsatisfied, the holder should refuse to
continue transferring or accepting waste, ensuring that waste disposal con-
tracts allow termination in this situation.58 Stringent precautions should be
taken if the arrangements do have to continue. The relevant authority
should be informed by those involved in the waste chain of any suspected
breach of the provisions of the Act.°?

Department of Environment view
Annex B to the Code of Practice provides a helpful summary of what the
Department of the Environment at least sees as the responsibilities under
the duty. The advice for the construction and demolition industries con-
tained in Circular 19/91 is repeated.
Waste producers are said to bear the main responsibility for:
— care of the waste while they hold it;
— the description of the waste which leaves them;
— if they select a final treatment or disposal destination, for ensuring that
the waste falls within the terms of any licence or exemption (jointly with
the waste manager); and
— packing the waste to prevent escape in transit.
A producer is said to be responsible ‘‘according to what he knows or
should have foreseen’. He should take note of anything he learns about
the handling of his waste, particularly if he learns or suspects that it is not
being taken to a legitimate destination.
The example given is where a carrier’s vehicles are seen returning for
collection in a shorter period than would be required to reach the correct
disposal site, or where the producer becomes aware that a carrier is il-
legally disposing of someone else’s waste. This evidence is ignored at the
producer’s peril.
Those importing
waste
are
advised
to act
in
most
respects
as if
they were
the
producer.
A
waste
carrier
should ensure
that
packaging
is
adequate and
make
	at
least
a
visual
inspection
	to see that the
waste complies
	with the
written description.
Only
if
the
waste
	alters in
any
way
will a
new
	descrip-tion
be necessary on the next
transfer.
	The
	carrier
will
need
to
check
	the
terms
of
any
licence
or
exemption where
he
transfers
waste
for
disposal
or
treatment.
Waste brokers (which,
as
noted above, could include
in the construction
context the employer/client)
	are
	advised
	to act as if
they were both pro-
ducer and the
final
waste manager. Ultimately, they need
to
be sure of the
legality of the whole waste chain from “cradle to grave’’.

102 Impact on Construction Activites
Otherwise it is likely that any contractor working on a construction site
who transports controlled waste away from that site or any contractor
coming to the site to collect waste will need to be registered. Controlled
waste is given the same meaning for the purposes of this Act as for the
Control of Pollution Act.®
Registration can be refused by the Waste Regulation Authority, to
whom application must be made, where either the applicant or another
relevant person® has been convicted of a ‘“‘prescribed’”’ offence and in the
opinion of the authority it is undesirable for the applicant to be authorised
to transport controlled waste. Existing registration may be revoked on the
same grounds.® Prescribed offences for the purposes of the Act are listed
in Schedule 1 to the Controlled Waste (Registration of Carriers and
Seizure of Vehicles) Regulations 1991 and in the main involve offences in
relation to the handling of waste.
The details of the application process are also outlined in those Regula-
tions. Section 4 of the Act provides for a right of appeal against refusal of
registration.
Another provision of the Act which is of interest is section 5 by which
the police and duly authorised officers of waste regulation authorities are
given the power to stop anyone appearing to be transporting controlled
waste in contravention of section 1 of the Act and to require that person to
produce his or his employer’s authority for transporting that waste. Powers
are also given to search the vehicle and take away samples for testing of
anything that is found. It is an offence intentionally to obstruct either the
police or such an officer in the exercise of their powers.®©

	of
a
67 SI 1980/1709.
68 Regulation 1.

-- 139 of 360 --

104 Impact on Construction Activites
the waste. One copy of the consignment note must be retained by the
producer. All the remaining copies of the consignment note are to be given
to any carrier removing the waste from the site.
The purpose of the Regulations is to allow the waste regulation authority
to trace the progress of the waste to its final destination. Hence under
Regulation 5, the carrier of the waste, having completed various parts of
the note, is to furnish copies of the consignment note to the disposer of the
waste. Keeping one copy for himself, the disposer of the waste must then
produce a copy of the consignment note to the disposal authority, again
retaining copies for himself.
The producer of special waste is to keep, at each site at which it is
produced, a register containing copies of all consignment notes relating to
special waste produced at the site. A carrier is to keep a register of consign-
ment notes relating to all special waste he has transferred for disposal. A
disposer at each disposal site is to keep a register containing copies of all
consignment notes relating to special waste disposal at that site.
The records are to be kept for two years in the case of producers and
carriers. Disposers are to keep copies of consignment notes until such time
as the relevant waste disposal licence is surrendered or revoked.© A dis-
poser of special waste is also to keep a record of where precisely on site the
special waste is disposed of.
It is probably unlikely that special waste will be received on a con-
struction site for what amounts to a disposal. It is more likely, however,
that it may be produced, perhaps through demolition or excavation of a
contaminated site, and that the duties under the Special Waste Regulations
will need to be complied with.
Failure
to
comply
with
the
Regulations
is
once
	more
	a
criminal offence.
The important
	point
is
that
compliance
	with the
Special
Waste
	Regula-
tions
does
not
obviate
the
need
to
comply
also
with the
duty
of care
under
section
34 of the
Environmental
	Protection Act
in
respect of
a
written de-
scription
and

Civil liability for damage caused by waste
Much of this paper has concentrated on the potential criminal liabilities in
the management of waste either brought on to a construction site or
produced on a site. However, damage caused by that waste may also give rise
to civil liability. This may arise either by statute or under the common law.
The

As with section 88(1) of the Control of Pollution Act, the provision is
without prejudice to any other claim that may arise from the damage
caused. Damage is defined as previously and again, liability is not strict as
the defences available under section 33(7) of the Act are again available in
a claim under this section.
The important point with both provisions is that they apply to the
“deposit” of waste and not apparently to the other forms of treatment
which might require authority. This will limit the situations in which the
section will apply to the construction site. Liability will in this context,
however, attach not just to the contractor who may directly make the
deposit but to any person who knowingly permitted the deposit. An addi-
tion in the Environmental Protection Act is “knowingly” before “‘caused”’.
Interestingly the subsection does not cover damage resulting from the
failure to comply with the duty of care under section 34 of the Environmen-
tal Protection Act. It is likely, however, that where there has been a failure
to comply with that duty, the enforcing authority may also bring a prosecu-
tion under section 33(1)(c) of the Act thereby remedying the position.
Both
civil
liability
provisions
need
to

viewed

	the
	person
	who made
	the
	deposit
	or
knowingly
caused
or

permitted
	the

to

place.’”?

Waste: its disposal and use 107
EC proposals on civil liability
Liability for damage caused by waste is also very much an issue at Euro-
pean Community level and an area where a number of proposals are under
consideration. The Council of Europe has published a circular on Civil
Liability for Damage Resulting from Activities Dangerous to the Environ-
ment (which the United Kingdom does not propose at the moment to sign)
and the Commission has issued, for discussion, a Green Paper on remedy-
ing environmental damage.’
The most important proposal, as far as waste in particular is concerned, is
the draft directive on Civil Liability for Damage Caused by Waste.74 The
directive proposes a system of strict liability for “damage” (defined as
damage resulting from death or physical injury or damage to property) or
“impairment of the environment” (defined as any significant physical, chemi-
cal or biological deterioration of the environment, insofar as it does not also
constitute “damage”’), from waste generated in the course “of an occupa-
tional activity”. “Waste” is defined as in the Waste Framework Directive.
Liability
	is
imposed
	on
	the
producer
	of the
waste
	(which
	includes
	the
person
with control
of the
waste
if
he cannot, within
a
reasonable period
of
time, identify the
producer) from
	the
moment
	that
waste
	arises until
its
final
	disposal
	(provided
	that
	disposal
	is
at
	a
properly
	licensed
	site
	or
installation
	and
	the
disposer
	of the
waste
	has not
	been
	misled
	as
to
its
character).
Member
	States,
by
national
	laws,
are to
determine
	the
remedies
	avail-
able but these
	must
	include injunctions
	restricting the
	act
	causing
	the
damage
	or
impairment
	or
ordering reinstatement, and
damages.
	Further,
and
importantly,
common
	interest
groups must
be given
the right
to
take
or
join
an action.
Exclusion
	clauses
will
be prohibited and
the
existence
	of
a
licence
	or
permit
	will
not
be
a
defence.
	Issues of
contributory
	fault
will,
however,
	arise.
A
	limitation
	period
	will
be
imposed
	of three years
	from
	the date of
awareness
	(or
deemed
	awareness)
	of the
damage
	or
impairment
	and
thirty
years
	from
	the date of the incident giving
	rise to
	the
	damage
	or
impairment.
Finally,
producers
	will
be required
	to
be insured against
liability
under
the directive
	and
to
include
	a
note of the
insurance
	in
any annual
	report.
The
	directive
provides
that the
Commission
	is to
study the feasibility of
a
European Fund
for
Compensation where
the
person
liable
cannot be ident-
ified or is
unable
to
compensate
	fully.

Personal liability
As a final point, the directors and managers of construction companies
should not sit too comfortably behind the corporate veil. In a provision
familiar from the Health and Safety at Work Act 1974 both the Control of
Pollution Act’> and the Environmental Protection Act7® provide that
where an offence has been committed under the Act by a body corporate
and
“is proved to have been committed with the consent or connivance of, or to have
been attributable to any neglect on the part, of any director, manager, secretary
or other similar officer of the body corporate or a person who was purporting to
act in any such capacity, he as well as the body corporate shall be guilty of the
offence and shall be liable to be proceeded against and punished accordingly.”
Experience shows
	that
	the
	enforcing
	authorities
	are
	already taking
advantage
	of these
	provisions
	and
	will
increasingly
	do
	so
	under
	public
pressure
	to
prosecute
	those seen
	as
truly
responsible
	for
environmental
offences
and
damage.

5 Hazardous substances and clean-up
Dr Stephan A Jefferis
Introduction
The purpose of this paper is to give an overview of the engineering and
technical issues associated with contaminated land and landfills. There are
of course social as well as technical issues that must be debated and
answers must be formulated to questions such as:
— What is an acceptable level of contamination?
— How clean is clean?
-- Should every contaminated site be cleaned?
—
	Should
	landfills
be
sited
only
in
geologically
suitable areas
	despite
the
extra
haul
costs?
—
	What
	price
should we
be
prepared
to
pay
for
landfill?
—
	How
	should
toxic
and
hazardous
	wastes be
disposed?
—
	What
proportion
of the
national
budget
should be spent
on
the
clean-up
of past
pollution
and
the
prevention
of
new
	pollution?
At
	the
	present time
	there
	are
	no
	simple answers
	to
these questions.
Furthermore
	there
	are
	not
	even generally
	accepted
	definitions of basic
terms such
as
“hazardous”
	and
“toxic”.
	However,
	two
	definitions can
be
advanced:
—
	hazard
	is
the
	property
	or
	characteristic of
a
	material, situation
	or
activity
which
gives
it
potential
for
harm; and
—
	tisk
	is
the
	likelihood
	or
	expected frequency
	of
a
specified
	adverse
consequence.
Thus
	the
	purpose
	of
engineering work
	associated with
	contaminated
land and
	landfills
	should be
to
reduce
	the
risk
posed by
the
	hazardous
substances
	that
may
be present
as
contaminants
	in soil or
which
	are
being
or
	have been disposed
	to landfill
	and
	to
do
	this at a
reasonable
	cost.
Unfortunately
	the techniques of risk analysis are relatively
	new and
although they are very useful for identifying relative risks associated with
different actions/remedial measures
	etc.
the evaluation of absolute risks
still leaves much room for debate.
109

-- 145 of 360 --

110 Impact on Construction Activites
At the present time perceived hazard tends to be the driving force for
much pollution control whereas risk ought to be the controlling factor.
Designers should be required to ensure that their facilities meet realistic
risk criteria (which may in themselves be difficult to accept) and, for
example, could take the form that a site should not cause more than 1 extra
cancer per 100,000 of the exposed population (the chance of death from
cancer in the general population is probably in the range 1 in 3 to 1 in 7).
Because
	there
	is
currently no
	generally
	accepted
	definition of
what
	is
hazardous
in
this
paper
the
term
contamination
	will
be used
to
describe
all
materials
that
could be
hazardous.
It
is
often held
that
contamination
	may
	be present
	in
all
formerly
	used
industrial
land.
However,
	if
all
materials
	which
	may
	be
damaging
	to
man,
his
structures
	or
	the
environment
	are
	regarded
	as
contaminants
	then
	it
must
	be
	accepted
	that
	all
land
	is
potentially
	contaminated. Nature
	has
produced more
toxic
chemicals
than
man
	and
even
in
England
very aggres-
sive
natural
soils
can
be found.
However, when
	considering
	the
contamination
	of
land
it is
usually
the
chemicals
etc.
that
have
been brought
onto
the
site
by
past
or
current
usersthat
	are
	of the
	most
	immediate
	concern.
	Some
	of the
	major problems
associated with
contaminated
	land
and
landfills
are:
— threats to human health;
— damage to flora and fauna;
— contamination of ground water;
-
	damage
	of
underground
	foundations and
structures;
-
	settlement
	of the
	ground
	as
a
	result
	of
	degradation
	of
	deposited
materials;
and
—
	migration
of
contaminants
	to
adjacent
land.
Contaminant migration
The
	migration
	of
contaminants
	is
possibly one
	of the
most
	serious issues
associated with
contamination.
	If
contaminants remained where
they were
initially
deposited and
	did not
move
	the
only necessary preventive mea-
sures
would
be
to limit direct
contact with
or
ingestion of the
contaminant.
However,
the
contaminants do
move
	and contamination
will
always tend
to
spread. In
this
respect
	contamination
	can be likened
	to heat.
	If a
rod
	is
heated
at
one end then the heat
will
be conducted along
it
and
the heat flow
can be stopped only
if it is cut
and
an insulator
is
interposed
in the bar. If
contamination
	is
present in the soil it will
tend to
spread outwards unless
stopped by some barrier. To control the spread of contamination
	it is
therefore necessary to identify the mechanisms by which contamination

-- 146 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up ATT
may spread in the ground and what may form a barrier. Contamination
may move as a result of:
— solution in ground water and movement with the water or diffusion
through it;
— solution in rain water percolating down through the soil;
— density differentials between liquid contaminants or solutions; and the
soil pore fluid;
— the generation and migration of gases;
— dust blown from the surface of soils especially if poorly vegetated;
— uptake by plant roots etc; and
— human activity.
Fundamentally
	all
natural
processes tend
to
reduce
differences
of
poten-
tial
in
the
environment. These
	potentials
	may
	be
of
temperature,
	water
pressure,
chemical concentration
	etc.
The
process
of diffusion
noted
above
is
a
typical
example
of
such
a
levelling
process.
Molecules
	in
a
liquid
are in
continual
	movement
	and
this
movement
	will
tend
to
smooth
	out
any
dif-
ferences
	in
concentration. For
	example,
	tea
will
diffuse
	from
	a
tea
bag
immersed
	in
water and
the
solution
will
take on
a
uniform
	colour
	after a
few
minutes
	even without
	stirring.
Thus
	the
spread
of
contamination
	is
a
natural process
and
to
stop
it
will
require
either that
the
source
is
removed
or
the
continued operation
of
some
	control system.
When
	considering
contaminant
	migration
it
is
also
important
to
keep
in
mind
	that
some
	materials,
	either
natural
or
man
	made,
which
	are
initially
harmless
may become
	toxic
or
hazardous
	as a
result
of
deposition/reaction
with the
	ground
	water
	etc.
	A
	trivial
	example
	of
such
	a
process
	is
the
generation
	of
methane
	and carbon
	dioxide
	from
	rubbish deposited
	in
a
landfill.
	These
	gases
	may
	be
	generated from
	the
	degradation
	of
paper
which
in
itself is
harmless.
	Another example
is
the
generation
of
sulphuric
acid
and
the release of
heavy
metals
	from some mine
	spoils
as
a
result
of
oxidation
of materials such
as
iron
sulphide
by atmospheric oxygen
	(these
materials
are
stable
at
depth
in
the
ground where oxygen
is
not available).
Interestingly both
methane
	generation and mine
spoil
oxidation
are
bacte-
rially
mediated and
	when
	considering any
	contamination
	present
	in the
ground
	it is
important
	to
keep
	in
mind
	that bacteria
	may
	create niche
environments where
	the
chemical conditions and
thus the reaction prod-
ucts are
quite different to
those that
would be expected
in the bulk
soil.
It
must
	also
	be noted
	that very small
	amounts
	of
a
contaminant may
“pollute” very large volumes of
soil or
ground water. For example
the guide
level for
many chlorinated solvents in drinking water is 1 part per billion.
These materials typically have a solubility in water of 1 part in one thousand.

-- 147 of 360 --

112 Impact on Construction Activites
Thus a spill of 1 kg of solvent could saturate 1000 litres of ground water with
the solvent and as it spread out into the surrounding ground water it could
contaminate a total 1,000,000,000 litres of ground water to the guide level.
Finally not all chemicals will behave in the same way in the soil and it is
useful to distinguish a number of material types.
Physical properties of contaminants
Solid materials of low solubility
These are materials of low solubility in ground water. They may move only
slowly from the region where they were deposited. However, caution is
necessary when describing any contaminant as of low solubility. For, as
shown above, materials can be unacceptable in water when present at
concentrations much below their solubility limit. Thus solubility is mainly
of concern as regards the rate of migration from the site of deposition and
says nothing about the resulting risk.
Many heavy metals in the soil may be in low solubility forms but are still
capable of rendering any ground water with which they come in contact
unfit for use as drinking water.
Soluble materials
Soluble materials
are
those
which
can dissolve
in
and
move
	with
the
ground
water
and
thus
may move
	relatively
rapidly
from
the
point
of
deposition
if
there
	is
a
significant
	flow of
ground
	water
	or
	infiltration
	of
rain
	water.
However,
	if
water
	movement
	is
limited,
	for
example
	in
clay
soils,
evenhighly soluble
	chemicals
	may
	remain
	as
solids.
	The
	author
	has seen
	ex-
amples
	of
highly soluble
	and even hygroscopic chemicals which have
	re-
mained
	as
bulk
solids in
the
soil
for
many
	decades.
Light non-aqueous phase liquids
These
	are liquids
which
	are
of
low
solubility in
water
	(non-aqueous phase
liquids)
and which
are less
dense than water.
These
liquids
when
spilled
or
released
	into the
soil will
sink
downwards
	through unsaturated
	soils until
they meet
the
ground water
table.
At
this
point they
will
cease
to sink
and
will float
on the water and then spread
laterally
from
the point of release.
In this
way
light
non-aqueous phase
liquids can travel substantial distances
horizontally from the point of release.
	An example of this
type of liquid
would be petroleum
spirit and spillages from garage forecourts have caused
problems in basements at some considerable distance.

-- 148 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up 113
Dense non-aqueous phase liquids
There
	are
also
non-aqueous
	phase
fluids
which
	are
denser
than
water
and
which
will
sink
down
	through
the
soil
but
will
not
stop
when
they
meet
the
ground
water
	but
continue
	to
sink
through
	it.
An
	example
	of
this
type
of
contamination would
be
the
chlorinated
solvents
that
caused
the
problems
in
Eastern Counties
Leather.
A
feature
of
dense
non-aqueous
phase
liquids
(dnapls)
is
that
they
can
move
	down
through
sands
and
gravels but
may
not
penetrate
finer
soils
such
as
silts
and
clays.
Thus
dnapls
may
sink
until
they
meet
	a
fine
	soil
layer.
	They
	will
	then
	move
	horizontally following any
natural slope
of the layer
and
finally
pond
in
any
natural
depressions
in the
layer.
Thus
	dnapls
may
	end
up
as
discrete
pools
at
considerable
	distance
from
	the original
	point
	of
release.
	These
	pools
	may
	be
very
	difficult
	to
locate
or
remediate.
Gases
Gases
	can
move
	only
through
soils
which
have
	a
continuous system
of
air
voids unless
the gas
pressure
is
sufficient
to
blow
water
out of the
soil
and
force
a
path
through
it.
A
soil
can
be likened
to
a
system
of
fine capillary
tubes
and
the
pressure necessary
to
blow
ground
water
out of these
‘tubes’
can
be
relatively
high
—
especially
in
fine
soils.
Thus,
in
general, gases
will
move
	through
soil
only
in
the
region
above
the
ground
water
table.
Below
the
ground
water
table gas
migration
can
be only
by
solution
in
and
move-
ment through
	or
with
the
ground
water
	unless
	high
driving pressures
	are
available.
The
	movement
	of air/gases (for
example
	landfill
gas)
through
	the
soil
may
be strongly influenced
by
the
prevailing
weather
	conditions.
—
	Tn
wet
	weather
	the surface
	may
	be flooded
	thus limiting vertical gas
escape which
may
lead
to a
pressure
build
up and
a
tendency
for
greater
lateral
migration
of the
gas.
—
	In
dry weather
	the
soil
may
dry and
crack thus
opening up
large chan-
nels for gas flow.
—
	In periods of
low pressure gases
may
	tend
to
be
drawn
	out of the
soil
and
in
periods of high pressure
air
may
be forced
in.
It
should be noted
that there
is a
continual
movement
	of
air into
and out of
soils
near the ground
	surface. This
movement
	is
driven by diffusion and
changes in atmospheric pressure. If this
movement were to cease much
plant life would die.

114 Impact on Construction Activites
Tars and semi-liquids
A number of former industrial processes and especially those involved in
coal gasification produced tars as by-products. The heavier tars may at first
sight appear to be solids but under sustained pressure they will flow. Thus if
a tar is covered with soil it may slowly squeeze through the soil and at some
much later time may break out at the surface.
Sorbed materials
Many soils are relatively inert and when chemicals are deposited in them
they act merely as a “‘sponge”’ holding liquids but not reacting with or
modifying them. However, for some combinations of soil type and con-
taminant the soil may act as a chemical as well as physical sponge. That is
the chemicals may bind to the soil. For example, organic soils such as peat
may very effectively bind organic chemicals such as petroleum spirit and
clays may very effectively sorb some heavy metals. In general finer soils
will be more effective chemical sponges than coarser soils as the chemicals
are sorbed on the surface of the particles (if a coarse particle is cut into
pieces its surface area will increase rapidly but the total weight of material
will remain unchanged). Thus fine soils have much higher surface areas per
unit weight than coarse materials.
Sorption
	can very substantially retard the
	migration
	of
	chemicals
through
the
ground
	and
much
	use
has
been
	made
	of the
sorption charac-
teristics
of clays
when
	designing
repositories
	for
nuclear
	and
toxic
waste.
Sorption
is
particularly useful
for
the
control of
materials
which
will
decay
naturally such
as
radioactive waste and
some
	organic
materials.
	The
hold
up introduced
	by
sorption
	may
	ensure
	that
	substantial
	degradation
	has
occurred before any material reaches
the
biosphere.
It is
also
important
to
keep
in
mind
	the effects
of
sorption
when
	under-
taking
site
investigation
	work
	as
certain parts of the
soil
profile
may
	be
much
more
	heavily
contaminated
	than
others.
As
a final
point
it
should be noted
that
any chemical which
is
sorbed and
does
	not
	decay
	naturally
	will
ultimately
	move
	forward
	with the
ground
water.
	Thus
	sorption
results in the
slowing
down
	of
migration
	but not an
absolute
	stop.
	However,
	with strongly sorbed materials the
	retardation
may
	be
by
a
factor of 1,000
or
more and
in clays
or
other
soils
where
	the
ground water
is
only very slowly
moving
sorption
may
provide
an effective
barrier for centuries.

The chemistry of contaminants
The
	above
	classification
	is
fundamentally
	based
	on
	physical
	rather
	than
chemical
	properties
of
the
contaminants.
	Chemically contaminants
	can
be
very
	diverse
	and
it is
not

	to
give
even
	an
	overview
	of
chemical
phenomena
	in

	paper.
	However,
	it is
appropriate
	to
note
	some
general
chemical
types.
Inorganic chemicals
Inorganic chemicals
	include such materials
	as
sodium
	chloride
(table
salt),
calcium
	carbonate
	(chalk)
	etc.
The
	heavy
	metals
	chromium,
	nickel cad-
mium
etc.
are
inorganic chemicals.
A
particular feature
of
inorganic
chemi-
cals
	is
that
	they cannot
	be
	broken
	down
	to
harmless
	sub-constituents.
Cadmium
	will
remain
toxic
regardless
of the
other
chemicals
	with
which
it
is
associated
though
it
may
be
made
	less

if it is
precipitated
from
solution
or
absorbed
into
a

or
other
solids
(contaminants
are
said
to
be
available
if

easy

	the
source
	of the
contamina-
tion,
and
the

or
target

	see
below).
Organic chemicals
Carbon
	is
unique
	amongst
	the
chemical elements
	in
the
	range
	of
com-
pounds
	that
it
can form.
	Chemicals
	containing
	carbon and
	hydrogen
	are
collectively
known
	as
organic chemicals and
they
are
the basis of
all
living
organisms and
many
	industrial
processes including
the
plastics
and
petro-
chemical
industries.
Aliphatic organic chemicals
Aliphatic organic chemicals
are
materials
which
contain
the basic
elements
carbon and
hydrogen
	either
as
straight
or
branched
	chains.
	Ethyl alcohol
and butane
are
examples
of such materials.
Aliphatic
hydrocarbons
tend
to
be
of
low
solubility in
water
	unless they contain elements which promote
solubilisation such
as
oxygen, nitrogen, sulphur
etc.
Aromatic organic chemicals
Aromatic organic chemicals
	are similar to the aliphatics save that the
chains are closed to
form ring structures.
	Benzene with a single six
mem-
bered carbon ring is the simplest aromatic chemical. Coal tars such as are
found on old coal gasification sites may contain several interlinked rings.

-- 151 of 360 --

116 Impact on Construction Activites
As a general guideline aromatic chemicals tend to be more toxic and/or
carcinogenic than aliphatic materials. They also tend to be slightly more
soluble. As a result aromatic chemicals may pose a greater environmental/
health threat than aliphatic chemicals. However, on many contaminated
sites both types of chemical will be found and thus the distinction between
aliphatics and aromatics may be of limited significance.
Toxicity
A fundamental concern about contamination is that it may cause harm to
human health. Many different classes of individuals may be at risk from the
contamination present on a site. These may include site workers, neigh-
bours, those who use ground water from neighbouring wells etc. Exposure
to the contaminants may be by contact, inhalation, direct ingestion of
contaminated soil, drinking or bathing in contaminated water, consuming
meat or milk etc. from cattle which have grazed on the site etc. As a result
it can be extremely difficult to quantify the dose received by any class of
individuals and exposure may have to be assessed on the basis of worst
credible levels. For populations living adjacent to sources of contamination
it also may be necessary to consider lifetime average doses.
Once some measure of exposure has been established it is then necessary
to establish the response. For this, dose-response relationships are necessary.
However, establishing the response is complex. At the simplest, chemicals
may have two types of toxic effect: acute and chronic. Acute toxics will
produce immediate symptoms (such as are identified in the definition of
special waste, see below). Data on acute toxicity actually determined for man
are quite limited and are mainly based on accidental ingestion of chemicals
though some data have been provided by methodical murderers. The bulk of
acute toxicity data are in fact extrapolated from animal tests.
Chronic
	toxicity
	is
that arising
	from continued exposure
	to
low
	con-
centrations
	(below
the acute
level).
The
	data on chronic
toxicity
are
even
more
	limited than
for
acute
	toxicity.
Most
	of the data on chronic
	toxicity
relate to
exposure
	in the
workplace where workers have been subjected
to
relatively
	high
	levels
	of
contamination
	over
	many
	years.
	This type of
exposure
	may
	have
	little
relevance
	to
contaminated
	land
where
	the
dose
may
	be
much
	lower and possibly over
	a
shorter time span.
	Indeed
	very
often the doses
	will
be
so
small that
they produce
	no
	measurable
	effect.
The fundamental
	issue
is
then whether
very small doses have
a
cumulativeeffect so that
	continued exposure
	or
	repeated doses ultimately cause
damage. For some chemicals
	it
would seem that there
is a
minimum
	dose
level below which there is no effect but for others and especially car-
cinogens it is generally held that they can produce additive effects at all

-- 152 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up hy
concentrations. This may be an overstatement of the real situation but at
least it is conservative.
The situation is further complicated by the fact that there may be sub-
stantial variations in response between individuals. The balance between
intake and elimination of chemicals from the body may be age-dependent
and there may be threshold effects and possibly a long time lag between
exposure and appearance of symptoms (e.g. asbestos). Also there may be
strong synergistic effects (where the total effect of a combination of constit-
uents is greater than the sum of their individual effects) or antagonistic
effects (the functional opposite of synergistic effects, where the total effect
is less than the sum of effects of the constituents). A further problem is that
symptoms may be non specific. For example the human body seems to have
a limited number of ways to react to insults. Thus many chemicals may
produce the same clinical manifestation.
The
Control
	of
Pollution
	(Special
Waste)
Regulations

provide

	any
	material
	containing substances

	in

	is

	to

	to

(a)

	than

would

	to
tissue

a

20

body

or
(b)
	exposure
	to
it
for
15

	or less
would
	be
likely
to
cause
	serious
damage
	to
human
	tissue
by
inhalation,
skin
contact
or
eye

These
definitions
are cited
as
examples
	of the
difficulty
of
defining hazard.
Clearly any deliberate
testing
would
be
quite
unacceptable.
Waste
	Management
	Paper
	No.
	23 gives
a
substantial
	body
	of
toxicity

and

	of
its

to
the identification of special wastes.
Even
	for
inanimate matter such

	or

	minimal
	data on the

	of

Source-path-target models
From
the preceding discussion on contaminant migration and toxicity it
can
be seen that there are many uncertainties associated with the analysis of
the effects of contamination. These include:

-- 153 of 360 --

118 Impact on Construction Activites
— uncertainties relating to contaminant migration;
— uncertainties regarding the effects of chemicals both singly and in the
complex mixtures typical of landfill leachate or contaminated land; and
— the multiplicity of targets that may be affected by the contamination
including fauna, flora, structures etc.
Because of these uncertainties it is now recognised that for most
situations it is necessary to adopt techniques of. probabilistic risk
analysis. In general these techniques will seek to analyse. the situation by
considering:
— the sources of contamination;
— the pathways by which the contamination may reach the targets; and
— the targets. }
The source is thus the contamination which may include many different
chemicals. The pathway is the route by which the contaminant reaches the
target which may be human, the environment generally or part of a struc-
ture. Examples of pathways include: drinking contaminated ground water
or bathing in it, direct ingestion of contaminated soil by a child suffering
from pica, eating of meat from cattle which have grazed on a contaminated
site or breathing air contaminated with vapours from the site.
To
do
a
full
analysis of the
potential
	effects
of
contaminated
	land
it
is
necessary
to
consider
the
many
possible sources
that
might
be
on
a
site,
the
multiple
pathways
	that
may
	exist to
the
many
	different
targets.
The
situa-
tion
is
thus
exceedingly
complex
and
it
is
for
this
reason
	that
it
is
necessary
to
adopt
statistical
techniques
of
risk
analysis.
Using
such
	procedures
	it
is
possible
	to
investigate
	‘‘what-if”’
	situations
and
	in
particular
	to
identify significant
	and
	insignificant
	chemicals, path-
ways,
targets
etc.
From
the
results
of
this
type
of analysis
it
may
be possible
to
set
limits
on
contamination
so
that the
risk
of
harm
to
specific targets in
a
number
	of
generic
situations
is
acceptably
	low.
Thus
	it
may
	be possible
to
produce
	tables of
acceptable
	levels for
various
contaminants.
	Many
	coun-
tries
including
the
UK
	already have
	tables
giving
guidance
	on
acceptablelevels
of
contamination
	etc.
However,
	for the
most
part they
are
not
based
on
a
common
	level
of
risk
and
in a
number
	of countries the figures are
nowbeing re-worked
	to
	put
	them
	on
	a
	more formal
	risk
	based
	footing.
Previously the tabulated values
may
have been dictated
by many
	different
issues including aesthetics
as
well
as toxicity.
In principle
a
full risk analysis will
always be preferable
to an analysis
by
reference to generic tabular values as individual site specific data can be
included. However, a full risk analysis may be very expensive and also

-- 154 of 360 --

currently the number of suitably qualified individuals to carry out such
analyses is limited.
Clean-up of contaminated sites
There
	are
a
large
number
	of
proven
and
developing
soil
and
ground
water
remediation
	technologies
but
as
yet rather
few have
been
employed
in
the
UK
with
the
exception
of
digging
up and
removal
of
the
contamination
to
a
landfill.
However,
removal
to
landfill
merely
transfers the
problem
to
a
new
(though
it
is
to
be
hoped
more
	secure)
location.
Thus
	there
is
pressure
	to
adopt
procedures
which do
not
involve
transfer.
The
suitability
of
a
treatment
method
	at
any given
site
will
be influenced
by
the characteristics of
the
site
and
	the
remedial
	technologies
	that
are
available.
Important
characteristics of the
site
will
include:
—
	the
types
of
contaminants
	at
the
site,
their
distribution
and
depth;
—
	the
subsurface
conditions
	(type
of
soil,
depth
to
the
water
table);
—
	the
surrounding
environment
	(houses, parks, industry);
and
— the time available for remediation.
Significant
factors
in
evaluating any
potential
treatment option
will
include:
— its availability;
— its state of development;
—
	its
demonstrated
	reliability
and
effectiveness;
—
	its
perceived
environmental
	impacts;
and
— its cost.
Large
sites
may
permit
the use of relatively
new
	technologies
	after
suit-
able
testing,
while small
	sites will
generally be
remediated
	using proven,
easily
available
techniques.
The source-pathway-target model
	is
useful
when
	categorising clean-up
technologies.
	Thus
clean-up
may
	involve
removal
	of the source, cutting of
pathways,
for
example by mechanical
	barriers,
or
hardening
the target (for
4
structure
this
could involve using
more chemically

Source removal
As already noted source removal can be achieved by digging out the con-
tamination and removing
it to a landfill.
Removal
	to landfill is
not properly
a clean-up procedure
	as it
merely transfers the problem. However,
	it is
practised very widely though its use may decline as landfill costs rise and

-- 155 of 360 --

120 Impact on Construction Activites
actual clean-up technologies become more cost effective. An important
point to note for disposal to landfill is that the chemical data used to assess
the significance of the contamination at the original site (for example the
parameters listed in the Interdepartmental Committee on the Redevelop-
ment of Contaminated Land, ICRCL guidance notes) may not be sufficient
to enable its disposal to landfill. Landfill disposal licences may pose quite
different controls from those used to assess contamination in-situ. For
example, the licence could limit the total amount of a rather short list of
contaminants that may be accepted in the landfill per day. or week but not
the maximum acceptable concentration in the soil. Thus, for example, on
site a soil might be designated as contaminated as a result of the concentra-
tion of organic material in it but the rate at which it could be accepted into
a landfill might be controlled by another constituent, for example mercury.
If the source is to be treated rather than removed then there are three
options. It may be treated in situ, it may be dug up and treated on-site, or it
may be taken to a remote treatment facility. Ideally contamination would be
treated in-situ as digging it up will always involve some spread of the con-
tamination and exposure of workers to physical contact, dust, vapours etc.
Source clean-up procedures include:
Bioremediation
The fundamental concept of bioremediation is the use of bacteria to
destroy organic contaminants. Bacteria may use organic contaminants as
food for their activity and in so doing break down the contaminants to
simpler molecules and ultimately to carbon dioxide, methane and water
etc. Bacteria cannot destroy inorganic contaminants such as heavy metals
though they may be used to extract or immobilise such materials.
Bioremediation is still a quite young technology but has great potential in
terms of cost and effectiveness for certain chemicals including petrol, diesel
fuel, light mineral oils, and aromatic chemicals such as benzene, toluene
etc. The basis of the process is to establish conditions in the soil which will
enable those bacteria which can break down the organic materials to
flourish. If the appropriate conditions are not established the numbers of
bacteria may remain small and the rate of clean-up may be unacceptably
slow. In general, establishing the right conditions will involve getting
oxygen in the soil. Many natural organic chemicals such as oils will have
survived for millennia in oxygen free environments and thus are effectively
immune to bacterial attack in such environments. However, if oxygen and
water are present they may be quite rapidly decomposed. At the present
time only bacteria naturally present in the host soil are used for
bioremediation as there is concern about the introduction of “foreign”

-- 156 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up 121
bacteria into the soil. However, it may be necessary to boost the numbers
of bacteria present in the soil by adding bacteria drawn from the site but
cultured in the laboratory.
The rate of degradation decreases as chemical compounds become more
complex, so that PCBs (polychlorinated biphenyls which can_bio-
accumulate in living tissue), carbon tetrachloride, trichloroethylene and
similar chemicals and pesticides are not readily biodegradable. Some com-
panies have developed specific micro-organisms to attack some of these
chemicals, but as already noted the use of imported bacteria is considered
to be doubtful.
In-situ
	bioremediation works
	best
	in
sandy
	soils,
	at sites
	where
	the
geology
is
uniform.
	Sites
with
well-defined point
sources
of
contamination
(e.g.
a
leaking tank)
are
more
	amenable
	to in-situ
bioremediation
than
sites
with
multiple
	and
	ill-defined
	sources
	of
contamination.
	It is
often
	a
pre-
ferred
	approach
	when
	the
contamination
	is
on-site,
not affecting
any
off-
site
location
and
there
is
time
available
to
use
bioremediation
	(probably
in
the
order
of
6
months
	to
several
years).
When
	contaminated ground
	water
	is
being
bioremediated,
	it
is
usually
recirculated,
	with
	oxygen
	or
	hydrogen
	peroxide
	and/or
	nutrients
	being
added
	before
	the
water
	is
re-injected.
	In-situ
bioremediation
	is
being
in-
creasingly
used
in
conjunction
with other
techniques
such
as
vapour
extrac-
tion
	and
	sparging
	(see
below)
	to
provide
	overall
	remediation
	of
a

However,
	it
should be noted
	that
bioremediation
	cannot reduce
	the con-
centrations
	of
many
	contaminants
	to
the
levels that
would
be required
for
drinking
water.
As
the
concentration
of the
contaminants
	drops
so will
the
number
	of bacteria
and
at
some
	point
the
rate
of
breakdown may become
very slow
and
effectively
zero.
	If
further
breakdown
	is
required
it
may
be
necessary
	to
provide
additional non-toxic organic material
to
maintain
the
bacterial
numbers
	so
that the
contaminants
	are
removed by co-metabolism
of the
contaminants
	and
added
materials.
Soil vapour extraction
The techniques
of
soil
vapour
extraction, forced
air
venting,
and
in-situ air
stripping
all
revolve
around
the extraction of
air
containing
volatile
chemi-
cals
	from unsaturated
	soil.
	The vapour laden
	air is
withdrawn under
vacuum from extraction
wells,
while fresh
air is
either injected
or
allowed
to flow into the subsurface around
the area that
is
impacted.
It
was
initially
used
	in the early to
mid 1980s
	at
petrol service stations
	where there had
been
spills or leaks of fuel, but
its
use quickly spread
to the remediation of
other chemical compounds including chlorinated solvents (though
	as was
noted earlier such solvents tend to be denser than water and thus cannot be

-- 157 of 360 --

122 Impact on Construction Activites
removed as vapour unless the local ground water level is lowered or air is
injected under pressure so that it displaces the ground water).
Vapour extraction is only applicable to relatively volatile compounds
and this restricts its use. It could not be used to remove heavy oils and tars
etc.
Soil vapour extraction is particularly attractive at sites where areas of
contamination are large and deep (greater than about 6 m), or when the
contamination is beneath a building in relatively permeable soils.
The recovered gases also may need to be treated prior. to discharge to
atmosphere (for example by carbon absorption) and this can significantly
increase the cost of the process. On some sites it may be possible to reduce
costs by venting the gases into an existing industrial furnace or similar unit.
An alternative to soil vapour extraction is air sparging. In this process air
is injected into the soil rather than extracted from it. The air may be
injected either into the saturated or unsaturated zones of the soil. An
advantage of the process is that the provision of air compressors is gener-
ally cheaper than vacuum pumps. Treatment of contamination below the
water table (that is the saturated zone) is only possible in sands or coarser
soils as the pressure required to force water from finer soils would be
excessive.
Steam stripping
In-situ
steam
stripping
is
effectively
a
similar
procedure
to
air
sparging
save
that
the
soil is
heated
to
encourage
	volatilisation of the
contaminants.
	The
procedure
	was
first
used
in
the
Netherlands
	in
the early 1980s,
with
steam
being
injected
to treat
2
m by
2
m columns
	of
soil at
a
former
	gas
plant.
Steam
stripping has not
become
	widely used
as
an
in-situ
remedial
	method
but
it is
used
in
a
commercial
	ex-situ
process.
Soil washing
The
basic principle of
in-situ
soil
washing (sometimes
	called
soil
flushing)
is
the injection of clean
water adjacent
to
the
contamination and
the extraction
of
contaminated
	water. In principle
any reasonably
soluble material
may
be
removed by
in-situ soil
washing and even some non aqueous
	liquids
may
be
removed
	if a
surfactant
is
added
	to
the injected water.
	However,
	field tests
have shown
	the process
	to
be
less
effective than was
	first
hoped.
	It
would
seem
	that the
problem
	lies
in the fact that almost
	all
real soils are signifi-
cantly heterogeneous. The injected/abstracted water thus follows preferred
flow paths through the
soil.
These paths are rapidly cleaned but much con-
tamination remains in the adjacent slower flow paths. When washing is

-- 158 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up 123
stopped the contamination diffuses back from the slower flow paths thus
re-establishing a significant overall average level of contamination in the soil.
In principle if the process is regularly repeated the average level of
contamination could be reduced to an acceptable level but in many soils this
would take an unacceptable time. Soil washing is now seldom used as an in-
situ process but once again the process is available as an ex-situ technique.
Electrokinetics
From
	the
foregoing
discussion
it
can
be
seen
that
many
	remedial
methods
are
	limited
	to
permeable
	sandy
	soils.
	One method
	which
	has
been
	used
with
	some
	success
	to
treat
	contamination
	at sites
on
	clayey/silty
	soils
is
electrokinetics.
	In
this
process electrodes
	are
	placed
	in
the
	soil
and
	an
electrical
	current
	is
passed
	through
	the
soil
to.
be
treated.
	This
induces
	a
flow of
both
	charged
	ions
	and water
	through
	the
	soil.
The
	process
	can
remove
	inorganic
	contaminants
	such
	as
heavy
	metals, cyanides,
	nitrates
and
	some
	organic
	materials
	may
	be
	carried out
	with
	the
	water
	that
	is
extracted
	from
	the
soil.
The
	process
	may
	be
substantially
	slowed
	by
any
buried metal
in
the
ground
and
it
may remove
	buried
plumbing!
Vitrification
In-situ vitrification
	is
	a
	thermal treatment
	process
	which
	converts
contaminated
	soil
into
a
durable
	glass
by
melting
the
soil
electrically.
The
process has
been under
development
	in
the
USA
	since
about 1980
when
	it
was
	initiated
as a
method
	to stabilise
high
level
radioactive wastes
	in-situ.
Electrodes
	are
placed
in
the
soil
and
the
soil
heated
	to
over
	2000°C,
	well
above
the
initial
melting
temperature
	of
soils.
	A
hood
placed
over the area
being
treated
is
used
to
draw
off
gases
for
treatment.
There
have been
only
limited
	full
size
applications (400
to
800
ton); these
	demonstrated some
problems
in
recovering
all
off-gases,
and
the
process
is
still
under develop-
ment, although
	it
has
	apparently been
	selected
	to
remediate some
	sites
impacted
	with organic contaminants,
	herbicides, pesticides,
PCBs,
	dioxins
and heavy
	metals.
	The
	vitrified
product
	is
about
	ten
times stronger than
unreinforced concrete and
largely
unaffected
by weathering.
Radio waves
This process
is
still
at the
development
stage but in a trial
demonstration 30
tons of soil
impacted with jet fuel,
chlorinated solvents and waste
	oil
were
heated by radio frequency waves prior to soil
vapour extraction the effect
of which was considerably enhanced by the heating.

124 Impact on Construction Activites
Ex-situ treatment
In-situ treatment may seem to be the ideal remedial strategy as it minimises
the exposure of workers to contamination and the generation of secondary
waste etc. However, it has been found that the natural heterogeneity of
soils has lead to rather poor overall clean-up performance. There is now a
movement towards ex-situ technologies. These may be carried out in plant
specially established on the site or at a centralised treatment centre. In such
plants the contaminated soil is treated as the feedstock of a chemical pro-
cess plant. A wide range of processes may be employed but the basic
technology is often a development of that used in-situ.
With all clean-up technologies and especially those that involve severe
treatment of the soil (e.g. incineration) it is necessary to consider the final
state of the soil. Severe treatment may leave it in a biologically dead state
and furthermore a state which may not be readily colonised by living
organisms. Also the processing may tend to release heavy metals naturally
present in the soil and make them more available.
Pathway interdiction
Cutting
	the
pathway
	involves placing
some
	form
	of barrier
	between
	the
source
	and
the
targets.
The
barrier
may
need
to
be horizontally
across the
source
as
a
cap,
underneath
it
and
vertically
around
the
periphery.
Capping
is
discussed
	at
page
	127
and need
	not
be considered
	further. Installing
	a
barrier
underneath
	a
large area of
contamination
	can
be very
difficult
and
techniques
	currently available
	are
limited
	to
the injection of grouts
	(fluid
materials
	which
	will
set)
underneath
	the
	contamination.
	The
	effects
	of
these
	techniques
	may
	be
rather limited
	and
	there
	may
	be
some
	residual
escape
of
contamination.
	However, many
sites
are
underlain
by
natural
low
permeability
	materials such
	as
clays.
Thus
	if
vertical walls
	are installed
around
a
site
to a
sufficient
depth
to
intersect
a
low permeability
layer thenthe
source
can
be contained. Vertical walls
are
now
	widely used
especially
to
prevent
	the
migration
	of
landfill gas.
The most popular
	method
	is
the
slurry trench
	cut-off
	wall.
	In
this
	process
	a
trench
	is
excavated
	in the
ground and
	as
the
excavation progresses
	a
slurry of
cement and
	clay
is
pumped
	into the trench. This slurry
is
designed
to exert sufficient
pressure
to
keep
the trench from collapsing during excavation and
to set
and harden
at
the
end of excavation
to
form
a
barrier.
Typically the width of the trench
may be
0.6
metres and the
depth may be of order 10 metres though much
deeper walls have been installed to intersect deep clay layers. Individual
walls may be several kilometres long and may take some months to install.
The permeability of the barrier can be such that the velocity of water

-- 160 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up 125
movement through it can be of the order of millimetres per year. A prob-
lem with clay-cement slurries is that they can shrink substantially on dry-
ing. Thus such walls may not be suitable for landfill gas migration control.
For gas control the technique has been developed to include the installa-
tion of a high density polyethylene membrane into the trench as excavation
progresses and prior to set of the slurry. At the present time these walls are
in regular use particularly for the control of gas migration from old land-
fills. There are a number of other techniques for wall installation and there
is particular interest in techniques which do not involve excavation as this
not only reduces the exposure of site workers to contamination but also
eliminates any need to dispose of contaminated arisings.
Hardening the target
This
	can
	only be
	considered
	for
inanimate
	targets
	such
	as
foundations
though
	it
could be
extended
	to
include
	flora
which
	can
	be
very
useful
in
reducing
problems
	associated with
old spoil
heaps
such
as
dusting
and
soil
erosion
by
rainwater.
	In
general
it
will
not
be
appropriate
to
try
to
harden
human
	targets
	—
	though
	the
	regulations specifying
	acceptable
	exposure
levels
may
	change
	with
time.
However,
	the
general
trend
of
such
regula-
tions
	is
towards
	lower acceptable
	levels
	and
	thus
	major
	clean-up
	pro-
grammes may
	need
	to
assess
	the
potential
for
regulatory softening
of the
targets
during
the
clean-up
process.
Landfill
As
	well
	as
the
contamination
	of land, issues
	related
	to landfill
	must be
addressed.
All
man’s
activities
generate waste and
this
waste
must
be disposed
in
a
manner
	such
that
it
does
not
degrade
the quality of
life
or
pose health
and
safety
problems.
	Volumes
	of
waste
	are
	large
and must
	be disposed
	in a
rapid and
efficient
manner.
	Until quite recently
landfills
were focused
	on
available holes
and
rather
little
attention was paid
to
contamination
issues.
The
	general
by-word
	was
	“dilute
	and disperse”.
	It
was
	assumed
	that pro-
vided there was
sufficient
ground
water available any contaminants would
be rapidly diluted
	to
insignificant
levels.
On many
	occasions
	this
philoso-
phy appears
to
have worked
	to an
acceptable extent
—
though quantitative
data
	are few.
	From
	dilute
	and disperse we have now moved
	to
contain-
ment, the aim being
to
ensure that there are no uncontrolled escapes
to the
environment of landfill gas or leachate (leachate
	is
the
complex chemical
solution that is
produced
	if water comes into contact with decomposing
waste and leaches soluble material from it).

126 Impact on Construction Activites
Landfill gas
The fundamental processes in a landfill involve the breaking up or lysis of
large molecules by bacteria to form simpler molecules, generally organic
acids. These materials are then in turn decomposed and the final reaction
products are predominantly methane and carbon dioxide. Thus in principle
any organic material may be broken down methane and carbon dioxide.
Both of these gases can be asphyxiant if they are present in sufficiently high
quantities that the available oxygen is reduced and methane is also poten-
tially explosive.
In practice the rate of breakdown of organic material in a landfill may be
relatively slow so that methane and carbon dioxide are still being evolved
many tens of years after placement of the original waste in the landfill. Also
some organic materials may be relatively refractory -and remain little
changed.
Landfill gas is one of the major concerns in landfill design and in the
remediation of old landfills. It can kill neighbouring vegetation and cause
explosions if it builds up within buildings etc. Procedures for the control of
landfill gas are now becoming more widely understood and it is to be hoped
that there will be few problems with gas escape from new landfills and that
the gas derived from landfills can be put to economic use for power genera-
tion etc. However, in power generation terms the quantity of gas available
on most landfills will be quite modest when compared with a major power
Station. As a result power generation may not be commercially viable
without some measure of subsidy.
Ironically
many
of the
major problems
of gas
migration
relate
to landfills
where
	the
technology had
developed
	from
	loose tipping
in
any
available
hole
to
controlled
tipping,
with
compaction
	and
daily
cover
layers of
soil
to
control
vermin, odours
etc.
The
effect
was
to
produce
landfills
in
which
the
free vertical
escape
of gas
to
the
atmosphere
was
hindered and
thus
lateral
migration was
	encouraged.
	‘“‘Technical
	advances” do
not
always
produce
the
desired
results.
Leachate generation
Leachate generation
is
also
a
major
issue in landfill
design and
licensing.
Aswaste degrades
it
produces
gases and
also
soluble
by-products which them-selves
	may
	be degraded during
	later stages of the reaction.
	If
water
	is
available these by-products
	will
dissolve
	as
also will
any soluble inorganic
materials such
	as
heavy metals. The resulting leachate may be
a
noxious
and polluting liquid that can cause much damage
to aquatic
life in a stream
or pollute water abstraction wells. In many countries there are now

-- 162 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up 127
regulatory
	controls on
	leachate
	escape
	and
landfills
must
	be
lined
to
pre-
vent
such
escape.
For
the
most
	part
the
water
	component
	of
leachate
	is
not
derived
	from
the
waste
	itself
but
comes
	from
	infiltration
	of
rainwater
	or
ground
water
into
the
landfill.
Thus
	landfills
must
	be
sited in
a
suitable
hydrogeological
setting
to
avoid
ground
water
inflow.
Landfill caps
They
must
	also
be
capped
and
sloped
so
as
to
shed water and
not
pond
it
(prevention
of
ponding
can
be
quite
a
problem
as
the
waste
may
	settle
by
the
order
	of
15%
	of
its
depth
in
the
years
following
placement
	and
there
may
be
substantial
local
differential
settlements
due
to
the
inherent
hetero-
geneity
of
wastes).
Capping
is
every
bit
as
complex
as
lining
a
site
save
that
the
cap
remains
	accessible
	for
maintenance
	or
renewal
	if
there
are
prob-
lems. In
contrast
the
liner
may
be
impossible
to
replace
without
digging
out
the entire
contents
	of the
landfill.
The
principal
elements
	of
a
landfill
cap
working
from
the
top
surface
downwards
	will
be:
—
	alayer
of
soil
to
support
vegetation. This
may
need
to
be
in
the
order
of
1
metre
thick.
—
	a
drainage
	layer
to
remove
	rain
water
which
	percolates
down
	through
the
vegetation
layer.
—
	animpermeable
	layer
to
prevent
water entering
the
body
of the
landfill.
This
layer
may
	be
formed
	from
	special
polythene
	sheet
or
compacted
clay.
Landfill caps
	must
	be
of very
	high
impermeability
	if
they
are to
shed
	a
significant
proportion
	of the
incident
	rainfall
and
if
they do
not
shed
this
rainfall
	very substantial quantities of
leachate
	will
be generated.
	An
	un-
capped
	or
	poorly
	capped
	landfill
	in
the
	UK
	might generate 3000
	cubic
metres
	of leachate per hectare per
annum.
Thus
	in effect
a landfill
becomes
	a
closed
tomb
	into
which water should
not enter
and from which
no water should
	escape. Gas,
of course,
	must be
allowed
to
escape but only
via
controlled routes such
as flares,
power
genera-
tors
	etc.
	Unfortunately
	if
there
	is
no water
	in a landfill
	the
degradation
processes
	are
enormously slowed and
the waste
may remain unchanged
	for
many
	years retaining
its
potential
to
pollute the
environment
	or
release gas
if, or,
more
	realistically,
when
	the cap and
liner
ultimately
	fail (it
must be
assumed
	that a landfill
containment system
will fail
unless there
is
some
	on-
going maintenance
	—
	and
	it can be difficult to ensure the continuance of
maintenance of a non-income producing structure for long periods of time).

-- 163 of 360 --

128 Impact on Construction Activites
To permit significant depths of water to build up within a landfill may be
an anathema to those charged with protecting the aquatic environment for
the greater the depth of leachate the greater the rate of escape at leaks or
through clays barriers etc. However, there will always be some materials
that must be disposed to landfill and it is important that as much degrad-
ation as possible occurs before such material ultimately is in a position to
escape to the environment.
Thus it may be preferable to ensure that there is always some water in a
landfill and in particular it can be advantageous to recirculate the leachate
from the base of the landfill to just under the cap to ensure that the waste is
continuously wetted with water.
The
	debate
	on
the
permitted
	water
	levels
within
	landfills
is
set
to
con-
tinue.
However,
	in
many
	countries
	the
maximum
	permitted depth
is
1
m
although
	landfills
	may
	be
over 100
metres deep.
Without
	leachate
	recir-
culation
	much
	of the
	material
	in
such
	landfills
	may
	degrade extremely
slowly
if
at
all.
Landfill liners
It
is
appropriate
	to
note
	that
there
	have been
	major
	technical
	develop-
ments
	in
landfill
lining
and capping
	in
recent
	years.
	It is
not
now
	normal
practice
to
rely
on
a
single
liner.
In
normal
	practice there
will
be
at
least
two and
often three barrier
elements
to a
liner.
Thus
there
might
be
a
layerof natural
clay.
	A
mat
of special
low permeability
clay
sandwiched between
two
	textile
	layers (to
hold
	the clay
during
	placing)
	and
	a
layer of
highdensity
polyethylene
	sheet
which
	is
laid in strips
and
welded
	together
	on
site.
The
use
of
composite
	liners
recognises
the
fact
that
all
liners
will
leak
but
that
properties
of different
liner
systems
	are
different
and
the overall
performance
of
a
composite system
can
be
much
better
than
the
sum
of the
parts.
Of
course such
composite
liner
systems
are
expensive and
thus the land-
filling
of
waste
	is
becoming
	progressively
	more
	expensive.
	We
	must be
careful
to
check
	that the
money
	spent on controlling leachate escape
	etc.
produces
commensurate environmental
	benefits.
The future
Much
	of the
technology associated with the
clean-up of
contaminated
	landis
relatively
	new and new developments
	are regularly appearing. Thus
there can be no doubt that
technology can be developed
	to
achieve any
realistic level of control that is
demanded. However, the costs are high. It is
therefore always necessary to ask whether the money
	is being well spent.

-- 164 of 360 --

Hazardous substances and clean-up 129
Environmental awareness seems to be well correlated with per capita
income. Individuals appear to become more conscious of their
environment as their quality of life improves. Those living at subsistence
level may have little interest in environmental quality and certainly no
means of influencing it. Clearly environmental quality is of great
importance. However, a moral balance must be struck between the
spending of large sums of money on clean-ups where risks are already
minimal. Pollution can spread between countries by air, seas and river
waters. Even purely selfishly we should consider diverting some of the
funds that are spent on clean-up (and the associated litigation) in the
developed world to improvements in developing countries. Also we must
be careful not to export pollution by setting up or encouraging production
in manufacturing plants etc. which do not meet basic environmental
standards.
As
a
final
note
we must
	ensure
	that
any
environmental
	initiative
brings
real
benefits.
For
example
at
the
present
time
there
is
a
substantial focus on
recycling.
	However,
	it
is
now coming
	to
be
accepted
	that
the
recycling
of
glass
and paper
may
	give
only
marginal
	benefits.
	Indeed
	any
benefits
will
be
negated
	if
a
special
car
journey
	is
made
	to
the
recycling
point.
At
the
present time
car
exhaust emissions
	are
probably
the single
most important
environmental
	issue in
the
developed
	world.
	Despite
	this
there
	is
much
greater public pressure
	to
deal
with other
more
	marginal
pollution
issues
such
as
contaminated
	land,
recycling
of
wastes
	etc.
than
to
reduce
car use.
Fundamentally
	this
is
entirely
selfish.
To
restrict car
use
would
	impact
on
lifestyle.
Recycling
	has
minimal impact
on
lifestyle
and
is
an
easy
sop
for
the
	conscience. Sadly
	it
may
	produce
	no
	environmental
	benefit
	at least
until
there
is a
stable
market
	for
recycled paper,
plastic,
glass
etc.

6 Contaminated land and groundwater problems
Michael Renger
Introduction
The environment, as with the construction process itself, may be divided into
sectors or phases, but there is inevitably an overlap and inter-relationship
between different paris of the environment, in the same way as with different
parts of a building. The purpose of this paper is to address those environ-
mental issues which are relevant at and, in particular, below ground level.
The redevelopment of contaminated land is a matter that is increasingly
being considered by contractors and developers as the availability of alter-
native sites for development become restricted as a result of the adoption
of policies for preserving virgin greenfield sites. However, in these circum-
stances, there is a dilemma presented to potential developers. On the one
hand, the redevelopment of contaminated sites is encouraged by initiatives
such as the Derelict Land Grant, the Urban Development Corporations
and the embryonic Urban Development Agency. On the other hand,
developers and landowners—and their financiers—are wary of possible
clean-up costs and liabilities associated with the development of sites that
have been subject to contaminative uses or land that may have been con-
taminated historically.
The
	concept
	of
contaminated
	land
is
not
new.
	In
1977
the
Government
issued
	guidance
	in
	the
	form
	of Circular 49/77
	which
	announced
	the
establishment
	of the
Interdepartmental
	Committee
	on
Redevelopment
	of
Contaminated Land
	(ICRCL)
	to
provide general
guidance
	on the
assess-
ment
	and
redevelopment
	of
contaminated
	land.
The
ICRCL
	subsequently
published
a
number
	of
papers dealing with
the
problems
of particular types
of
contaminated
	sites.1
Subsequently,
	the
Government
	issued
specific
guidance
on the
develop-
ment
	of
contaminated
	land
in the
form
of Circular No.
21/87.
The purpose
