132 Impact on Construction Activites
of this Circular is to provide advice and guidance to local authorities and
developers on the identification, assessment and development of contami-
nated land. The approach taken in this Circular is effectively reactive in
that it relates to proposals for the redevelopment of contaminated land and
advises that contaminated land is identified at an early stage in the planning
process, that appropriate policies are developed for its use and that plan-
ning applications are decided on the basis of adequate information. Con-
tamination therefore only became an issue when it had to be taken into
account during redevelopment. The treatment of the contaminated mater-
ial was an integral part of the development process, as with the treatment
or compaction of unstable ground. The Welsh Office in conjunction with
the Welsh Development Agency undertook a survey in the mid-1980s to
record information about sites in Wales thought to be contaminated.
However it was the House of Commons Environment Committee on Con-
taminated Land that processed the extension of this approach, to the iden-
tification of all sites that have been subject to contaminative uses within
Britain. The first report of the Environment Committee on Contaminated
Land, published in January 1990 made a number of recommendations
relating to contaminated land including registers of contaminated land, the
powers of enforcement bodies, and civil liability for damage.

In their evidence to the Environment Committee, the Department of the
Environment rightly drew the distinction between “derelict” and “con-
taminated”’ land.
“Derelict land is currently defined as ‘land so damaged by industrial or other
development that it is incapable of beneficial use without treatment’. Contami-
nated land is more difficult to define, but may be regarded as land which repres-
ents an actual or potential hazard to public health or the environment as a result
of current or previous use.”
Land
	may
	thus
	be
	derelict
	but
	not
	contaminated,
	such
	as
	a

	quarry;
	alternatively,
	land
	may
	have
	been
	previously
	derelict,
but
now
	restored
without

	of

underlying contamination.
Indeed

	an

nevertheless
	would

the

having been
previously used
for
a
gas

“Crucially, if nobody is ever going to build a housing estate on that oil

Some of the major environmental consultancies have attempted to pro-
duce their own estimate of the amount of contaminated land within the
United Kingdom. One consultancy, extrapolating from a pilot study in
Cheshire, estimated that there was something of the order of 50,000 to
100,000 potentially contaminated sites in the United Kingdom.
The Development Director of the Black Country Development Corpor-
ation identified the problem of contamination as one of the biggest single
issues in the process of regenerating the area. His own estimate was that
there were over 300 hectares of contaminated land within the Corpora-
tion’s area alone.
HMSO published in 1992 a statistical volume entitled ‘“The UK Environ-
ment” which Michael Howard QC, MP, in his capacity as Secretary of State
for the Environment, described as bringing together “both the major long
term statistical series and also less readily available data from other areas
and research programmes”’. The volume contains numerous graphs and pie
charts, but there is a notable lack of statistics in the brief section on “‘con-
taminated land”’. Significantly, contaminated land is simply treated as a
sub-category of derelict land and the depth of Government information on
the subject is summarised as follows:
‘Major industrial conurbations of the North, the Midlands, South Wales and
Scotland have large areas of land formerly used by industry and now derelict or
reclaimed for other uses. Much of this land may be contaminated. However,
many other parts of the country are or were centres for industries or trades which
may cause contamination of the soil, and the sites of former town gas works,
scrap yards and waste disposal facilities, for example, are found throughout the
country.

Legal liability—common law
No single body of law deals with the issue of liability for injury caused by
contaminated land. Instead, the law that can be applied is drawn from both
common law principles and a range of legislation.
The common law principles relevant to the protection of the environ-
ment derive principally from the law of tort. The law of tort can provide
protection for the private interests of individuals and can therefore provide
private individuals with redress for injury caused by contamination.
Rule in Rylands v Fletcher
The rule in Rylands v Fletcher contains a principle upon which action can
be taken in relation to contamination. The principle, first expounded by
Blackburn J is:
“.
	..
that
the
person
who
for
his
own
purposes
brings onto
his
land
and
collects
and
keeps
there
anything
likely
to
do
mischief
if
it
escapes,
must
keep
it
in
at
his
peril,
and,
if
he does
not
do
so, is
prima
facie
answerable
for
all
the
damage
which
is
the
natural
consequence
of
its
escape’’.©
This principle has
	been
	applied
	to
the
	escape
	of water,
	gas,
	chemicals,
colliery spoil
and
numerous
	similar matters.
However,
it is
not of
such
wideapplication
as
may
first
appear
as
the rule
is
hedged
in
by
limitations.
(1)
	Non-natural
	user:
The most important
	limitation on the principle
	is
that
there
must have been
	a
non-natural
	use of land.
Therefore,
	for
the rule in
Rylands
	v
Fletcher
to
apply with regard
to
contaminated
land, the use that
	produced
	any contamination must be
	a
“non-
natural”
	use.
	To
	assess
	whether
	the use
	is a
non-natural
	use,
	there
must be consideration of
“all the
circumstances
	of time and place and
produce
of
mankind” Read
v
Lyons? (1947)
House
of Lords.
It is
now
6 Rylands
v Fletcher (1868)
LR 3HL
330.
7 Read
v
Lyons [1947] AC
156, [1946] 2 All ER
471.

-- 172 of 360 --

Contaminated land and groundwater ex
common for an ordinary industrial use to be considered as a natural
use if it is sited with due care and consideration.
Therefore, contaminated uses that have taken place in heavily
industrialised areas may well be considered to be the result of a
‘natural’ user of the land and, as such, the rule in Rylands v Fletcher
will not be applicable.
(2) Escape: There is a requirement that the substance must escape from
the land where it is kept before the rule in Rylands v Fletcher will
apply. In relation to contamination, escape can be considered widely
so that seepage to groundwater through, say, a defective tank, can be
regarded as an escape from the land.
The
rule
in
Rylands
v
Fletcher
is
further
restricted
by
the availability
of
a
number
	of
defences
	to
an
action
brought under
the
rule.
It
is
a
defence
	if
statute
allows
for
the
activities
to
be
undertaken
	e.g
a
discharge consent
or
waste
	disposal
licence,
both
of
which
	can
be
controlled
	by
conditions
	and
therefore
	allow
a
certain
level
of
contamination
	to
take
place. In
addition,
there
is
a
defence
	of
common
	benefit
where
	a
party derives
some
	benefit
from
the
harmful
activities.
These
	defences, together
	with
	the
	above
	limitations
	have
	diluted the
liability
imposed by
the
rule in
Rylands
v
Fletcher.
The
rule
was
considered
by
the
Court
of
Appeal
in
the
recent
case
of
Cambridge
	Water
Company
	v
Eastern Counties Leather
plc (1992)8 with
which
I
will
deal
later.
The
	common
	law
	also
	provides
	other
	remedies
	for
instances
	of con-
tamination.
	The
relevant
branches
	of
law
are
trespass
and
negligence.
Trespass
Trespass
	involves
	direct
interference with
another
	person’s
rights
without
lawful excuse. In relation
to
contaminated
	land, trespass
may
be
a
possible
cause of action
	where
	there has
	been
	an unjustifiable intrusion
	by
one
person
	upon
	the
land
in
the
possession
	of another.
	However,
	in
terms
	of
environmental
	law, trespass has
been construed narrowly. There must
be
a
causal
	link
between
	the directness of the
	act
and
	the inevitability of
its
consequences.
In
Esso
v
Southport Corporation (1956)
an
oil
tanker was
grounded and
discharged much
of
its
cargo of
oil to
lighten the ship. This was then carried
on
	to
Southport’s beach by
the tides
and the wind. The House
	of Lords
8 Cambridge Water Company
v
Eastern Counties Leather plc—Court of
Appeal—The
Times,
29 December 1992. See also postscript.

-- 173 of 360 --

138 Impact on Construction Activites
considered this could not be trespass as there was no inevitability about the
oil being deposited onto the shore as it depended upon the action of the
tide and wind. Applying this principle to other environmental media such
as the air and soil, means that an action in trespass is unlikely to be a source
of redress for environmental pollution. However, it may be possible to
argue that for land contamination or contamination of groundwater
geological and hydrological conditions make contamination an inevitable
consequence of a particular act.
Negligence
The third area of common law that may provide redress for land con-
tamination is that of the law of negligence. Liability in negligence arises
when damage which is not too remote is caused by the breach of a duty of
care owed to the injured party. The scope for liability in negligence is
therefore very wide. However, the law of negligence is based on fault and
in relation to contaminated land, fault may be very difficult to establish.
This was considered in depth by the European Council when finalising the
Green Paper on remedying environmental damage, to which I shall return
later. However, negligence may provide an appropriate method of redress
for one-off incidents where fault can be apportioned.
The
	difficulty in
proving
	that
contamination
	is
caused
	by
the
fault
of
a
particular
person
who owed
a
duty
of
care,
means
	that
negligence
is
rarely
used
to
provide
a
cause
	of
action. In
addition, the availability of
statutory
remedies
	in
relation
	to
contaminated
	land
	means
	that
this
common
	law
means
	of
redress
may
not
be pursued.
Nuisance
Liability in
nuisance
	arises
	where
	there
	is
interference with the lawfulrights of
another.
The
common
	law principles of
nuisance, negligence and
the rule in
Rylands
v
Fletcher
have
recently
been considered
	in
the case of
Cambridge
	Water
Company
	v
Eastern Counties Leather plc (1992).8
The
	water
	company
	supplied water
	to an area in
Cambridgeshire
	and
abstracted from
	a
borehole
	located
	in a
chalk aquifer.
The company
	ac-
quired premises, including the
borehole,
	in
1976,
to
provide water
for the
public supply and
commissioned
	a
pumping
station in 1979.
A
water quality
directive issued
by
the
Council
of the
European Communities
	in
J uly 1980
specified the
maximum
	permissible concentrations of
compounds
	including
PCE,
	a
chlorinated solvent.
	The water company
	identified
	PCE
	in the
water abstracted from the borehole above the permissible concentration
and ceased pumping in 1983. It developed a new borehole at a cost of

-- 174 of 360 --

Contaminated land and groundwater 139
approximately £1 million. Having carried out a hydro-geological inquiry,
the water company claimed the PCE had originated from the leather tann-
ery of Eastern Counties Leather at Sawston and commenced proceedings,
contending that the leather company was liable in nuisance and under the
rule in Rylands v Fletcher.
Tanneries had been located at Sawston for over 350 years and Eastern
Counties Leather was incorporated in 1879. It was found that spillages of
PCE were likely to have occurred when drums of PCE were poured into a
reservoir for pumping to de-greasing machines. The use of PCE ceased in
1976.
The High
Court
held
that
the actions
in
negligence and
in
nuisance
failed
because
	the
leather
company
	could
not
have reasonably
	foreseen
	in
1976,
or
before,
	that
the spillages
would
	result
in
detectable
	quantities
of
PCE
being
found
in
the aquifer.
The High
Court
also
rejected
liability
under
the
rule in
Rylands
v
Fletcher
holding
that
Sawston
was
an
“industrial
village”’
and
	that
	the
	storage
	of
PCE
	at
the
	leather
	company’s
	premises
	was
	a
natural

of the
land.
The
Court
of
Appeal
considered
	that
the
rule
in
Rylands
v
Fletcher
was
inapposite
in
this
case.
	It
was
held
that
the
rule
makes
	a
person
	liable
for
the
event
	of
an
escape
	rather
than
for
his
actions
and
in
this
case
liability
attached
by
reason
of the actions of the leather
company
	in spilling
PCE.
	It
did not
escape
into
the
aquifer
through,
for
example,
cracks
in
a
negligently
fabricated storage
tank.
There
	was
	no
	appeal
against
the
High
Court
	finding
that
there

	no
liability
in
nuisance
	or
negligence.
However,
	the
Court

Appeal
did con-
sider
further
the
tort
of
nuisance and
its
application
in
Ballard
v
Tomlin-
son?
	to
the right of an
	owner
	to
naturally
	occurring water
	which comes
beneath
	his
land
by
percolation
through undefined
	underground
	channels.
The
	court held
that
interference with
this
“natural”
	right
which
	resulted
from
	the spillage of
PCE
	was
	a
nuisance.
	It
was
	sufficient
	to
say
that the
action of the leather
	company
	in spilling
PCE
	caused
	the
contamination
and
that
where
	the
nuisance
	is
interference with
a
natural
right
incident
to
ownership,
	then
	liability
	is strict.
	The
	water
	company
	was
	awarded
damages
	plus

but the leather
	company
	have been granted
	leave
	to
appeal
in the
House
	of Lords.
This decision
	is
significant in relation
	to the
contamination
	of
ground-

	Contamination
	can percolate through
	soils
over
many
	years.
Sub-
stances that
may
not be regarded
as
contaminative may now be considered
ne

as such in future. The local geological and hydrological conditions are
relevant to whether percolation can occur and affect water sources.
Legal liability—statutory remedies
There are a number of statutory provisions that can be applied to liability
caused by contamination land. These can be divided into two: certain pro-
visions impose a clean-up liability, whereas others impose a criminal
liability.
Clean-up liability
As can be expected, the statutory provisions relating to clean-up liability
centre around the deposit of waste and the pollution of water. Both are
issues relating to contamination:
(1) Deposit of waste: Waste disposal was one of the uses identified as a
contaminative use by the Government in its proposals for a register. In fact,
the existence of the register would have provided the information for ac-
tion with regard to the clean-up of waste.
Section 61 of the Environmental Protection Act 1990 imposes a duty on
Waste Regulation Authorities to inspect land in their areas to see whether
or not that land is in such a condition that it may cause pollution of the
environment or harm to human health as a result of noxious gases or
liquids caused by the deposit of controlled waste. In addition, where a
Waste Regulation Authority considers that land is polluted, it is under a
duty to inspect the land and to keep its condition under review.
The
	critical
provision
	in
section 61
is
that
where
	it
appears
	to
a
WasteRegulation Authority
that
the
condition
of the
land
is
such
that
pollution
of
the
environment
	or
harm
to
human
	health
is
likely to
be caused,
it is
under
a
duty
to
carry out
	remedial works and
	to
take such other steps
	as
are
reasonable
to
avoid pollution
or
harm.
The
statute entitles
the
authority
as
of right
to
carry out
such works.
	Where
	such clean-up
work
	is
carried out
the
authority
is
entitled
to
recover
the cost
from
“the
owner
of the land
for
the time being”, provided
	that
such
costs
were reasonably
	incurred.
	It is
important
	to
note
	that the
authority
	will
look
	to
the
owner
	for the time
being.
It is
not
concerned
	with
who
	was
	the
owner
	at
the time
when
	the
pollution occurred; and
is,
regardless of responsibility for or
knowledge
	of,
the existence of the
contamination.
Section 61 contains
	a
provision that in deciding whether
	to
recover the
cost of clean-up and
if so
how much, authorities should have regard to any
hardship which the recovery may cause. This may provide relief where

-- 176 of 360 --

Contaminated land and groundwater 141
contamination is historical and the present owner does not have the re-
sources to pay for the clean-up. In these circumstances, the authority may
realise that if it carries out the clean up, it will be unlikely to recover its
costs from the present owner.
This section of the Act is not yet in force, and the powers of the Waste
Regulation Authority to control and tackle land pollution are part of the
interdepartmental review of contaminated land currently being carried out
by the Government.
Section 42 of the Environmental Protection Act provides that the Waste
Regulation Authority is under a duty to ensure “‘that the activities author-
ised by the [waste management] licence do not cause pollution of the
environment or harm to human health or become seriously detrimental to
the amenities of the locality affected by the activities”. For the purpose of
performing the duty, the authority has power to carry out an emergency
clean-up and to recover the cost from the holder or former holder of a
waste management licence. Like section 61, this section is not yet in force.
(2)
	Statutory nuisances: Section 79
of the
Environmental
	Protection Act
imposes
	a
duty
on
	local
	authorities
	to
inspect
	the
land
	in their
	area
	for
“statutory
	nuisances” and
	to
investigate
	any
complaints about
	such
nuis-
ances
	made
	by
the public.
	Statutory nuisances
	include
	a
broad
	range
	of
matters such
as
smoke,
noise,
smells
and
dust
and
also ‘‘any
accumulation
or
deposit
which
	is
prejudicial
to
health
or
a
nuisance”’.
	Section 80
of the
EPA
	empowers
a
local
authority
	to
serve
	an
abatement
	notice
	which
	re-
quires
the
nuisance
	to
be
removed
	within
	a
stated
time
	period
on
	either
“the
	person responsible
	for
the
nuisance”
	or
“where
	the
nuisance
	arises
from
	any
defect of
a
structural character, on
the
owner
	of the
premises”.
Section
81 of the
EPA
	empowers
	a
local
authority
to
take steps
to
abate
the
nuisance and do
whatever
may
be necessary
in
execution
of the notice.
This
may
involve extensive clean-up works.
(3) Pollution
	of
water:
	Contamination
	of land can
	result in
pollution
	of
controlled waters
which
include
rivers,
streams,
lakes,
ponds,
groundwater
and
coastal waters. In the
context
	of
this
paper,
	it
should be
emphasised
that “‘controlled
	waters” include any waters contained
	in
underground
strata.
	Section 161 of the
Water Resources Act
(‘‘the
WRA’’) empowers
the
National Rivers Authority (‘the
NRA”)
	to
carry out anti-pollution
works and operations
in relation
to
controlled waters.
The
NRA
	can take
preventative action to forestall pollution of controlled waters
	or
	take
remedial steps where pollution has already occurred. Remedial work may
be undertaken for the purposes of preventing the matter from entering
controlled waters, removing the matter, remedying or mitigating any

-- 177 of 360 --

142 Impact on Construction Activites
pollution caused by its presence in the waters, or the restoration of waters
including flora and fauna to the state they were in before the pollution
occurred. Where the NRA carry out work they are entitled to recover
the expenses of so doing from any person who “caused or knowingly per-
mitted” the matter to be present in controlled waters. These criteria are
similar to that for criminal liability and are dealt with below.
Criminal liability
Criminal
	liability
in
relation
to
contaminated
	land
is
an
area
causing
con-
cern not
only
to
industry, but
also
to

	markets,
	who
	finance
	the

	and

Again,

the

the

the

(1)
	Deposit
	of
waste:
	Section
	33 of
the
EPA
	deals
	with
	the
deposit
	of
controlled waste
on

It
provides
that
the

causes

	with

	licence.
It

all

exercised
	all

to

	of the

or

under
	instructions
	from
his

	and
neither

	nor
had reason
to
suppose
that the
acts

constituted
contravention;
or
that
the
acts
were
	done
in an
emergency
	in
order
to
avoid
danger
to
the

and
	that,
as

	as
reasonably
	practicable
	thereafter, particulars
were furnished
	to
the
Waste Regulation
	Authority.
	Penalties
	that
can
	be
imposed by
a
magistrates’ court
	are
	a
fine
not
	exceeding £20,000 and/or
imprisonment
	for

	not
	exceeding
	six
months. For
	a

	which

	to
the
Crown
	Court,
	there
	is
an
	unlimited
	fine
and/or
	a
maximum
period
of
imprisonment
	of
two
years.
Section 34 of the
EPA
	imposes
an
obligation on
all
parties
in
the
waste
chain
	i.e.
	“any person
	who
	imports, produces,
	carries,
	keeps,
	treats or
disposes
	of
controlled waste
	or, as a
broker, has control of
such
	waste’’.
Such persons have
a
duty
of care
as

this
includes taking
all
measures
	“‘to
prevent
the

of the waste from
his
control
or that of
any other person”’. In the context of
contaminated
	land, the
duty
of care
applies
	to
	waste
	that
	escapes and therefore
	results in
contamination.
Failure
	to
comply
	with the duty
is a
criminal offence and
	an offender
	is
liable to
a
fine.
With regard to statutory nuisances, under section 81 of the
EPA,
	it is an
offence to contravene an abatement notice served by
a local authority

-- 178 of 360 --

Contaminated land and groundwater 143
without reasonable excuse, for which a fine not exceeding £20,000 may be
imposed, where the offence is committed on industrial premises. There are
a number of possible defences. It is, for example, a defence to prove that
the best practical means were used to prevent or counteract the effects of
the nuisance, where the nuisance is caused on industrial, trade or business
premises. Best practical means is construed bearing in mind questions of
cost. It has been accepted by the courts that the profitability of an oper-
ation, taking into account the cost of any further steps to be taken, is a
relevant factor, but the mere fact of increased expenditure or even un-
profitability is not sufficient on its own.
(2) Statutory nuisance: If a person on whom an abatement notice is
served, without reasonable excuse, contravenes or fails to comply with any
requirement or prohibition imposed by the notice, he is guilty of a criminal
offence (section 80, Environmental Protection Act).
(3) Water pollution: Section 85 of the WRA provides that it is a criminal
offence to pollute controlled waters. Section 85(1) states:
‘A person contravenes this section if he causes or knowingly permits any poi-
sonous, noxious or polluting matter or any solid waste matter to enter controlled
waters.”
Again,

	reference
	to
“causes
	or

	permits”.
In

	in

	a

	act
	rather
	than
	mere
	passive
inaction.
	The

	permits”
	is

	The

	will

	a

that the

	bears

	the requisite

However,

It is a

	There

	as

The penalties upon conviction by the Magistrates’ Court are a fine up to
£20,000 and/or a period of imprisonment of up to three months. Upon
conviction by the Crown Court the penalties are an unlimited fine and/or a
period of imprisonment of up to two years.
Under both the EPA and the WRA criminal liability of a body corporate
can be extended to directors, managers, secretaries or other officers of the
body corporate if the offence is proved to have been committed with ‘“‘the
consent, or connivance, or to have been attributed to any neglect on the
part of” any such person.
Sources of practical guidance
Practical guidance on the investigation and treatment of contaminated land
is available from a wide variety of sources.
A Code of Practice for the identification of potentially contaminative
land and its investigation was published by the British Standards Institute
in its Draft for Development series of publications. This publication should
not be regarded as a British Standard and the Code of Practice should be
applied on a provisional basis only. Nevertheless, the Code of Practice
(DD175:1988) provides guidance on the identification and investigation of
sites where contamination may exist and where hazards from undesirable
substances may occur.
The
	Draft
	for
	Development
	adopts
	the definition of
	‘potentially
contaminated
	land” being
“‘land that,
because
of
its
nature
or
former
uses,
may
	contain substances
	that
could give
rise
to
hazards
	likely
to affect
the
proposed form
of
development”’.
Protection of workforce
Contamination may
	be
a
potential health
	risk to
those
working
	on
a

and
to

	of the

are

in

during

The Health and Safety Executive have

	of contaminated
	land.
These include
the need for a

equipment.

Petroleum based land contamination
Earlier this year (1993) the Institute of Petroleum published a Code of
Practice for the investigation and mitigation of possible petroleum based
land contamination. The Code of Practice, which has been written by
experts from the oil industry and reviewed by both the Department of
Environment and the National Rivers Authority, aims to provide site
managers with an appreciation of the process of site assessment and
remediation work.
The importance of such a code was highlighted in May this year by David
Pirret, General Manager of Shell UK’s retail division, who expressed the
view that at least one third of British petrol stations would be producing
some degree of ground contamination.
It is now Shell’s corporate policy to undertake a pre-disposal survey of
all sites and to remediate those found to be contaminated. It is reported
that the cost for just one small site has been in the region of £4 million,
exceeding the site’s market value.
It is
thus not
surprising
that
new
	petrol
stations
are
now
	being
designed
with
stricter
safeguards
so
as
to
minimise
the
potential
for
contamination
in
the
future.
Again
	Mr
Pirret
indicated
	that
‘to build
an
average
	forecourt
costs
£92,000
per
site
more
	today
than
would
have been
necessary
	10 years
ago
in
order
to
comply
with
new environmental
	and
safety
standards and
regulations’.
The
Construction
	Industry
Research
	and Information Association
	has
a
number
	of projects
under
way
in
relation
to
contaminated
	land,
including:
— COSHH in construction;
—
	safe

practices on

	sites;
—
	remedial treatment
	of

	land;
and
—
	urban hydrology.
The

	(BRE)

(1)

	is

	of preventing
	the

(2)
	Contaminated land—site surveys and assessment of the effectiveness
of barriers;
(3) Structures on contaminated land—external protection measures; and

-- 181 of 360 --

146 Impact on Construction Activites
(4) Fire and explosion hazards associated with the development of con-
taminated land.
The National Rivers Authority (NRA) set out their policy to protect the
quality of groundwater in their publication Policy and Practice for the
Protection of Groundwater.
The NRA is currently preparing detailed maps, to be published over the
next three years, identifying abstraction points throughout England and
Wales. The authority is establishing source protection zones (SPZs) around
abstraction points. There are to be three zones, with zone one being closest
to the abstraction points. The zones will relate to the time groundwater
takes to travel to the point of abstraction. The NRA is a statutory consultee
on a wide range of development control matters, and the establishment of
the SPZs may affect the development potential of many areas, including
the redevelopment of contaminated land within an SPZ or on a major
aquifer.
Government strategy
In the

	of

	in

	the

	affirmed

‘‘what

	as

	The

	therefore used
	‘“con-

	to

	the

	in

	of certain sub-

without

The

(The

	entitled

The Government’s most recent strategy on dealing with the legacy of
contaminated
	land was outlined in a
speech by David MacLean,
	Minister
of State for the
Environment and Countryside, on 14
May
1993.
The speech
contained an invitation to let the
Government know “what you think about
liability for pollution of our land... at a simple level, the problem with

-- 182 of 360 --

Contaminated land and groundwater 147
contaminated land is ‘who should pay for it when the polluter has gone
away?’”’.
The issue of contaminated land is important and needs to be tackled. In
the Minister’s words “‘it figures in urgent projects for the regeneration of
our urban and industrial areas; it figures in negotiations between business-
men and their bankers where land is used as security for working capital;
and it figures when the directors of companies, large and small, have to sign
their annual accounts showing the value of their operational land and of
their contingent liabilities, including clean-up costs’.
The key to contamination is to make it a matter of risk analysis and risk
management. The Government wish “‘to see contaminated land kept or
brought back into beneficial use wherever practicable, as long as there are
no unacceptable risks to human health or the environment, taking into
account the actual or intended use or uses of the land. This is the ‘fitness for
purpose’ standard for reclamation which (the Government) have applied
for many years”’.
The Minister stated two policy objectives namely:
(1) actual contamination problems to be tackled in a sensible way; and
(2) the avoidable pressures for new development in the countryside to be
minimised.
He considered that the matter required:
(1) vigilance to identify the risks;
(2) aconsidered appraisal of the actual hazards; and
(3)
	if
it
is
appropriate under
	the
fitness for
purpose
	standard,
	the use
of
well
thought
	out
	and
	cost
	effective
	measures
	to
contain, control
	or
eliminate those
risks.
The
Minister went
on
to
commit
	the
Government
	to
delivering
‘‘a
policy
of
rising
	environmental
	standards;
	in
the
	context of
a
better
	informed
public; but only
where the
benefits
exceed
the costs”.
The
starting

for
the
Government
	review
	is
‘the legislation
	and
	controls relevant
	to
con-
tamination’.
	However,
	the
review
	is
not restricted
	to
the legislation
	and
the
Government
	are
looking
to
achieve
their
environmental
	goals
by
other
means. These
include:
(1) relieving industry and commerce of the burden of regulation; and
(2) the use of economic instruments to influence behaviour.

148 Impact on Construction Activites
The Government wish “to get away from imposing controls to prohibit
action, with the attendant bureaucracy and inflexibility that always
involves. We want to get into place the right mixture of pro signals, incen-
tives and penalties, to reward environmental improvement and penalise
environmental impairment . . . in effect, we want deregulation plus econ-
omic instruments to provide a market method for applying the ‘polluter
pays’ principle’.
The Minister went on to state that the Government had “‘no intention of
going down the Super Fund road. We do intend to take into account its
lessons—about unrealistic clean-up standards for land contamination and
about excessive legal bills incurred as people fight over whose pocket is
deep enough to pay for the work”. The Government have thus reaffirmed
their traditional policy of ‘‘fitness for purpose” as contained in the ICRCL
guidance. :

In conclusion, the Minister stated that ‘‘buyers, lenders and insurers
need to assess the risks of contamination before committing their money in
relation to any land whose current or past uses may have left contamina-
tion behind. ‘Caveat emptor’ still applies; where contamination is con-
cerned, it is ‘let the informed buyer beware’ ”’.
Future European developments
The Government’s role is ‘‘to provide guidance on methods and standards
for addressing the problems. A particular priority is to provide authorities,
developers and consultants with guidance on a common basis for risk
assessment and the evaluation of solutions”. There have been two recent
European initiatives, namely the Council of Europe’s Convention on Civil
Liability for Damage Resulting from Activities Dangerous to the Environ-
ment and the Commission of the European Community’s Green Paper on
Environmental Liability.
The cost of clean-up liability associated with contaminated land and the
experience of the USA Super Fund in this context has led the UK and
Europe to consider the establishment of civil liability for environmental
damage. The first report of the Environmental Committee on Contami-
nated Land recommended that attention be given to the question of creat-
ing statutory liability for damage caused by contamination to land,
particularly where this causes damage to neighbouring property or the
environment.
On
14
May
1993, the
European Commission
	published
a
Green
Paper
on
remedying environmental
	damage
	(“‘the
Green
Paper’’).
The Green
	Paper
envisages
the
introduction
	of
a
harmonised
	strict
liability
regime,
coupled
with
a
compensation mechanism
	for cases of
environmental
	damage when
no
	liable
party was
	identified
	or
was
	able
to
pay.
The
	Commission
	con-
sidered
	formulating
	fault
based
	liability
for
environmental damage.
	Such
liability
requires
proof
that the
liable
party
committed
	a
negligent
or
other-
wise
wrongful
act
which caused
damage,
fault
being
dependent upon
a
duty
of care
	or
	rule of law. Strict liability
	eases the
	burden
	of establishing
liability
because
	fault
need
not be established.
	However,
	potentially
liable
parties
need
to
know
	the
scope of the costs
they
would be expected
to
pay
in cases of
damage. The European Commission
	identified
	a
trend
in
both
national legislation
and international instruments regarding environmental
damage towards
a
strict liability
regime
for certain activities
dangerous
	to
the environment.
The Green Paper also considers the difficulties arising from the question
of determining how to apportion liability where more than one party is

-- 185 of 360 --

150 Impact on Construction Activites
responsible for the damage, determining who and what is damaging the
environment and in defining environmental damage.
Insurance is considered to be a means of controlling the risk of economic
loss, but the uncertainties which make civil liability for environmental
damage difficult also create problems for environmental insurance. The
type, extent and probability of environmental damage are unpredictable
and the level of cover to be provided cannot be determined easily. The
Green Paper points out that some insurers have soughtto limit coverage of
accidental losses to damage occurring by a “sudden” event, excluding
damage caused gradually such as a slow leak from an underground tank.
The possibility of particularly hazardous industries or activities posing
particular hazards providing insurance to cover their potential liability is
considered and the proposed directive for civil liability for damage
resulting from waste would require the liability of the producer and the
eliminator to be covered by insurance or other financial security.
The purpose of the Green Paper is to stimulate community wide discus-
sion among parties with an interest in the issues raised. Various bodies in
the UK have responded. The Commission’s consultation period ends on
1 October 1993, to be followed by a joint hearing with the European
Parliament.

their
response
to

Paper
the
Environment
	Policy
Group
	of the

	recognised
	that
	a

	and worry

will
	be

	is
now hamperingnormal commercial
	transactions.
	Meanwhile,
	public
	concern about
	the
consequences
	of historic
pollution
is
increasing.
	Uncertainty
	leads
to
re-
luctance
	to
	develop
	on
	contaminated
	land,
	which
	in

	puts
	more
pressure
	on greenfield
	sites.
The
	potential

the past

	and

The CBI
	calls
for
certainty
	and
	predictability
	about
	the extent of
its
major
liabilities so
as
to foster the
innovation and commercial
	risk-taking
So.
	essential
	to
	business investment, growth and
	employment.
	Business
needs
	Government
	and
the

	to

	framework
	of law and policy which

	It
comments
	that
civil

	to allow individuals to obtain compensation
for damage to property and persons. There is nothing to suggest difficulties

-- 186 of 360 --

with the existing liability laws in EC Member States on the scale which
would justify attempting to harmonise them, with all the difficulties in
trying to ensure parity in practice, between fundamentally different judicial

In addition the CBI point out that the past and present should be treated
separately. This generation can help to put right the damage caused by
previous generations, but it cannot afford to do more than make a start.
The CBI urges the Government to rule out any possibility of retroactive
environmental liability for damage caused by lawful activities which the
perpetrator could not, at the time, have known were harmful and which
complied with the accepted standards of the day. The CBI further recom-
mend the introduction of a limit to the period of liability of 3 years from the
time when the damage was discovered and the creation of a 30 years’ long
stop.
The CBI are opposed either to an extension of strict liability-or to any
attempt by the EC to introduce joint and several liability into the regula-
tory or public law.
Advisory Committee on Business in the Environment (ACBE)

cluded that there was no clear evidence to support the case that the United
Kingdom’s existing large default based legal framework was inadequate
and should be extended to include a greater element of strict liability.

Council of Europe’s International Convention on Civil Liability for
Damage Resulting from Activities Dangerous to the Environment
The Convention applies the mechanism of strict liability to a wide range of
dangerous activities. This is because the EC considers that in environmen-
tal matters the risks attendant on some occupational activities are such that
the traditional system of fault is clearly inadequate.
Broadly, the activities regarded as dangerous to the environment are
activities which produce or use dangerous substances, such as toxic sub-
stances, activities which make use of genetically modified organisms and
waste treatment activities and waste dumps. Under the Convention, lia-
bility rests with the person who controls the dangerous activity and where
several installations are implicated, all the operators concerned are jointly
and severally liable.
Under the Convention, operators are liable for damage arising from an
accident. They are also answerable for creeping pollution and it is noted
that the long term consequences of creeping pollution sometimes prove
very serious.
The Convention
	covers
personal
injury,
damage
	to
property,
damage
	by
impairment
of the
environment,
financial
loss
resulting
from
impairment
ofthe
environment
	and,
in
particular, the
Convention
	requires
operators
	to
take
	any reasonable measures designed
	to
	restore
	and
	reinstate the
damaged
	environment. The Convention
	requires
	that
	where some
	par-
ticularly
	dangerous
	activities are
	concerned,
	States
	will
have
	to
require
operators
	to
take out
	compulsory
	insurance
	as
soon
	as
the
environment
insurance
market
	is
sufficiently
developed.
To
obtain
compensation,
	the victim
must prove
	that
he
or
she has
suf-
fered
	damage
	and
	that the
damage
	was caused
	by
one
	of the activities
covered by
the
Convention. The Convention
	provides
	that
anyone mayhave
access
to
information
	on the
environment
	held
by
the authorities and
persons
	who have suffered
	damage may
	request,
	through
	the courts, that
the operators concerned provide them with the specific
information they
hold, for inclusion in their
files
with a
view
to possible legal proceedings.
Voluntary organisations are given a
key
role.
	They may request the
courts to prohibit a dangerous activity which is unlawful, order an operator

-- 188 of 360 --

Contaminated land and groundwater 153
to take measures to prevent damage or order an operator to take reinstate-
ment measures.
To date, seven states have signed the Council of Europe’s Convention.
Neither the European Community nor the United Kingdom has signed. It
is unlikely that the European Community will now sign as they have pub-
lished the Green Paper on remedying environmental damage.
Conclusion
In conclusion the way ahead is still not clear. The need for information on
the extent and nature of contamination is urgent at all levels—government,
regulator, owner, financier. The current lack of clarity creates uncertainty,
to the detriment of society as a whole.
Both the European Green Paper and the Government review offers all
interested parties an opportunity to contribute towards the development of
future strategy and policy. Full advantage must be taken of this
opportunity.

Annex C: Schedule of contaminative uses
Notes:
(a) ‘‘Contaminative use” is defined in section 143 of the Environmental
Protection Act 1990 as “‘any use of land which may cause it to be
contaminated with noxious substances’’.
(b) “Land” includes: any ground, soil or earth; houses or other buildings;
the air space above it; but excludes all mines or minerals beneath it. It
also includes land used for integral, partial and ancillary processes
which form part of the primary use.
(c) A contaminative use is not to be taken to fall into the description if it
is carried out as a domestic activity in connection with a private
dwelling.

7 Hazardous waste liability issues for construction
contractors and design professionals in the US
Jack S Kannry, PE
Introduction
Construction contractors and design professionals (principally engineers
and architects) throughout the United States have a common growing
concern as to an emerging problem that ultimately could be very costly, if
not handled properly during the planning, design and construction phases
of a typical building or heavy construction project. That is the encountering
of unanticipated hazardous wastes at the construction site.
Aside from the fact that an awareness of this problem is relatively new to
the construction site, it is particularly alarming to the typical foundation,
highway or site work construction contractor due to a lack of critical
knowledge and understanding. Familiarization is necessary as to: respon-
sibility for dealing with this problem under a maze of federal and state
legislation; methodology and specialty equipment and training needed in
handling such materials; and the nature and scope of potential and perhaps
uninsurable liability exposure upon becoming involved in any related
physical activities. Similar concerns exist for the design professional in the
pre-construction stages as well.
This
	paper
	will
	review
	the legal
aspects of potential
	liability
	for
the
construction contractor
and
design
professional,
as
well
as
offer
some
prac-
tical
suggestions
for
protection
against
a
calamity,
upon encountering
	haz-
ardous wastes
at
the
jobsite.
Nature and scope of the
hazardous
	waste
	problem
	at
the
construction
	site
Since the 1970s, there has
developed
a
keen
national
environmental
	aware-
ness in the
	United
	States,
	which
	led
	to a
number
	of federal and
	state
statutes of
growing
	significance.
	These have and
will
continue
	to
impact
upon construction projects,
	as
distinguished from those contracts
	specifi-
cally
designated
	for
removal and disposal of hazardous substances, which
are not really the focus of this
paper. Although such construction projects
will look very much
	like those of the past, the hazardous waste factor adds
a new dimension. It might arise, for example, on a highway being built
161

-- 197 of 360 --

162 Impact on Construction Activites
through an old dump, a pipeline installed through an existing refinery, a
housing project constructed in an abandoned cement quarry, or site work
at an existing industrial location.
During excavation and other subsurface operations, workmen may con-
front buried leaking oil drums, strange coloured soil with unpleasant
odours, gaseous vapours or other suspicious looking liquids, any or all of
which may indeed constitute potential hazardous wastes and/or toxic sub-
stances. The immediate question to be answered in those situations is what
to do in the field. Should all operations be stopped in the area, should
attempts be made to move or isolate the potentially hazardous substances,
should they be transported off the site?
Clearly, what is to be done by the construction contractor largely
depends upon the risks of imminent danger to personnel, as well as the
contractual provisions that may possibly cover this-type of problem.
However, even if the contract contemplates additional compensation when
dealing with hazardous waste, that may be insufficient. A typical con-
struction contractor is likely to be ill-equipped to remove such waste, in
terms of personnel, availability of support facilities, actual know-how and
the legal requirements involved in that type of operation.

Hazardous waste liability 163
A subsequent federal statute (the Comprehensive Environmental
Response, Compensation and Liability Act?) broadened the scope of
hazardous wastes by adding materials prohibited under pre-existing stat-
utes, such as the Federal Water Pollution Control Act and the Clean Air
Act, as well as substances specified by the United States Environmental
Protection Agency (““EPA”’), and exceeding a thousand in number.‘ All of
these were then grouped under the broad heading of hazardous
“substances”. Some classic examples of these are the so-called PCBs
(polychlorinated biphenyls) and asbestos materials, about which much has
been written, but there are, of course, many more. Hazardous substances
do not include petroleum, crude oil and natural gas.

waste disposal facilities. In addition, it authorizes suit when the past or
present handling, storage, treatment, transportation or disposal of any haz-
ardous waste may present an imminent and substantial endangerment to
health or to the environment. Such suit is permissible against any person
who has contributed or who is contributing to such handling, storage, treat-
ment, transportation or disposal, to restrain such person or to order such
person to take such other actions that may be necessary, or both. That
requirement has been interpreted to impose strict liability® and to apply to
persons who in the past contributed to the handling, storage, treatment,
transportation or disposal of hazardous waste, regardless of how far into
the past those activities occurred.
The Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation and Lia-
bility Act (““CERCLA”’)3 was enacted in 1980 in an effort to eliminate
unsafe hazardous waste sites throughout the United States. Amended in
1986 by the Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act of 1986
(“SARA”’),7 CERCLA authorized the EPA to clean up hazardous waste
sites itself and created a “Superfund” to pay for such EPA activities.
Superfund is financed through a combination of appropriations, industry
taxes and judgments obtained through legal actions to recover response
costs from those found to be responsible for the hazardous waste problems.
The statutory scheme to facilitate the clean-up of hazardous toxic waste
sites includes established procedures for either removal actions (clean-up
and removal of released hazardous substances) or remedial actions (taken
instead of or in addition to removal actions, generally to prevent or mini-
mize the release of hazardous substances). Although the line between
removal and remedial actions has not been precisely drawn by the U.S.
Congress, removal actions are typically short-term measures taken to pre-
vent immediate harm, whereas remedial actions are generally long-term
projects designed to effect permanent solutions (e.g., perimeter protection
using dykes, trenches or ditches; clay cover; neutralization of hazardous
substances; and monitoring to assure that such actions protect the public
health and welfare, and the environment).

In a similar vein, the question arises as to the liability exposure of a
construction contractor who, as an incident of excavation or other
subsurface operation in which he is engaged on a major underground pro-
ject, moves a quantity of hazardous waste or substance out of the way of
such operation. Does he thereby becomes a transporter, handler and/or
disposer of such hazardous waste, so as to subject him to the statutory
liability under discussion? Such questions are of great concern, and a
number of recent decisions by federal courts in various locations
throughout the United States suggest that there is definitely potential
liability for the construction contractor.
In broad context, a federal appellate court in Texas expressed the view
that CERCLA does not automatically exclude the imposition of “chilling
liability on the defendants’ businesses: banking, real estate, construction,
and development’’!7 (emphasis supplied). The same court also noted,
albeit in the context of ownership or operation of a facility at which haz-

“This definition of disposal does not limit disposal to a one-time occurrence;
there may be other disposals when hazardous materials are moved, dispersed, or
released during landfill excavations and fillings.’’®

Hazardous waste liability 169
In the most widely reported of these cases, Kaiser Aluminum, supra, the
defendant in a CERCLA action had been a prior owner of the contami-
nated site, which then impleaded as third-party defendant the construction
contractor retained to excavate and grade part of the property for a pro-
posed residential development. During performance of that work, the con-
tractor spread some excavated soil over other portions of the property.
That soil was subsequently determined to contain a number of hazardous
substances, including paint thinner, lead, asbestos and petroleum hydro-
carbons. The defendant alleged in its third-party complaint that the con-
struction contractor had exacerbated the contamination by spreading such
soil over previously uncontaminated parts of the property. The federal
district court dismissed the impleader action, finding that such contractor
was not a potentially responsible party under the CERCLA definitions.
However, the appellate court reversed, holding that allegations that the
contractor “excavated the contaminated soil from one area of the property
and moved it to another are sufficient to allege potential liability.”2> By
virtue of those acts, the contractor was considered an operator of a facility
at which it disposed of hazardous substances and it also accepted hazardous
substances for transport to sites selected by it.
Statutory settlement procedures
One of the purposes of the most recent federal statute (““SARA’’) was to
implement a detailed settlement procedure designed to help facilitate
negotiated settlements between the EPA and potentially responsible
parties (“PRPs”). The intent here is to recover and conserve federal
Superfund monies.
PRPs are defined in CERCLA to include any and all parties who are
responsible for the release of hazardous waste, such as current owners and
operators of hazardous waste facilities, past owners and operators who
accepted the waste on-site, generators of hazardous waste, and transporters
of waste to a disposal facility. Once a hazardous waste site becomes listed
on the EPA’s National Priorities List, established under the National Oil
and Hazardous Substances Pollution Contingency Plan,*+ the PRPs are
given the option to enter into a settlement agreement with the EPA. The
EPA is not obliged to enter into such an agreement, and any decision to do
so is wholly discretionary. The settlement agreement occurs in the form of
a court ordered consent decree and contains promises by the PRPs to
23 976 F.2d at 1343.
24 40 C.F.R. §300.

-- 205 of 360 --

170 Impact on Construction Activites
perform and finance the clean-up, pursuant to a clean-up plan approved by
EPA. Where the parties have failed to settle, the EPA may commence the
clean-up, using Superfund monies. Once the clean-up is completed, the
EPA generally seeks recovery of all costs from the PRPs under CERCLA’s
cost recovery provisions.
It is also noteworthy that the federal statutory scheme does not prohibit
any party, potentially responsible for response or remediation costs in
cleaning up a hazardous waste site, from seeking contribution from other
potentially responsible parties in an action brought by the federal govern-
ment to recover such costs. Moreover, a party potentially responsible for
such costs may also maintain a separate private suit against another poten-
tially responsible party to recover such costs.
State “Mini-Superfund” statutes

There

shown to have been the result of negligence, gross negligence or reckless, wanton

In New Jersey, the comparable statute is known as the New Jersey Spill
Compensation and Control Act,?”? which imposes a general strict liability
on any person who discharges a hazardous substance or is responsible for a
hazardous substance which the state has removed; however, clean-up or
response action contractors have been excepted and held to a negligence
standard in that jurisdiction. As to Connecticut, that state’s general stat-
utes, under its Water Pollution Control Act,?8 have created an Emergency
Spill Response Fund, with liability generally appearing to be based on a
negligence rather than strict liability standard.
For the construction contractor and design professional alike, the con-
cern here should nevertheless be, as it is under federal law, that in dealing
with hazardous wastes at the construction site, extreme care must be
exercised to avoid being categorized as an operator, generator, transporter,
handler or disposer of hazardous wastes, or one who has “contributed to”
or ‘‘arranged for” one or more of such activities. Any of these could trigger
some degree of liability to the federal or state government, or to another
potentially responsible party in a private litigation.

	of

172 Impact on Construction Activites
should be legislatively insulated, at least to the extent of the response
action contractor (if not more), as earlier stated, that simply is not the case
at present. Thus, the lack of such statutory protection underscores the need
for construction contractors to exercise extreme vigilance in this area as
work progresses on the deep foundation or other subsurface site, where
unforeseen hazardous wastes may be encountered.
There can be no argument with the proposition that clean-up contracting
is a dramatically growing business throughout the nation, and oppor-
tunities in that field may appear quite attractive to the aggressive U.S.
construction contractor, particularly for one who has heavy earthmoving
experience and is understandably concerned about the lengthy and con-
tinuing downturn in the construction industry. However, as to those com-
mentators who suggest that there exists a natural transition from
construction to clean-up contracting, it should be emphasized that the
nature of a response action contractor’s business is dramatically different
from that of a construction contractor. A response action contractor has
know-how, experience, facilities and personnel well-equipped to deal with
hazardous wastes in a safe, efficient manner, and thereby to limit potential
liability exposure. Such a contractor is further buttressed, as noted above,
by statutory liability limitation and even statutory indemnification if ade-
quate insurance coverage is unavailable.
A notable trend in recent years, underscoring the sharp dichotomy bet-
ween these two types of businesses in the United States has been the recent
public sector trend of treating the clean-up contract as a professional
services contract, requiring a high order of technical field engineering and
laboratory testing prequalifications, and thereby avoiding the customary
competitive bidding statutes applicable to most public construction
contracts.
Pre-bid inquiries
Having now reviewed
	the
broad
	statutory
	scheme
	and
types of
potential
hazardous
	waste
	liability
	for the
	construction contractor and design
professional on
	a
subsurface
	project, the focus of
this
	paper
	shifts to
consideration
	of
what
	these
	entities
	can
	do
	to
minimize
	the
	risks
	and
protect
themselves during
the
design and bidding period,
as
well
as
in the
construction phase
itself.
The previously mentioned cradle-to-grave concept
of
owner
	liability for
hazardous wastes under
	RCRA
	was softened under
	SARA,
	wherein
	an
“innocent purchaser” defence was established to
environmental
	liability,
applicable if the owner, at the time of acquisition, made
	“all appropriate
inquiry into the previous ownership and uses of the property” and con-

-- 208 of 360 --

Hazardous waste liability 173
ducted “appropriate inspection”’.29 This duty of inquiry led to the develop-
ment of a new branch of environmental engineering services for owners,
known as the site assessment. It can be a very valuable tool for the con-
struction contractor during the pre-bid period. Briefly, the site assessment
typically comprises three components: an audit, an assessment and a
report.
Audits typically include a review of available historical records and regu-
latory files, which would involve checking the ownership of the site for the
past 50 years or more to see how the land was used. For example, was it a
manufacturing plant employing hazardous substances, some of which may
have been disposed at the site? The audit would also include a walking tour
of the property, an automobile tour of the immediate vicinity, interviews
with property owners, and a report documenting activities on the property
and providing an opinion on actual or potential contamination.
One of the difficulties with this phase of site assessment is that a poten-
tial owner may not want to tip its hand to the existing owner as to its
intentions prior to negotiating a purchase price, and hence there may be a
tendency to tiptoe around what really needs to be done in an environ-
mental assessment to make a valuable judgmental determination. This
problem particularly arises where a prospective purchaser is contemplating
assembling a series of contiguous parcels for a large development such that,
if existing owners get wind of that intention, the prices can soar.
An engineering firm represented by the author was recently involved in
that type of situation, where the prospective purchaser wanted a limited site
assessment, excluding actual entry onto the land or into the buildings under
consideration. Subsequent to purchase, when potentially hazardous sub-
stances were discovered at the site, there was one very unhappy purchaser.
This led to significant litigation against prior owners, as well as against the
engineering firm, for the diminution in property value, prospective cleanup
costs and related damages, the outcome of which is far from certain.
The assessment phase, which involves a more detailed and specific fact-
finding effort, generally includes field sampling, laboratory analyses and
other testing to investigate specific concerns identified during the audit.
The purpose of such assessment is to confirm or refute suspected con-
tamination with real data. However, that does not necessarily mean that
such sampling and testing will eliminate all uncertainty. It is fairly common
that the extent of sampling activities is balanced against the owner’s
willingness to assume risk, as compared to the amount of time and money
available for such detailed assessment.
29 42 U.S.C. §9601(35)(B).

-- 209 of 360 --

174 Impact on Construction Activites
The results of the above fact-finding are incorporated by the environ-
mental engineer into a site audit and assessment report for the owner. This
is an extremely valuable document, which the construction contractor
should seek to obtain and review prior to entering into a construction
contract on any project where there is the remotest possibility of the exis-
tence of hazardous wastes. Even if there has been no site assessment
undertaken by an engineer on behalf of the current or a prior owner, the
mere fact that the contractor is on record as having requested a copy of any
such report, or that a site assessment be made prior to contracting if no
such report exists, could stand it in good stead if problems of this type
develop during construction.
In the situation where the owner of the project is a public body, it is
recommended that requests for such site assessment reports be in the form
of a federal Freedom of Information Act and/or state Freedom of Inform-
ation Law request. This should be done at the earliest possible time, proba-
bly as soon as the contractor takes out a set of plans and specifications, and
even before it determines if an actual bid is to be submitted. That lead time
could be important, inasmuch as FOIA and FOIL requests generally neces-
sitate several weeks, at best, before one is likely to obtain any of the
requested documents.
Beyond
this
type
of

inquiry, the

	also

	the plans,

	and

	on

	subsurface

included
	in

with respect
	to

	or

	encountered

	investigation phase.

	also

	to

	in

site

on

setting forth the steps
to

in

	that

are
encountered, which
may

may
not
require

	for their
	removal; any
	special

	with

and any exculpatory language, such

as

	problems

i.e.,

	to

	Onthe other hand,
if the contractor
is
dealing with a private owner, an aware-
ness as to the significance of these type of provisions can be extremely

helpful to it and counsel in negotiating a more favourable and fair balanc-
ing of the risks in the area of hazardous waste.
Another recommended pre-bid area of inquiry is the contractor’s own
investigation of the project site, as well as that of the surrounding land and
water bodies near the project location. Thus, e.g., if the nature of the project
is that of covering over an old garbage landfill, that may put the contractor
on notice as to the possibility of initial exposure, since of necessity it may
have to move or relocate some of the decomposed matter in that landfill to
fulfil the contractual obligations. Similarly, if the project is adjacent to muck
swampland, and one can hardly see the colour of the water, with nonaroma-
tic vapours arising from the surface, the contractor may well be in for a very
unpleasant water table condition and a significant problem, of not just dew-
atering, but of how to dispose of the dewatered material.
To illustrate the potential extreme of this type of situation, a contractor
client recently consulted the author on a problem that had him running in
circles. It seemed that his project called for the construction of a sewer line
in an area adjacent to an extremely stagnant body of water that contained,
in this instance, not hazardous waste, but oil and grease. Clearly, his de-
watering operations, in order to perform the deep sewer installation, in-
volved the removal of water containing that oil and grease. The New York
State Environmental Conservation Department, however, would not per-
mit him to discharge that water back into the same contaminated adjacent
body of water which had generated this oil and grease, but rather insisted
that it be disposed of elsewhere by very expensive means.
In
that situation,
while there
may
	have been
no
way
of
totally
avoiding
such
result,
surely
it is
far
better
to
recognize
it
at

pre-bid
stage,
so
the
contractor
	is
aware
	of
what
could be
a
very

risk,
and
at
least
has the
opportunity
	to
consider
	that in
its
bid,
rather than
face
a
very
unpleasant
and
unexpected
	surprise
at a
later
point
in time.
Thus,
	if
the
contractor
	is
fortunate
	enough
	to
have
	the

	to
negotiate any
	of the contract provisions
	prior
to

	into the con-
struction contract
	where hazardous
	waste
	might
	be
	encountered
	during

	the best rule of
thumb
	is
to
minimize
	absolutely,

any responsibility on
its

for

or

Ideally, the

	would
	seek

	if

with
a third party (probably
a

response action contractor)
	to
remove and dispose of the haz-
ardous waste, and with the construction contractor
	to
be appropriately
compensated
	for any delays and increased costs in construction work
arising out of that particular situation.

With respect to the design professional’s vulnerability in the pre-bid
period, its likely direct involvement in site investigation, as part of the
design function, and the preparation of construction contract documents,
requires a different type of inquiry in that period from that of the con-
struction contractor. It must scrupulously put the owner on notice, in very
timely fashion, as to the need for various types of subsurface investigations
and testing throughout the project site, particularly where there be any
suspicion as to the presence of hazardous wastes. Should the owner baulk,
due to economic or time constraints, or perhaps for other practical reasons,
the design professional cannot afford the risk of getting caught up in the
owner’s problems where the price is inadequate site investigations data for
a prudent design. Clearly, that situation creates a dilemma, requiring a
balancing of legal and business factors for the design professional in deter-
mining how to proceed in performing professionally within the owner’s less
than palatable parameters.
Contractual liability and protective mechanisms
The spectre of potential strict liability for the construction contractor and,
perhaps, the design professional as well, in handling or disposing of haz-
ardous wastes, necessitates recognition and careful consideration of
various contract provisions which can affect that type of responsibility.
There
is
often
to
be
found
a
contract
provision
which
requires

	by

	and
	an

that

by

provides

	and

	many

	to

	the

	the

	On

will

	Hence,

	can usually

	information furnished
	in

	or

coupled

This can
	be

	area,
particularly since the

	provide
	that

	may

	for the

encountering such conditions. While
	haz-
ardous wastes may
	not be specifically identified
	as a potential source of

	to that

Hazardous waste liability 17
A no-damages-for-delay clause, which prohibits the contractor’s recov-
ery for project delays from any cause whatsoever, is quite common these
days in construction contracts throughout the United States, and generally
has been found to be enforceable, with certain very limited exceptions. In
some jurisdictions, depending on the representations and information
contained in the contract documents, unanticipated hazardous substances
causing project delays would likely be regarded as unforeseeable and
outside the application of a no-damages-for-delay clause. Likewise, discov-
ery of hazardous materials contrary to the express representations in the
contract documents may also be a basis for escaping application of such
exculpatory provision.
On this point, in New York, for example, delays resulting from the
owner’s breach of a fundamental obligation of the contract have also been
considered an exception to exculpatory language that there shall be no
damages for delay.3° Active interference by the owner has been held to be
such a type of breach, with a number of courts holding that the failure
timely to provide a site for project construction constitutes such inter-
ference. Recognizing that there as yet appear to be no judicial determina-
tions in New York or elsewhere considering this issue in a hazardous waste
context, the contractor deprived of his ability to proceed because of the
discovery of hazardous waste may nevertheless have a compelling argu-
ment that the owner has effectively interfered with the contractor’s entitle-
ment to site access, and hence that the delay damages flowing from that
condition are compensable.
Another
significant area
for

to

in

	problem

	New

in

other jurisdictions coverage is generally supported,2 in the absence of an

Generally, complaints in hazardous waste actions seek the repayment or
restitution of funds expended or to be expended investigating and cleaning
up a contaminated site or preventing further contamination of a site. Various
courts have held these claims are not for damages resulting from property
damage, since they have no relationship to the actual diminution in land
value, for which that coverage is intended. In fact, there have been instances
where the total bill for clean-up costs far exceeded the overall value of the
land involved, let alone any diminution in value; in one such Pennsylvania
federal case, the court limited damages to the value of the property.33
The time has not yet come where any federal or state legislature has
indicated a receptiveness to insulate the construction contractor in the
Same manner as the clean-up or response action contractor, due to an
inability to obtain adequate insurance coverage. However, this may well be
due to the lack of much significant litigation to date over claims for clean-
up costs against the construction contractor.

Hazardous waste liability 179
Liability Act of 1980 (CERCLA) and the Resource Conservation and Recovery
Act of 1976 (RCRA), provide that individuals and firms may be held liable for
damages and claims related to such work under a doctrine of joint and several
strict liability. It is not the intention of this contract that the contractor be
exposed to any hazardous waste liability arising out of pre-contract site con-
tamination, the activities of others, including the owner, or for any hazardous
waste liabilities which may arise from the non-negligent performance by the
contractor of any work hereunder. Accordingly, for purposes of this contract
only, excepting the wilful negligent performance by the contractor or its sub-
contractors, the owner shall reimburse the contractor and/or its subcontractors
for, or otherwise indemnify, defend and save the contractor harmless from, any
and all demands, suits, judgments, expenses, attorney’s fees and losses arising out
of, or in connection with, bodily injury (including death) to persons or damage to
property which may arise from the presence or origination of hazardous sub-
stances, pollutants or contaminants on the site, irrespective of whether such
materials were generated or introduced before or after execution of this contract
and irrespective of whether the owner was aware of, or indirectly involved in, the
generation or introduction of such materials.
The contractor and/or its subcontractor shall, under no circumstances, be
considered the generator of any hazardous substances, pollutants or contami-
nants encountered or handled in the performance of the work. Without contra-
diction of any assertion by the owner of third party liability, as described above,
for the purpose of this contract only, it is agreed that any hazardous materials,
pollutants or contaminants generated or encountered in the performance of the
work shall be the responsibility of the owner.”
Notwithstanding such favourable contract language, it should be borne in
mind that the parties to such a contract cannot, by their agreement, over-
ride any contrary statutes with respect to liability, especially to the federal
or state governments. Nevertheless, this type of provision can dramatically
reduce the risk with respect to private parties’ litigation.
Protecting
the
record during construction
	upon encountering
hazardous
	waste
We
	next consider
what
the
construction contractor and design professional
should do
when
	hazardous
	wastes
	are
actually
encountered,
	not
only from
the physical standpoint, which
is
likely
simply
to
mean
	the
stopping
of
work
and
notifying the
owner
	of the
problem,
but rather
from
the
perspective
of
protecting
the
record and
the rights of the parties.
In virtually every construction contract, there
are
a
series of notice pro-
visions,
which require the contractor
to
apprise the
owner
	of
problem
areas
being encountered, may
require decisions of the architect or
engineer, and
may
	also serve
	as a
prelude for seeking additional compensation
	for the
associated increased costs. Such notice requirements typically deal with the
subjects of unforeseen conditions encountered at the jobsite (such as

-- 215 of 360 --

180 Impact on Construction Activites
hazardous wastes), disputed work notices and force account records, as
well as notices of delay, disruption, work stoppages and the like, which also
require maintenance of supporting cost records. The obvious purpose of
these notice provisions is to enable the owner and its architect or engineer
to be alerted in a timely fashion, and thereby to be able to maintain
contemporaneous cost records to compare against the contractor’s at such
time as the problem is resolved, whether it be through a subsequent change
order or a claim in litigation.
Failure to comply with such contractual provisions with respect to exist-
ing or potential claims can yield a very harsh result of being nonsuited on
this procedural ground. The importance to construction contractors of
complying with the contractual notice provisions cannot be over-
emphasized, since there are more than enough problems to be faced with
litigating a substantial and complex construction claim on the merits with-
out being barred at the threshold by procedural impediments.
Other steps which should be implemented in protecting the record are the
maintenance of detailed job diary entries by the field personnel, setting forth
the encountering of hazardous waste, in terms of what was seen, by nature,
description and quantities, what, if anything, was disturbed or touched, as
well as the substance of field discussions with other parties. Timely
correspondence, apprising the other parties of this problem, which may well
delay completion of the project and increase the costs, are also essential.

	a
	picture
	may
	be

	in

	of

this

as
well.
Claims
	against the
construction contractor and design professional
As noted
in the cited decisions
from
several courts
throughout
the
UnitedStates,
it is
becoming
	increasingly
common
	for the construction contractorto
be
a
named
	party in
hazardous waste
	litigation
under
	CERCLA
	and
exposed to serious
liability.
To
a
much
lesser
degree thus far,
but neverthe-less a very real risk, is the exposure of the design professional to such
liability. In either instance, the claim would generally be for participating in

-- 216 of 360 --

Hazardous waste liability 181
or contributing to the handling, transporting or disposal of such wastes, or
arranging to have others do it. The contractor or design professional might
be a party defendant in an action by the government or by another poten-
tially responsible party. While the experience in either situation is certainly
not going to be pleasant, the contractor and design professional are not
wholly without recourse.
In the first instance, even in the absence of a contractual indemnity
provision, of the type reviewed earlier, the contractor should be able to
claim over against the owner of the property, who more than likely played
a significant role in the generation or storage of the hazardous waste or, if
not, could be in a position to pass that exposure onto a prior owner. In
either case, it would certainly help the contractor’s cause.
As to the engineer who performed the site assessment or designed the
project, while the construction contractor does not have any direct privity
of contract with that party upon which a claim can be based, there is a
limited body of law in some jurisdictions which may permit the contractor
to implead such engineer as a third-party defendant, on the ground that it
owed a duty beyond contractual that was certainly foreseeable, and that its
negligent performance directly caused the complained of damages. At the
very least, this potential avenue of liability should not be overlooked by
prudent counsel for the construction contractor. Obviously, the design
professional would have similar reciprocal rights.
Claims by the construction contractor
Regardless
of
how
well the
construction contractor
may
be
protected
in
its
contract
	for
liability
to
others
for
clean-up
costs

	that

due

	wastes,
the situation
could occur
where
	it

	arising out of delays,
disruptions
and increased
costs
from
those circumstances.

	to

should be on the

in
the future,
as
the

	of

	to

	in the

	States.

	there have

	a
number
	of federal

illustrate the point.

BCA

	the contract
34 Darwins Construction Co.,
