documents indicated that certain ceiling tiles could be removed as ordinary
construction debris, the construction contractor was able to show the exist-
ence of a latent condition of contamination with asbestos dust, and
required much more expensive asbestos removal procedures to abate. The
contractor recovered such unanticipated increased costs under the contrac-
tual differing site conditions provision.
Insofar as measure of damages is concerned in this area, the contractor
may find that there are items of work, if not the contract as a whole, that
may turn out to be performed precisely in the manner called for under the
original contract documents, and with no design or other changes, but that
the overall cost far exceeds that which was included in the lump sum price
or applicable contract unit prices, as the case may be. In essence, there
could be a loss of productivity claim.
The measure of these damages, which the courts of New York and
elsewhere have been most receptive to, is quantum meruit, i.e., the total
actual job costs plus an allowance for overhead and profit, less the amounts
paid to date by the owner for such items or elements of work. Where the
contract is of a unit price type, the amounts paid for the items of work in
question are clearly determinable. In a lump sum contract, the problem
becomes a bit more difficult. However, in that situation, the contractor is
usually required to submit at the start of the job a trade breakdown for
partial payment purposes, which would allocate amounts for various items
and elements of work, and to which the owner agrees. This would then
constitute the basis for determining the amount paid in that type of
situation.
One word of caution on such approach is that the courts in some jurisdic-
tions, notably New York, will not allow the use of the pre-bid estimate to
measure damages for labour inefficiency, in lieu of amounts paid. Thus, if it
is not possible to determine the amounts paid needed to subtract from the
total costs on the quantum meruit approach, the contractor may well have
to consider the alternative and generally less preferred approach discussed
below.
That would
be
to
measure
	labour
inefficiency,
predicated
upon
an inef-
ficiency
	percentage
	or
	factor,
	a
method
	that
	has not
	been extensively
adopted by
courts
in
many
jurisdictions.
	However,
	the
contractor should
be aware
	that there
	are
	a
number
	of
subcontractor
	trade association
Studies that
	provide such factors for
various items and types of
work
which, together with appropriate contractor and expert testimony show-
ing performance
	of similar elements of
work
	on the
same contract
	when
unimpeded
	at a
higher productivity rate, can nevertheless be viable. In a
federal case decided under New York law, the court noted a loss of
efficiency claim may be difficult to prove, but that a loss of productivity

-- 218 of 360 --

Hazardous waste liability 183
percentage, based upon a proper evidentiary foundation, may well be
allowable.35
Sometimes the contract work may be delayed, interfered with and
impeded by the encountering and disposing of hazardous waste, such that it
is performed in later periods of time than originally contemplated under the
original contract (even if not beyond the specified contractual completion
date). It may well be the contractor is paying its labour higher wage rates
pursuant to applicable union contracts, than would have otherwise been paid
had the work been capable of being performed at the time periods contem-
plated, and upon which the contract price was based. Typically, in New York,
many union agreements expire on 1 July, at which time higher rates go into
effect. Although the guantum meruit approach to increased costs of contract
work would actually have built into it such wage rate increases for those
elements of work, it may be prudent to back out the wage rate increases as a
separate claim item, on the principle that the chances of success are
increased by not having all the contractor’s eggs in the same basket.
Similarly, there may well be material and equipment cost escalations
incurred by the contractor for the same reason as the labour escalations,
and they as well would be claimable.
Depending upon the extent of the delay encountered, the contractor
might also be entitled to claim extended field and home office overhead
costs, the latter being a more difficult item to prove, because it is the rare
contractor that is able to show direct home office overhead costs spec-
ifically attributable to the project. However, there are methods which have
been permitted by some courts to establish an estimated, but acceptable,
dollar value for that element.
Conclusion
This paper has been intended to acquaint the reader with the general legal
scheme in the United States, both federal and state, affecting construction
contractors and design professionals in the hazardous waste area. Contrac-
tual pitfalls and protective mechanisms, as well as broad claim parameters,
have also been reviewed. The bottom line here, worth reiterating, is that if
the contractor encounters unforeseen hazardous waste at its construction
site, it should not be touched, the work in that area should be stopped and
immediate notification given to the owner and its engineer, both orally and
in writing.
35S. Leo Harmonay,
Inc. v
Binks Manufacturing Co., 597 F.Supp. 1014 (S.D.N.Y. 1984), aff'd
w/o opinion 762 F.2d 990 (2d Cir. 1985).

-- 219 of 360 --

184 Impact on Construction Activites
Depending upon the specific contractual requirements which the con-
tractor or design professional has agreed to, it should avoid, to the extent
possible, being put into the position of having to handle, move, transport
off the site, or otherwise dispose of, any such hazardous waste, either
directly by the contractor, or arranged for or contributed to by it or the
design professional. Any one or more of these activities can be a legal basis
for the federal or state government, or for a private party, to claim against
one or both such parties for all or part of the clean-up costs and related
damages. .
Moreover, the construction contractor should not assume the role of a
clean-up contractor on a construction project, in the absence of qualified
technical expertise and capabilities, which it likely does not possess. If it
contemplates doing otherwise, before the first shovelful of hazardous waste
is turned, the construction contractor had best be sure that its attorney is
well qualified in this field.

8 Construction sites and the human factor: a
challenge for the construction industry
Helen Garthwaite
Introduction
The last decade has seen a significant and sustained increase in society’s
concern for the environment including the protection of human health.
From origins in the early pressure campaigns to “Save Moby Dick” and
“Ban the Bomb” environmental issues have become recognised as key
items on both the domestic! and international? political agenda. Much of
society’s environmental concern has been and continues to be directed at
the construction industry as in the case of the on-going controversy over
the construction of the motorway across Twyford Down.
This paper seeks to examine the nature of the challenge (and the
consequential risks and liabilities), facing the construction industry as a result
of today’s raised environmental consciousness by focusing on recent and
proposed developments in statutory control of construction sites with par-
ticular regard to those statutory controls which impact on human health and
safety. In so doing, the paper considers emerging and associated trends with
a view to identifying underlying policies and pressures and predicting future
developments on the health and safety front as an insight into the parallel
issues for the construction industry in the wider environmental context.
Construction sites and the human factor
Few would dispute that construction sites and construction activities
present a significant risk to the health and safety of individuals, whether
on-site construction workers or simply neighbours or passers-by. The
health and safety record of the construction industry is notoriously poor.

Noise and vibration
Construction activities are inherently noisy and often take place in areas
which are usually quiet and so this is a frequent cause of public complaint.
Problems also arise for employees working in confined spaces.
:
Us

Dust and dirt
This may range from the unsightly and annoying to, in the case of haz-
ardous dust or fibres, in sufficient quantities, the directly harmful, and is
another common cause of public complaint. Effective site control has been
identified as an essential means to control dust but in the absence of a clear
publication explaining these site control techniques, the industry record in
this area is poor.®
An entertaining illustration of the diverse nature of construction site
risks and site prompted complaints was that recently encountered by an
associate of the author. Asked for advice by a client conducting piling
operations in the North of England in close proximity to a local TV trans-
mitter, he armed himself with a copy of the Control of Pollution Act 1974
and BS.52287 and prepared himself to advise. Unfortunately, the problem
did not relate to noise or vibration and he was slightly disconcerted to find
himself discussing the possible consequences of the piling rig interfering
with the reception of the latest episode of ‘Coronation Street”!
In passing, it is also worth noting that the permitted levels applied in
assessing whether or not there is a construction site hazard are often higher
than the permitted levels used when assessing hazards from permanent in-
stallations. A clear example of this is seen in the assessment of permitted
levels of noise and concentrations of dark smoke as illustrated in Figure J over.

for the purposes of this paper it is proposed to focus on recent changes in

The statutory measures relating to human health and safety in the con-
text of the construction site comprise of a complex mesh of legislation
structured around the Health and Safety at Work Act 1974 and the Fac-
tories Act 1961 (under which a series of regulations have been passed) as
well as the Occupiers’ Liability Act 1957. It is not proposed in this paper to
review such existing legislation but rather to concentrate on recent and
proposed developments in key areas with a view to identifying legislative
policies.

Outline
of
existing legislation:
Construction
site
noise
is
governed by
one of
the few parts of the Control of Pollution Act 1974
(COPA)
	which remainin force following the enactment of the Environmental Protection Act
1990. Section 60 of the
	COPA empowers
	local authorities to serve on
persons carrying out or responsible for the carrying out of construction

-- 224 of 360 --

Construction sites 189
works a notice imposing requirements as to the way in which works are to
be carried out. Notices can only control works within the perimeter of the
construction site. It is not necessary for the local authority to establish
nuisance and the Notice may be served prior to commencement of works.
Appeal against a Notice lies to the Magistrates Court. Failure to comply
with the Notice without reasonable excuse® is an offence punishable on
summary conviction.
Under section 61 of the COPA it is possible to apply in advance to the
local authority for consent to the carrying out of works. This affords a
means of avoiding the risk of a section 60 Notice providing a route for
dialogue with the local authority and an opportunity for early agreement
on satisfactory noise control measures. Despite common reference to com-
pliance with these provisions in the Preliminaries section of Bills of Quan-
tities and Employer’s Requirements this procedure is often not utilised on
grounds of time, the cost of reaching agreement with the local authority
and a feared reduction of the contractors’ flexibility to control site oper-
ations (although the latter is probably misconceived as if good practices are
not adopted a section 60 Notice would almost certainly result). Contractors
seem to prefer taking the risk of delay following service of a section 60
Notice rather than a possible delay due to section 61 negotiations.
Aggrieved neighbours occupying premises near construction sites affected
by construction site noise may bring proceedings in the Magistrates Courts
under section 59 or seek an injunction in the civil courts in respect of
nuisance.
Other existing UK legislation with the aim of controlling noise includes
legislation requiring employers to carry out an assessment of the exposure
of employees to noise levels and provide personal protection where em-
ployees are exposed to noise levels in excess of specified levels? and legis-
lation implementing the various directives which set emission standards for
certain types of plant and equipment.!°

Outline of Existing Legislation: UK statutory control of air pollution is
governed in the main by the Environmental Protection Act 1990 (EPA
1990) which deals with the authorising of polluting processes and emis-
sions. The EPA 1990 also contains statutory nuisance powers under which
the local authority can require (through issue of an Abatement Notice) the
person responsible for dust, steam, smells or other effluvia prejudicial to
health arising on premises such as harmful fumes and gases and accumula-
tions of rubbish and (in some cases) smoke to stop such nuisance.

stricter requirements to The Control of Asbestos at Work Regulations
1987. The 1987 Regulations were already extensive, extending the em-
ployer’s duty under the Health and Safety at Work Act 1974 not only to
employees but to any other person who may be affected by the work
including visitors, neighbours and even trespassers where the duty exists.
The new amendments require the preparation of a written method state-
ment before asbestos removal operations start which is to include details of
location, nature and proper duration of the work, the methods to be ap-
plied in handling asbestos, the characteristics of protective equipment and
decontamination of workers and the protection of other persons on or near
the site. However, there is no duty to submit the method statement to an
appropriate enforcing authority. The Regulations also require the notifi-
cation of employees and other persons who may be affected in the event of
any escape of asbestos.
The Regulations were driven by the need to implement a number of EC
Directives in this area including the Directive covering Exposure to Car-
cinogens!> which also was responsible for amending regulations!4 to the
Control of Substances Hazardous to Health Regulations 1988 (COSHH).
Site activities and site management
As mentioned above, the framework for health and safety legislation in the
UK is provided by the Health and Safety at Work Act 1974 (HSWA). The
Act creates a number of general duties on employers to ensure that, so far
as reasonably practicable, the health, safety and welfare of employees (and
others who may be affected by their work) is safeguarded. A breach of any
one of these duties (sections 2-4 and 7) is a criminal offence as is any
failure to observe any requirement or prohibition contained in regulations
issued under the HSWA. The HSWA was designed to allow the creation of
supplementary or interpretive regulations initially to cover certain risks or
activities. More recently the Act has become a channel for the UK imple-
mentation of the requirements of EC Health and Safety Directives as will
be seen below.
The

	on

(section 37). In the case of a health and safety breach, directors or man-

As far as the general duties of the Act are concerned, civil actions for
breach of statutory duty are excluded by section 47. However, breach of a
duty imposed by regulations is actionable except where the regulations
expressly provide otherwise (hence the controversy in relation to the pos-
sibility of increased civil liability under the proposed Construction (Design
and Management) Regulations, see below).

Recent developments: The Health and Safety at Work Act 1974 provides
the statutory framework for the implementation of European Directives
concerning health and safety in the form of subsidiary regulations sup-
ported by voluntary standards and approved codes of practice issued under
section 15 of the Act. European legislation is more descriptive and detailed
in approach than the newer UK regulations which are ‘‘object-setting”’.
The UK emphasis is seen as more flexible and suited to keeping abreast of
advances in technology. Standards and codes of practice are not legally
binding but compliance with them will usually satisfy the requirements of
the relevant regulations.
The period of the UK Presidency of the EC coincided with a significant
increase in legislative activity in the health and safety arena with many of
the regulations below coming into force at the beginning of 1993.
The various UK health and safety regulations affecting construction sites
and activities include the following.
The Provision and Use of Work Equipment Regulations 199217
These Regulations require employers to ensure that working conditions
and hazards are taken into account when selecting equipment; that the
equipment is suitable for its use and properly maintained; that use should
be restricted to trained persons where there is a specified risk and that
adequate information, instruction and training are provided. In force 1
January 1993.
The Personal
Protective
Equipment
at
Work
	Regulations 199218
These Regulations
require
employers
to
provide personal protective equip-
ment (PPE) where
	health and

cannot be adequately controlled
	by
other means;
	assess the risks
and
select suitable
PPE;
instruct,

train
employees
in the use of
PPE; maintain
PPE
and provide
accommoda-
tion for storage; and ensure proper
	use of
PPE. The new Regulations
17 Implementing the Use of
Work Equipment Directive (89/655/EEC).

replace pre-1974 legislation and make minor amendments to modern regu-

The Manual Handling Operations Regulations 1992/9
Under these Regulations employers are required to avoid the need for
manual handling by, so far as is reasonably practicable, removing or reduc-
ing the risk. New duties are placed upon employees to inform their em-
ployers about any health condition that might affect their ability to lift
loads. In force 1 January 1993.
The Control of Substances Hazardous to Health (Amendment) Regulations
199220
This amendment expands the COSHH Regulations 1988 to give greater
emphasis to the prevention of exposure to carcinogens at work by means of
substitution and enclosed systems of work. COSHH assessments will have
to be regularly reviewed and health surveillance records maintained for 40
rather than 30 years.

The “‘six pack”’
HSE has published a number of Guidance documents on the various Regu-
lations which were brought into force earlier this year. These are referred
to affectionately in the safety business as the ‘‘six pack”’ (because they are
likely to create the biggest ever boom for safety consultants). The ‘‘six
pack”’ is not so affectionately regarded by clients, contractors and consul-
tants who fear that they “will crush genuine health and safety conscious-
ness under a mound of bogus paperwork in triplicate’? (Colin Harding,
Chairman of George & Harding).
Proposed Construction (Design and Management) Regulations and
Approved Code of Practice
The objective of the proposed regulations
The main objective of the proposed Regulations and accompanying
Approved Code of Practice (ACP), is to ensure effective co-ordination of
health and safety matters throughout the duration of a construction pro-
ject. The proposals aim to:
— address the co-operation and co-ordination issues on construction sites
i.e. provide a framework for site organisation in respect of health and
safety matters; and
—
	recognise
	the
	role
	of
clients
	and
	designers
	in
achieving acceptable
standards
of health
and
safety on
construction
sites.
Traditionally,
	the responsibility
for
site
health
and
safety has
been
	that
of the contractor.
However,
the
proposed
Regulations
will
bring
clients
and
designers
into the
frame, placing obligations
upon them
as
well
as
contrac-
tors, to
ensure
	an
	integrated and planned approach
	to
health
	and
	safety
throughout
	the
life
of the project.
The Regulations
	build
upon
	the existing
requirements
	of sections 2-4 of
the
	Health and Safety
	at
Work
	Act 1974 and do
	not detract
	from
	the
primary duty under
the
Act
for
employers, and
particularly contractors,
to
safeguard
the health and
safety of
their
employees.
The proposed Regulations in outline
(1) The Regulations
will
apply
to all
projects which include ‘‘construction
work’. This is
widely defined in the Regulations
as the carrying out of
any building, civil engineering or engineering construction work and

-- 231 of 360 --

196

(3)

Impact on Construction Activites
includes (inter alia) construction, fitting-out, renovation, repair, main-
tenance, demolition of a structure, commissioning of plant and ser-
vices and cleaning. A “‘structure”’ is also widely defined, covering not
only buildings but extending to (inter alia) tunnels, pipes, lagoons,
pylons, formwork, scaffolding and manufacturing plant. (The list
would seem to be comprehensive!)
The Regulations involve clients to a greater degree than before whilst
recognising that the client does not have day-to-day control of the
site. The following duties are placed on clients: .
— a duty to appoint a “Planning Supervisor” and a “Principal Con-
tractor’ to co-ordinate health and safety during the design and
construction phases of a project. Those appointed must be ‘‘com-
petent” and the appointments should be made by the client at the
“appropriate time”. The client is not precluded from acting as
“Planning Supervisor” or as ‘‘Principal Contractor’’ providing the
client is competent to perform the duties involved.
— a duty to notify and give certain details of sites to the enforcing
authority.
— a duty, so far as is practicable, to ensure that adequate financial
provision is made and enough time allowed to enable work to be
done without risk to health and safety.
— a duty to ensure that the Planning Supervisor is provided with
information about the state or condition of any premises or land at
or on which construction work needs to be carried out.
— aduty to keep the health and safety file prepared and delivered to
him by the Planning Supervisor available for inspection.
The Regulations
place
explicit
duties on
designers
to

so far

—
	their
designs
do
not
expose
those building,

(including

or

a

to risks to
health
and
safety;
and
—
	their
designs include
adequate information about any aspect of the
designs
e.g.
materials
which might
affect
health
and
safety
during
construction work.
The role of the Planning Supervisor is to:
—
	oversee
	the

of the structure
ensuring
that the
designer has
taken account
of health and
safety factors in
terms of
construction
maintenance and

-

	of the financial
and time provi-
sions for
construction work;
-

the
first

which
	will deal with the way the project is to be carried out,
managed
	etc.; and

— procure the preparation of a health and safety file for each struc-
ture comprised in the project and deliver this to the client upon
completion of the construction work.
(5) Once the construction phase is reached, the Principal Contractor will
take over and up-date the health and safety plan and the health and
safety file. In addition, the Principal Contractor is to:
— co-ordinate co-operation between contractors and make sure that
they comply with relevant health and safety legislation and any
rules contained in the health and safety plan;
— make sure that all contractors are appraised of risks to health and
safety; and
— take the necessary steps to control access to the site.
(6) Site contractors are under a specific duty to co-operate with the Prin-
cipal Contractor and inform him of possible risks to health and safety
in addition to the duties they have as employers under the existing
legislation.

Risks, liabilities and concerns
Criminal
	liability:
The primary
	effect of the
proposed Regulations
	is
to in-
crease the
scope
for criminal prosecution
of those
charged with
duties.
It is
fair to say that the risk
of criminal prosecution
for clients
and designers (in
particular
	where
	the designer
	is
appointed
	as
	Planning Supervisor)
	is
increased. Given
	the
strict
nature of the duties
imposed on specific individ-
uals in circumstances where the performance of such duties may not be
within the control of the person primarily charged with the duty, there is a

-- 233 of 360 --

198 Impact on Construction Activites
possibility that a person may be exposed to criminal prosecution by the actions
of another over whom they have no direct control. It is advocated by many in
the industry that this cannot be correct in principle and that such obligations
should be qualified by the familiar “so far as reasonably practicable”’ test.
Civil Liability: There is also an increased risk of civil claims, both in negli-
gence and for breach of statutory duty. With regard to a claim in negli-
gence, the proposed Regulations and ACP add to the body of knowledge in
the management of health and safety matters and so, the failure to have
regard to the Regulations or ACP may give rise to claims in negligence
particularly where there has been a successful prosecution for breach of a
health and safety regulation. In the absence of an exclusion in the proposed
Regulations of a right of action for breach of statutory duty, there is also
increased potential for exposure to claims for damages by persons harmed
as a result of non-compliance. It is interesting here to compare the pro-
posed Regulations with the Management of Health and Safety At Work
Regulations 1992 which contain an exclusion of rights of action for breach
of statutory duty.?? The responses from the construction industry and
others during the consultation period have included suggestions that a
similar exclusion should be included in the proposed Regulations
(seemingly with some success—see below). Increased potential exposure to
damages from civil claims is a particular concern for designers and Plan-
ning Supervisors since the strict nature of their duties under the proposed
Regulations may expose them to a significant increase in their professional
indemnity insurance premiums if, indeed, such duties can be brought
within the scope of available professional indemnity insurance cover.
Other concerns
For

the
major
concerns
	would seem
	to
be
the
following:
(1)
	Uncertainty
as to

	is

“any person
for

carried out’.
This
seems
	to

and
may

confusion,
for

between landowners, developers engaged
	to

an

and funders.
It

that the definition should be
clarified
by
the addition of the criteria that the
client

	the contractor
in

Construction sites 199
(2) The requirement to appoint a Planning Supervisor for all projects:
Currently the proposals require a client to appoint a Planning Supervisor
for all projects including projects of a minor nature such as cleaning, main-
tenance and repair notwithstanding that the role of a Planning Supervisor
for these projects is likely to be limited since they are unlikely to include a
design stage where the Planning Supervisor’s main duties arise. This is
likely to hold significant financial and time consequences for landlords
undertaking a cleaning and maintenance programme for their portfolio. It
has been suggested that the requirement for a Planning Supervisor and a
health and safety plan should be limited to large projects and projects
involving high risk activities as envisaged in the Directive.
(3) The appointment of the Planning Supervisor and Principal Contractor:
(i) Who should the client appoint to fill these roles? Should the client
be permitted to appoint a project supervisor whose appointment
runs from the inception of the project to its completion and who is
not either a planning phase or execution phase co-ordinator or
both? At present, this does not appear to be permitted by the
proposed Regulations and ACP which envisage the Planning
Supervisor being drawn from the design team (probably the Archi-
tect as design team leader) and the Principal Contractor as being
either the main or managing contractor or a specialist contractor
whose activity forms a large part of the project. Concerns have
been voiced over the ‘“‘self-policing” role of the Architect with
preference being for the appointment of an independent Planning
Supervisor.
(ii)
	A
	“competent person”?
	No
	guidance
	is
given
	on
	what
	is
to
be
regarded
	as
“‘competent”’
	for
purposes
	of
appointing
such persons
or
as
to
how
a
Client
is
to
assess
this.
The
client,
particularly
a
lay
client,
can
only do
his
best
to
assess
whether
	a
person he appoints
has
sufficient
experience and
knowledge
	to
enable
him
to
fulfil
his
responsibilities.
	More
	often than not he
must
	rely on
	his
consul-
tants
	and contractors
	as
to their
experience.
	How
	is
he
to
assess
competence
	in
health and
safety issues? In the
absence
	of recog-
nised qualifications
	is
he expected
	to
investigate consultants’ and
contractors’ track records? Will
clients
be
prompted
	to
seek con-

	in

in the

	the consultant

	that he
is

	not only

It
has been advocated
that the requirement upon the client to ensure competence should
be either removed or commuted to a less onerous requirement.

-- 235 of 360 --

200 Impact on Construction Activites
(iii) When should the appointments be made? The proposed Regu-
lations provide that appointments should be made at the appropri-
ate time. The ACP suggests that “‘appropriate time” means as
early as possible during the planning of the project allowing
enough time for both persons appointed to carry out their duties
effectively. This is likely to vary with the procurement route fol-
lowed. A client may not have the necessary understanding of the
various Stages of the project to enable decisions as to the timing of
the appointment to be taken with confidence.
(4) The extent of the client’s duty under draft Regulation 6: Draft Regu-
lation 6 provides that the client is to ‘‘ensure, so far as is practicable, that
tenders make adequate financial provision and that sufficient time is
allowed for everyone working on the project to comply with their statutory
duties”. This is extremely wide and has no basis in the underlying
Directive. The provision is likely to be of particular concern to lay clients.
Whilst the client can check that the tender includes provision for health
and safety requirements, how is he to determine that such provision is
adequate without the assistance of professional advice? What action is the
client supposed to take if he thinks that the tender is too low or the
programme too tight? This provision is likely to impact on the way in which
projects are assessed for viability as a result of increased project costs
without any guarantee of improvement in contractor’s health and safety
procedures which are dependent ultimately on the contractor’s manage-
ment skills and technical competence.

	The requirement
on

to

	the

	of the

	of

draft

	considerably

Another
	factor to be aware of
is the potential impact of the health and
safety file for a project. It is
intended that this will be added to and updated
during the life of the project. The ACP envisages that the file should

-- 237 of 360 --

202 Impact on Construction Activites
contain information on the design of the structure, the design “‘as-built’’,
information provided by the client to the Planning Supervisor or gathered
by the Planning Supervisor in the preparation of the health and safety plan
and information which will assist persons carrying out construction work
on the structure at any time after completion of the project.
Again, this is likely to impact on project costs and timing since greater
emphasis will need to be placed on the preparation of “‘as-built’’ rather
than ‘“‘record”’ drawings. Preparation of ‘“‘as-built”” drawings will need to be
focused on more closely during construction with a view to delivery of a
complete package at practical completion of the project if this information
is to be available within the timescale envisaged by the proposed Regula-
tions. The existence of a health and safety file may impact also on the
commercial value of a client’s investment and it may well be that in the
future prospective tenants and purchasers of buildings will request copies
of the health and safety file in addition to the usual package of collateral
warranty agreements.
The consultation period for the proposed Regulations and ACP ended
on 29 January 1993 and has been followed by various discussions between
the HSE and industry bodies as part of a comprehensive review of com-
ments received from various sectors of the industry. The proposed Regula-
tions were due to come into force on 1 January 1994 but following
Significant pressure from the industry it is understood that the proposed
Regulations are currently being revised by the HSE. Recent indications are
that revised Regulations will not be available prior to December 1993 and
therefore, it would seem certain that implementation of the proposed
Regulations will be delayed. Current predictions are that a six month or
even a twelve month delay period is likely.
Emerging and associated trends
General
Whilst
	it is
possible
	to
draw upon
	the recent
	developments
	in
statutory
control of health
and
safety of
construction
	sites
and
activities
as a
useful
insight into the
nature of the
challenge facing the
construction industry,
a

	picture can
	only be gained by considering these with identified
associated trends.
Emerging
trends
from
recent
developments
	in statutory controls
From consideration of the recent and proposed developments in statutory
control in the health and safety arena, the following trends can be seen:

-- 238 of 360 --

Construction sites 203
— European developments play a major part in influencing (and some
have observed, dictating) the agenda for progress in health and safety
law and standards in common with the wider area of environmental
protection.
— Pro-active legislation appears to be still the order of the day although in
the past pro-active provisions of a voluntary nature have not been well
observed or utilised.?4
— There is a clear shift in emphasis in the regulation of commercial
activity in the health and safety context from the “work face” of the
industry to the corporate management.
— Despite moves towards consolidation of legislation recently exemplified
by the Clean Air Act 1993 and in the wider environmental context in
the Environmental Protection Act 1990, there is no single source of
legislation in either the health and safety or the wider environmental
fields. This is illustrative of the ad hoc response of government to social
concern. This is also borne out by the absence of a manual consolidat-
ing formal guidance for the industry in either area?> which impacts on
the effectiveness of training and education in the industry.
— There is an increasing focus on site organisation and management in
addition to regulation of specific high risk activities increasing the
potential exposure of all sectors of the construction industry to criminal
and civil liability.

Europe,?5 the media and the continued activities of lobby groups suggest
that calls for increased controls on the construction industry will continue at
their current pace. Investors’ and shareholders’ attitudes are also being
increasingly influenced by health and safety and environment concerns.
Financial performance of a company is no longer judged simply on its
balance sheet but also in terms of health and safety and environmental
performance illustrated in the growing demand for corporate disclosure and
accountability. Demand for such information by shareholders and investors
is not altogether altruistic. The threat of potential liabilities and the spectre
of more stringent regulation will impact on a company’s financial performance.

Construction sites 209
site management, will be dynamic and will be a challenge for the con-
struction industry in the 1990s. Despite Government moves to de-
regulate and the purported relaxation on the part of the HSE, there is a
compelling argument for the construction industry to improve its track
record in both the health and safety arena and the wider environmental
context. There is much evidence to suggest that companies with effective
health and safety policies and management will be better able to accom-
modate the wider demands of environmental legislation in view of the
significant overlap in these areas. The dominance of Europe in influ-
encing legislative activity and the clear commitment of the EC to health
and safety and environmental improvement increases the likelihood of
further legislative activity and with it new risks and liabilities for all
sectors of the construction industry.
To date the construction industry’s initiatives appear inadequate to meet
this challenge. There is a need for greater awareness of, and renewed
initiatives to meet, the practical and legal difficulties resulting from legisla-
tive imposition and commercial pressure. Raising the construction indus-
try’s health and safety and environment consciousness is a formidable task.
Self-regulatory measures undertaken by the construction industry in the
past have been singularly unsuccessful and used as evidence to support
calls for legislative imposition. Perhaps now is the time for the construction
industry to look again for new self-regulatory measures.
One

9 Risk assessment and environmental audit for
contaminated land
Peter J Young
Introduction
From a purely physical standpoint, the environment has been subject to
relatively constant risk from soil and water contamination over the years, but
our perception of this risk has changed recently as a result of well publicised
incidents both in the UK and overseas (Loscoe (UK), Lekkekerk (The
Netherlands), Love Canal (USA) and more recently, Lumsden Road in
Portsmouth). However, the consequences of damage to the environment are
changing, particularly in financial terms, and this is making landowners and
others with a financial interest in land look more closely at the magnitude of
risk associated with contaminated land and groundwater.
Contaminated
	land can
vary
from
the
most
visually
obvious and
intrusive
derelict industrial
works
	to
apparently
clean
and
pleasant
fields
supporting
healthy
vegetation.
Appearances
	are
often
deceptive and cosmetic
improve-
ment
	of
badly
contaminated
	land has
caused
many
	developers
to
underesti-
mate
	the
problems
	that
lie
beneath
	when
	acquiring such
land.
	Indeed
	the
early practice of
visual
improvements
	to
contaminated
	sites
has too often
diverted attention
	away from
	the
fundamental problems
	of
environmental
damage
	of, for
example,
water
resources,
	which remains beneath
	the
green
sward
	of
a
topsoil
	and
	re-seeding
	improvement. The importance
	of
such
environmental
	impacts,
and
the
liability
arising
from environmental
	impair-
ment,
	is
changing
	rapidly
due
to
the
introduction
	of
new
	legislation,
	even
though
there
may
be
no
change
in the
degree
of
contamination
present.
This
paper introduces
	the
concept
	of
environmental
	risk
and describes
an
approach
for
determining whether hazards
exist in a
site,
whether
those
hazards pose
a
risk to
the
environment, what
the likelihood
	is
of that
risk
occurring, and what
	the
	consequences may
	be
	for the
	landowner and
regulatory authority.
Defining and identifying contaminated land
The introduction of the
Environmental Protection Act
in 1990 has been the
principal driving force behind changes in attitude towards contaminated
land. In the 1980s contamination in soil and groundwater was merely one
213

-- 249 of 360 --

214 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
additional factor to be costed for in the purchase of land for redevelop-
ment, and investigation was frequently patchy or non-existent.
Occasional problems arose with such attitudes which led to major addi-
tional remedial works when difficult or extensive contamination was dis-
covered post-purchase. One of the most extreme examples of this is
described in a paper by Sury and Slingsby,! where low level radioactivity
was found on a site purchased for housing redevelopment. It is understood
that the costs of dealing with the contaminated soil was of the order of £10
million, from a site of total land area of some 12 hectares.
This example, and many others described to the House of Commons
Environment Committee in 1989, led to the final Committee report? on
Contaminated Land being published in 1990, with recommendations to
establish registers of contaminated land, such that the extent of the prob-
lem could be better understood, and buyers of land -would have early
warning of such problems.
The Government response? to the report accepted that a register of
land which may be contaminated should be compiled. It did not believe
that it would be practical to compile a register of actual contamination,
however, because of the difficulty of establishing that actual contamina-
tion exists. A policy decision on progressing some form of register is
currently the subject of a wide-ranging Government review. Estimates of
the amount of potentially contaminated land that exist in the UK range
from 27,000 hectares‘ through to 240,000 hectares.5 Such large discrepan-
cies are a function of definition, as some estimates exclude operational
industrial sites, or sites of less than one hectare in extent. For example,
there are about 19,000 petrol filling station sites in the UK at present, and
if each one is assumed to be potentially contaminated as a result of fuel
leakage, then this accounts for about 7,000 hectares. It is likely that only a
limited proportion of such sites are actually contaminated. There is also a
blurring of meaning between derelict and contaminated land. The two
have distinct definitions.

Risk assessment and audit 215
Contaminated land is not the same as derelict land; nor is the term
contaminated land applied in the strict scientific sense of indicating the
presence of an introduced substance which is harmful. If the substance is
harmful, it is a “‘pollutant’’.
The Department of the Environment (DoE) defines contaminated land
as ‘“‘land which represents an actual or potential hazard to health or the
environment as a result of current or previous use’. Contrast this with
derelict land which is “‘land so damaged by past activities that it is in-
capable of beneficial use without treatment”. Clearly the two are indepen-
dent. A worked out limestone quarry may be derelict but will not be
contaminated. In contrast many established chemical factories will be con-
taminated but not derelict. Contaminated land poses a potential threat to
the environment, but derelict land is a threat to future development, al-
though it may be aesthetically unattractive as well.
The principal types and sources of contaminated land are listed below:
Types of contaminant
— Toxic or carcinogenic chemicals (e.g. cyanide, benzene);
— Toxic or phytotoxic metals (e.g. lead, cadmium, zinc);
— Organic contaminants (e.g. oils, solvents, phenols);
— Corrosive substances (e.g. acids, sulphates);
— Flammable, toxic or asphyxiating gases (e.g. methane, hydrogen sul-
phide, carbon dioxide);
— Combustible material;
— Asbestos; and
— Radioactive substances.
Causes of contamination
— Leaks and spillages from tanks and pipes;
—
	Storage and
transport of
raw materials
or
intermediate products;
—
	Storage and
transport of
waste materials;
—
	Disposal
of
waste materials on
or
adjacent
to
the
site;
— Stack emissions; and
— Demolition of buildings which have contained contaminating material
(e.g.
asbestos lagging,
impregnated brickwork).
Sources of contamination
— Mining and extractive industries;
— Iron and steel works;

-- 251 of 360 --

216 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
— Metal treatment and finishing;
— Chemical industries;
— Scrap yards;
— Fragmentation plants;
— Paint and dyestuff industries;
— Sewage works and farms;
— Railway land, especially large sidings and depots;
— Application of sewage sludge to agricultural land;
— Fly-tipping;
— Cable-burning;
— Bonfires;
— Dockyards and dock basins;
— Petrol stations and refineries;
— Waste disposal sites;
— Power stations; and
— Deposition of air pollutants.

information
	on

The Role of the NRA
As well as catering for UK domestic needs, recent UK water legislation,
including the Water Act 1989 and the Water Resources Act 1991,
embodies relevant European legislation such as the EC Groundwater
Directive (80/68/EC) and has provision for granting the National Rivers
Authority (NRA) powers to carry out works and operations to rectify
water pollution and to recover the costs. These powers, similar to those
which were available under the Control of Pollution Act 1974, are
contained in section 161 of the Water Resources Act.
The rate at which contaminants enter the groundwater system depends
upon the nature of the geology and the hydrogeological properties of the
aquifer. The NRA has statutory responsibilities for the protection of
groundwater and has issued its policy and practice for the protection of
groundwater!° which set out its objectives and the way in which it seeks to
proceed with aquifer protection.
An important part of the NRA’s concept of aquifer protection is that of
classifying groundwater vulnerability. The concept recognises that the risk
of pollution from a given activity is greater in some hydrogeological,
geological and soil situations than others. Using these criteria to classify
vulnerability and by preventing or controlling certain activities in the most
vulnerable areas, protection will be concentrated in the areas which need it
most. This concept could be of particular relevance to contaminated land
studies in that once the NRA has published vulnerability maps for an area,
a tool is available to assist in desk-based risk assessment for contaminated
land. It is understood that a start has been made in the preparation of these
maps but coverage of the whole of England and Wales will not be available
until some time in the future.
In
their
consultation
document
	the
NRA
	set
out
very
clearly
their inten-
tion
to
pursue remedial
	action
	wherever
	possible
	in
respect
	of
contami-
nated
	land.'°
	They
	are also
	seeking
	to
	ensure
	that
	they
	are
	properly
consulted
	at
the
planning
stage of
any
development
	and
to
exercise
all
the
powers
	available
to
them
in
cleaning up widespread contamination,
	‘“hav-

to local
circumstances and
available

	standards

	also

applied to groundwater and surface water resources which are abstracted

Use of guidance
When using the guidance quoted it is worth bearing in mind that large
variations exist between different guidance quoted by different organisa-
tions in the same country, and between the standards set by different
European and national governments. Two examples are chosen for total
lead and total sulfur to give an indication of the wide range which exists in
the published guidance. For example, the New Jersey concentration used
for total lead to give rise to a clean-up of a site is 100 mg/kg, and is
therefore twenty times less than that set by the ICRCL of 2,000 mg/kg for
contaminated land to be used for open space. Similarly the total sulfur
concentration regarded as background in Holland is only 2 mg/kg although
action is not recommended until 200 mg/kg. This is still considerably below
the threshold levels of 5,000 and 20,000 mg/kg adopted by the UK in the
ICRCL guidance. The conclusion to be drawn from these comparisons is
that the actual risks and damage to the environment from specific con-
centrations of contaminants will vary according to the circumstances of the
site and remain poorly understood. There is therefore no substitute for
professional and experienced site specific risk assessment of the implica-
tions of measured contamination when making decisions about the need
for clean-up and the appropriate measures to be taken.
It is
essential that
when
	evaluating
contamination more
	consideration
	of
the
problem
is
given than
a
simple
comparison
against
published
guidelines,
which
	may
	be
	neither
	appropriate
	nor suitable
	for
the
	individual
	site
in
question.
	It is
also
worth
	remembering
	that the
most
	relevant
	legislation
makes
	specific
reference
	to
the
need
	to
assess
	properly
	contaminated
	land
and
its
implications
	for
the
environment,
	but
does
	not
specifically
	require
that
assessment
	or
clean-up
is
achieved
to
published
guidelines.
Stage 1 assessment: desk study
Investigation should take
a
phased approach
	to
understanding
	the history
of
a site
and
the extent of
all
forms
of
contamination.
	By
applying
a
staged
approach,
	it is
likely that the
	most
	cost-effective investigations can be
designed and implemented appropriate
	to the level of
risks
and hazards
identified. It
should be noted, however,
	that this
approach requires
a
rea-
sonable time period and cannot be compressed
	into a
matter of days.
A thorough desk study will establish the need for further investigation,
and is essential to designing the most appropriate and cost effective site

-- 255 of 360 --

220 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
investigation. Collation and interpretation of the information gathered will
provide some indication of the likely nature of contamination, such as
whether the soil contains heavy metals or whether gas is being generated
within the site, and possibly the extent of contamination across the site.
Knowledge of the nature of contaminating materials, the layout and history
of the site and the local environmental conditions, including site geology,
will enable the most appropriate investigation strategy to be chosen.
Site history
Much of the effort involved in investigating the history of a site is in
knowing how and where to find the appropriate information. Sources of
site information include:
— Maps: Ordnance Survey current and historical, geological survey maps
and town plans.
— Statutory authorities: District and Regional Authorities (or Island
Councils), River Purification Boards, Health and Safety Executive.
— Trade information from directories, trade associations.
— Photographic records, particularly aerial photographs.
— Technical data from published literature.
-
	Anecdotal
	information,
	for
example
	from
local
studies,
libraries, local
residents,
newspapers.

Contamination indicators: Any further evidence of contamination should

monitoring or sampling at this stage generally to identify hazards.
Site safety: To identify any potential site hazards. Power cables, under-
ground services, underground voids including mine shafts and tanks, unsafe
buildings and structures, water or liquid bodies and hazardous materials on
the surface such as asbestos must be noted.

Hazard ranking
Hazard

	can

	for

	legislation on existing landholdings,
through
	the ranking of alternative
	sites for land transactions,
	to the pri-
oritisation of grants and remediation
	effort.
	Often
	it is

	to

	a large number of land-

result initial hazard ranking systems based on

-- 257 of 360 --

222 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
readily accessible data are of considerable benefit in prioritising alternative
sites. Obviously such simplified systems have severe limitations, but if ap-
plied rigorously and with adequate professional expertise, they can form an
important part in the decision-making process for acquisition and divesti-
ture of land, and the application of remediation funds.
One simple system which has been used with considerable effect in the
ranking of 35 landholdings owned by Scottish Enterprise, is based on the
concept of combining the sensitivity of a site with its contamination poten-
tial to define environmental risk. It is now being combined with economic
assessment techniques to define a national remediation strategy for Scot-
land. Site sensitivity is defined from individual factors relating to the loca-
tion and geology of the site. In addition account can be taken of adjacent
landuses and the sensitivity of site neighbours. In its simplest form publicly
available data from geology and Ordnance Survey maps can be used to
derive a score or sensitivity analysis from surface water, geology and
groundwater considerations.

Stage 2 assessment:
	site
investigation
The following aspects should be considered
in

soil
contamination, gas migration, groundwater, and surface water con-
tamination. The importance of each aspect will of course depend upon the

nature of the proposed end use and on the contamination present. The
investigation must be flexible to react to conditions encountered on site.
Soil contamination
In planning a site investigation a representative soil sampling strategy must
be designed taking into account the information obtained in the desk study
as well as allowing for adequate coverage of the site. Various guidance for
carrying out site investigations is provided in the British Standards Institu-
tion (BSI) Codes of Practice!2-13 and by other agencies.!4 The soil sampling
strategy should be designed to:
—
	assess
areas
most
likely
to
be
contaminated
	using
historical data;
—
	locate the
most
contaminated
	areas
(hot
spots);
—
	adapt
	the
	planned sampling
	strategy
	as
	the
	investigation
	progresses
depending
on
conditions
found
during
the
site
investigation;
—
	apply
a
statistically
	valid
	sampling
	framework
	to
cover
	the
site as
a
whole.
Samples
	should be
	selected
	from
	areas of
likely
contamination
	from
visual
	and
	olfactory
	evidence and
	to
give
a
good
	indication
	of the con-
tamination
	profiles.
	The
	skill
of the

	is
important
	in
the

	emphasis
	should always be
placed
	upon.
	obtaining
sufficient

whilst
in

Simple
on-site
monitoring
techniques
can
assist this
selection process.

To assess whether or not gas will be a hazard to the development of a
site, the following points will require consideration and investigation:
— existing composition and pressure of gas within the site boundary;
— extent of gas migration from the site;
— potential of organic materials for future degradation and subsequent
gas production; and
— volume of gas in the soil atmosphere.
Waste Management Plan No. 27, see reference 8, provides guidance on a
number of issues relating to landfill gas including survey and investigation
techniques.
Surface water and groundwater
In order to assess the impact of a site on receiving watercourses, an under-
standing of the local hydrology is essential. The quality of surface waters may
be affected by a number of factors including site topography, geology or the
nature of contamination. Contamination of groundwater may arise as a
result of materials leaching from the base of the site into the water table.
Information
on surface
water
contamination
may
be
gathered through
on-
site
sampling and
flow
gauging
	of
watercourses.
	An
	assessment
	of
ground-
water
	quality involves
	a
more
	detailed investigation including
	borehole
construction,
water
sampling and
analysis.
Groundwater
velocity,
quality
and
direction of
flow,
and
also
the
flux
and
rate
of
contaminants
	leaching
from
the
base
of the
site
govern
the
impact
on
an
underlying
aquifer.
Analysis
Analysis
of
all
samples
collected on
site
should be
undertaken by
an
experi-
enced and
	approved
	laboratory.
	For
	soil
	and water
	samples
	a
phased
approach
	to
analysis
is
often
most
	suitable
	as this
can
	avoid
	unnecessaryexpenditure.
The
results
of the
initial
analysis give an
indication of the
type
and degree
of
contamination
	present and
allow
more
	selective analysis of
the
remaining samples.
Safety on site
It is
essential
	when
	carrying out
	site
	investigations that
	adequate and
appropriate precautions
are
taken
to
ensure the safety of
all
those involved
in the site
work. Safety procedures for site
workers should be exhaustive
where the likely risks are poorly understood. The appropriate legislation

-- 262 of 360 --

Risk assessment and audit 227
which may apply is extensive, incorporating the general provisions of the
Health and Safety at Work etc Act 1974. Safety considerations of hazards
should include:
— protective clothing;
— hygiene;
— gas hazards;
— work in excavations;
— fire hazards;
— electrical hazards; and
— underground hazards.
Data interpretation and risk assessment
Once a site investigation has been carried out the significance of any
contamination identified must be assessed in terms of the proposed use of
the site. The DoE sets out a number of questions which should be
answered following investigations. Information on ‘“‘background” levels of
contaminants together with the existing guidelines can be useful in
assessing whether contaminants are present in sufficient concentrations to
present hazards to the targets identified. However, many investigations are
focused too narrowly and do not address all the risks which the
contamination represents to the environment and planned land use. If the
need for clean-up and potential liability is to be correctly assessed, more
than the immediate health risks must be addressed. Whether on the basis
of a desk study or site investigation eight separate issues must be
considered. These are briefly described in the following paragraphs.
Health
	effects:
Exposure
	to
contaminants
	can give
rise to
health
problems
through
	ingestion (eg
contaminated
	food
	or
water), inhalation (eg
toxic
gases
	or
dust,
including asbestos),
	or
direct skin
contact with
irritants or
harmful
chemicals.
Pollution
	of
water:
	Supplied drinking water
	may become contaminated
	if
water pipes pass
	through
	soils
	containing organic
	compounds
	such
	as
phenol,
	or
	soluble metal
	compounds. Contamination
	of
groundwater
	or
surface waters can
arise
from leaching of rainwater through contaminated
land.
	Breaches
	in
buried tanks and pipework may
	release
	contaminated
water and liquids into the ground. Several surface water pollution incidents
have resulted from removal of hardstanding over contaminated ground,
and subsequent leaching or
washing of contaminants whilst rehabilitation
work is in progress.

-- 263 of 360 --

228 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
Phytotoxicity: Substances which are harmful to plant growth are termed
phytotoxic. Phytotoxicity is particularly associated with certain metals
(copper, nickel, zinc) but other substances including boron, oils, coal tars,
phenols and sulphate also exhibit phytotoxic effects. Generally these
effects occur at concentrations which are not toxic to humans. Carbon
dioxide is directly phytotoxic and together with methane can be indirectly
toxic to plants through depletion of oxygen levels in the soil.
Chemical attack: Conditions which may lead to chemical attack of building
and service materials include sulphate attack on concrete and attack of
plastic materials by phenolic compounds. Chloride or extremes of pH may
also present structural problems, particularly with metal structures. Corro-
sion of underwater pipework can lead to ingress of potentially toxic fluids
into water supplies, and sulphate attack on concrete can lead to structural
failure in buildings.
Fires: Fires may be propagated underground if material of a suitable
calorific value is present together with an ignition source and a sufficient
supply of oxygen. Examples include ground containing coal or coal dust, oil
shales, oils and domestic waste. Underground fires are very difficult to
extinguish and the principal hazards include the emission of toxic gases and
subsidence into void spaces caused by the fire.
Explosions: Flammable gases, for example methane produced by the
degradation of organic material in the absence of oxygen, may form explo-
sive mixtures if they accumulate in confined spaces under buildings, in
service ducts, or other enclosed spaces. Any process which generates a
spark can trigger an explosion if an explosive gas mixture is present.
Asphyxiation:
Where
	degradation
of
organic materials
occurs,
for
example
at landfill sites,
concentrations
	of
carbon
	dioxide and
	other gases
	may
exclude
	oxygen from confined spaces resulting
	in an
	asphyxiating
atmosphere.
Odour
problems: These may
be associated with
landfills,
and
may
also
be
a
problem
	in
land
contaminated
	with organic substances such
	as
coal
tars.
Although odour problems do not necessarily represent
	a
	particularly
hazardous
	situation, the
nuisance imposed
can often be
difficult to
control
and even prevent occupation of
developments
	on the land.
Having carried out a risk assessment according to each of the above
criteria, and making use of the guidelines discussed earlier, it
may be

-- 264 of 360 --

Risk assessment and audit 229
possible to agree suitable trigger levels for the site in question. Such trigger
levels for clean-up must address all the risks that contamination and its
treatment will represent. For example, if a remedial option involves the
removal of contaminated soils to a licensed disposal site, the future pres-
ence of contamination at that site and the noise, dust and traffic associated
with the transport of contaminated soil should all be considered. Such a
broader approach will advantage the remedial method which destroys the
contaminant rather than just moving it from place to place.
Putting risk and hazard in perspective
In many cases the interpretation and presentation of the information on
contamination gained from the desk study and site investigation, will be
required to meet a diverse range of interested parties. These parties will
have to take regard of contamination issues as a critical input to their own
decision-making. Firstly the existing liabilities of the current landowner
need to be defined. The implications of the contamination identified on the
value and future use of the land should be clear, and the remediation
options, if required, specified in a way which allows the value of the land to
be re-appraised. Liabilities may also arise with respect to responsibilities to
the statutory authorities such as the planning authority, the environmental
health department and the NRA. For example, the contaminated run-off
from a derelict factory site can be regarded as an unconsented discharge
and as such be in contravention of the relevant water legislation.
Increasingly the contamination assessment will have been carried out as
part of the process of sale or redevelopment of land involving financial
investment. Banks and financial institutions will have regard to contamin-
ation in assessing the security of their loans or the acceptability of the
financing package proposed for the redevelopment of the site. Insurers and
investors of companies involved in land transactions will also need to be
satisfied with respect to the implications of contamination. It may be
appropriate to seek specific site-based environmental insurance for the
land, which will only be forthcoming if the work has been carried out and
presented to the satisfaction of the insurer. There may be specific corporate
or foreign based standards which must be met, particularly when attracting
inward investment or selling the land to multi-national or foreign based
companies. Under such circumstances specific legal agreements may be
required and lawyers may be involved in the land transaction process.
Finally, the local community has a clear right to be protected from any
contamination presenting a public health hazard. It is increasingly likely
that any contamination report prepared on behalf of a landowner will
immediately or subsequently come into the public domain. This is a

-- 265 of 360 --

230 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
particularly important issue when discovering contamination which was not
perhaps widely perceived to be present or which draws attention to lia-
bilities or third party responsibilities with financial consequences. Given
this wide range of purposes to which the report on contamination is put, it
is clear that the quality and content of any contaminated land risk assess-
ment will come under much greater scrutiny than has hitherto been the
case.
Case Study 1: service station
This case study describes the development of a service station on an
actively gassing, completed industrial landfill.
Investigation and impact prediction
In 1984 the Department of Transport commissioned a preliminary inves-
tigation of the site, comprising an extensive desk study and limited field
works. The area previously consisted of a gently domed hill made up of
natural deposits of sand and gravel which was extracted by 1974, leaving a
series of water-filled lakes and random mounds of overburden. Reclam-
ation of this area began in the 1970s with the construction of an adjacent
motorway and the shaping of a recreational lake. The remaining space
was used for landfilling with industrial wastes between 1978 and 1984.
The final restoration profile was similar to the ground levels pertaining
prior to mineral extraction at the site, and the waste covered with up to
two metres of inert fill. Waste thicknesses of between 4 and 20 metres
were predicted in the desk study and proved in subsequent field investiga-
tions. A detailed site investigation was undertaken to establish design
parameters for foundations, gas control systems and settlement reduction
techniques.
The
	investigation involved construction
	of
a
grid of
boreholes
	across
the
	site.
	Boreholes were
	completed
	with multi-point
	gas
	samplers
	to
monitor
	gas
pressure and composition
	at
different
	depths
	in
the
	sameborehole.
	The

	investigation

	slightly
	positive gas
pressures
	throughout

	lower than have been mea-

Trace gas analyses were carried out on samples of gas trapped in Tenax
tubes, with a view to assessing corrosiveness, toxicity and odour aspects of
the gas.
Fifteen halogenated and two sulphonated compounds were detected in the
analysis, and the likely amounts of chlorine, fluorine and sulfur which could
convert to corrosive acids were calculated (see below). The results showed
that the gas at this site was markedly less corrosive than is observed at
domestic waste sites.

Toxic compounds, such as benzene, were found in the analysis. However,
dilution of the landfill gas to below its lower explosive limit would ensure
that all toxic trace components are diluted below their thresholds of tox-
icity and no other air quality standards would be breached.
Odorous
	trace

	detected
	in

	alkyl

	and

	Consideration
	of

	that

1,500-fold
	would
	be required
	to

	of these

	On

	it
was evident
	that

gas
must be controlled.
Gas protection measures
The
gas control system was designed
to
achieve the following objectives:
(a)
	To eliminate the risk of explosion, flammability,
toxicity,
asphyxiation
and odours due
to the presence of landfill gas at the
site.
(b) To completely exclude methane from
	all buildings, accessible
services, ducts and enclosed spaces.

-- 267 of 360 --

232 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
(c) To use a set of control systems which are the simplest and require the
least maintenance to achieve the above two objectives.
(d) To design sufficient capacity and duplication of gas control methods
to facilitate successful operation throughout the remaining gas
producing lifetime of the site for all possible gas emission rates.
(e) To prevent unnecessary air ingress to minimise risks of underground
combustion.
In order to achieve these objectives the following principles were applied
to the detailed gas control system:
(i) Two levels of control exist; the area supporting the buildings or
similar structures have more stringent controls than the remainder of
the site (i.e. parking and landscaped areas).
(ii) Buildings have a vented void space beneath all floors and special
service entries to prevent gas entry in addition to an active gas ex-
traction system in the surrounding fill. Permanent continuous gas
monitors with an alarm system are installed to demonstrate that the
system is excluding gas in accordance with the design criteria.
(iii) The car parking and other hardstanding areas require a collection
system which can control gas emissions over the entire surface area.
In practice this means maintaining a system of active gas extraction
which prevents a positive pressure of gas beneath the low
permeability layer from being sustained.
(iv) The landscaped, picnic and other vegetated areas require sufficient
gas control to prevent significant surface gas emissions, to keep all
vegetation healthy and prevent any detectable odour from the gas.
Specific gas control measures are as follows:
Amenity
	building:
The
	highest
level
of control was applied
to
this
area.
	It
comprises
	the use of
	two permeability
	contrasts
	below
	the structure.
Pumped
	extraction
	from
	horizontal
	pipework
	laid in
a
granular blanket
below
the clay
capping
layer
provides
the
primary
protection.
A
ventilated
sub-floor void provides secondary
	protection, with
	a
membrane
	incorpor-
ated
in the
concrete
floor slab.
Gas monitors
installed in the sub-floor voidin
confined
	areas within the
amenity
building provide warning
	of
any
gas
ingress,
	although
	this is
essentially
	a
reassurance measure. Monitors
	are
linked
	to a
staged alarm system which
	activates a series of
procedures
relating to the level of gas detected.
Refuelling area: The refuelling area, consisting of petrol and diesel fore-
courts and payment kiosk is in an area of only approximately 8m of
fill.

-- 268 of 360 --

Risk assessment and audit 233
Consequently a philosophy of isolation was adopted. This consisted of
complete removal of all refuse. The base and sides of the excavation were
then lined with clay and the remaining hole infilled with a silty fine sand.
Clay capping was placed over the sand fill. As a further back up a vertical
well system connected to an independent ring main also surrounds the fuel
area, although this is not pumped routinely.
Parking and recreation areas: A single level of control was applied to these
areas, using the permeability contrast of a granular collection layer over-
lain by clay capping, coupled with gas extraction from horizontal pipework
laid in the granular layer.
Site sealing layers: In the area of the site where refuse remained, capping
material was removed and stockpiled. A 500mm clean granular layer was
applied. This reduced the thickness of the granular blanket to approx-
imately 200mm. A further 200mm granular material was overlaid to pro-
vide a transmission layer for gas. This in turn was overlain by a geotextile
separator underlying 500mm of compacted clay beneath a polythene mem-
brane. A blinding layer of sand was placed over the plastic layer, and site
capping material restored to an approximate thickness of 800mm.
Gas
collection
from
the
granular transmission
layer
is
accomplished
from
a
network
	of
horizontal
	pipes. All
collected gas
is
burnt
in
a
muffle
flare.
The
site
is
now
	operational.
Case Study
2:
existing
housing
on gassing
landfill
This
	case
	study describes
	the
successful
	remediation
	of
a
housing
	estate
found
	to
be constructed
	on
	a
former domestic
	waste
	landfill
	which
	was
giving
rise to
significant
quantities of
landfill gas.
The
interior
and
exterior
works were
	carried out
in
order
to
render
	all
of the
houses
	safe
and
sale-
able with
minimum
	disruption
to
the residents.
The work
is
representative
of
a
number
	of
examples where Aspinwall
has
been required
to
investigate
and remediate
	private
housing developments
	constructed before such
de-
velopments were prohibited due
to
the
landfill
gas
hazard.
Investigation and impact prediction
An
	initial
	desk study identified residential
	development
	situated on and
adjacent
	to a
	former
	landfill in
South East England. The subsequent
investigation involved the installation of gas monitoring boreholes and
piezometers, the excavation of trial pits and the collection and analysis of
waste samples. The boreholes and piezometers were monitored over an

-- 269 of 360 --

234 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
eight week period and gas samples collected for laboratory analysis. The
results were used to fully assess the gas regime at the site and define risk to
the houses.
Degradable wastes approximately 2.0m thick were identified beneath
certain houses within the limits of the permitted landfill. The degradable
wastes were overlain by a layer of inert clayey fill. Only 1.0m of the degrad-
able waste was situated above the perched water table. Analysis of waste
samples showed that about one third of the organic material remained for
degradation and therefore the presence of flammable gas should be antici-
pated to persist for a considerable period of time.
Concentrations of methane and carbon dioxide were sufficiently high to
represent a risk to the properties above with up to 70% methane detected,
although gas pressures were negligible indicating a slow rate of gas
production. Gas migration was also identified beyond the fill material and
the number of houses requiring remediation were identified. Remediation
works were designed on the basis of minimum disruption to occupiers,
whilst ensuring a multi-barrier approach to the protection against gas
ingress to each property.
Remediation
The remedial works were implemented on an individual property basis and
consisted of the installation of passive venting systems and treatment of the
upper surface of the groundfloor slab of properties to prevent gas ingress,
together with sealing works around service entries. Within the properties a
detailed house to house survey using a portable flame ionisation detector
identified a variety of defects such as cracking of floor slabs, voids around
service entries and gaps between vertical walls and the floor slab. These
defects were sealed comprehensively and the effectiveness of the seals
confirmed by subsequent house surveys for gas.
In
order
to
intercept gas
which
could potentially
accumulate beneath
thefloor slabs, a
programme
	of installing
vent trenches
	immediately
	adjacent
to
the sides of the
houses was undertaken. According
	to
the location the
venting
areas
could be
filled
with
10mm
	no
fines
gravel
and
left as
a
gravel
path
or
area, or
covered
	with paviors.
Alternatively
	a series of
pipe
workattached
	to
the
houses venting
at
roof
level
was used where more perma-nent covering of the trenches was required
	or
beneath individual exten-
sions such
as
porches.
The work was
all
carried out under
strict
supervision and using
a
variety
of small mobile plant to
minimise the contract time
at
each property and
general disruption to the residents. Careful attention to reinstatement
work, including paving, small extensions and landscaping ensured that very

-- 270 of 360 --

Risk assessment and audit 235
soon after completion no obvious signs of the work remained. This
approach combined with a vigorous public relations exercise to keep all
residents fully appraised of progress led to little publicity of the work and
only a temporary adverse effect on property prices. To the best of our
knowledge the houses are not only safe and in a better condition than
before the risk from landfill gas was identified, but they are also saleable
and insurable in the normal way.
Conclusions
In conclusion it is clear that the importance of applying professional experi-
ence in evaluating the consequences of contaminated land has never been
more important. The implications of wrongly assessing contamination have
increased rapidly and can lead to major financial decisions which must be
based on a rigorous and consistent assessment of risk. In providing advice,
it should always be remembered that a practical approach and intelligent
appraisal should characterise the assessment, and not the dogmatic applica-
tion of guidelines which are not intended to be legal and definitive stan-
dards. In many instances the environmental significance of potential
contaminants is poorly understood. Practical and realistic advice given to
developers, owners and occupiers will maintain confidence whilst allowing
the public’s interest to be protected. It is essential that the issue of contami-
nated land is dealt with properly and thoroughly to demonstrate that con-
tamination need not blight the potential use and future value of land.
In this way an increased awareness of the problem of contaminated land
can be used to encourage the redevelopment and regeneration of former
industrial areas, in preference to the use of more and more land for urban
development. It must be considered preferable to ensure that the legacy of
past activities is progressively cleaned up and dealt with to leave a dimin-
ished risk and impairment of the environment to future generations.
Furthermore a realistic and thorough assessment of contamination is the
only way to prevent blight of land and ensure that land values are not
unreasonably affected by fears arising from potential contamination. The
first step to achieving these objectives must be the thorough and consistent
review, investigation and assessment of contaminated land to give the right
information to meet all interested parties’ interests and to plan sensible,
cost-effective and lasting remediation strategies.
