10 Environmental management systems and
construction
John Barber
Introduction
‘Fog everywhere. Fog up the river, where it flows among green aits and
meadows; fog down the river, where it rolls defiled among the tiers of shipping,
and the waterside pollution of a great (and dirty) city. Fog on the Essex
marshes, fog on the Kentish heights . . . Fog in the eyes and throats of ancient
Greenwich pensioners, wheezing by the firesides of their wards; fog in the stem
and bowl of the afternoon pipe of the wrathful skipper, down in his close cabin;
fog cruelly pinching the toes and fingers of his shivering little ’prentice boy on
deck.”
This vista of swirling fog appears in the opening paragraphs of Bleak
House. Charles Dickens used the scene as a metaphor to convey the murki-
ness of the Chancery proceedings in Jarndyce v Jarndyce. Today, it would
equally describe the polysyllabic impenetrability of European Community
legislation on environmental matters, and of the British Standard, BS 7750,
Environmental Management Systems,! but it also serves as a literary re-
minder for lawyers that pollution is not a new phenomenon, and it provides
a clue to what has to be done to solve the present environmental
conundrum.
The
	pollution
	which
	confronts
	us
	today
	is
a
different
	set
of
problems
from
	that of
Dickens’
	time.
History
tells
us
that
the
notorious
	fogs
which
then affected
	London, Kent and Essex were gradually defeated through
a
combination
	of legislation, action,
	and technology.
	As
	we
	embark
	on
	a
fresh
crusade
against pollution,
we
may
learn
from
the past
that
these three
elements—legislation,
	action
and
technology—are
	all
essential
ingredients
in
any solution
for
dealing with
environmental problems.
Legislation played
an essential part
in
defeating
the fogs
by
regulating
or
prohibiting certain
activities,
by
setting standards, and by
establishing
stat-
utory bodies. But law alone could have achieved
	little
without action
by
1 For example, consider the following sentence from
the Introduction
to
BS
7750:
“To be
effective, they need
to
be conducted within
a
structured
management
	system, integrated
with overall
management
	activity and addressing
	all
aspects of desired environmental
performance.”
237

-- 273 of 360 --

238 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
relevant statutory bodies and other organizations, and the possibilities pro-
vided by technological evolution and development.”
In addition to their legislative powers, Governments have the ability to
apply taxpayers’ money to promote appropriate action and technology,
either by commissioning works or research, or by providing fiscal incen-
tives. Governments can also, with appropriate funding, establish inspection
agencies to enforce regulations but, apart from the cost, such enforcement
has limited effect in motivating organizations to take positive action.> The
urgent question for hard-pressed politicians is whether Governments and
indeed the European Community (which has become a major source of law
on environmental matters) can find other ways which are more effective
and less costly to the taxpayer to promote positive activity and produce
conformity with regulations.
The new approach, which may answer the politician’s prayer, is to focus
attention on the role of management within organizations by either encour-
aging, or imposing requirements for, formal documented management sys-
tems. The management system approach has already been widely adopted
in relation to conformity with “quality”? requirements and regulations, and
it is now being extended to health and safety, and to environment.
Environmental management systems are concerned with the initiation
and control of actions, with a view to assuring conformance with legal
requirements.
Management systems
The idea of a management system is to provide a planned, documented and
systematic approach towards some management function. Such an
approach has long been applied to financial management and accounting
functions. The 1950s and 60s saw the advent of management systems for
the control of time and progress, with the development of critical path
analysis and other management tools. In the late 60s and early 1970s,
management systems came to be applied to the achievement of quality
objectives, with a gradual change in the primary means of providing or
obtaining quality assurance. The emphasis shifted from reliance on final
N
The
law
also
provides means
of transferring
liability or
obtaining indemnities
in
respect of
environmental problems,
for
example, through insurance
or
the doctrine of caveat
emptor.
This
may
actually inhibit action, but that
is
another
	topic, see
J
N
Barber,
‘Guide
	to
Collateral Warranties”, Association of
Geotechnical
Specialists, (1993).
Prosecuting and fining a
corporation does not motivate individuals and may
actually disable
the organization from undertaking positive steps.
Holding individual directors personally
responsible may simply deter good people from taking on the job.
Ww

-- 274 of 360 --

Environmental management systems 239
inspection by the purchaser, initially to systematic and documented inter-
mediate inspections by the purchaser in the early quality assurance
schemes, and more recently to management systems implemented by the
supplier.
This shift involved initiatives in many different countries and inter-
national organizations, but a significant step was the publication by the
British Standards Institution in 1979 of the first edition of BS 5750, Quality
Systems. This was an outstanding document, tightly drafted with proper
regard to matters such as authority to control. It provided an effective basis
on which individual organizations could develop and operate their own
quality management systems. It introduced some radical innovations, in
particular, the idea of “‘auditability” and “‘auditing”’ as a means of checking
the implementation and effectiveness of the system. It also introduced a
key figure, the “management representative’, responsible for the imple-
mentation of the management system, thus ensuring that controlling the
achievement of required quality standards would be treated as a manage-
ment function alongside controlling time and money.
The
	spread
	of quality
	management
	systems
	was given
	fresh
	impetus
when

	5750 was
	adopted
	(with

	as

as

“gener-

	Standards
	relating
	to
quality
	assurance
	(EN
29000).””
The

may
be

	either
as a

	For

	been suggested
	that the
procedures
	for

	of
Conformity
	in

	Construction Products

	in

	as

“Should

4 Council Resolution 90/C 10/01.
5 See Knight’s European Construction Documents, pp 2-4.
6 Council Directive 93/38/EEC, Article 32. (OJ L199/100)

-- 275 of 360 --

240 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
The introduction of quality management system requirements in regula-
tions and procurement has become possible not only because of the pub-
lication of relevant Standards, but also because of the apparent willingness
of organizations to develop and implement appropriate systems. This ap-
parent willingness led to an intuitive feeling that if the management system
approach works and is accepted in regard to product quality, it might also
be applied to safety, health and environment regulations and requirements.
This intuitive feeling has been quickly taken up in EC legislation. For
example the Temporary or Mobile Work Sites Directive,” although it does
not use the words “management system’, requires a coordinator to pro-
duce a “‘safety and health plan” setting out the specific rules applicable to
the site concerned, and then “‘to make arrangements to check that the
working procedures are being implemented correctly’’. This is effectively a
management system. It does not, however, refer to a Standard to specify
the contents of the plan or the nature of the arrangements to check com-
pliance. There is, as yet, no appropriate Standard.
The Eco-management and Audit Regulation® calls explicitly for adop-
tion of an “‘environmental management system’’, which is defined to mean:
“that part of the overall
management
	system which
includes
the
organizational
structure, responsibilities, practices,
	procedures,
	processes
	and
	resources
	for
determining
and
implementing
the
environmental
policy.”
There
is
a
difficulty,
however,
	that
as
yet there
is
no
European
	Standard
specifying
the
requirements
for
an
environmental
	management
	system.
An
ISO
	Committee
	has
been
	set
up?
to
produce
	a
Standard
	but
it
will
ob-
viously take
some
	time.
The
	British
Standards
	Institution
	has
been
	quick
off
the
blocks with
its
new
	Standard,
	BS
7750: 1992,
Environmental Man-
agement
	Systems,
but
this
has not yet
been adopted
as
a
European
	Stand-ard
and
it is
premature
	to
assume
	that
it
will
be
adopted.
EC Eco-Management and Audit Regulation
The
early
EC
initiatives
on the
environment,
	prior
to
the Single
EuropeanAct
	in
1987,
were
	rather
piecemeal, possibly
	because
	the original
Treaty
provided only
a
tenuous
	basis for legislating on
	environmental
	matters.
There
were,
however, Environmental Action Plans published
in 1973, 1977
and 1983, prior
to the Act, and
many
	legislative
measures were adopted.
7 Council Directive 92/57/EEC (OJ L245/6).
8 Council Regulation (EEC) No
1836/93 (OJ L168/1)
9 ISO Technical Committee TC107, Environmental Management. The

Environmental management systems 241
The early emphasis was on pollution control rather than prevention. One
of the early measures, however, was the Assessment Directive, adopted in
1985,1° which requires environmental assessments for certain categories of
new projects. It is still a key Directive affecting construction projects.
The current EC environmental initiatives are more soundly based on the
Single European Act. This provided an explicit mandate for the EC to deal
with environmental matters, and it set a number of goals, of which one is
particularly relevant to environmental management systems, namely “‘to
preserve, protect and improve the quality of the environment”. It also laid
down principles, of which two are particularly relevant, namely that en-
vironmental protection measures should become integrated into other EC
policies and that preventive action should be taken.
The Act provided the basis of the Fourth Environmental Action Pro-
gramme, also published in 1987,!! which laid down a number of priorities,
including:
“(c) prevention of industrial accidents
(d) combating noise pollution at source
(1) the development of improved waste management in relation to the reduc-
tion of quantities, treatment, recycling and reuse
(q) effective implementation of Council Directive 85/337/EEC of 27 June 1985
on the assessment of the effects of certain public and private projects on
the environment and the integration of the environmental dimension in
other Community policies
(r) implementation of appropriate standards in order to ensure a high level of
public health and environmental protection.”
More
	recently,

10 Council Directive 85/333/EEC (OJ L175).
11 Council Resolution 87/C 328/01 (OJ C70/3).

242 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
made to that draft and even to the “‘final’’ draft which was published in
1992.13 For example, the scheme established by the Regulation became the
‘““Eco-management and audit scheme” instead of the ‘‘Eco-audit scheme”’.
Reference to ‘environmental protection systems” disappeared. Any com-
mentaries or other documents based on drafts of the Regulations should
accordingly be treated with caution.
The Regulation establishes a “‘Community scheme allowing voluntary
participation by companies performing industrial activities”. The scheme is
entitled the “community eco-management and audit scheme’’, and is some-
times referred to by the acronym, CEMAS. The scheme is voluntary in the
sense that companies are not obliged to register, but once a company has
registered to participate, it is effectively locked in. If the company there-
after does not comply, the Regulation provides that the “site shall be
deleted from the register”. There is no provision for voluntary deregistra-
tion. A major part of the scheme is the publicity attached to registration
and, therefore, also to deletion from the register. Article 16 also provides
for “appropriate legal or administrative measures in case of non-
compliance with the provisions of this Regulation”’.
The objectives of the scheme are stated to be:
“to
promote
	continuous
	improvements
	in
the
environmental
	performance
	of
industrial activities by:
(a) the
	establishment and
	implementation
	of
environmental
	policies,
	pro-
grammes
	and
management
	systems
by
companies,
in
relation
to their
sites;
(b)
	the
systematic, objective
and
periodic
evaluation
of the
performance
of
such
elements;
(c)
	the
provision
of
information
on
environmental performance
to
the
public.”

Environmental management systems 243
There is a tendency amongst construction professionals to link the word
“site’’ with construction activities, given its usage in standard forms of
contract to describe the land possession of which is given to a contractor. It
is apparent, however, that the word is not used in this sense in the Regula-
tion. “Industrial activity’’, which is the essence of a site for the purposes of
the Regulation, is defined to mean:
“any activity listed under sections C and D of the classification of economic
activities in the European Community . . . as established by Council Regulation
(EEC) No 3037/90 with the addition of electricity, gas, steam and hot water
production and the recycling, treatment, destruction or disposal of solid or liquid
waste.”
Reference to the classification shows that section C comprises manufac-
turing industries and section D comprises mines and quarries. Construction
and property development are classified in separate sections. Unless
muckshifting activities come within the last part of the definition as ‘‘dis-
posal of waste’’, it appears that construction companies will not generally
be able to register under the scheme.!4
On the other hand, Annex 1 para DIl of the Regulation then says:
“Provisions shall be made to ensure that contractors working at the site on the
company’s behalf apply environmental standards equivalent to the company’s
”	
own.
It appears that organizations involved in construction work on sites
which are registered under the scheme will come within the ambit of the
Regulation indirectly.

Requirements for environmental management systems
Article 12 provides that companies implementing national, European or
international standards for environmental management systems shall be
considered as meeting the requirements of the Regulation, provided that
the standards and certification procedures are recognised by the Commis-
sion and the certification is undertaken by a body whose accreditation is
recognized in the Member State where the site is located.
The requirements concerning environmental management systems are
set out in Annex 1, section B, under six headings:
(1) Environmental policy, objectives and programmes;
(2) Organization and personnel;
(3) Environmental effects;
(4) Operational control;
(5) Environmental management documentation records; and
(6) Environmental audits.
Most of the requirements would appear familiar to anyone accustomed
to working with EN 29000 (BS 5750), but there are subtle differences. For
example, B2 requires steps or procedures to ensure awareness among per-
sonnel at all levels of “the potential environmental effects of their work
activities and the environmental benefits of improved performance.”
The main difference, however, comes under item B3 where the en-
vironmental effects which are to be examined and assessed are listed, and
item B4, where the ‘‘functions, activities and processes’? which may cause
these effects are to be “‘identified’’ and then “‘planned and controlled”’.
Under item B3, the company is required to establish and maintain pro-
cedures to:
“record all legislative, regulatory and other policy requirements pertaining to the
environmental aspects of its activities, products and services.”

-- 280 of 360 --

Environmental management systems 245
This brings home the point that the main objective of the scheme is to
promote compliance with legislative, regulatory and other policy require-
ments, that is, with the requirements of European Community or national
legislation or regulations. This is to be contrasted with the objective of
quality management systems, which is compliance with the requirements of
the purchaser who pays the money.
Past activities
The scheme is directed towards the control of present and future activities
in the industrial sector. The Regulation is remarkably coy about how far
there might be a need to look at past activities and particularly at con-
tamination caused by past activities on the site. It might be expected that
unearthing of the effects of past activities could arise under the process of
“environmental review” of the site, but this process is only to cover the
issues listed in Annex 1 Part C, which do not include the effects of past
activities. On the other hand, Annex 1 Part B includes among the effects to
be examined and assessed “effects arising, or likely to arise, as con-
sequences of ... past activities”. This is particularly relevant to con-
struction, for example, if the work involves excavation on the site.

1S

Environmental management systems 247
(1) Overall management control of activities on a site: Whereas manufac-
turing activities tend to be managed by a single organization on a
single site on a long-term basis, construction activities are generally
carried out on a project-by-project basis on different sites. Each con-
struction project involves short-term activities, but leaves a long-term
product. Management responsibility for each project may be divided
between several organizations with none having overall management
responsibility for, or control of, activities on the site. Owners or oper-
ators of sites have traditionally sought to distance themselves from
control of construction activities.
(2) Planning and design: It is rare in the manufacturing sector for plan-
ning and design to be carried out by an organization which is indepen-
dent of the one responsible for production, but it is quite common on
construction projects. In the split reponsibility situation, the organiza-
tion responsible for planning and design does not control construction
activities, but it is concerned with the performance and effects of the
completed works and the eventual activities to be carried out using
the completed works.
(3)
	Existing
	ground
	conditions:
	Manufacturing
	tends
	to
	be
	concerned
with
new
materials
brought
onto
the
site,
whereas
construction
is
also
dependent
	on the
ground
which
exists
on
the
site.
These
	differences
	are
	considered below,
	particularly
	in
so far
as
they
undermine
	the
compatibility
	of
BS
	7750:
	1992 with
the
eco-management
and
audit
scheme.

The

Thus

17 European Construction Institute, Total project management of construction safety, health

-- 283 of 360 --

248 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
A study of that report reveals, however, that SHE aspects are only
considered to include the immediate environmental effects of construction
activities, and do not include the effects of the works when constructed or
their use. A different view of integration may sometimes be appropriate.
For example, the IAPH ‘Guidelines for Environmental Planning and
Management in Ports and Coastal Area Developments”!® recommend
that, in planning the design and construction of a new port, consideration
should be given to the eventual management of the port activities, includ-
ing emergency measures to deal with oil spills, as provision should be
integrated into the design.
It may be equally important to enable aspects of the management function
to be disentangled. One of the most significant innovations of management
systems is the concept of auditing. Where it is intended that an organization
should be accountable to an outside body for the faithful operation of a
management system, that accountability is monitored though external audit.
Difficulty can arise over the scope of the external audit. Is it intended to be
an audit of all management systems throughout the organization, or is the
scope to be limited in some way? While it may sometimes be beneficial to
have an integrated approach to management systems, auditability may
favour division or divisibility into discrete compartments.
It is also questionable whether the management representative ap-
pointed under a quality management system would have time to take on
the burden envisaged by the Guidance Notes in BS 7750: 1992 of ensuring
that “developments in environmental legislation and regulation, and in
environmental issues, concerns and understanding relevant to the organ-
ization, are monitored.”

by the individual organization and its sub-contractors. It does not embrace

This may be contrasted with the approach adopted for nuclear work,
which has its own quality system Standard, BS 5882. On nuclear work, the
‘‘quality programme” starts with the promoter, and authority for an overall
quality programme cascades from the promoter to cover all organizations
involved. This is necessary as by statute, on nuclear work, the promoter
bears primary responsibility for satisfying the regulatory body as to safety.

Companies in the construction or property development sectors will not

-- 285 of 360 --

250 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
above, they may be required to show that they apply “environmental stand-

registered under the scheme. Based on experience with quality systems, it
will probably be necessary or appropriate for construction sector companies
to operate their own environmental management systems as a means of
satisfying this indirect requirement. BS 7750: 1992 needs to be adjusted to
recognise the position of organizations in the secondary role and the rela-
tionships between the separate organizations involved on a site.
The statutory requirement for involvement of the owner or operator of
the site in the environmental management system gives rise to a lack of fit
with quality management systems in which there is no such requirement.
Existing ground
Construction activities commonly involve moving, exposing or disturbing
the existing ground, with potential environmental effects, particularly
where the ground is contaminated. This adds special significance to the
requirement in the Regulation for the company to examine and assess the
effects arising, or likely to arise, as consequences of past activities. This
requirement is repeated in BS 7750: 1992 section 4.4.3, and it there applies
to any organization implementing an environmental management system.
Where
	construction
	activities are
contemplated,
	the
requirement
	mustbe interpreted
	in
both
the
Regulation
	and
the
Standard
	as
referring
to
all
past
activities
	on
	the
site,
not
	merely
	past
activities
	of the
	company
	or
organization
itself.
Commercial objectives
Management
	systems
	may
	be
	classified
	according
	to
	whether
	they
	are

by requirements
	to

	bodies,

	long-term
profitability.
As
management
	systems
be-

	widely required through
contract, there
may

be regarded
	increasingly
as a

	in the

attraction of quality
management

been
	that

provide
improved long-term
	profitability.
	This carrot can be the
primary
	driving
force
to
effective

	of the system.
The improved long-term
profitability
is
derived principally from savings
in

	costs
	and rework
	or rectification,
	and from enhanced mar-
ketability.
The short-term impact
is
generally additional costs in setting up
and operating the system (although savings may also be achieved in the
short-term through more efficient working practices). There has to be an

Environmental management systems 251
up-front investment to provide long-term profitability. The financial bene-
fits are heralded in various British Standards, but without reference to the
time lag between investment and return.
With environment, and also with health and safety, there have been
suggestions that economic benefits will be achieved. For example, the ECI
Report asserts that:!9
‘‘As with all management and control there are up-front costs, both at a corpor-
ate and project level, associated with the introduction and implementation of
SHE management. These highly visible costs are often seen as a barrier to imple-
mentation. However, it has been demonstrated that these ‘costs’ are more than
offset by the ‘hidden’ benefits of planning and doing things right. The introduc-
tion of SHE management makes good business and commercial sense and is as
much a part of the role of management as is the control of quality and
productivity.”
No
source
	is
quoted
for
the
assertion
of
demonstrated
	offsets.
Although
there
	may
	be savings
	from
	environmental
	management,
	for

	and misleading
	to

	is

Other legal significance
There
	has been
a
tendency
for the quality
management movement
	to
play
down
	the contractual
	or legal significance of quality systems. Their motto
19 ECI Report, op cit at 39.
20 Ibid pp 40 and 43.

-- 287 of 360 --

PAS? Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
“right first time every time’’, if invariably fulfilled, would make the legal
system redundant. All management systems do, however, have legal signifi-
cance in two ways. Firstly, management systems generate documentary
records which may be available as evidence in the event of a dispute.
Secondly, the management system procedures may define what constitutes
standards of skill and care or reasonable precautions, whether in contract
or tort, or for the purpose of statutory requirements.
The legal significance of management system records generally and the
need to plan their archiving with proper regard to limitation periods have
been addressed elsewhere.?! Environmental management systems are
likely to have additional significance in relation to statutory requirements
or arrangements.
For example, the Control of Pollution Act 1974, section 61 allows for
prior consent to be granted by a local authority in regard to steps proposed
to be taken to minimise noise resulting from construction works. This can
avoid the risk of a prosecution under section 60, but it requires the contrac-
tor to plan his operations with regard to the environmental effect, noise,
and then to control his operations in accordance with the plan.
A second example is the Environmental Protection Act 1990, section 34,
which imposes a duty of care as respects waste. Section 35 allows for the
grant of waste management licences subject to conditions, including the
precautions to be taken.
A third example is the Building Regulations 1985, Clause D2, which
requires that “‘Precautions shall be taken to avoid damage to health caused
by substances found on or in the ground to be covered by the building.”
The eco-management and audit Regulation and BS 7750: 1992 both
make the point that environmental management systems are to be based
on the relevant legal requirements, but the corollary is equally important.
The significance of environmental management system procedures as a
criterion of compliance with legal requirements and the danger of setting
unnecessarily high or vague standards need to be understood when prepar-
ing the procedures, as does the significance of the records in establishing
compliance or non-compliance.?2

in

Aspects,

11 Professional liability, risk transfer and contract
documents
Michael Mendelblat
Introduction
What has gone before has largely been concerned with the impact on
construction in the 1990s resulting from improved environmental protec-
tion through legislation and the application of common law. My talk fits
into the section of proceedings relating to liabilities, remedies and policy,
and my concern is to look at the various ways in which there will be an
impact on professional liability, risk transfer, and contract documents. Ob-
viously, these subjects are all intertwined. In basic terms, parties who may
be liable for environmental damage will seek to transfer their risk to other
parties to a transaction, the professionals involved, or to contractors. In any
transaction, the vital element will be the commerciality of any proposed
transfer of liability. Each party will also be concerned to ensure that, even
where a commercial arrangement will permit transfer of liability, that lia-
bility is effectively passed on. Further, unless insurers will back the risk
assumed by professionals, an obligation may be enforceable on paper only.
In
many
cases,
the
new environmental
protection
regime
can
be
grafted on
to
current
systems
for
allocation of
risks
without causing
too
many
surprises.
Indeed,
	the
main
	pressure
	for
change
	in
this
area has not
been from new
environmental
	legislation,
but rather the courts’
	move
	against tortious
	lia-
bility
which gathered pace
from
the
mid
1980s
onwards, reaching
its
culmin-
ation
in
the
Murphy
	v
Brentwood!
	and
DoE
v
Bates?
cases,
following
which
parties not
	protecting themselves
	in
contract
	were apparently without
	a
remedy
against builders
for
economic
	loss.
Their only course was
to
seek
to
show
reliance on
another
party’s
advice
or
blame
professional advisers with
whom
	they were
in
contract
for
not
having protected them.
However,
	a
trend
which
is
likely to
lead
to a
greater
web
of contractual
liabilities,
indemnities,
etc., is
the
imposition
of
liability in
novel
situations.
This
may
	lead
to
a
safety
first
approach. For example,
	the
enquiry before

A

3 [1993] 3 All ER 147.

-- 290 of 360 --

Professional liability 255
reasonable care will arise in tort if, firstly, it is foreseeable that if his advice
is negligent, the recipient will suffer damage; secondly, where there is a
sufficiently proximate or close relationship between the parties; and,
thirdly, where it is just and reasonable to impose liability. Foreseeability
alone is not enough.
A duty of care will readily be found to exist where there has been injury
to persons or to property which is not part of the structure in respect of
which the professional was engaged. Otherwise, unless special circum-
stances apply, the professional will not owe a duty of care to avoid econ-
omic loss. Special circumstances may include those where a third party is
known to an adviser to have relied on his advice in relation to a particular
transaction. Further, there are other special cases where such a duty has
been found which are less relevant to the present topic, such as surveyors’
liability for a house purchaser’s survey to the purchaser of a modest prop-
erty, as in Smith v Bush‘ or liability of solicitors to beneficiaries of wills, as
in White v Jones.5 In both these types of cases, the Defendant knew or
ought to have known the identity of the Plaintiff(s) and the implication of
his actions in relation to the transaction affecting the Plaintiff’s interests.
To
	illustrate
	these
	principles, the
	recent
	case
	of

it.

it,
and

could
not

to
rely
on
it,
save

	there

	for

	loss in

those
Plaintiffs in

spelt out,

in
any
special

as

Smith and White
cases.

(2) BSI Draft for Development DD 175 for Identification of Potentially

The above do not constitute a single definitive code of practice, and the
BSI “Draft for Development” states that it is to be applied on a provisional
basis only. Therefore, ground investigation specialists must take account of
all the above and other relevant specialist publications when considering
investigation and treatment of contaminated land. As an example, the
ICRCL guidance does not cover either the full range of contaminants which
may be encountered or all proposed uses, thus requiring the designer to
consider site-specific solutions. Dutch guidelines (the “ABC” values) may
appear to fill the gap in some cases, but are regarded by some experts as too
onerous and/or of limited applicability to the UK in view of the different
geology of that country. This would suggest that the designer should ap-
proach them with great caution and beware of unnecessary over-specification.

Professional liability 259
tion of any premises or land at or on which construction works need to be
carried out, is clearly apt to be fulfilled in practice by a professional.
Indeed, the planning supervisor is likely to be drawn from the design team.
Designers themselves have duties under the Regulations, in particular as
follows:
(1) To ensure that their designs do not expose those building, maintain-
ing, cleaning, or repairing the structure to risk to health and safety.
(2) To ensure that their designs include adequate information about any
aspect of the designs which might affect the health and safety of any
person carrying out construction work.
Each of these obligations will be directly actionable against the designer
by way of criminal prosecution and endure for the duration of a project.
Designers’ brief within the scope of their professional responsibility (but
otherwise irrespective of their terms of appointment) will therefore extend
to risks they can reasonably foresee in respect of future work to the struc-
ture and the only limitation is as to work such as alteration or demolition
which is too remote from the circumstances of the original design. Further,
they cannot rely on an employer and/or contractor to build their design
safely irrespective of cost and time. They must minimise risks as far as
reasonably practicable at the design stage. If a designer is also planning
supervisor, he remains responsible throughout the project for ensuring that
cost and time allow all personnel working on the project to comply with
relevant health and safety provisions. This may lead to a reassessment on
such factors before contemplating any major variation order.

Professional liability 261
However, this phrase does not connote tortious liability, as the remedy
provided for by the section (subsection 6) is criminal. The duty is to take all
such measures applicable to a person in certain capacities to:
(1) Prevent contravention of section 33 of the Act (unauthorised or
harmful deposit, treatment or disposal of waste).
(2) Prevent escape of waste from his control or that of any other person.
(3) On transfer of the waste, to secure that it is to an authorised person
and with an appropriate written description.
A person is in the relevant capacity if he imports, produces, carries,
keeps, treats, disposes or has control as a broker of waste. Again, it is not
inconceivable that a professional might directly be liable for contravention,
although DoE guidance indicates this is doubtful.!9 A contractor almost
certainly will be, as he may find himself producing, disposing of or acting as
a broker for controlled waste.2° An employer may also be liable; e.g. as a
broker in respect of transfer between contractors. Perhaps more important
to the professional is the prospect that his client may look to him to take all
such measures as are reasonable in the circumstances to ensure compliance
by contractors with the duty of care. These may include a greater level of
site regulation and supervision, so as to prevent the contravention by any
other person (e.g. an unlicensed manager or carrier) of section 33 of the
Act. He may also need to ensure that the waste does not escape from the
control of the contractor or of any other person. He may be particularly
concerned where the client acts as broker for waste (for example, by letting
a disposal sub-contract) to see on transfer that the transfer is only to an
authorised person, and that the written description of the waste is
transferred.
The

himself
may

liability
if

	of the waste.

Professional liability 263
Limitation of liability
Professionals may of course seek to protect themselves by limitation of
liability, and I have already commented on the court’s refusal to counte-
nance a clause whereby the professional effectively exonerates himself
from liability against fee claims. However, there are other less drastic ways
in which the professional may seek to reduce his liability for, for example,
the following:
No greater obligation or no greater liability clause
The professional may seek to limit either his scope of liability or the
amount of damages he will pay, or both, when giving a warranty to a party
other than his original employer. In many cases, the wording only relates to
the scope of liability, rather than the quantum of damages, and a more
effective approach may be to consider the type of damage for which the
professional is to be liable; for example, including the cost of remedial
works, but excluding consequential loss.
The “‘State of the Art’ defence
The
professional
must
	exercise
reasonable
	skill
and
care,
having regard
to
the
state
of
knowledge
	available
to
the
general
body
of
practitioners
at
the
time
of
his
acts. It
may
	be
difficult to
assess
responsibility
in
a field
where
no authoritative
guidance
is
available.
Take,
for

the
present

lished
	documentation
	in

	to

	and treatment
	of con-

	land.
I

	attention
	earlier?!

	of

	one
	is
a
BSI

for

	That

drawn

has not

	The

	on

	If

	must
	not

to

	However,
	due weight must be given

	and

	Where

This

264 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
Net contribution
This type of clause seeks to mitigate the effect of joint and several liability
and comes in numerous forms, but each with the common intention that
the professional pays no more than what would be his proper share, if all
the relevant parties were before the court. Some variants require the client
to sue to judgment all parties who may be liable for the consequences of a
particular event. Alternatively, the warranty may not come into force un-
less other professionals or contractors have entered into warranties of
similar effect. Other forms may require the client to take the risk of insol-
vency of one or more of the other parties to the extent that they may have
been liable between themselves.
Such
clauses
find
their
place
in
the
BPF
	forms
	of
warranty and
are,
of
course,
	commonly
	insisted
	upon by
insurers,
	so
	as
to
avoid
	the
	“deep
pocket”
	effect,
particularly
	where
	professionals
	are
	the
only
party
	left
standing
	after
	the
insolvency
	of
contractors
	and
	sub-contractors.
	They
would
	certainly
	assist
the
professional
	in
the
event,
	for
example,
	of
lia-
bility to
the
client
by
reason
	of the
‘clean-up and recover”
	type
of
provi-
sion,
or
breach
	of the
duty
of
care
	as
regards treatment,
	etc.,
of waste.
However,
	their
	usefulness
	should
	not
	be over-stated,
	as
they
	are
	only
intended
	to
cut
down
	recovery
	where
	liability
is
properly
	that
of
more
than one
party. In an
initial
investigation
situation,
only one party
would
be involved
	although
	others
	might
	eventually
	come
	on
	board,
	for
	ex-
ample,
the
type
of situation I
mentioned
	earlier in
relation
to
section 61
of the

	Protection
	Act,
where
	the

	is

to

	in

In the

I think

	This, surely,

-- 300 of 360 --

Professional liability 265
(1) Other recourses of the client beyond seeking recourse from
professionals.
(2) Possible liability of professionals to, and recourse from, other parties.
The first question raises a number of subsidiary issues. It is not my
intention to deal with remedies arising from the property elements of a
transaction, as I am considering only aspects of recovery in the con-
struction documentation. However, in passing, I think it would be as well to
remind you of the implications of the Cambridge Water Company?? deci-
sion. The tort of nuisance has clearly not exhausted its usefulness where
damage to adjoining property is concerned. Thus, there will be liability for
pollution of an aquifer by another party, even if that pollution was not
deliberate and, at the time that it took place, probably not such as to breach
then applicable standards. In the light of this, it must be prudent for any
purchaser to seek such indemnities as he can in respect of earlier use of the
property from his vendor. In practice, save for an obviously polluting (and
very recently ceased) use, such indemnities are likely to be both uninsur-
able and unavailable. Remedies in tort against those on the design or
construction team originally responsible will almost certainly be equally
unavailable, which must of course lead to the need to identify, so far as is
possible, the potential of a “Brownfield” site for future pollution.
By
contrast,
	another
	line
of
authority
(see, for
example,
	the
Stephens
v
Anglian Water
Authority?3
	case) indicates
that
abstraction
	of
water
	freely
percolating
under
the
owner’s
	land
is
not
actionable,
even
	if
subsidence
	is
caused
	to
an
	adjoining owner’s
	land
	by
such
	action.
	In
both
	cases,
	the
Plaintiff
benefited
from
the exercise of
a
natural
right.
Even
so,
the lack of
deliberate action
in
the
defendant’s
	actions
in
the
Cambridge
	case
seems
	a
valid distinction
from
the older
cases;
e.g.
Ballard
v
Tomlinson.*4
However,
at
present,
landowners
cannot regard
that
as a
useful distinction, unless
and
until
the
House
	of
Lords
rule
otherwise.

266 — Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
originally thought and a contract takes longer, costing more, then an em-
ployer will seek to blame the investigating professional, but at the same
time he will be trying to resist claims from his contractor for delay and the
cost of variations.
Whether he can do so will of course turn on the construction of the
particular contract. For example, the ICE 6th Edition Conditions state that
the contractor is deemed to have inspected the site and to have satisfied
himself as to its form and nature, including the ground and subsoil. Without
more, these words were held in the Australian case of Dillingham v
Downs?» to protect the employer where he supplied part but not all of the
site information available to him. The information was misleading but not,
in itself, incorrect. It was not implied that the information was complete or
exhaustive. A duty to supply full information could only arise if such re-
sponsibility was accepted by the employer or the contractor relied on him
to do so. Such a duty would not normally arise if tender documents were
silent as to ground conditions.
However, a new sub-clause in the ICE Conditions (6th Edition) (11(1))
operates so as to correct this by providing that the employer is deemed to
have made available all information on the ground and the subsoil ob-
tained by him. The effect (perhaps not the drafting intention) appears to be
to prevent the employer hiding behind non-disclosure and to allow the
contractor to take the benefit of undisclosed information if he can establish
that the employer had this prior to tender.
The
responsibility
for
interpreting such
information
	is
the
contractor’s
—
which
	may
	be
a
limited
	duty
if
some
	or
all
of the
employer’s
	reports
	are
interpretative.
Further,
	Clause
	11(3)
provides
	that the
contractor
	is
deemed
	to
have
based
	his
	tender
	on
	information
	made
	available
	by
	the

	However,

Professional liability 267
Perhaps today the case would be decided in terms of reliance but it seems
to me exceptional since the standard form had been amended to prevent
any amendment to the contractor’s design (for whatever reason) from
ranking as a variation. In any event, Bills or Employers’ Requirements will
often contain disclaimers as to the accuracy of ground or soil information.
The ICE Conditions go on to allow the contractor, at Clause 12, a mea-
sure of recovery for conditions which an experienced contractor could not
have reasonably foreseen.
The New Engineering Contract (‘““NEC’’) at Clause 60.1 (12) only allows
recovery for conditions which, at the contract date, an experienced contrac-
tor would have judged to have such a small chance of occurring that it
would have been unreasonable for him to have allowed for them. In judg-
ing ground conditions, the contractor is assumed under the NEC to have
taken into account the site information provided to him and other informa-
tion he could reasonably have been expected to have obtained.
The ICE Conditions may contain a conflict between Clauses 11(3) and 12
if the former is taken to be relevant to the extent to which a contractor
should have foreseen ground conditions. However, the combined effect
may be that knowledge of actual conditions ascertainable from information
supplied to him (even if beyond a reasonable contractor’s foresight) must
be imported to the contractor. Both ICE Clause 12 and NEC Clause 60.1
(12) seem to me to be directed to ensure that actual conditions are taken
into account, but that the contractor’s foresight is seen objectively.
Guidance notes suggest that one less confrontational approach would be
to adopt a boundary line between employer’s and contractor’s risk in terms
of stated criteria; for example, as to the extent of groundwater levels to be
encountered. This may only rarely be practicable; say, in the event of a site
comprehensively investigated prior to tender.
Contributory negligence
I
now
	turn
	to
discuss the
Barclays
	Bank
	v
Fairclough?’
	case,
	which
	is
an
incident of
liability
arising
from methods
	of
working
in
disposal of
hazardous
substances.
	There
	are
	two
	areas
	where
	the case
	is
of
interest. Firstly,
on
contributory negligence, which
may
serve
to
reduce contractors’
	liability.
The
	contractor had been employed
	to
carry out cleaning works
	on the
roof of the bank’s records
	store
	which was
	made
	of
corrugated asbestos
27 Unreported,
13
May
1993;
Judgment reversed by Court of
Appeal
on 6 May
1994 holding
Fairclough 100%
	liable on the basis that there were concurrent breaches of an absolute

-- 303 of 360 --

268 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
cement sheeting. The bank contracted with Fairclough, who in turn sublet
the work down a chain. The work was eventually sub-sublet to a contractor
located by the simple means of looking him up in the Yellow Pages. He was
described as an industrial cleaner, which was of importance on another
point.28
The court found that Fairclough were in breach of a term that they
should execute the works with reasonable care and skill. They could and
should have taken simple and relatively inexpensive precautions to prevent
contaminated water and debris entering the building. The work resulted in
contamination of the bank’s documents by asbestos dust.
However, at trial the bank’s claim was not pleaded in tort with a view to
avoiding a finding of contributory negligence. The Law Reform (Contribu-
tory Negligence) Act 1945 only relates to tortious claims, so the result was
the ungainly spectacle of Fairclough saying that they were liable in tort and
the bank saying that they were not! The court found that there was liability
in tort because the damage was to “other property”’ of the bank; namely, its
files. The object of the contract was, in fact, performed as the roof was
cleaned. The court held that although there were duties in contract, a duty of
care in tort could exist so far as personal injury or damage to other property
was concerned. Contributory negligence will therefore be applicable to
claims which could be brought in both contract and tort but not if the
defendant’s liability arises from some contractual provision which does not
depend on negligence on the part of the defendant. The defendants were to
comply with all statutory provisions and had failed to comply with the As-
bestos Regulations, which might at first sight appear to be breach of a
contractual provision in terms of “strict liability”. However, the court decided
that such failures, coupled with the contractor’s knowledge that he should
have taken reasonable precautions, also constituted failure to exercise reason-
able skill and care; hence attracting the finding of contributory negligence.
The
court held
that the
bank
was
itself
the architect
under
the contract,
and could
	and should have secured
	that
proper precautions were
	carried
out
	by drawing
	the
contractor’s attention
	to
dangers
	in their
	system
	of
work. Their negligence
	resulted
in
a
40%
	reduction
	in
damages. The
case
may
	leave
open
	the issue of
whether contributory negligence
	is
availablefor
pure
economic
	loss
cases
assuming
	there
is
concurrent
	liability in con-tract
and
tort.
In the
Fairclough
case, the point did not
need
to
be decided.
However,
	His
Honour Judge Havery
referred
	to a
passage from
the
judg-
ment
	of Mr. Justice
Hobhouse
	in Vesta
v
Butcher:29
28 See below under “‘Recourse from Other Parties”’.
29 [1986] 2All ER 488.

-- 304 of 360 --

Professional liability 269
“Where the Defendant’s liability arises from a contractual obligation which is
expressed in terms of taking care (or its equivalent) but does not correspond to a
common law duty to take care which would exist in the given case independently
of contract.”
This is a definition of a situation where that judge held that contributory
negligence may be applicable.
Such authority as there is seems to me to suggest that, where there would
be no duty of care independently of any contract but the contract requires
the exercise of skill and care, it is possible for there to be contributory
negligence. I do not read the Vesta case in the Court of Appeal as having
decided this point, and I suggest that if concurrent liabilities exist in con-
tract and tort only by virtue of the existence of the contract, that is still
sufficient to attract contributory negligence as being within Mr. Justice
Hobhouse’s definition in Vesta. It is true that the Court of Appeal in
Vesta>° approved an earlier New Zealand case to the effect that concurrent
liability in contract is “‘immaterial’’, but surely this means that such liability
need not be taken into account, rather than that the associated tortious
liability is to be ignored. Some support for this may be found from a further
passage in the Barclays Bank judgment:
“In my judgment, it is clear beyond argument that the expression ‘act or omission
which give rise to a liability in tort’ [which is taken from the 1945 Act] includes
acts or omissions which only in the particular circumstances, e.g. knowledge, give
rise to a liability in tort.”

270 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
Possible liability of professionals to, and recourse from, other
parties
In the absence of a contract, a professional’s liability to or from other
parties involved will be determined by the Civil Liability (Contribution)
Act 1978. There may be liability for contribution as between professionals
and, say, contractors, if both are liable to the employer. However, there
may also be direct liability. Consider the not uncommon case where a
professional’s appointment is novated to a contractor. Tf it is to be the case
that the contractor largely takes responsibility for site conditions, and the
novation provides that the professional will be liable to the contractor for
all breaches of duty of skill and care connected with the project, even if
pre-novation, then a professional may be liable to the contractor for any
failings in respect of an initial site investigation, even though at the time he
was not employed by the contractor.
However,
	one
	can
foresee
more
	difficult
problems
	for
the
contractor
	in
deciding
whom
	to
proceed
	against
if,
by
pursuing
the
professional,
	he
has
to
maintain
	that
the
professional
	failed
	to
spot
the
obvious,
	whereas
	as
against
the
employer
	in
terms
	of, say,
the
New
	Engineering
	Contract
	he
may
wish
to
maintain
that
he
discounted
a
risk so
small
that
no
reasonable
contractor
would
	be
concerned
	with
it!
The
operation
of the
Civil
Liability
(Contribution) Act
1978 requires
an
assessment
	of responsibility
	between
	defendants
	liable to
the
plaintiff for
the
same
damage.
In practice, the role
of
professionals
and
contractors
may
be
easy
to split
in
design terms
	but
less so
where
	overlap
relates
to
oper-
ational
matters
for
which
a
contractor
may
need
to
be
carefully
supervised
by
the
professional.
This
may
	be
particularly relevant
	in
the area of
hazardous
	operations,
such
	as
was
	the case
	in

	Had

Professional liability 271
sufficient concentrations to cause risk of injury to health . . . There is no evidence
that industrial cleaners or specifically those undertaking the work of cleaning
asbestos cement roofs will generally have the skill or knowledge in relation to the
avoidance of health hazards generated by asbestos which I accept exist in the
building industry . . . I do not understand [an expert’s] reference to the building
industry to be intended to extend to persons who carried out industrial cleaning
on buildings.”
Reversing up the contractual chain, perhaps the judge’s view would have
been different if even the industrial cleaners had had the benefit of a
proper approach to safety policy applied by the employer.
The future
Finally, I turn to the DTI report of the Advisory Committee on Business
and the Environment. The purpose of the report was to seek the views of
lenders, industry and insurers on environmental liability. Two proposals
may be of relevance to professionals’ and contractors’ liability. A ‘“‘state of
the art” defence was proposed to add to current defences to civil claims or
prosecutions by regulatory authorities, for example as in section 85 of the
Water Resources Act 1991. The effect of such a defence may not be dis-
similar to that applicable to professional liability, but would, if applied,
presumably cut down the scope of claims against employers, even in what is
now a strict liability situation, although at present the “‘state of the art”
issue is more commonly decided as between employer and professional.
The question of retrospective liability was also considered, in particular
in the context of the position of the current owner of a polluted site which
is harming third parties, even though the pollution was caused by a pre-
decessor. He may be liable in nuisance if, knowing of its existence, he fails
to take any reasonable steps to bring it to an end. Under section 61 of the
Environmental Protection Act 1990, he may be liable in more limited
circumstances (see above)33 if the land is liable to cause pollution to the
environment or harm to human health for the Waste Regulation Auth-
ority’s costs of avoiding such pollution or harm. His right of action against a
predecessor may be limited.
The Committee doubted whether
current
owners should be
liable
under
statute for activities
which were
lawful
at
the time,
and appeared
to
suggest
a
dilution of section 61
by
distinguishing
between innocent owners
	of pol-
luted
sites,
and long-term owners whose
	original
activities, whilst lawful
at
the time, resulted in pollution. Advisers
	to
long-term owners presumably
could raise a
‘“‘state
of the
art’?
defence
	in respect of activities lawful
at
the
33 See above under ‘“‘New Statutory Provisions”.

-- 307 of 360 --

272 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
time. However, the position would be different if they were retained to
review sites on a periodic basis, as they would then need to review their
advice in the light of changing statutory and advisory material becoming
available.
It is worth noting that the committee was divided on strict liability with
industry strongly against and lenders/insurers not fundamentally opposed
but seeking a set of supporting rules, of which the above are two examples.
Conclusion
To sum up, I think that whilst the scope of professionals’ liability in tort
has, on the face of it, been greatly limited in recent years. There are
indications from Linden Gardens? that judicial policy in this respect may
have turned an unexpected contractual corner, which may once again
widen the scope of potential liability. Opinion is presently divided as to
whether collateral warranties remain the best method of adjusting risks; on
the other hand, I think that it is still true to say that professionals should at
least be able to anticipate who will sue them, but not necessarily for how
much.
So
far
as
liability
of

In

	potential
	for

	will
	surely
	be
	determined by

	to

	and

	find

However,
	this

for

	to

	to specialists,
	perhaps

12 Use of dangerous materials—the duty to warn
and other problems
Philip Naughton
Dangerous materials
In this paper “dangerous materials” means substances or assemblies which are
dangerous in themselves or which are capable of becoming dangerous or
giving rise to a danger. And a “danger” is a risk of significant injury to a
person.
In the
construction
	industry,
such materials
fall
into
	a
number
	of
catego-
ries
which
include:
(a)
	Substances which
are
inherently
dangerous
such
as
asbestos,
mercury,
phenols,
	radioactive
	materials.
	Generally
	their
use
	is
restricted
	and
controlled
	by regulation—such
	as
the
Control
	of
Asbestos Regula-
tions
1987.
(b)
	Substances,
	or
accumulations
	of
substances
	which
	are
dangerous
	be-
cause
	of
their specific use
	or
application
	or
location,
	such
	as
tough-
ened
glass
windows
	in
a
skyscraper
or
liquid
nitrogen
stored
in
bulk.
(c)
	Substances
	or
components
	which
become
	dangerous
	or
cause
	danger
as a
result
of
a
change
	in
conditions,
	for
example by
water penetra-
tion.
High Alumina Cement
	is
an
example.
Scope of paper
The

in

	to

	Everyone connected

	a

	or

	may

National and international regulations apply
or

to
the different parties.
Obligations

as

and statutory

The

as to the steps, if

been incorporated into a building. An obvious example
is the discovery of

the incorporation of a material such as crocidolite (blue asbestos) in the

The initial use and incorporation of dangerous materials is generally
covered by regulation. Non-compliance with regulation and statute may
give rise to a risk of prosecution as well as a risk of a civil claim. Generally,
the application of the criminal law to the use of materials will not be
covered. Again, the use of dangerous materials as a part of a manufacturing
process is outside the scope of this paper.

	if

out removal of asbestos. The extent of such work is described, incidentally, in
the Linden Gardens? case. Obligations may arise on transfer of a property.
Claims alleging misrepresentation and attempts to avoid a contract may
follow answers given to an environmental questionnaire in pre-contract en-
quiries prior to acquisition of a company or property. Other claims may arise
even though no enquiry is made: see the main discussion in this paper below.
It may happen that a worker is unable to bring a claim against his
employer because, for example, the employer is no longer in business and
his insurer is entitled to avoid liability because of non-disclosure. He may
then seek to claim against the designer of the process, perhaps an engineer,
the supplier of the material, the owner of the building or plant. His chances
of success will depend upon classical obligations found fully developed in
the law of negligence but might also arise as a claim for breach of statutory
duty,3 a breach of the Defective Premises Act 1972, the Occupiers’ Lia-
bility Act 1957, the Consumer Protection Act 1987 or the rule in Rylands v
Fletcher. If a specifier wishes to avoid such liability, it must seek agreement
with his employer that the latter will indemnify him against any such claim.
Dangerous materials and the duty to warn
If

show that at the time of supply the state of scientific or technical knowledge
was not such as would permit discovery of the relevant defect.
In the two cases cited, the Plaintiff was an individual who had actually
suffered personal injury. If no one is injured, can a cause of action arise?
This issue can only be answered after first reconsidering the flurry of recent
decisions of the House of Lords in which Anns v Merton was overruled and
other cases, particularly Junior Books v Veitchi and Pirelli v Oscar Faber7
were isolated, almost like exposed infants on Spartan hills, in the hope that
they might die off from lack of attention. The attack begins with the dis-
senting judgment of Lord Brandon in Junior Books.8 He reiterated the
limit of liability of a manufacturer as being that laid down in Donoghue v
Stevenson”, that is to exercise such reasonable care as to ensure that goods
can be used without causing damage to persons or their property. He went
on to put this into the wider principle embodied in that case that when a
person can or ought to appreciate that a careless act or omission on his part
may result in physical injury to other persons or property he owes a duty to
all such persons to exercise reasonable care to avoid such careless act or
omission. The duty was based upon the existence of a danger of physical
injury or injury to property where the relevant property was property other
than the very property giving rise to the danger of physical damage. This
left no room for liability for economic loss. Lord Brandon, this time giving
the opinion of the House returned to the point in Leigh and Sillavan v
Aliakmon.'° As everyone knows, in D&F Estates y Church Commrs') the
Lords stepped backwards and stated in clear terms that, in general there
would be no liability in tort if as a result of a breach of duty, economic loss
alone was suffered or only damage to the physical property giving rise to
the risk of danger to other property or persons. Anns hardly survived the
onslaught in D&F and in Murphy v Brentwood DC,!2 The Lords at last
took the step of reversing Anns and insisting that the test as stated by Lord
Brandon was correct.
The
	unfortunate
	effect
of
this
trend
	in
the
law was
	made
	plain
by
the

in

	Bates.!3

	of

The Lords have not overruled Pirelli nor even said that it is a unique
case. In Murphy, Lord Keith, at page 466 considered that:
“where the tortious liability arose out of a contractual relationship with profes-
sional people, the duty extended to take reasonable care not to cause economic
loss to the client by the advice given. The plaintiffs built the chimney as they did
in reliance upon that advice. The case would accordingly fall within the principle
of Hedley Byrne v Heller & Partners’’.'4
It will be recalled that in Pirelli the use of the wrong material had caused
a chimney to begin to crack and that eventually it would collapse, if not
demolished. There are real problems in treating Pirelli in this way, par-
ticularly because the only reason why the action was brought in tort was
because the limitation period in contract had expired and it is not clear why
a Hedley Byrne type of liability could only arise when damage occurred. It
appears to conflict with the better analysis in Forster v Outred,!5 which was
distinguished. Hedley Byrne was not even cited in argument in Pirelli. And
it would seem that Lord Bridge in Murphy (at pages 475D and 481D) was
not enthusiastic about leaving in place this relic of an unfortunate and
messy period of development of our law of tort.!© But if it is good law (and
it will be necessary to go to the House of Lords to show otherwise) it would
seem that there is a continuing liability in tort owed by an architect or
engineer to take reasonable care not to cause economic loss and this might
extend to the specification of dangerous materials which the employer was
obliged to take out or neutralise in order to avoid risk of injury.'”
In the speeches in D&F and Murphy, reference is made to the decision
of the Supreme Court of Canada of Rivtow Marine v Washington Iron
Works ([{1973] 6 WWR 692) and particularly the dissenting judgment of
Laskin J which had been adopted by Lord Wilberforce in Anns. In Rivtow,
the Plaintiffs were the owners of a log barge (the “‘Rivtow Carrier’’) fitted
with two cranes. The Defendants discovered that cracks were appearing in
the legs of the cranes. Not later than February of 1966, they knew that if
not repaired they might lead to collapse of the cranes. In September of
1966 an almost identical crane owned by another logging company col-
lapsed, killing its operator. The Rivtow Carrier was ordered back to its

duty to warn Rivtow of defects which were likely to cause serious injury if
not corrected even if no injury occurred and that the additional loss of
profits resulting from having to undertake this work at an inconvenient

In Hobbs (Farms) v Baxenden Chemicals,2© which is referred to in re-

was subsequently killed or injured. A question arises as to whether a cause
of action in tort would arise if, on receipt of a warning, a farmer suffered
loss of profits or additional costs if he was obliged to take farm buildings
out of use. If Rivtow is good law it would seem that such a claim might be
made but if a large number of claims might result the public policy “‘flood-
gates” drawn shut in such cases as Spartan Steel v Martin?! might close
again. See further The Zinnia, referred to below.

Hobbs is the latest in a series of English cases concerning the contractual
duty to warn. Two other relevant cases were reported in 1984.22 In Victoria
University of Manchester v Hugh Wilson?3 His Honour Judge Newey held
that an architect was entitled to use novel materials or techniques but that he
should warn his client specifically of what he was doing. He held further that
it was an implied term of the construction contract that the contractor would
warn the employer of defects in the design of the works which they believed
to exist. In Stag Line v Tyne Shiprepair Group (‘‘The Zinnia’’)?4 Staughton J
considered the liability of shiprepairers, inter alia, to warn shipowners when
they discovered, after completion of repairs, that the wrong material had
been used in the relining of the stern tube with a resulting risk of possible
danger to life if the wrong material was not discovered. At page 218
Staughton J found this to be a “novel concept that a contractor who has
broken his contract should be under a duty to inform the other party”. He
went on to reflect upon the self interest of the contractor:
“At

Use of Dangerous Materials 283
installer of a structure or materials which are subsequently discovered to be
dangerous owes a continuing duty to the owner or occupier of premises
affected to warn them of the danger. If additional loss is suffered as a result
of a failure to warn (such as loss of profits if a store is obliged to close for
repairs which could have been carried out before opening or at a better
time) a separate cause of action may arise even if the terms of contract
exclude liability for the cost of repairs themselves.
It may be of interest to compare the conclusion I have reached in the
above paragraph and the terms of Clause 30.9 of JCT 80. If a final certi-
ficate is ‘‘conclusive evidence’”’ that the quality of materials is to the reason-
able satisfaction of the Architect, what duty is owed by the contractor who
discovers that materials used are dangerous to life and the architect could
not reasonably be satisfied as to their use? Is there a continuing duty upon
the contractor to warn of the risk? I suggest that there is such an obligation.
The important difference between Hobbs and Stag Lines and the Man-
chester case is that in Hobbs the obligation extended to a large number of
customers who might only be traceable by advertisement or otherwise by
great effort and who may have purchased the dangerous product many
years before. Also, in that case, the manufacturer was, or appears to have
been, a contracting party. What is the position when a subcontractor sup-
plies dangerous equipment or materials? Suppose, for example, that a
manufacturer of electricity cables subsequently discovers defects in insula-
tion materials leading him to conclude that short circuits may occur in
buried cables which might lead to fires in occupied office buildings. If a fire
occurs and people are injured they may have a claim against him if he knew
of the risk but failed to take steps to warn those responsible for main-
tenance of the building. But suppose a fire does occur in one building and
as a result the owners of many others are obliged to replace the electric
cabling (probably at huge expense). Was the manufacturer obliged in con-
tract to warn his purchaser? Or can he rely upon his terms of supply which
may limit his liability to replacement of defective material? Further, and
applying Rivtow, can the owners claim directly if as a result of a failure to
warn they have suffered greater expense than would have resulted from
more timely notice?

the principle of Rylands v Fletcher?® did not apply because there had been

is injured as a result (unless the circumstances come within the ambit of the
Consumer Protection Act). The exclusion of civil liability for breach of the
wider duties under the Health and Safety at Work Act gives parliamentary
support for this denial of strict liability.

was properly brought within the law of nuisance or was made under the
rule in Rylands v Fletcher, a necessary ingredient of the cause of action was
that the type of damage suffered was foreseeable at the time when the
relevant things were being collected on the defendants’ land. It seems that

foreseeable and as a result although pools of neat perchloroethylene con-

law to the water company. It will be disappointing for environmentalists
that ignorance of the risk of pollution will be a defence to such claims.
However, if damage had been foreseeable, the passage of years would
not of itself have provided a defence. Nuisance gives rise to a continuing
liability. If the use or effect of escape of a substance results in interference
with the use of my neighbour’s land then for so long as the nuisance
continues, fresh damage is suffered and fresh claims can be made. Thus an
act many years ago29 may result in modern—and expensive—claims. It is
important, therefore, to consider the long term effects on neighbouring
land and property when determining the use of property or when planning
its demolition.3°

13. European Commission Green Paper on
remedying environmental damage
John Uff
Introduction
This Paper is an initial response addressed primarily from the viewpoint of
consultants and advisers on construction projects. The question addressed
is the possible impact under UK law on the liability of such professionals
and their likely response in terms of protection and avoidance of liability.
The principle adopted in the Green Paper is “the polluter pays”. Yet many
or most polluters will at some stage have taken professional advice and so
the question necessarily arises, who ultimately pays? or perhaps, who is to
be regarded as the polluter? A summary of the Green Paper is included as
an Appendix.
Basis of liability
The Green Paper adopts the approach of individual civil liability in private
law as the proposed basis for recovery of appropriate compensation. This
limits recovery to property in respect of which there exists some legal
interest, so that property which is not ‘“‘owned”’ (such as the sea and the
atmosphere) would have to be governed by other conventions. One of the
principal objectives of the proposed Community action is to facilitate trade
throughout the Community without distortion of competition, which would
be the case where there existed substantial differences of liability for pollu-
tion in different Member States. Thus, the Green Paper concerns itself
primarily with rights of and between individuals.
The proposed
	basis of individual
	liability
may
	be fault-based
	or
strict.
Such
distinction
is
well
understood under
UK
	law, but the position
may
be
otherwise elsewhere
	in the
	community.
	In
making
	the choice
	between
fault-based
or strict liability
it is
relevant
to
have regard
to the difficulties
of
establishing
	fault in particular
	cases,
	e.g.
	where expensive and
	time-
consuming
	testing and monitoring
	is
required, and where
	the picture
	is
complicated by
past, even
	historical, activities on a site.
Conversely,
	strict
liability
may be said to stifle
innovation and industry.
One advantage of a
statutory approach to such liability is that its basis can be defined with these
287

-- 323 of 360 --

288 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
difficulties and their practical consequences in mind. The availability of
insurance is also a major factor to be considered.
In many cases there will be more than one person potentially liable.
Depending upon decisions as to limitation and cut-offs, there could be a
number of persons attracting primary liability who in turn would seek to
pass on liability to others responsible to them, and at the same time claim
contribution from others against whom proceedings are, or could be,
brought. Such a situation clearly militates against simple fault-based lia-
bility and in favour of some stricter well-defined system of liability. The
Green Paper does not discuss the procedural mechanismnis for the recovery
of compensation, but the principle of subsidiarity would doubtless require
this to be through national court systems. If a material volume of litigation
were to result, the UK courts would become overburdened, and the parties
seriously prejudiced, unless some comparatively simple and uniform basis
of liability were established.
One important aspect of such liability would be to establish throughout
the Community not only a common basis of liability, but common pro-
cedural rules covering, e.g. third party and contribution proceedings, so as
to discourage “‘forum-shopping”’ where (as will often be the case) potential
defendants are multi-nationally based. As with other EC legislation, such
as the directive on product liability, individual national laws will remain in
place, at least in the short and medium term. Thus, a polluter or other
person liable to or for a polluter will also face potential liability under a
battery of existing laws. In the UK, this will cover the common law of
nuisance, negligence and other torts as well as substantial areas of statutory
liability, principally the Public Health Acts and the Environment Protec-
tion Act. Thus, whether and how any proposed new basis of liability would
succeed in eliminating distortion of trade between Member States must be
open to serious question.
Problems of legal definition
The
	terms
	“damage”
	and
“environment”
	invite definition.
	As
to
the
for-
mer
	there
are a
wide
variety of possible
approaches. The
hint of actual
or
potential “pollution”
	in
any degree
	is
likely to
have
	an
adverse
	effect
on
the valuation of any property,
at least in areas of sensitivity,
such
as
hous-ing.
Such damage
is
often
ephemeral and
illusory,
although
real
enough
to
a
vendor who
	receives
	less
money
	on account of possible pollution.
	It is
thought that a
more robust and commercially sound definition is
needed,
and one that at least takes into account everyday levels of pollution which

-- 324 of 360 --

Commission Green Paper 289
are or must be tolerated. Recent UK legislation contains some interesting
examples.!
The definition of ‘“environment” raises directly the issue as to what is to
be protected: merely human life and artefacts or cultural heritage, plant
and animal life and the wider environment including the seas and the
atmosphere? The existence of other regulatory systems is relevant to the
appropriate scope of any Community directive.”
Establishing liability will require proof of causation. There may be.alter-
native possible causes of damage. As with the question of liability, there
are potential problems of inconsistency between the laws and procedures
of different Community members and further complications where several
different parties are alleged to bear responsibility. Again, there is a strong
case for devising a relatively direct and uniform approach to the question in
order to avoid excessive reliance on scientific evidence perhaps on behalf
of one or some, but not all, of the parties alleged to be responsible.
Existing environmental legislation
Any new proposals must be viewed against existing legislation and,
perhaps even more important, against proposals in the pipeline. The his-
tory of environmental legislation in the UK and elsewhere over the past
two decades gives a strong impression of major and far-reaching proposals
being hastily assembled only to meet with delays, impediment and block-
ages at various stages prior to implementation; and sometimes to signifi-
cant lack of support even after implementation. Furthermore, the
increasing overlap between UK domestic legislation and European Com-
munity requirements poses increasing problems of duplication and even
inconsistency, creating initiatives which might be seen as pulling in dif-
ferent directions. Given that there is a limitation upon available resources,
any division of opinion as to whether efforts should be devoted e.g. to
avoidance of pollution or towards adequate compensation after pollution,
must be seen as potentially inhibiting of serious progress. The Green Paper
addresses itself exclusively to the latter remedy. Other agencies address the
former or both.
Examples
	of lack of financial
or political
commitment
	to
new
	initiatives
which
have gained widespread support
are
numerous.
	In the
UK,
the
new
regime
for
health and
safety
at
work
commencing
with the
Act
of 1974
is a
continuing example;
	likewise the legislation
providing
for
civil liability for

The position of professional advisers
Foregoing discussion sketches the background against which professionals
are liable to face censure and proceedings arising from environmental
damage. While the professional adviser will ordinarily be concerned with
particular activities in a particular location, the nature of pollution is such
that it is readily and sometimes uncontrollably transferred from one medium
to another e.g. terrestrial pollution readily becomes pollution of the water or
the atmosphere. Such transfer renders the consequences of pollution wide-
spread and potentially outside the ambit of the commission undertaken by the
professional in the first place. This problem will not be tackled by concentrat-
ing on remedying the damage and providing compensation to persons suffer-
ing damage. Only action at the source of the pollution can prevent its spread.
To express the point in another way, environment protection is said to be
a holistic concept requiring all aspects of a problem to be considered.
Professional terms of engagement and practice, however, focus on particu-
lar matters upon which advice or other services are to be given. Further, in
order to avoid possible consequences of the UK Unfair Contract Terms
Act 1977, a person seeking to limit the area of his responsibility should be
advised to do so in terms of defining (and limiting) the areas and issues for
which he is responsible, rather than limiting or excluding responsibility for
the consequences of his actions. This may be seen as directly inconsistent
with a holistic approach to the problem of environment.
The Green
	Paper
makes
	several
references
	to
channelling
liability.
This
apparently
refers
to
some advance
designation
or
determination
of the

of

presumably between
	the potentially polluting
parties.
It is
said
to
have
	the
advantage
	of directing

	having
	the

	of

	the relevant

clear that a
great deal of

liability
under

let

all
Member
	States.
