292 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
The basis of professional liability
Professional liability on the basis of reasonable skill and care is firmly
entrenched in English law!! and UK insurance practices are equally firmly
based on the same principles.!2 In the Bolam case Mr. Justice McNair (who
was addressing a jury) gave what is still regarded as the classic exposition of
professional negligence in the following terms:
‘
‘... where you get a situation which involves the use of some special skill or
competence, then the test as to whether there has been negligence or not is not
the test of the man on top of the Clapham omnibus, because he has not got this
special skill. The test is the standard of the ordinary skilled man exercising and
professing to have that special skill. A man need not possess the highest expert
skill; it is well-established law that it is sufficient if he exercises the ordinary skill
of an ordinary competent man exercising that particular art.”
With very few exceptions, this passage represents the law applied in
cases where contractual (or, where applicable, tortious!) negligence is
alleged against a professional person, whether that person is to be regarded
as a person of ordinary professional competence or special competence. In
the case of Argyll v Beuselinck'4 an attempt to render a solicitor liable in
negligence on the basis of a higher standard of expertise than that of
ordinary competence was unsuccessful, and Bolam has continued to be
regarded as the classic exposition under English law.
There is, however, a further possible exception to the Bolam principle
which might be relied upon. While the Green Paper is silent on the
potential position of professional advisers, the discussion on strict lia-
bility inevitably raises the possibility of an application of the case of
Greaves v Baynham Meikle.'> This is the case in which an engineer was
held liable on the basis of fitness for purpose when undertaking design
work for a “‘package deal” contractor, who was himself liable on the same
basis.'© It is difficult to say that the Greaves case embodied any point of
principle other than the finding that in those particular circumstances the
intention of the parties was that the duty undertaken by the engineer
(and therefore his liability) was to mirror that of his principal. In his
judgment, however, Lord Denning raised a number of different ap-
proaches when considering whether to uphold the finding of the judge of
a “higher duty”’. He said:
11 Bolam v Friern Hospital Mgt Com. [1957] 1 WLR 582.
12 But compare Wimpey v Poole (1985) 27 BLR 58.
13 See per Lord Keith in Murphy v Brentwood DC [1991] 1 AC 398.
14 [1972] 2 Lloyd’s Rep 172.
15 [1975] 1 WLR 1095.
16 Cf. Wimpey v Poole supra.

-- 328 of 360 --

Commission Green Paper 293
“The law does not usually imply a warranty that (the professional man) will
achieve the desired result, but only a term that he will use reasonable care and
skill. The surgeon does not warrant that he will cure the patient. Nor does the
solicitor warrant that he will win the case. But when a dentist agrees to make a set
of false teeth for a patient, there is an implied warranty that they will fit his gums:
see Samuels v Davis.”’!7
What then is the position when an architect or an engineer is employed
to design a house or a bridge? Is he under an implied warranty that, if the
work is carried out to his design, it will be reasonably fit for the purpose?
Or is he only under a duty to use reasonable care and skill? That question
may require to be answered some day as a matter of law. But in the present
case I do not think we need answer it. For the evidence shows that both
parties were of one mind on the matter. Their common intention was that
the engineer should design a warehouse that would be fit for the purpose
for which it was required. That common intention gives rise to a term
implied in fact.
Later in his judgment Lord Denning quoted from Bolam and continued:
“In applying that test it must be remembered that the measures to be taken by a
professional man depend on the circumstances of the case. Although the judge
talked about a ‘higher duty’, I feel sure that what he meant was that in the
circumstances of this case special steps were necessary in order to fulfil the duty
of care: see Readhead v Midland Railway Co.”’'®
There appear
to
be
no
reported
cases since
Greaves
in
which
the
“higher
duty”
	has
been
	applied.
	Nevertheless,
	it
remains open
	to
argument
	that
similar
circumstances
should
give
rise
to
a
degree
of
liability
commensurate
with
that
of the
party
to
whom
	the services
are
being rendered.
	It
can
be
argued
that
there
are
many
	instances
where
there
is
no such
coincidence
of
liability,
an
obvious
	example
	being under
	the ‘“‘Excepted
	Risks”
	in
clause
20(2) of the
	ICE
	Conditions
	of
Contract,
	where
	“fault
	defect
	error
	or
omission
in
the
design
of the
works”
is
excepted from
the
contractor’s
risk,
even
	though
	the
engineer
	is
unlikely
	to
be
under
	the
same
	liability. It is
therefore
open

argument whether
in
a
particular

absence

	a
professional adviser should be taken
	to
assume
	the
same
	responsibility
for
damage
	or loss
as
his
principal.
As
	already noted, ordinarily
	a

	indemnity
	insurer
	will
not
cover
	liability
beyond
	reasonable
	skill
and
	care.
	Recent experience
	with
collateral
warranties has reinforced the interest of insurers in
defining
their
own degree
	of
risk
and
	in
discouraging
	their
assureds from undertaking
commitments beyond
	their level of insurance.
	It is
thus likely that the
17 [1943] KB 526.
18 (1869) LR 4 QB 379, 393.

combination of insurance interests and advice from professional bodies will
result in professional advisers seeking to limit their liability, at least in the
UK, to reasonable skill and care or other equivalent defined levels of
liability, whatever definition of liability is eventually decided upon in ac-
cordance with the EC proposals. This will have a number of important
consequences:
(1) It will be vital for site owners to ensure that they are adequately or
effectively insured, since they are unlikely to be able simply to rely
upon their professionals to maintain effective cover against any latent
damage;
(2) Attempts to involve professionals in litigation will inevitably run into
the problems discussed above, namely the involvement of substantial
expert and scientific evidence and arguments on liability and
causation.
The
Green
Paper
does
not discuss
contracts
as
such,
but there
will
clearly
be
in
existence
	a
number
	of contracts,
some
	of
which
will
contain
	arbitra-

agreements. Outside
	the
UK,
	these
	will
be
enforceable
	as
of

under
the legislation
equivalent
to
the
Arbitration
	Act
1975.19
Again,
if

were intended
	to
provide
	a

	of

	of

meanswould
have
to
be

to

In the
light
of the
above,
it

	there can
be created

	of access
	to

	unless
through some form
of pro-ject
or

the insurer

if

	through
	protracted
	litigation
or
arbitration.
	The
	avail-
ability
of effective
insurance,
at
least
in
the

be

to
serious
doubt
in
the
light

present reaction
of the

industry.
The
alternative
may
have
to
be
a
statutory fund.
Compensation funds
The Green Paper
	discusses existing
	compensation
	funds established
	in
various countries including
Sweden, France and Holland.
	One
	of the best
publicised
schemes
is
the
US
“‘Superfund” administered by
the
US
Federal
Environment
	Protection Agency,
	set
up
in 1970.
The fund
is
provided by
taxes on potentially polluting industries and
	is
used
	to
respond
	to short-
term emergencies, compensation being recovered when
	potentially
	liable
parties can be identified.
Enforcement
	action
is
taken both
at
Federal and
19 Enacting provisions of the New York Convention 1958.

-- 330 of 360 --

Commission Green Paper 295
State level. It is said that about 70% of enforcement actions are at State
level.2° This statistic could be seen as confirming the viability of sub-
sidiarity as one of the aims of the European initiative. Another well-
established compensation system is the International Fund for Compensa-
tion for Oil Pollution Damage. This was established in 1991 through an
international convention, complementing the 1969 Brussels Convention
placing liability on shipowners. This fund again is established by private
industry contributions. There are other existing proposals within the EC
for environmental compensation funds.
Discussion
The paper does not really focus on how civil liability would interact with
either the proposed environmental fund or the idea of “channelled” liability.
The system would in fact operate at distinctly different points in time e.g:
(1) A fund would be available for immediate compensation, but there
would need to be mechanisms for recovery of monies paid out from
those liable, as under the US Federal scheme.
(2) A liability scheme involving channelled or predetermined liability
would operate reasonably quickly if liability were strict, e.g. under
existing civil procedure, a substantial proportion of the liability could
be enforced within a reasonably short time by summary judgment.?!
(3) If payment were dependent on claims under insurance policies, there
would certainly be potentially substantial delays in a number of cases.
Likewise, if liability were fault-based, litigation would inevitably be
protracted.
(4) The ultimate settlement of liability through various contribution pro-
ceedings, possibly including subrogated actions, would take their usual
course through the courts of member countries, probably taking in
England a minimum of 2 years and a maximum of substantially more
than that. In addition, civil procedure in different Member States is
subject to very substantial variation e.g. between the English adver-
sarial system with experts appointed by the parties, and the French
inquisitorial system relying heavily on a court-appointed expert.
What
	are the
consequences
	for
professionals involved
	in
commissions
which might give
rise to
pollution and
	liability
under
	the
proposed new
20 Appendix 1 to
paper on Environment
Issues by Wilder and Plant, 1992,
LSE Conference.
21 RSC Orders 14 and/or 29.

296 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
regime? The danger is that they might become involved as potential con-
tributors to claims arising from the new basis of liability, where such claims
would not have existed under present law. The new claims could arise
from:
(a) redefined and wider liability, especially on the part of the person to
whom liability is channelled;
(b) liability for wider ranges of damage;
(c) compensation payments giving rise to damages which would not have
been suffered but for the wider basis of liability.
A crucial matter not discussed in the Green Paper is the ambit of the
parties who would be covered by any new liability directive. It is implicit
that the Green Paper is directed primarily at “the polluter” and the discus-
sion indicates that this is intended to be the commercial party undertaking
the hazardous or polluting operation such as the oil company or industrial
plant operator. It is unclear whether there is an intention to include profes-
sionals within the wider basis of liability. There is perhaps less concern
about environmental consultants and similar organisations whose principal
activity is to investigate, control or remove pollution. Persons taking on
such work at least do so knowing the consequences of error or failure.
More concern is merited by consultants such as engineers, architects and
surveyors, as well as contractors, who undertake work within their ordinary
competence which might give rise to pollution, immediately or some time
in the future, as a result of some act or omission done with no proper
knowledge of its consequences. The imposition of liability on such persons
whether strict or fault-based, will have far-reaching effects, particularly if
they do not or cannot obtain insurance cover corresponding to the new
level of liability proposed by the Green Paper.
Assuming
	that
only
the “client”
	is
the
party
who
	will
be subject
to
the
newly-defined
	specific
liability
the
question
is
then
what degree
	of conse-
quent contributing
	liability
might
become
	in effect
imposed
	on
or
soughtagainst consultants.
There
can
be no
doubt
that
primary
liability
attaching
to
the
client
could spark
off
attempts
to
claim
indemnity
on the basis of the
Greaves
	case
	discussed above.
	In addition, there
	would
	be potential
	lia-
bility
attaching
to
the giving,
or failure to give,
appropriate advice
as to
the
potential
	liability
of the client
under
	the
new European
	directive.
	There
could be
little
doubt
that
enhanced
	liability
could spark
off
higher
levels of
contribution being sought from professionals.
It is
therefore appropriate
	to raise again the
	argument
	in favour of
removing
	at least advisors from the whole ambit of potential liability for
pollution. Such liability is inevitably based on the availability of insurance

-- 332 of 360 --

Commission Green Paper 297
which must ultimately be paid for through enhanced fees. Few professional
firms have assets which would make proceedings worthwhile without insur-
ance being available. The expressed aim of the Green Paper of creating
readily available compensation is more likely to be achieved by properly
structured project-based insurance22 which specifically excludes claims
against professionals: to permit such claims simply divides the risk between
policies contingent (usually) upon whether the professional can be shown
to have been negligent, invariably through protracted litigation which the
Green Paper would seek to avoid.
Conclusion
The Report suggests an integrated liability regime whereby compensation
could be sought against individual parties, if identified, on the basis of
liability as discussed in the Green Paper; while a joint compensation mech-
anism would be available as a fallback. What does not appear to be
discussed is:
(1) Whether action would be pursued by individuals suffering damage or
by an agency such as the EEA.
(2) Whether there would be a fund to provide for immediate compensa-
tion and clean-up with actions being pursued subsequently.
(3)
	The Green
	Paper
discusses
	civil
liability
only,
whereas
	the
US
	EPA
has criminal
jurisdiction
also.
The
	creation
	of
anything
	other than fault-based
	liability will
inevitably
result in
disparity
between
	liability
and
the
owner
	or
promoter
and
that
of
professionals
(and
contractors)
employed by
him.
The
only
viable
means
of
recovering
compensation
	without protracted
litigation
is
through means
	of
project
	or
site-based
	insurance
	or
compensation
	funds.
	Subsequent
	sub-
rogated
litigation
would
	prove
	enormously complex
	and
costly.
It is
to
be
doubted
	that the
involvement
	of
professionals
at this
stage,
merely
for
the
purpose
	of
recovering through
their insurers,
will
ever
be
cost-effective.

Appendix
Summary of Green Paper
Section I introduces the subject and the reasons for the Community now
considering action, which is essentially:
(a) perceived public demand;
(b) requests within the Commission;
(c) action by other national and international bodies; and
(d) distortion of competition through the different systems that presently
exist in Member States. .
The Paper addresses two questions only:
(1) the basis of civil liability (no consideration is given to criminal or
other liability);
(2) remedying damage not met by the application of civil liability
principles.
Section
	2
discusses
	fault-based
	liability
and
	strict liability.
Multiplicity
	of
liable
parties
will
often
be
a
problem.
The
type
of
damage
	to
be
protected
against
	is
considered and
	also
	whether
	limitation on
	liability
	should be
accepted.
	Defining
	“environment”
	and
	“damage”
	for
the
purpose
	of
any
legislative
proposals
is
discussed,
together
with
the
role
of
insurance.
Approaches
	at
national
	and
	international
	level are
discussed.
	A
recur-
rent
theme
is
the
‘“‘channelling”’
of
liability
e.g.
under
the
Brussels
Conven-
tion
whereby
	the
owner
	of
a
ship
at
the
moment
	of an
incident
is
liable to
the
exclusion
	of others
who
	might
be
sued.
Under
	the
Council
	of
Europe
Convention
	liability
is
channelled
	to
the
person having
control.
	It is
con-
sidered
	that
	such provisions should
	not
	affect
	subsequent
	civil
	actions
under appropriate
	national laws
	to
recover
	compensation
	based
	on such
channelled
	liability.
The Green Paper
thus
envisages
a
system under which
some designated
	party
	would
	be primarily
	liable
	on
	a
	statutory
	basis
(perhaps
similar
to
the
product
liability
regime).
Section 3 proceeds
to
discuss joint
compensation systems which
are said to
be “insurance-like”. The Green Paper envisages
	a
fund similar to
other
national and international arrangements, which would be available in thefirst
place for
emergency
	action. This system
	is seen, however,
	as incon-
sistent with the ‘‘polluter pays” principle. It is noted that proposals already

-- 334 of 360 --

Commission Green Paper 299
exist for the establishment of European funds for compensation for en-
vironmental damage caused by waste and landfill. Any new proposals
would need to take account of existing policy.
Section 4 suggests a combination of civil liability (whether fault-based or
strict) together with a compensation system or fund where civil liability
cannot effect restoration or compensation.

14 The Cambridge Water Company case—a
postscript
Michael Renger and Victoria Phillips
The Conference on Construction, Environment and the Law was held
before the appeal to the House of Lords had been heard in the Cambridge
Water Company case. This note updates the legal position following the
House of Lords’ judgment delivered on 9 December 1993.
Introduction
The House of Lords’ judgment in the case of Cambridge Water Company
Limited v Eastern Counties Leatherwork PLC reversed the Court of Ap-
peal’s £1 million damages award against a tannery that had polluted ground-
water with solvents. This outcome should not, however, fool anyone into
imagining that common law actions in the environmental field will no longer
be brought. The judgment has done much to clarify the circumstances in
which actions in nuisance and under the rule in Rylands v Fletcher can be a
successful vehicle for the recovery of damages. Where the circumstances are
right, this flashing of green lights by the highest judicial authority possible
will increase the likelihood of actions being brought, although litigation will
now be unlikely in circumstances where pollution damage was not foresee-
able at the time when the actions leading to it occurred.
The House
	of Lords’ response
	to
the issues
outlined
in
Part I
As
a
preliminary,
	the
Lords,
	whose
	collective
	opinion was expressed
	by
Lord Goff
of
Chievely,
	stated
	that, strictly
speaking,
	Cambridge
	Water’s
appeal
to
the
Court
of
Appeal
should have been decided under
the rule in
Rylands
	v
Fletcher since
	Cambridge Water
	had appealed
	on
	this basis.
However,
	it
said that the similarities
	between
	the
two
	torts
nuisance and
Rylands
v
Fletcher
were such
that
it
would
discuss
both
in
its
judgment.
Is there
any requirement for
damage
	to
be foreseeable
in a
nuisance claim?
The Lords expressed the view
that the Court of
Appeal had wrongly
inter-
preted the Victorian case, Ballard
v
Tomlinson (29 Ch.D. 115).
The
	case
should not have been taken as authority for a statement that it is not
necessary to show that a defendant foresaw or could have foreseen the
301

-- 337 of 360 --

302 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
possibility
that
groundwater
	would
	be polluted
	at
the
time
of
the
events
that led
to
the
pollution.
	It
should only
have
	been
	taken
	as
authority
	for
holding
that
a
landowner
	has
a
right
to
abstract
water percolating
beneath
its
land
and
that
neighbouring
landowners
with
similar
rights
to
abstract
do
not
	have
	the
right
to
pollute
	that
water.
	Although
	the
right
to
abstract
clean
water
can
be
protected
by
an
injunction
or
action
for
damages,
	such
protection
can
only be given
if
all
the
other
elements
for
a
successful action
based
on
nuisance
or
the
rule
in
Rylands
v
Fletcher
are
present.
Having
dismissed Ballard
v
Tomlinson,
	the
Lords went
on
to state
most
firmly
that in
a
nuisance
	action
a
defendant
	should
	not
be
held
liable
for
damage
	of
a
type
which
he
could
not
reasonably have
foreseen.
	It
pointedout
that
this
had been
the
conclusion
in
an
authoritative
	case
on
nuisance,
The
Wagon
Mound
	(No
2)
[1967] 1
AC
	617,
which
the
Court
of
Appeal
in
the
Cambridge
	Water
case
had omitted
	to
consider.
	If
foreseeability
were
not the
rule
it
would
	be
easier
for
a
plaintiff
to
recover
	damages
	for inter-
ference with
his
property
(generally
an
action
in
nuisance)
than
to
recover
damages
	for
personal
injuries
suffered (generally
an
action
under
the
tort
of
negligence
where
it
is
well
established
	that
the
plaintiff
must prove
that
the
type
of
damage
	caused
was foreseeable
	in
order
to
succeed).
Can
it
be
‘damage’
if
nobody
is
aware
of
it
at
the
time
it
occurs?
The
Lords
	did not
answer
	this
question
directly.
However
	it
did
hold
that
there
could
not
be
an
actionable
nuisance
	until
damage
had
occurred,
and,
relating
this
principle
to
the
present
	case,
it
decided
	that
no
damage
	had
occurred
	until
the
regulations
	implementing
	the
EC
	directive
	came
	into
force.
	In
this
case
	awareness
	of the
damage
	preceded
	the
date
	that
	the
regulations
came
	into force
(although awareness
	was
	apparently
triggered
by
the
Department
	of the
Environment’s
	advice
	which
	was based
	on the
fact
that the
regulations were forthcoming).
However,
	the
Lords went
	on
to
hold
that,
provided
	the action
was
	not
brought
until after
the
damage
	had occurred,
the
fact that the
damage
	did
not occur
until
some
	time
after
the actions
which
	led to
it
did not matter.
Therefore
	an action
	will
not
	be precluded by
a
lack of
awareness
	that
damage
has
been caused
when
it first
occurs.
What
is
the
meaning of
‘non-natural
user’ in
an action
under
the rule in
Rylands
v
Fletcher
The Lords departed from the very restricted
meaning
	that has been given
to the phrase ‘“‘non-natural user” in past cases, including the High Court
judgment in the Cambridge Water case.

-- 338 of 360 --

Cambridge Water Company 303
In the High Court Kennedy J. had stated that using solvents in a tannery
in a village where the inhabitants relied on the tannery for employment
could not be regarded as non-natural. The Lords disagreed stating that
“the storage of substantial quantities of chemicals on industrial premises
should be regarded as an almost classic case of non-natural use.”
The comparable principle under nuisance was also mentioned, although
it was not directly discussed in the context of this case. The Lords stated
that liability is restricted by “‘the principle of reasonable user — the princi-
ple of give and take as between neighbouring occupiers of land’’. If the use
to which the defendant puts his land is reasonable, he cannot be liable for
harm to his neighbour’s enjoyment of his own land. However if the use is
not reasonable he can be liable.
Should liability under the rule in Rylands v Fletcher be limited to situations
where the type of damage caused was foreseeable?
Having found that foreseeability was an element in nuisance, the Lords
went on to assert that foreseeability of the type of damage is also a require-
ment for recovery in an action based on the rule in Rylands v Fletcher.
However the Lords were obviously aware that they were adopting a
pragmatic approach rather than a strictly legalistic one. The relevant part
of Lord Goff’s judgment is peppered with slightly apologetic statements
attempting to justify the realignment, such as “it appears to me to be
appropriate now to take the view”’.
Foreseeability and the passage of time
It is
clear that the
	PCE
	which escaped during
	the
	1970s
	had
	gradually
accumulated
in
large
pools
at
the
base
of the
chalk aquifer
beneath
Eastern
Counties’ premises and
is
still
dissolving slowly
into the
groundwater.
Cambridge Water attempted
	to
	argue
	that,
	even
	if
damage
	was
	not
foreseeable
	at
the
time
the
PCE
	was
	spilled,
Eastern Counties should be
liable for
damage
	which occurred from
	the date on
which
	it
subsequently
foresaw
or
should have been
able
to
foresee
that
damage. Arguably,
in
this
case
this
might be from
the date
that the
EC
legislation
came
	into
effect.
In
other pollution
cases the relevant point
in
time
might be the
point
at
which
the
detrimental
effects of the relevant substances on the
environment were
generally known.
The Lords dismissed Cambridge Water’s argument, holding
	that
“long
before the relevant legislation
came
	into force the
PCE
	had become
	irre-
trievably lost in the ground below.” This aspect of the judgment
is
somewhat
puzzling since it appears that it
would have been possible in 1985 (and would

-- 339 of 360 --

304 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
still be possible today) to extract PCE from the groundwater by pumping it
out from below ground using boreholes on Eastern Counties’ premises.
However, it appears that the central point which the Lords wished to
make is that foreseeability must be measured at the time when the actions
leading to the pollution are carried out and cannot be reassessed at a later
date when the defendant is merely continuing to harbour the conditions for
causing pollution on his property.
This is somewhat difficult to reconcile with earlier cases such as Sedleigh-
Denfield v O’Callaghan [1940] AC 880 which state that even if a defendant
has not created the nuisance, once he knows or should reasonably know of
the existence of a nuisance being caused by conditions on his property he is
under a duty to take reasonable measures to bring it to an end. What is
reasonable appears to depend on a number of factors, including the costs of
the work and the financial resources of the parties involved.
It is
likely that
this
point
will
be
argued
	again
in
more
	detail in
future
pollution
cases.
The implications of the judgment
The foreseeability test
The
	foreseeability
	test
now
	appears
	to
be
firmly
established
	for
nuisance
actions,
although
	its
place
in
actions
under
	the
rule in
Rylands
	v
Fletcher
may
be
less
secure.
Many
will
see
the
decision
of the
Lords
to state
that
foreseeability of the
type of
damage
is
a
requirement
for
success
in
an action
under
the rule
as
a
logical
way
of restating
that
aspect
of the
tort
which
	holds
	the
defendant
liable
	for the
	‘natural
	and
	anticipated
	consequences” where
	he
	has
brought
	onto
	his
land material
	which
	is
“likely
to
do
harm”
	if it
escapes.
Law
	students,
	lawyers and
their clients
will
no
	doubt
	be
grateful
	for
the
simplification:
it is
easier
to
predict
liability
if
Rylands
v
Fletcher
is
brought
closer
to
nuisance and negligence.
However,
the issue
may
well
be
reopened
in
the future
by
those suggest-ing, for
example,
	that the rule in
Rylands
	v
Fletcher requires only
fore-
seeability of
some
	direct
damage
	to
neighbouring
	land
in the event of an
escape, rather than foreseeability of the type of
damage
caused.
It is
interesting
to
note that
Kennedy
J.,
who
agreed
that foreseeability of
the type of
damage
	was
a
necessary prerequisite
	for
nuisance and foundthat
it
had not been established on the facts in the
Cambridge Water Com-
pany
	case,
	appears
	to
have reached
a
different result
when applying the
Rylands
	v Fletcher criteria.
	He dismissed the action under the rule in
Rylands v Fletcher only on the basis that there had been no non-natural

-- 340 of 360 --

Cambridge Water Company 305
user, stating ‘‘I would have held that some damage was foreseeable from
the escape of the stored quantity of organochlorine . . . |here contemplate
direct damage to neighbouring land.” |
The general implications of applying the foreseeability test are obvious.
Knowledge about the effects of many substances on the environment has
developed rapidly over the last twenty years or so. Companies whose actions
during the “pre-enlightenment”’ period led to damage are less likely to be
found liable because the type of damage which they caused was not reason-
ably foreseeable at that time. Companies which have caused pollution via
more recent actions will not necessarily be able to argue that the type of
damage which they have caused was not foreseeable at the time of the
actions that led to the damage. Potential liability will have to be examined in
the light of the particular substances and activities in question in each case.
In the future, plaintiffs will doubtless pay very careful attention to the
evidence which they produce showing the state of knowledge at the relev-
ant time. The decision that the damage caused by Eastern Counties was not
foreseeable was a decision on the facts of the case taken by the High Court
judge who considered the evidence presented to him back in 1991. His
decision was not reopened by either the Court of Appeal or the House of
Lords who deal only with matters of law. It is not clear exactly what
evidence about the pollution of groundwater by solvents the High Court
heard. It will certainly be open to future plaintiffs to argue that knowledge
of the problem was much more widespread than is reflected in the
Cambridge Water Company judgments, especially when research from the
United States which was freely available is considered.
As more and more companies which carry out environmentally haz-
ardous activities employ well-qualified specialist environmental managers
and nominate directors to take control of environmental affairs the courts
will also become less tolerant of defendants who plead that it was reason-
able for their knowledge to fall short of state of the art. It will no longer be
the site foreman or works supervisor whose foresight is assessed. This will
also make it harder to argue that damage was not reasonably foreseeable.
The damage test
The
	use of the date regulations
	come
	into force
	as
	the
	point
	where
“damage”
	crystallises
	and
	an action
	becomes
	sustainable,
	is
likely to
be
disputed
if it is
used
as a
benchmark
	in future cases.
Environmental
	science
often
	moves
	well
	ahead of regulatory standards and
	plaintiffs will fre-
quently be aggrieved
at the spread of pollution onto their land or into their
water supply despite the fact that there are no relevant regulations forbid-
ding the presence of the relevant substances.

-- 341 of 360 --

306 Liabilities, Remedies and Policy
The user test
The Lords appear to have greatly widened the potential for using the rule
in Rylands v Fletcher in environmental cases by departing from the re-
stricted meaning given to the phrase ‘“‘non-natural user”’ in previous cases.
Presumably the test of reasonable user under nuisance and the test of
natural user will be linked in the future and it will be difficult to plead that
any industrial use involving chemicals is a “‘reasonable user’.
The policy angle
Although the English common law is a separate system operating indepen-
dently of statute, the Lords were obviously acutely aware of the wider debate
concerning liability for activities which may pollute the environment. The
European Commission has recommended moving towards a no-fault ap-
proach in a Green Paper on the subject! and the House of Lords Select
Committee on the European Communities printed a report responding to
this just five days after the judgment in the Cambridge Water Company case.2
Lord Goff pointed
out
that
“‘as
a
general
rule,
it
is
more
	appropriate
for
Strict
liability
in
respect
of
operations
of
high
risk to
be
imposed by
Parlia-
ment,
than
by
the
courts.
If
such
liability
is
imposed by
statute,
the
relevant
activities
	can
	be
	identified,
	and
	those
	concerned
	can
	know
	where
	they
stand.
Furthermore,
	statute
can
where
	appropriate
	lay
down
	precise
crite-
ria
establishing
the
incidence
and scope
of
such
liability”.
He
	also
	expressed
	the
	view
	that
	the
common
	law should
	not
	impose
liability
for
damage
	which had occurred
	before
	legislation
which
classified
it
as
damage
	took
place,
stating:
“I
wish
to
add
that the
present
case
may
be
regarded
as
one
of
what
is
nowadays
called historic
pollution,
in
the sense
that
the
relevant
occurrence
(the
seepage
of
PCE
	through
the
floor
of
[Eastern Counties’] premises) took place before
the
relevant
	legislation
	came
	into force;
	and
	it
appears
	that,
	under
	the
	current
philosophy,
	it is
not
	envisaged
	that
statutory
	liability
should be
imposed
	for
historic
pollution
.
.
.
If so,
it
would
be strange
if
liability
for
such pollution
were
to arise
under
a
principle of
common
	law”.
This attitude
is
sure
to
rub
off
on
Lord
	Goff’s judicial
brethren. Future
common
	law
cases are
unlikely
to
tighten
up
the
liabilities
imposed by
the
common
	law
whilst legislation on the
same
	subject
is
pending.
1 7099/93;
COM(93)47
	final
	-
Communication
	from
the
Commission
	to
the
Council and
European Parliament and
the
Economic and
Social
Committee: Green Paper
on
Remedy-ing Environmental Damage.

-- 342 of 360 --

Cambridge Water Company 307
Conclusion
The judgment has buried one of the major concerns of industry, its insurers
and bankers: being asked to pay for historic pollution which occurred in an
earlier less environmentally conscious era when damage was not reason-
ably foreseeable.
However potential plaintiffs will be encouraged by the hefty blow dealt
to the ‘“‘non-natural” use line of defence and reassured by the new “‘cross-
tort’ nature of the foreseeability test. They now know that they must
concentrate their efforts on proving causation and showing that defendants
lacked the level of environmental awareness that should have been shown
by a responsible operator in their particular industry sphere.
The ‘‘lost” pollution argument seems unlikely to survive for long in an
era of rapidly improving standards of environmental management. Future
EC driven legislation may also lead to the imposition of no-fault,
no-foreseeability liability in any event.

AND
THE ENVIRONMENT
Edited by John Uff
Helen Garthwaite, John Barber
Centre of Construction Law and Management

	are
	now

	and

	process.

	have

examined
	to

raised
by
these
changes.
